# 1 Kings

ANNOTATIONS ON THE FIRST BOOK OF THE KINGS

COMMONLY CALLED THE THIRD BOOK OF THE KINGS

## THE ARGUMENT OF THE TWO BOOKS OF THE KINGS

The books of the Kings record a history of four hundred thirty-eight years, whereof Solomon reigned forty over all the twelve tribes; but after his reign those tribes were divided into two kingdoms: Two tribes, Judah and Benjamin, made one kingdom, called Judah; wherein the posterity of David and Solomon reigned three hundred seventy and two years. In which time nineteen kings of the same stock succeeded each other: Only one interruption was made for the space of six years by Athalia an usurper, 2 King. 11.3. Of the kings of Judah some were very good, others idolaters. The other ten tribes made another kingdom, called Israel, which continued but two hundred thirty and seven years, till the sixth of Hezekiah, when Hoshea the last king of Israel was carried away captive, 2 King. 17.6. In that time there were twenty kings of Israel, and ten several stocks, whereof one destroyed another. For first, Jeroboam’s stock was destroyed by Baasha, 1 King. 15.29; secondly, Baasha’s by Zimri, 1 King. 16.11; thirdly, Zimri’s, and fourthly, Tibni’s by Omri, 1 King. 16.17, 18.22; fifthly, Omri’s by Jehu, 2 King. 10.11; sixthly, Jehu’s by Shallum, 2 King. 15.10; seventhly, Shallum’s by Menahem, 2 King. 15.14; eighthly, Menahem’s by Pekah, 2 King. 15.25; ninthly, Pekah’s by Hoshea, 2 King. 15.30; tenthly, Hoshea was carried away captive by Salmaneser, 2 King. 17.3.6. In this time of twenty kings over Israel there were but eleven over Judah: Whereof one (namely Asa) reigned in the time of eight several kings of Israel, 1 King. 15.9 and 16.29, of which were the first five several stocks: And another, namely Azariah or Uzziah reigned in the time of six other kings of Israel, 2 King. 15. v. 27, of which were four other stocks. From the division of the ten tribes to the captivity of Israel, there was not one good king of Israel. That kingdom remained idolatrous from the beginning to the end. The kingdom of Judah continued one hundred thirty and five years after the captivity of Israel, from the sixth of Hezekiah, 2 King. 18.10, to the last of Zedekiah, 2 King. 25.2. Twenty-six years after the captivity of Zedekiah mention is made of lifting up Jehoiachin, 2 King. 25.27, which fill up the history of four hundred thirty and eight years.

## THE ARGUMENT OF THE FIRST BOOK OF THE KINGS

The first book of the Kings records part of the forementioned history, from the end of David’s reign to the end of Jehoshaphat’s, which was the space of one hundred and eighteen years: Wherein we have David’s last acts; Solomon’s glorious reign; the division of the twelve tribes into two kingdoms; the reign of the first four kings of Judah, and eight of Israel, after the said division.

## THE TITLE

*{First}* So in the Hebrew title.

*{Kings}* It contains a history of kings.

*{Third}* The LXX, the vulgar Latin and old English translators call the two books of Samuel, Kings, because they set down the history of the two first kings, Saul and David. After that account this is called the third.

# CHAPTER 1

*{Now king David was old}* He was seventy years old, as appears 2 Sam. 5.4. He was thirty years old before he came to the crown, and he held the crown forty years. This was the greatest age that any king of Judah or Israel attained unto.

*{and stricken in years}* Heb. *entered into days*. The days or time of departing out of this world are here meant.

*{and they covered him with clothes}* This they did, to increase heat much decayed in him. For clothes may increase heat, where any is; but they cannot beget heat where none is.

*{but he gat no heat}* His natural heat was spent by travails, labors and afflictions: Otherwise he might longer have retained heat and strength, as well as others. For many others, even in David’s time, lived much above seventy years, and yet retained good vigor and strength.

**V. 2.** *{Wherefore his servants said unto him}* These *his servants* were, as Josephus says, physicians. Physicians, by reason of their study and experience can best tell what is fittest, as for others, so for old men, in any kind of malady. Because kings use to have certain peculiar physicians to attend their persons, and on all occasions to give advice about their bodily health, and to minister physic unto them, they are called *his servants*. We use to call them the king’s physicians.

*{Let there be sought}* Heb. *let them seek*. Them who are officers, and appointed messengers of the king, 1 Sam. 16.16.

*{for my lord the king}* This was a title of reverence given to a king, in testimony of his sovereignty; and expressed in the singular number, though his servants, in the plural number, said it; because it was the mind of every one of them.

*{a young virgin}* Heb. *a damsel, a virgin*. The Hebrew word which signifies *damsel*, is well translated *young*. For the heat of young maids does most naturally work heat in old and cold bodies when they embrace one another. David’s wives and concubines now living, were all ancient, and in that respect not fit for that purpose for which this damsel was provided. As she was to be young, so also a virgin; because married women by bearing and nursing children do impair their natural heat. Besides a virgin was fittest in this manner to be associated to the king.

*{and let her stand before the king}* To minister and do service unto him. So is this phrase expounded, ver. 4.15. Deut. 10.8. 1 Sam. 16.22. 1 King. 10.8. Prov. 22.29.

*{and let her cherish him}* Heb. *be a cherisher unto him*. By providing and bringing the king such savory and pleasant things as he may well relish and taste delight in; and also by lying in bed with him.

*{and let her lie in thy bosom}* This was a wife’s privilege, Gen. 16.5. Deut. 13.6. Mich. 7.5. It is supposed that she was married to David before she lay with him.

*{that my lord the king may get heat}* Nothing could do it more kindly.

**V. 3.** *{So they sought for a fair damsel}* They sought till they found one fit for their purpose.

*{throughout all the coasts}* They left no tribe, no city, no village, no house, in which they inquired not for such an one; at least, where they had cause to search. Or by a proverbial speech it may imply a diligent search.

*{of Israel}* They would not go out of the church to seek a fit nurse for the king of Israel.

*{and found Abishag a Shunammite}* Of the city Shunem, in the tribe of Issachar, Josh. 19.18. There was another woman so styled, and that upon the same ground, 2 King. 4.8,25.

*{and brought her to the king}* For this was the end of their seeking.

**V. 4.** *{And the damsel was very fair}* This made the king to take the more delight in her.

*{and cherished the king}* See ver. 2.

*{and ministered unto him}* By doing the office of a nurse-keeper.

*{but the king knew her not}* By reason of his frigidity. This is a modest and seemly phrase, whereby both matrimonial, and also all other copulation of man and woman is set out, Mat. 1.25. Judg. 19.25. Yea, it is used to set out the filthy sin of man with man, Gen. 19.5. Judg. 19.22. See Gen. 4.1.

**V. 5.** *{Then Adonijah the son of Haggith}* This was the eldest son of David then living, 2 Sam. 3.4. There were indeed three sons of David elder than Adonijah, namely, Amnon, Chileab, and Absalom, 2 Sam. 3.2,3. But Amnon was slain by his brother Absalom’s servants, 2 Sam. 13.29. And Absalom was slain by Joab’s servants, 2 Sam. 18.15. And it is supposed that Chileab died before this. His mother’s name Haggith is set down for distinction’s sake. The mothers of all David’s sons born in Hebron are by name expressed. They were six in number, and had each of them a several mother, 2 Sam. 3.2, &c. There is nothing anywhere said of this Haggith, but that she was David’s wife and Adonijah’s mother. The former was a great honor to her: The latter if she were living at this time, or if she had foreseen this act of her son, would have been a great grief unto her.

*{exalted himself}* Though he were the eldest son, yet in aspiring to the crown he exalted himself; because David had declared Solomon to be designed king by God himself, 1 Chro. 22.10.

*{saying, I will be king}* Heb. *reign*. This he did while his father was yet living. His plot and practice therefore was treason, though he had a right to the kingdom after his father’s death.

*{and he prepared him chariots, &c.}* Herein he did as Absalom had done before in a like case, 2 Sam. 15.1. The particulars mentioned were evidences of an aspiring haughty mind. Of these words see chap. 9.19.

**V. 6.** *{And his father had not displeased him}* By any rebuke, or otherwise restraining him from his undue courses.

*{at any time}* Heb. *from his days*. Neither in his younger nor elder years.

*{saying, Why hast thou done so?}* These words show that David failed in reproving his son when he had just cause so to do. Here also may be intended David’s failing to punish his son, when he committed notorious crimes. This was Eli’s fault, 1 Sam. 2.29.

*{And he also was a very goodly man}* He as well as Absalom, 2 Sam. 14.25. He was goodly in that he was fair in face, comely in countenance, and proper in body. This questionless was one cause, yea, a principal cause, that David so doted on those two wicked sons, Absalom, and Adonijah.

*{and his mother bare him after Absalom}* Another mother than Absalom’s is here meant: for Absalom’s mother was Maacah, and Adonijah’s, Haggith, 2 Sam. 3.3,4. Now because David had no son by any wife or concubine betwixt these two, Adonijah’s mother is said to bear him after Absalom.

**V. 7.** *{And he conferred with Joab}* Heb. *his words were with Joab*. He consulted with Joab and others, how to bring the plot to pass. The same Hebrew word which is here used, is in such a sense used, Numb. 31.16, and translated *counsel*.

*{Joab the son of Zeruiah}* 2 Sam. 2.13. 1 Chro. 3.16. & 11.6. This Joab was general of David’s army, 2 Sam. 8.16.

*{and with Abiathar the priest}* He was high priest: Therefore Solomon when he put down Abiathar, instituted Zadok in his room, chap. 2.35.

*{And they following Adonijah, helped him}* Heb. *helped after Adonijah*. They hearkened to Adonijah’s motion, ver. 5. 2 Sam. 2.10.

**V. 8.** *{But Zadok the priest}* He was priest next in order to Abiathar.

*{and Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* This man was chief captain and commander over choice soldiers. See 2 Sam. 8.18. 1 Chron. 11.22.

*{and Nathan the prophet}* Nathan was a man whom David was wont to consult with about the things of the Lord, 2 Sam. 7.2, and by whom God used to declare his mind unto David, 2 Sam. 12.1.

*{and Shimei}* This Shimei may be he who cursed David, 2 Sam. 16.5, &c. and whom David pardoned, 2 Sam. 19.23. That great favor which David therein showed him, might move him to cleave close to David, and to refuse to associate himself with those who sought to take the crown from the king in his lifetime. Or that Shimei may be the kinsman who is mentioned ch. 4.18.

*{and Rei}* This was some statesman, of whom there is no other mention made.

*{and the mighty men which belonged to David}* These were the guard or garrison-soldiers under Benaiah, called Cherethites and Pelethites, 2 Sam. 20.23, who remained faithful to David in Absalom’s rebellion, 2 Sam. 15.18. Of these see 2 Sam. 8.18. See also of them ver. 38.

*{were not with Adonijah}* They could not be seduced to take part in this treason.

**V. 9.** *{And Adonijah slew sheep and oxen}* Under these two kinds may be comprised all manner of cattle, whether of the flocks or of the herds, which were fit for a sacrifice or for a feast: for they were slain for both these ends. So Gen. 31.54.

*{and fat cattle}* This clause is added to show that they slew the best they had. Under the forementioned kinds may be comprised all manner of clean fowls.

*{by the stone of Zoheleth, which is by En-rogel}* Or, *the well Rogel*. This was without Jerusalem, 2 Sam. 17.17, on the east side, Joh. 15.7.

*{and called}* Invited to his feast, thereby to win them to his side: for this is an ordinary way of insinuating one’s self into others.

*{all his brethren the king’s sons}* All except Solomon. It seems that all David’s sons here mentioned more respected Adonijah than Solomon.

*{and all the men of Judah the king’s servants}* Out of this general must be excepted the mighty men, ver. 8, and the servants hereafter mentioned, ver. 33.

**V. 10.** *{But Nathan the prophet, and Benaiah, and the mighty men, and Solomon his brother}* Of these see ver. 8,26,32.

*{he called not}* For he had no hope to win any of these to his side.

**V. 11.** *{Wherefore Nathan spake unto Bathsheba the mother of Solomon, saying}* Nathan knowing that God had appointed Solomon to be king, uses means to bring it to pass. That Nathan knew that Solomon should succeed David in the throne, will evidently appear, by comparing 2 Sam. 7.12.13 with 1 Chron. 22.8,9,10.

*{Hast thou not heard that Adonijah the son of Haggith}* 2 Sam. 3.4. He names his mother, to inflame the zeal of Bathsheba the more: and for that end he propounds the matter interrogatively: for certainly she had heard of it.

*{doth reign}* Has taken upon him to be king, and goes about to settle himself on the throne.

*{and David our lord knoweth it not?}* David being so weak, as he is said to be, ver. 1, might well be ignorant of those things which fell out, though they were publicly done. But this Nathan adds, to show that all that was done might be undone, because it was done without the consent of the living king.

**V. 12.** *{And now therefore come, let me I pray thee, give thee counsel}* He uses this preface to move the rather to hearken to his counsel.

*{that thou mayest save thine own life}* Heb. *soul*.

*{and the life of thy son Solomon}* Nathan well knew that usurpers would do what they could to destroy such as have a right claim, and also their friends.

**V. 13.** *{Go and get thee in unto king David}* He well knew that the disposing of the crown rested in David, and that all the people would be ready to follow that course which he should direct them unto.

*{and say unto him}* Nobody’s words could more prevail with David than Bathsheba’s, especially in this case wherein David had engaged himself unto her, ver. 30.

*{Didst not thou, my lord, O king}* This title she uses as a subject and as a wife. See 1 Pet. 3.6.

*{swear}* We do not read of any such oath made by David in his former history. But all particulars concerning David were not written. See Joh. 20.30. David himself confesses that he did so swear, v. 30, and this oath may be comprised under that phrase that is used, 2 Sam. 12.24, of David’s comforting Bathsheba. For to swear that Solomon should succeed him in the throne, must needs be a great comfort to her.

*{unto thine handmaid}* This is a title of reverence, 1 Sam. 25.24,41. See ver. 16. Though she were his wife, yet being his subject, she was his handmaid.

*{saying, assuredly Solomon thy son shall reign after me, and he shall sit upon my throne}* This was the substance of David’s oath. The former phrase of reigning, sets out the substance of the promise; the other of sitting on the throne, the evidence thereof.

*{Why then doth Adonijah reign?}* This being contrary to David’s promise, she is advised to set it down so as it might make him the more earnest in putting down Adonijah.

**V. 14.** *{Behold, while thou yet talkest there with the king, I also will come in after thee}* Nathan so disposes the carriage of this business as the king might not discern it to be a plot.

*{and confirm thy words}* Heb. *fill up*. He promises to ratify the truth of what she should say, and further to add what he should conceive requisite to be added.

**V. 15.** *{And Bathsheba went in unto the king into the chamber}* For the king then kept his chamber.

*{and the king was very old}* See ver. 1.

*{and Abishag the Shunammite}* See ver. 3.

*{ministered unto the king}* See ver. 4.

**V. 16.** *{And Bathsheba bowed, and did obeisance unto the king}* She presented herself to him in such an humble and reverent gesture as became a wife and subject. See ver. 13.

*{and the king said, What wouldest thou?}* Heb. *what to thee?* This implies a readiness in the king to grant what she should desire.

**V. 17.** *{And she said unto him, My lord}* See ver. 13.

*{thou swarest by the LORD}* Such as fear God, use to swear by no other, Deut. 6.13. This will make them more careful to keep their oath, and fearful of breaking it.

*{thy God}* This relative is to be taken inclusively, not excluding others. See Joh. 20.17. She thus applies this relation to the king, to move him the more to make good his oath. See chap. 8.28. 1 Chro. 15.2.

*{unto thine handmaid, saying, Assuredly, &c.}* See ver. 13.

**V. 18.** *{And now behold, Adonijah reigneth}* Endeavors to reign, and carries himself as king.

*{and now my lord the king}* See ver. 13.

*{thou knowest it not}* This she adds, to free herself from undue suspicion of David’s consent to that which Adonijah had done, and to aggravate Adonijah’s usurpation the more, in that he presumed to sit on the king’s throne without the king’s knowledge.

**V. 19.** *{And he hath slain oxen, &c.}* See ver. 7,8,9.

*{but Solomon thy servant}* As she styled herself David’s handmaid, v. 13, so here she styles her son David’s servant, in way of reverence, and to testify both her and his subjection to David.

**V. 20.** *{And thou}* Or, *and now*.

*{my lord, O king}* See ver. 13.

*{the eyes of all Israel are upon thee, &c.}* All expect whom you will declare to be your successor in the throne. Thus they who expect any favors from God, are said to have their eyes on God, Psal. 25.15. & 123.2. This she adds to put David in mind, that if he once declared his will, and directly set down who should succeed him in the throne, the people would be ready to accept and acknowledge that man to be their king. This by experience is verified ver. 40.

**V. 21.** *{Otherwise it shall come to pass, when my lord the king shall sleep}* Shall be dead and laid in grave, Gen. 47.30. See chap. 2.10.

*{with his fathers}* As his fathers and predecessors before him.

*{that I and my son Solomon shall be accounted offenders}* Heb. *sinners*. See Gen. 43.9. She meant such offenders as should be accounted traitors and conspirators against him who had a just title to the crown, being the eldest surviving son of the king; and in that respect be put to death: so as both infamy and violent death might be feared.

**V. 22.** *{And lo while she yet talked with the king, Nathan the prophet also came in}* This was so ordered, as David might not suspect the matter to be beforehand plotted between them; though indeed it was so, v. 14.

**V. 23.** *{And they told the king, saying, Behold, Nathan the prophet}* They who attended the king, gave notice to him of the prophet’s coming, before he himself saw him. They might well think that such a prophet came upon some special errand. Of Nathan see v. 8.

*{And when he was come in before the king}* As Bathsheba was going out, Nathan came in, v. 28. See v. 32.

*{he bowed himself before the king with his face to the ground}* In the lowliest manner that he could, according to the custom of those times, he yielded civil obeisance to his sovereign. A prophetical function exempts not from civil subjection.

**V. 24.** *{And Nathan said, My lord, O king}* With gesture and speech he manifests his dutiful respect to his sovereign.

*{hast thou said?}* I cannot let it sink into my mind, that you should give approbation to that which is done.

*{Adonijah shall reign, &c.}* See v. 13.

**V. 25.** *{For he is gone down this day}* They went from Jerusalem, which was on a hill, and therefore may be said to go down.

*{and hath slain oxen, &c.}* See v. 9.

*{and behold they eat and drink before him}* They feast together. See v. 9,41.

*{and say, God save king Adonijah}* Heb. *Let king Adonijah live*. This was an usual acclamation at the election or coronation of a king, v. 34. 1 Sam. 10.24. 2 Kings 11.12.

**V. 26.** *{But me, even me thy servant}* This doubling of the word imports that Adonijah had no good meaning in passing by the Lord’s prophet. Here again the prophet acknowledges his subjection unto his sovereign, under this title servant.

*{and Zadok the priest, and Benaiah, &c.}* See v. 8.

*{and thy servant Solomon}* Solomon was before this designed king: yet while the old king lived, he was his subject and servant. Nathan here gives him this title; to show that Solomon had no aspiring mind, but was willing to continue a loyal subject so long as David should live.

*{hath he not called}* See v. 10.

**V. 27.** *{Is this thing done by my lord the king?}* He propounds this question, the more to stir up David’s indignation against Adonijah’s fact.

*{and thou hast not shewed it unto thy servant}* David was wont to communicate his weighty counsels to Nathan, 2 Sam. 7.2, and thereupon Nathan seems to wonder, that David should conceal so weighty a matter as that from him.

*{who should sit on the throne, &c.}* See v. 13.

**V. 28.** *{Then king David answered, and said, Call me Bathsheba}* By Nathan’s pressing the same thing upon David that Bathsheba had done, v. 17, &c. the king is made the more forward to give Bathsheba satisfaction.

*{and she came into the king’s presence}* Heb. *before the king*.

*{and stood before the king}* Bathsheba and Nathan came in course to the king one after another, as appears by comparing ver. 14,22,32 together. It is probable that Nathan was he to whom David said, *Call me Bathsheba*; and that he went out to call her in: who being come in stood before the king, to expect what he would determine concerning Solomon and Adonijah.

**V. 29.** *{And the king sware, and said, As the LORD liveth}* Heb. *the Lord lives*. This was an usual form of an oath, Judg. 8.19. Forms of oaths use to be concise. The first particle *as* is prefixed by our English, to make up the sense. The next verse shows, that this oath is a confirmation of a former oath which he had made to this very purpose. He does thereby manifest his unalterable resolution concerning Solomon: the rather, because Solomon was appointed even by God himself to succeed David in the throne, 2 Sam. 7.12. 1 Chro. 28.5. Hereby also David does more settle the mind and heart of Bathsheba, and frees her from her former fears; See v. 17.

*{that hath redeemed my soul out of all distress}* He means hereby temporal deliverances from external dangers: which he does the rather mention in this his oath, to show the great and weighty reason that he had to perform what he had sworn to the Lord.

**V. 30.** *{Even as I sware to thee}* See v. 17.

*{by the LORD God of Israel}* See c. 8.15.

*{saying, Assuredly Solomon thy son, &c.}* See v. 13.

*{even so will I certainly do this day}* David understood that Adonijah had proceeded so far, as no delay, for determining the question in hand, must be made: and therefore he assures her that he will do what she desired instantly, and not put it off one day.

**V. 31.** *{And Bathsheba bowed, &c.}* See v. 16,23.

*{let my lord king David}* See v. 13.

*{live for ever}* Very long. This was an usual salutation given to kings, Neh. 2.3. Dan. 6.21. It is here used by Bathsheba, to show that she preferred David’s life and reign before her son’s.

**V. 32.** *{And king David said, Call me Zadok, &c.}* See v. 8. It appears that Nathan being sent to call in Bathsheba, left her alone with the king, that they might not seem to plot together. David here calls for a priest, a prophet, and a great commander in war, that the priest might declare the meaning of the law, and the prophet the special mind of God, and the commander in war might put their advice in execution. The king calls for those three, because he had ever found them faithful to himself, and perceived that they had not followed Adonijah, as others had done.

*{and they came before the king}* They were the more ready to come, because they all agreed in the same case.

**V. 33.** *{The king also said unto them}* This copulative also has relation to the king’s calling them in. He did both call them in, and also give them direction.

*{Take with you the servants of your lord}* He means such as abode faithful to David, and were special attendants on him, and his guard, v. 8. In the Hebrew it is, *your lords*, in the plural number: whereby he may mean his own and his son Solomon’s servants. Because many nobles, soldiers, and common people did accompany Adonijah, David wisely advises to take a sufficient company of faithful and valiant soldiers, that might be able to defend Solomon against all Adonijah’s company; and also for the greater honor of his son.

*{and cause Solomon my son to ride upon mine own mule}* Heb. *which belonged unto me*. It was a royal honor to ride upon a mule, Esth. 6.8, and David would have Solomon set upon his own mule, to show that it was with his own consent. Of mule see chap. 10.25.

*{and bring him down to Gihon}* Gihon was a famous place, so called, by reason of the river Gihon, 2 Chro. 32.30. & 33.14. It was so far distant from En-rogel, as the two companies might be a good distance the one from the other, Gihon was on the west side of the city over against En-rogel, v. 9.

**V. 34.** *{And let Zadok the priest and Nathan the prophet}* Both these are used for the greater solemnity of that which was done.

*{anoint him there king over Israel}* This was an usual rite of inaugurating kings; especially in troublesome times, 1 Sam. 10.1. & 16.1. 2 King. 9.1. & 23.30. It was used in doubtful cases, to show that they were deputed of God to that high calling; and that God would pour his Spirit upon them, and enable them to that whereunto he appointed them.

*{and blow ye with trumpets}* This added much to the solemnity. See 2 Sam. 15.10. 2 King. 9.13. & 11.12,14.

*{and say, God save king Solomon}* Heb. *let king Solomon live*. See v. 25.

**V. 35.** *{Then ye shall come up after him}* As attendants upon him, and maintainers of what is done.

*{that he may come and sit upon my throne}* To be declared thereby to be the deputed king.

*{for he shall be king in my stead}* When I am taken out of this world, or am disabled from ruling any more.

*{and I have appointed him to be ruler}* What better warrant could they have? It was before said, v. 20, that the eyes of all Israel were upon David, that he should tell them who should sit on the throne after him. This they did the rather expect from David, because they knew him to be a wise man; such an one as could best judge which of his sons were fittest to succeed him; and also because he had before this told them that God had chosen Solomon to succeed him, 1 Chron. 28.5.

*{over Israel and over Judah}* Judah is distinctly named, for excellency’s sake; and the rather, because David was of the tribe of Judah. Besides, there were emulations betwixt the ten tribes and Judah, 2 Sam. 19.41, before the division of the kingdoms, 1 King. 12.19.

**V. 36.** *{And Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* See v. 8.

*{answered the king and said}* In his own and the others’ name, as Mat. 16.16.

*{Amen}* This is an Hebrew word which imports an approbation, ratification and earnest desire of all that whereunto it is annexed: thereupon it uses to be added to prayers.

*{the LORD God of my lord the king}* This is not to be taken exclusively, as if the Lord were only the God of David, and not of others: but it is thus applied to David, to show that the king and all his people might be assured that God would ratify and bless what he had done, because the Lord had by many evidences showed himself to be in special manner the God of David, and David was one of God’s peculiar people, and special confederates.

*{say so too}* These words are an exposition of that title, *Amen*: that God would accomplish that which the king had said. For God’s words are deeds, Gen. 1.3. Psal. 33.9.

**V. 37.** *{As the LORD hath been with my lord the king, even so be he with Solomon}* In directing, protecting, prospering, and bestowing all kinds of blessings.

*{and make his throne greater than the throne of my lord king David}* Increase of blessing must ever be prayed for, ver. 47. And it is no discouragement to a good father, who has been endowed with many excellent gifts, and has received many special blessings from God, to pray that his son may be more excellent, and more abundantly blessed of God, than he.

**V. 38.** *{So Zadok, &c.}* See v. 8.

*{and the Cherethites and Pelethites went down}* These were the mighty men and king’s servants before mentioned, v. 8,10,33, such as waited on the king to guard his person, 2 Sam. 8.18 and 15.18. The Cherethites and Pelethites have their names from certain places, where they or their progenitors had their abode. The Cherethites were of the out-coasts of the Philistines, as appears, Ezek. 25.16. Zeph. 2.5. 1 Sam. 30.14.16. The Pelethites also might also be of some bordering coasts. The forementioned out-coasts of the Philistines were brought into the confines of the Israelites, being subdued by them. It appears that they were very valiant men, and taken to be attendants upon the king’s person, and proved always faithful to him. In the insurrection of Absalom they held close to David, 2 Sam. 15.18. They were sent against Sheba the son of Bichri, another traitor that rose against David, 2 Sam. 20.7. And here they leave Adonijah and remain with David. The Chaldee paraphrast takes the Cherethites for *bowmen* or *archers*, and the Pelethites for *slingers*, such as the Benjaminites were, Judg. 20.16. 1 Chro. 12.2.

*{And caused Solomon to ride upon king David’s mule, &c.}* See v. 33.

**V. 39.** *{And Zadok the priest took an horn of oil}* An horn was a fit vessel as to hold oil, so to pour it softly on one’s head.

*{out of the tabernacle}* To show that it was holy oil, as Psal. 89.20. Of the tabernacle, see ch. 8.4. See on 1 Sam. 10.1.

*{and anointed Solomon}* See v. 34. Solomon was after this anointed again. See the reasons thereof 1 Chron. 29.22.

*{And they blew the trumpet}* See v. 34.

*{and all the people said, God save king Solomon}* See v. 25. This shows that the people’s hearts were so set on David, as they rejoiced in him whom he should appoint his successor.

**V. 40.** *{And all the people came up after him}* They would not leave him after he was anointed.

*{and the people piped with pipes}* Or, *flutes*.

*{and rejoiced with great joy}* There was a special work of God’s Spirit in them, that so quickened up their spirits. See 1 Chro. 12.40.

*{so that the earth rent with the sound of them}* This is an hyperbolical speech, to amplify the exceeding great rejoicing and loud acclamations of the people. Reports of great cannons will make mountains shake and cleave: so will great thunderclaps. By this exceeding great rejoicing they testified that high esteem which they had of Solomon, and their good affection to David; and also, a persuasion that Solomon being proclaimed king in his father’s lifetime, all contentions and civil wars about the crown would be prevented, and that they should live in quiet and enjoy many blessings under Solomon. Their acclamations might be the greater, to affright Adonijah and all that took part with him the more.

**V. 41.** *{And Adonijah and all the guests that were with him heard it}* This is an evidence of the people’s extraordinary shoutings.

*{as they had made an end of eating}* After the foresaid acclamations their feasts were soon ended, and their rejoicing was soon turned into terror. This sound was like the handwriting which appeared to Belshazzar at his riotous feast, Dan. 5.5.

*{and when Joab heard the sound of the trumpet}* See v. 34.

*{he said, Wherefore is this noise of the city, being in an uproar?}* It belonged to Joab’s office to suppress tumults, as Acts 21.31,22. For Joab was the general of the army, 1 Chron. 11.6.

**V. 42.** *{And while he yet spake, behold, Jonathan the son of Abiathar the priest came}* Jonathan was a nimble man, and of a quick spirit, and oft employed to inquire after news, and bring tidings. This was he who together with Ahimaaz the son of Zadok was left by Enrogel, to espy Absalom’s proceedings, and to declare the same to David, 2 Sam. 17.17. Now that Abiathar is turned from David to Adonijah, it is probable that he appointed this his son to remain about king David’s court, to espy what he could about the case in hand. For we may observe, how he punctually tells the particulars that passed not only openly in the city, but also privately in David’s chamber, v. 43, &c.

*{and Adonijah said unto him, Come in, for thou art a valiant man}* Heb. *a man of virtue, *or,* a virtuous man*. The word is translated *virtuous*, being applied to a woman, Prov. 12.4. Such a man is styled *a good man*, 2 Sam. 18.27.

*{and bringest good tidings}* Adonijah herein flattered himself with vain hopes.

**V. 43.** *{And Jonathan answered, &c.}* At the first he tells Adonijah the main substance of the matter, which was clean contrary to the good tidings which he expected, namely, that David had made Solomon king.

**V. 44.** *{And the king hath sent with him Zadok, &c.}* See v. 34, &c.

**V. 45.** *{And Zadok the priest, &c.}* See v. 34.

*{and they are come up from thence rejoicing}* Both those whom king David employed about setting Solomon on the throne, and the people also there present gave apparent evidences of their joy therein, which could not but much damp the spirit of Adonijah, and the spirits of all that took part with him.

*{so that the city rang again}* This is another proverbial speech, whereby the greatness of their rejoicing is amplified. We say of loud sounds, That they make a place to ring again.

*{this is the noise that ye have heard}* This is a direct answer to that question which Joab propounds, v. 41.

**V. 46.** *{And also Solomon sitteth on the throne of the kingdom}* He has an actual possession of the kingdom: Whereby he shows that their endeavors to make Solomon king were not in vain.

**V. 47.** *{And moreover, the king’s servants}* He means hereby those who had great offices and commands under the king, and were employed about crowning king Solomon.

*{came to bless our lord king David}* i.e. to salute him, to pray for him, and to praise God for the successor who was appointed to reign after him, See v. 37.

*{God make the name of Solomon better than thy name}* This was the substance of the blessing wherewith they blessed David. By name they mean such virtues and graces as had made David famous; and such an increase of them in Solomon, as might make him more famous.

*{And make his throne greater than thy throne}* By throne is here meant royal pomp and glory, together with dominion. See v. 37.

*{And the king bowed himself upon the bed}* This he did in a religious manner, worshipping God, even in his bed, and praising him, as Gen. 47.31.

**V. 48.** *{And also thus said the king, Blessed be the LORD}* This shows that he bowed to God.

*{God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15.

*{which hath given one to sit on my throne this day}* Though Nathan plotted what was done, and David gave advice for the doing of it, yet David ascribes both plotting and acting all to God: For men’s purposes and performances are ordered by God.

*{mine eyes even seeing it}* This phrase implies the acting of what was done in his lifetime; though it is also probable, that they brought Solomon in his royal robes to David, and in that respect David may be said to see it with his eyes. Seeing with eyes is an Hebrew pleonasm, and imports emphasis.

**V. 49.** *{And all the guests that were with Adonijah were afraid}* Joy may soon be turned into fear, which quickly seizes on traitors.

*{and rose up}* This phrase imports speed and diligence.

*{and went every man his way}* To his own house and affairs, leaving Adonijah and his cause.

**V. 50.** *{Then Adonijah feared because of Solomon}* By reason of a guilty conscience he feared lest Solomon should take away his life; knowing that he had sinned against God, who appointed Solomon to be king; and had gone against David’s mind, who had manifested the will of God therein.

*{and arose, and went}* See v. 49.

*{and caught hold on the horns}* The altar was made with four horns, Exod. 38.2. To them they used to tie the sacrifices, Psal. 118.27. On them a man might lay fast hold.

*{of the altar}* They supposed that the altar was a place of refuge, Exod. 21.14, and that it had a privilege of immunity from punishment. At least Adonijah thought that he might gain time thereby to pacify his brother’s wrath (which he did, v. 52,53) and seek mercy of God, as Joab, ch. 2.30.

**V. 51.** *{And it was told Solomon, saying}* Solomon had those that espied what was done on Adonijah’s part, as well as Adonijah on Solomon’s part. See v. 42.

*{Behold, Adonijah feareth king Solomon}* See v. 42.

*{for lo, he hath caught hold on the horns of the altar}* This was an apparent evidence of fear.

*{saying, Let king Solomon}* Here he acknowledges Solomon to be king, and thereby bears witness against himself, that he was a traitor in aspiring to the kingdom.

*{swear unto me to day}* His guilty conscience could be satisfied with nothing but an oath.

*{that he will not slay}* Herein he acknowledges himself guilty of death.

*{his servant with the sword}* This title servant is an acknowledgment of subjection. But this acknowledgment arose from fear. Fear does not ordinarily work a true and sound subjection: It appears by the request that Adonijah made to Solomon’s mother, ch. 2.13, &c. That he still retained a turbulent mind and a traitorous heart, affecting the crown; though at this time he saw that in his father’s lifetime there was no hope of obtaining it.

**V. 52.** *{And Solomon said}* Solomon was ready to forgive his brother.

*{If he will shew himself a worthy man}* Heb. *a son of virtue*. If he will carry himself so as becomes his place, and show himself a loyal subject, he is pardoned upon his future good behaviors.

*{there shall not an hair of him fall to the earth}* This is a proverbial speech. The loss of an hair is the smallest loss that a man can have: Hereby is implied, That the least evil should not befall him for what he had done before. See 1 Sam. 14.45. 2 Sam. 14.11. Acts 27.34.

*{but if wickedness shall be found in him, he shall die}* No hope of escaping hereafter shall be given him. See ch. 2.23.

**V. 53.** *{So king Solomon sent}* A message of pardon.

*{and they brought him down from the altar}* Into the presence of Solomon. There were ascents up to the altar, and therefore he is said to be brought down from it.

*{and he came and bowed himself to king Solomon}* By an external complemental behavior he verified that subjection whereof he made acknowledgment under this title servant, v. 51. Hypocrites in outward show can carry themselves as those who are indeed true-hearted.

*{and Solomon said unto him}* He graciously accepts his person.

*{Go to thy house}* Attend on your own affairs, and meddle not with the affairs of the kingdom.

# CHAPTER 2

**V. 1.** *{Now the days of David drew nigh}* Life is in Scripture very frequently set out by days, to note out the brevity and uncertainty thereof, Gen. 3.14,17. & 5.5. See on Eccl. 8.8.

*{that he should die}* Words of dying men make deepest impressions, Gen. 49.1. Deut. 33.1. Josh. 23.2. Joh. 19.26,27. 2 Pet. 1.14,15.

*{and he charged Solomon}* He appointed sundry particular things to be done by Solomon after he should succeed him on the throne; and withal laid a fatherly command upon him, not to fail in the performance thereof. The generals of this charge are expressed v. 3, and sundry particulars v. 5, &c.

*{his son}* Being designed to succeed him in the kingdom, ch. 1.39, and by virtue thereof he had power to perform what was given him in charge.

**V. 2.** *{I go the way of all the earth}* Of all men living on the earth, Josh. 23.14. For *it is appointed unto men once to die*, Heb. 9.27.

*{be thou strong therefore}* Heb. *be a man*. Of a valiant mind, courageous; not fearing any opposition that should be made for doing the things that were given in charge. See Jam. 1.6, &c.

*{and shew thyself a man}* Though you be young in years, ch. 3.7. 1 Chron. 22.5, yet show yourself a man in understanding, 1 Cor. 14.20.

**V. 3.** *{and keep}* This implies faithfulness: Now faithfulness to God makes valiant.

*{the charge of the LORD}* That which God has given in charge to be kept. This is a general duty, under which the particulars following are comprised. That it is so, is evident by the manner of inferring the duties following, thus, *to walk*, &c.

*{thy God}* The Lord by choosing Solomon before all his brethren to be king, 1 Chron. 28.4,5, showed himself to be in special manner his God. This therefore does David press as a forcible motive.

*{to walk in his ways}* To observe that course of life which he has prescribed, See ch. 3.14.

*{to keep his statutes}* Such rites and ceremonies as he has enjoined.

*{and his commandments}* Contained in the moral law.

*{and his judgments}* For governing the state.

*{and his testimonies}* These were signs of the covenant betwixt God and them. Notwithstanding this distinction of the forementioned several points, they are oft in Scripture promiscuously used.

*{as it is written in the law of Moses}* Deut. 17.18,19. It is called *the law of Moses*, because God used his ministry in delivering the law to the people.

*{that thou mayest prosper}* Or, *do wisely*. Deut. 29.9. Josh. 1.7. To observe the forementioned charge, was the ready way to blessing, See 1 Chro. 22.11.

*{in all that thou doest}* God’s blessing extends itself to all good things that a man takes in hand.

*{and whithersoever thou turnest thyself}* Whether to worship God, or to exercise your kingly office.

**V. 4.** *{That the LORD may confirm}* To you and your posterity, age after age.

*{his word}* Or, *promise*. God’s word is oft limited to his promise, especially when his word is of some good thing to come.

*{which he spake concerning me}* Or, *unto me*. That good which is promised to one for the benefit of his posterity, does much concern himself; especially when it is made as a recompense of reward unto him.

*{saying, If thy children}* Promises of reward extended to children upon God’s accepting and approving their parents, are limited to children’s answerable disposition and conversation, ch. 8.25. & 9.4. Psal. 132.12. It was God’s promise with such a limitation, that Hezekiah pleaded, 2 Kings 20.3.

*{take heed}* So prone are men to swerve into by-courses, as there is great need of much circumspection to keep them in the right way.

*{to their ways}* By a way the course of one’s life is set forth; and that course which properly belongs to men by virtue of their calling, is their way.

*{to walk}* To walk is a natural action of the body, it intends motion and progress therein: For they who walk go one step after step. It sets out a continual doing of a thing.

*{before me}* As in my presence, setting me in their sight, and considering that I behold and take notice of them within and without; and thereupon not daring to do anything that may displease me.

*{in truth}* Sincerely, uprightly; not deceitfully, not hypocritically.

*{with all their heart, and with all their soul}* By heart and soul the several and distinct inward faculties of a man are meant, as understanding, will, and affections. All and every part of these must wholly be given to God, and no part of them to any other thing, Deut. 6.5. Josh. 22.5. 2 Kings 23.3,25.

*{there shall not fail thee (said he) a man on the throne of Israel}* Heb. *be cut off from thee, from the throne*, 2 Sam. 7.12. Your children shall by a lineal descent one after another succeed you in your kingdom. This succession long continued in his posterity after the flesh: But in that Christ, who also descended from him, had given unto him *the throne of David*, Luke 1.32, most properly and fully was this promise accomplished. Solomon David’s immediate son reigned over Israel and Judah both; but the posterity of Solomon reigned only over Judah, ch. 12.19,20.

**V. 5.** *{Moreover thou knowest also}* Here begins the first particular that David gives in charge to Solomon, See v. 1.

*{what Joab the son of Zeruiah}* See ch. 1.7. 1 Chron. 2.16.

*{did to me}* By breaking the agreement which David made with Abner, 2 Sam. 3.13, and with Amasa, 2 Sam. 19.13. Joab’s murdering these two might have tended to David’s dishonor and danger.

*{and what he did to the two captains of the host of Israel}* Under this word Israel all the tribes except Judah are comprised: for when Saul’s son held the kingdom against David, Judah held close to David, 2 Sam. 2.4.9. Judah also first brought David to his kingdom after he fled from Absalom, 2 Sam. 19.40,41.

*{unto Abner the son of Ner}* 2 Sam. 3.27.

*{and unto Amasa the son of Jether}* 2 Sam. 20.9, &c.

*{whom he slew}* This has relation to them both.

*{and shed}* Heb. *put*. This has relation to the last clause of this verse.

*{the blood of war in peace}* By doing hostile actions to confederates in time of peace, even when David had entered into covenant with them; so as there was peace betwixt David and them, 2 Sam. 3.27. & 20.9,10.

*{and put the blood of war}* A bloody sword used in war.

*{upon his girdle that was about his loins}* Into the scabbard that was on his thigh.

*{and in his shoes that were on his feet}* While he embraced the two captains aforesaid, he slew them, so as their blood ran into his shoes. His insolency is further set out by these phrases, in that he carried a bloody sword, and walked with blood in his shoes, as if he had done a worthy deed.

**V. 6.** *{Do therefore according to thy wisdom}* Wisely observe him; he is of a stirring spirit; so as you may sometime or other take just advantage against him, See v. 9.

*{and let not his hoar head}* This phrase implies old age; and Joab must now needs be very old, having been a general all David’s time, 2 Sam. 2.13. Yet would not David have him spared for his old age’s sake. Old age gives no dispensation or immunity from punishment to those that justly deserve it. David himself gives the reason why he himself executed not vengeance upon him, 2 Sam. 3.39. David at the times wherein those murders were committed, was in great troubles; and Joab being general of the host, had a great command over many soldiers. Besides his brother Abishai was also a great man in those days: so as prudence kept David from executing that vengeance in his own time, which now he saw might seasonably be done by his son. Joab had also now given another just occasion for Solomon to take away his life, namely, his conspiring with Adonijah.

*{go down to the grave in peace}* By a natural death without revenge: but cut him off by the sword of justice, See v. 9. See also Gen. 42.38.

**V. 7.** *{but shew kindness to the sons of Barzillai the Gileadite}* As Barzillai desired, and David promised, 2 Sam. 19.37,38.

*{and let them be of those that eat at thy table}* This was a testimony of great kindness, especially when it was done by kings, See 2 Sam. 9.7.

*{for so they came to me}* They brought all manner of provision to David, 2 Sam. 17.28,29. & 19.32. Therefore David thinks it not enough that he himself had showed kindness to Barzillai’s sons, but he requires his son Solomon also to continue the like. A grateful heart is never satisfied in recompensing good turns.

*{when I fled because of Absalom thy brother}* Heb. *from the face of Absalom*. Absalom had raised such a conspiracy against David, as he durst not abide at Jerusalem, where his rebellious son was, but was forced to fly from him, and that for fear of his life, 2 Sam. 15.12,14. & 17.2.

**V. 8.** *{And behold, thou hast with thee Shimei the son of Gera, a Benjamite of Bahurim}* 2 Sam. 16.5. His name, parentage, tribe, and place of abode are expressly mentioned, as for distinction, so for aggravation. For by that terrible fact of his he brought shame and disgrace upon his name, upon his father, upon his tribe, and upon the place of his abode. Bahurim was a city in the tribe of Benjamin, about the out-coasts thereof, 2 Sam. 3.16. & 17.18.

*{which cursed me with a grievous curse}* Heb. *strong curse*. The curse wherewith Shimei cursed David, may justly be said to be grievous or strong: for therein most horrible and most detestable crimes were laid to the charge of a most innocent person, 2 Sam. 16.7,8.

*{in the day when I went to Mahanaim}* A city in the tribe of Gad beyond Jordan, Josh. 13.26. The first reason and ground of this name is rendered, Gen. 32.2. See the notes there.

*{but he came down to meet me at Jordan}* This he did after Absalom was slain; so as it might be fear that moved him to come in and acknowledge his fault, 2 Sam. 19.18,19, &c.

*{and I sware to him by the LORD}* This David did to make him rest more confidently upon the king’s mercy. See ch. 1.17,51.

*{saying, I will not put thee to death with the sword}* Heb. *If I put thee to death*. This is a form of imprecation, wherein the penalty is understood, Gen. 14.23. 2 Sam. 19.23. Hereby we are given to understand, that men should be very tender of making imprecations against themselves.

**V. 9.** *{Now therefore hold him not guiltless}* Though I for my time pardoned him, yet you may take some occasion of punishing him; and then put him in mind of what he has done to me. So did Solomon, v. 41,44.

*{thou art a wise man}* Though God had not yet given unto Solomon such an extraordinary measure of wisdom as he did afterwards, ch. 3.12, yet David observed in him much understanding and wisdom, even above the ordinary sort of young men, and thereupon gave him this commendation, and referred the managing of the cases propounded to him, unto his own discretion.

*{and knowest what thou oughtest to do unto him}* David would not have him do anything against his own judgment and conscience.

*{but his hoar head}* See v. 6.

*{bring thou down to the grave with blood}* David supposed that Solomon might find, if he narrowly observed the carriage of Shimei, some other just cause to take his life, and then add his cursing of David, as an aggravation of his fault. By blood he means the sword of justice.

**V. 10.** *{So David slept with his fathers}* He died. Death is a kind of sleep, Deut. 31.16. Compare Act. 2.29 with 13.36. And it is fitly resembled unto sleep in sundry respects. 1. Sleep has power on the body only and outward senses. 2. It frees a man from labor, trouble and care. 3. It refreshes the body. 4. It does not always keep the body under the bond thereof: for they that are only asleep use to wake again. Thus death seizes on the body only; the soul is immortal; and the dead rest from their labors, and are freed from all worldly troubles and cares: and they shall rise again, and being raised, become incorruptible and glorious. By reason of this fit resemblance, the grave, whereinto dead bodies use to be put, is styled *a bed*, Isai. 57.2.

*{and was buried in the city of David}* This city was called Zion. David won it and built it, 2 Sam. 5.7,9. Zion was a stronghold, fort or castle, which the Jebusites held till David’s time, 1 Chro. 11.5. David having won it from them so enlarged it and built about it, as he made a fair city there, and there built a palace for himself, 2 Sam. 7.1,2. In these respects it was called the city of David, 2 Sam. 5.7. Thither he caused the ark to be brought, and there pitched a tabernacle for it, 1 Chron. 16.1, where it abode till Solomon had built the temple, and therein he set the ark, ch. 8.1. By reason of the ark being in this city, it was styled *The city of God*, *God’s holy hill*: and it was a type of the church of Christ, Psal. 2.6. & 48.1,2, &c. & 87.1, &c. Here was the usual royal burying-place, where both David and also his successors that were of any good note or name, were buried, ch. 11.43. 2 Chron. 12.16. & 14.1. & 16.14. & 21.1. David’s sepulcher was made of such durable materials, and so well kept and repaired time after time by his posterity, as it continued till the apostles’ days, Act. 2.29, which was for the space of above thousand years.

**V. 11.** *{And the days that David reigned over Israel}* Here Israel is put for all the tribes.

*{were forty years}* 2 Sam. 5.4. 1 Chro. 29.26,27.

*{seven years}* This word seven, and thirty-three following are to the full sums expressed for round reckonings’ sake: for David reigned seven years and six months in Hebron, 2 Sam. 5.5.

*{reigned he in Hebron}* Hebron was an ancient city. In it Abraham had his abode when he came first into the land of Canaan, and there erected an altar to the Lord, Gen. 13.18. There was that famous burying-place, wherein the three great patriarchs and their wives were buried, Gen. 23.2. & 49.31. This being conquered, and so taken from the Canaanites, Josh. 10.36, it fell to the lot of the tribe of Judah, and was given to Caleb, Josh. 14.13. & 15.54. It was seated on a mountain, and was an habitation of giants, before Caleb subdued them, Josh. 14.12. Here David had his throne till his kingdom was thoroughly settled. When all the tribes of Israel, as well as Judah, came in and subjected themselves to him as their king, then he set his throne in Jerusalem. In this respect it is said, *and thirty three years reigned he in Jerusalem*.

**V. 12.** *{Then sat Solomon upon the throne of David his father}* 1 Chron. 29.23. Herein was God’s promise accomplished.

*{and his kingdom was established greatly}* For none now durst attempt anything against him that might disturb his quiet possession. See 2 Chron. 1.1.

**V. 13.** *{And Adonijah the son of Haggith}* Ch. 1.5.

*{came to Bathsheba the mother of Solomon}* He knew well that a mother may prevail much with her son: thereupon he first addresses himself to her.

*{and she said, Comest thou peaceably?}* Heb. *Is peace thy coming?* 1 Sam. 16.4. His former practice made her still suspect him: and she could not but fear, that David his father being dead, he might work some new mischief against his brother Solomon: as Esau thought of killing his brother Jacob after their father was dead, Gen. 27.41.

*{and he said, Peaceably}* Heb. *Peace*. This he pretends, whatsoever his intent was.

**V. 14.** *{He said moreover, I have somewhat to say unto thee}* Heb. *A word of me to thee*. I have a request to make to you, v. 16.

*{And she said, Say on}* She would try what his request was, before she granted it.

**V. 15.** *{And he said, thou knowest that the kingdom was mine}* I being the eldest son surviving, after my father, by birthright the kingdom belonged to me. See ch. 1.5. Yea, it was mine also by the people’s approbation, ch. 1.25.

*{and that all Israel set their faces on me}* That I should reign: They expected, and that with a desire, that I should be their king. When men have an earnest desire after anything, they use to turn their faces towards it, and to fix their countenance upon it, Luk. 9.53. Jer. 42.15.

*{Howbeit the kingdom is turned about}* It is diverted another way.

*{and is become my brother’s}* My younger brother is now set and established upon it.

*{for it was his from the LORD}* By God’s appointment, 1 Chro. 22.10. & 28.5. That which he here acknowledges, was a certain truth; and questionless it was known by him to be a truth: yet it is much to be doubted, whether he in truth and sincerity made this acknowledgment or no. Solomon’s answer to his mother, v. 22, seems to imply the contrary. Certainly Adonijah in seeking the crown before, v. 5, sinned against his knowledge.

**V. 16.** *{And now I ask one petition of thee}* This was it that he intended, v. 14.

*{deny me not}* Heb. *turn not away my face*. For when one’s request is denied, he is made ashamed; and in shame turns away his face. See v. 20. When one’s request is granted, his face is said to be accepted, Gen. 19.21.

*{and she said unto him, Say on}* Plainly make known your petition.

**V. 17.** *{And he said, Speak I pray thee, unto Solomon}* Without his consent he durst not attempt such a matter.

*{for he will not say thee nay}* Heb. *not turn away thy face*. See v. 8,16,20. 2 Chro. 6.42. Psal. 132.10.

*{that he give me Abishag the Shunammite}* See ch. 1.3.

*{to wife}* See v. 22.

**V. 18.** *{And Bathsheba said, Well, I will speak for thee unto the king}* She was herein too forward to gratify him. Though she were a wise woman, Pro. 31.1, &c. yet she dived not into the bottom of Adonijah’s heart, nor discerned the depth of his intention, as her son Solomon did, v. 22.

**V. 19.** *{Bathsheba therefore went unto king Solomon to speak unto him for Adonijah}* This was to make good her promise.

*{and the king rose up to meet her}* He was sitting on his throne, and from thence he rose so soon as he espied his mother coming to him.

*{and bowed himself unto her}* To testify his reverent respect to his mother, as she had done to David, ch. 1.16.

*{and sat down on his throne}* This he did, having conducted his mother thither. Of Solomon’s throne, See ch. 10.18, &c.

*{and caused a seat to be set for the king’s mother}* This he did to honor his mother.

*{And she sat on his right hand}* This was the highest place next to the king, who sat in the midst, 2 Chro. 18.18. Neh. 8.4. Psal. 45.9. & 110.1. Mat. 20.21.

**V. 20.** *{Then she said}* Being sat down.

*{I desire one small petition of thee}* Being ignorant of the importance of the thing, and dangerous consequences that might have followed thereupon; she calls it small.

*{I pray thee, say me not nay}* Heb. *turn not away my face*. For men use to make those turn their faces from them whose requests they dislike. See v. 17.

*{And the king said unto her, Ask on, my mother}* This shows his readiness to gratify her.

*{for I will not say thee nay}* Supposing that that which his mother desired, had been just and safe, and but small, as she said. So as even Solomon himself was so forward and rash in binding himself by promise, to grant his mother’s request, before he knew what it was.

**V. 21.** *{And she said, Let Abishag the Shunammite be given to Adonijah thy brother}* They were brothers by father, but not by mother. See ch. 1.10.

*{to wife}* Abishag had been as his father’s wife, and lain in his bosom. This was one thing which incensed Solomon: for it was an incestuous desire that Adonijah had.

**V. 22.** *{And king Solomon answered, and said unto his mother}* This he did in displeasure.

*{And why dost thou ask Abishag the Shunammite for Adonijah?}* This interrogation implies an aggravation of Solomon’s displeasure.

*{ask for him the kingdom also}* It might have been a means for Adonijah to have gained many of the people, if Abishag (much admired for beauty and seemly behavior in ministering to David, and so honored as to be David’s bedfellow) had been his wife. Solomon discerned an intended plot to get the kingdom by the motion which Adonijah had made to Bathsheba, v. 17, & she to Solomon, v. 21.

*{for he is mine elder brother}* See ch. 1.5. This may give him a show of title.

*{even for him}* This doubling of the person shows that Solomon deeply apprehended the plot.

*{and for Abiathar the priest, and for Joab the son of Zeruiah}* See ch. 1.7. & 1 Chro. 2.16. By joining of those three together, is implied, that Adonijah, Abiathar, and Joab were all as one; all intending and plotting the same thing. This stirred up Solomon the more to execute the several judgments following on them all, v. 25,27,34.

**V. 23.** *{And king Solomon sware by the LORD}* His deep apprehension of the plot, made him bind himself by oath to cut them off who first made the motion. See ch. 1.27.

*{saying, God do so to me and more also}* Heb. *thus so add*. A form of imprecation, whereby more severe vengeance is wished for, if the thing sworn be not performed. See on Ruth 1.17.

*{if Adonijah have not spoken this word against his own life}* Heb. *soul*. To cause it to be taken away. See v. 24. Because the soul is the fountain of life, and life no longer abides in a man, than his soul remains united to his body, the soul is frequently put for life, as Gen. 9.5. & 32.30. Exod. 4.19. & 21.23. 1 Sam. 19.5. 1 Kings 19.3.

**V. 24.** *{Now therefore as the LORD liveth}* See ch. 1.29.

*{which hath established me in my kingdom}* See v. 12.

*{and set me on the throne of David my father}* Made me king in stead of David, who is dead. A throne is the proper seat of a king, Gen. 41.40. Deut. 17.18. 2 King. 10.3. 2 Chron. 23.20.

*{and who hath made me an house}* A fair and royal family, 1 Sam. 2.35. & 25.28. 2 Sam. 7.27. Exod. 1.21.

*{as he promised}* 2 Sam. 7.12,13. For *God is faithful who promised*, Hebr. 10.23.

*{Adonijah shall be put to death this day}* Because Solomon observed Adonijah’s ambitious and seditious spirit still to abide in him, and to remain restless, he thought it not meet to put off his judgment one day, or to suffer him any longer to live.

**V. 25.** *{And king Solomon sent by the hand of Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* See ch. 1.8. He gave charge and power to Benaiah to slay Adonijah.

*{and he fell upon him that he died}* It was of old the custom for captains and great officers, in cases of apparent treasons and other capital crimes, upon the king’s word to slay malefactors, as 2 Sam. 1.15, & 4.12.

**V. 26.** *{And unto Abiathar the priest}* See ch. 1.7.

*{said the king}* Solomon observing a new plot, begins to restrain all the plotters.

*{Get thee to Anathoth unto thine own fields}* Anathoth was one of the cities in the tribe of Benjamin that belonged to the priests; and the fields thereabouts were in that respect the priests’ fields. Solomon commands Abiathar to get himself thither, that there he might live a private life, and meddle no more with the priest’s office. By this means was he kept from intermeddling, as with ecclesiastical matters, so with all public affairs; and that because he had fallen into apparent treason by joining with others.

*{for thou art worthy of death}* Heb. *a man of death*. Because he conspired with Adonijah, ch. 1.7. & 2.22.

*{But I will not put thee to death}* Here Solomon mixes mercy with justice, and deals more favorably with him than with the rest: The reasons hereof follow.

*{Because thou barest the ark of the Lord GOD before David my father}* 1 Chron. 15.11,12. 2 Sam. 15.24,29. This was a duty proper and peculiar unto priests, Josh. 3.6. Of the ark, see ch. 6.19. & 8.4.

*{And because thou hast been afflicted}* Heb. *thou hast afflicted thyself*.

*{in all wherein my father was afflicted}* You have had a part and share in all my father’s troubles, by your own voluntary following of him, 1 Sam. 22.23. & 23.6,9. & 2 Sam. 15.24. Solomon’s piety to God and to his father David moves him to spare Abiathar: To God, in that he was God’s priest: To David, in that he was an ancient follower of him above forty years, 1 Sam. 22.23. He might have also respect to David’s promise, 1 Sam. 22.23. Solomon in mitigating Abiathar’s punishment, did as God himself oft does to such as in the ordinary course of their life do many good things; if in some cases they deserve severe punishment, he either mitigates it, or puts it off to future times, 2 Chron. 19.3. Esai. 39.8.

**V. 27.** *{So Solomon thrust out Abiathar from being priest unto the LORD}* If priests or other ministers of the church unwarrantably and unlawfully intermeddle in the great affairs of state, and commit capital or other crimes contrary to the good laws of the kingdom where they live, they make themselves liable to the penalties of those laws, and to censures of civil magistrates. Of putting Abiathar from his place see v. 35.

*{that he might fulfil the word of the LORD}* 1 Sam. 2.31,35. God oft in his wise and secret providence causes his word to be fulfilled by many that aim at no such thing, as Gen. 41.9. & 50.20. Compare 2 Sam. 12.11 with 2 Sam. 16.22. Act. 3.17,18. But this manner of expressing the point, *that he might fulfil*, implies that Solomon aimed at this very end: and that he was induced thereupon to take this occasion of thrusting Abiathar from the priesthood.

*{which he spake concerning the house of Eli}* Abiathar was the fourth from Eli: for he was the son of Ahimelech, the son of Ahitub, 1 Sam. 22.20, the son of Phinehas, the son of Eli, 1 Sam. 14.3. There is a metonymy in the word house: for house is oft put for them that dwell in the house, or appertain thereunto: as for a man’s family, stock, or posterity, Gen. 39.5. & 45.2. Judg. 4.17. Psal. 115.12.

*{in Shiloh}* Shiloh was a city of Ephraim, where the tabernacle was for many years, and where the tribes were wont to assemble together in Joshua’s and the Judges’ times, Josh. 18.1. Judg. 21.19. 1 Sam. 4.3,4.

**V. 28.** *{Then tidings came}* Tidings of what befell Adonijah and Abiathar. For such public affairs soon spread far and near.

*{to Joab}* For Joab lived not at the court. In some copies it is read, *to Solomon*: but corruptly.

*{for Joab had turned after Adonijah}* This was a traitorous turning, ch. 1.7.

*{though he turned not after Absalom}* Vulg. Lat. *after Solomon*. So the Septuagint and Josephus, but all corruptly. It is true indeed, that Joab turned not *after Solomon*. But that is not so pertinent to the present point: for there are two, among other weighty reasons, why mention is here made of Joab’s not turning after Absalom. One to prevent an objection, which is this: Joab remained faithful to David, when Absalom, Ahithophel David’s counselor, and others, conspired against David. Therefore it is meet that Joab be spared; notwithstanding this last fact. The manner of expressing the case thus, *though he turned not after Absalom*, implies, that that he is not thereupon to be spared, for though a subject in sundry particulars show much faithfulness, yet if afterwards he prove a traitor, he may be dealt with as a traitor. If it be said, Abiathar was not so dealt withal; it may be replied, 1. That Solomon discerned a different disposition in the one and the other. Joab was of a more stirring spirit, and a man of war. 2. Abiathar had not grieved David as Joab had done, 2 Sam. 3.24,25,30, & 19.5,6. 3. Joab had in two other cases committed willful murder. 2 Sam. 3.27. & 20.10. Another reason of this clause, *though he turned not after Absalom*, was to aggravate his conspiracy against Solomon. For it gives evidence that Joab had his hand in this conspiracy against Solomon, not simply upon a seditious disposition, nor simply in dislike of David’s government; for then he would have manifested such a disposition in Absalom’s case; but it appears, that what he now did, he did in dislike and hatred of Solomon. For he could not be ignorant that David by God’s appointment, had deputed Solomon his son to be king after him. Besides, Solomon took this advantage to fulfill the charge which his father upon his deathbed gave him concerning Joab, v. 5.

*{and Joab fled unto the tabernacle of the LORD}* This was counted a place of refuge. It is said ch. 1.49, of the conspirators, that *every man went his way*, namely, to his own house. And Joab might think that as David had passed by his murders, so Solomon might pass by this conspiracy, & he quietly live a private life. But when he heard that Adonijah was slain, and Abiathar put from his priestly function, he thought it high time to shift for himself, it seems he knew not where to secure himself either in or out of the land. Therefore he flies to the altar the rather, because Adonijah had there found favor, c. 1.52. Other reasons see ch. 1.50. Of the tabernacle see ch. 1.4.

*{and caught hold on the horns of the altar}* See ch. 1.50.

**V. 29.** *{And it was told king Solomon}* Kings use to have many eyes and ears; and matters of consequence are soon brought to their knowledge.

*{Then Solomon sent Benaiah, &c.}* See v. 25.

**V. 30.** *{And Benaiah came to the tabernacle of the LORD}* He was sent thither by the king.

*{and said unto him, Thus saith the king}* No grudge of his own, but the king’s charge put him on to do what he did.

*{Come forth}* Namely, from the tabernacle. Though it be not expressed in the king’s speech that he should call him forth, yet the king might say as much, or at least intend as much, to keep the altar, if it might be, from being polluted. See Exod. 21.14.

*{And he said, Nay}* He feared that if he came forth, he should not be spared.

*{but I will die here}* He said this, either in hope that they would not slay him there, or in a superstitious conceit of the place, that it might be some advantage to his soul to die there.

*{And Benaiah brought the king word again, saying}* For he was loath of his own head to shed blood at the altar.

*{Thus said Joab, and thus he answered me}* This intends the same words that Joab uttered.

**V. 31.** *{And the king said unto him, Do as he hath said, and fall upon him}* These words may have a general reference only to the taking away of his life. In this sense do many take Solomon’s charge, and Benaiah’s execution thereof, because it is not expressly said he was slain at the altar; and because they might pull him thence by force. For it was counted a kind of pollution of the altar, to shed man’s blood thereat. The law commands to take from the altar him that is to be put to death, Exod. 21.14. So they dealt with Athalia, 2 Chron. 23.14,15. Christ aggravates the murdering of Zacharia by this circumstance, that he was slain at the altar, Matth. 23.35. Hereby also is Pilate’s cruelty aggravated, Luk. 13.1. If Joab were slain at the altar, it was because it is better that blood be there shed (which was but a ceremony) than murder and treason be not punished, which is a moral matter.

*{and bury him}* For the honor of his place, 2 King. 9.34, and for his former service. Decent burial may be allowed to such as are not worthy to live.

*{that thou mayest take away}* This is an especial reason why Solomon took advantage from Joab’s late conspiracy to cut him off. For blood defiles the land: *and the land cannot be cleansed of the blood that is shed therein, but by the blood of him that shed it*, Numb. 35.33. Certainly, David had it in his mind to revenge the blood of Abner and Amasa, whom Joab slew. The reasons why he did it not are rendered v. 6.

*{the innocent blood which Joab shed}* Abner’s and Amasa’s blood is styled innocent, because they were not worthy of death in that cause for which Joab slew them. If it be objected, that Abner had slain Asahel Joab’s brother, 2 Sam. 2.23, I answer, 1. He killed him in war, and that provoked thereunto by Asahel, 2 Sam. 2.19, &c. 2. Joab did it not in a right and legal way, 2 Sam. 3.27. 3. It was another matter for which Joab slew him; namely, a fear that David would prefer Abner before him, and make him general of the army. Upon the like ground Joab slew Amasa, 2 Sam. 20.10.

*{from me, and from the house of my father}* By this phrase he means all that came from his father, or should come from him, the posterity of both.

**V. 32.** *{And the LORD shall return his blood upon his own head}* The vengeance of the blood which he shed shall fall even upon himself. See v. 37. His unjust & treacherous shedding the blood of others has deserved that his blood be shed. Because the head is the principal part of man, it is by a synecdoche put for the whole man. The phrase is used in conferring blessings, as Gen. 49.26. Deut. 33.16. Prov. 10.6, and inflicting judgments, as ch. 8.32. 1 Sam. 25.39. Psal. 7.16.

*{who fell upon two men more righteous and better than he}* In that cause they were more righteous and better than he: for he was a murderer, and they innocents; for they were unjustly slain. That may be comparatively true which is not simply. Abner and Amasa were more righteous than Joab, though they themselves were in many things unrighteous. Abner knew that God had appointed David to be king, 2 Sam. 3.9,10. Yet he did what he could to set up Ishbosheth the son of Saul to be king, 2 Sam. 2.8,9. & 3.6. And Amasa took part with Absalom against David, 2 Sam. 17.25.

*{and slew them with the sword}* 2 Sam. 3.27. & 20.10.

*{my father David not knowing thereof}* David knew nothing of Joab’s purpose in those cases till Joab had executed the same, 2 Sam. 3.16,17.

*{to wit Abner the son of Ner}* 2 Sam. 3.27.

*{captain of the host of Israel}* As Joab was the general over the army of Judah, so Abner over the army of Israel, 2 Sam. 2.8.

*{and Amasa the son of Jether}* 2 Sam. 20.10. He is said to be captain for that time, because David had made him general over the army of Judah, 2 Sam. 20.4, &c.

**V. 33.** *{Their blood shall therefore return upon the head of Joab}* i.e. the vengeance of their blood, as v. 32.

*{and upon the head of his seed for ever}* i.e. his posterity, 2 Sam. 3.29. See ch. 8.13. & 1 King. 5.17.

*{but upon David and upon his seed}* i.e. his children.

*{and upon his house}* i.e. upon all that belonged unto him.

*{and upon his throne}* Upon his kingdom.

*{shall there be peace for ever from the LORD}* By peace he means all manner of blessings. By executing judgment on the murderer the guilt is taken from the magistrate.

**V. 34.** *{So Benaiah the son of Jehoiada went up}* Benaiah being now the general of the army, is used as an instrument to execute just vengeance, v. 15. He is said to go up, because the altar whither he fled was on the side of an hill.

*{and fell upon him and slew him}* See v. 25.

*{and he was buried in his own house}* See v. 31.

*{in the wilderness}* A place in which there are but few inhabitants is called a wilderness, Mat. 3.1. Act. 8.26. In this respect Joab might suppose that he could not be safe in his own house, and therefore fled, as v. 28.

**V. 35.** *{And the king put Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* See ch. 1.8. & 1 Chron. 11.22.

*{in his room over the host}* He made him general.

*{and Zadok the priest did the king put in the room of Abiathar}* Zadok by lineal descent came from Phinehas: to whom the covenant of priesthood forever was confirmed, Numb. 25.11,12,13. See Zadok’s pedigree, 1 Chron. 6.3, &c. See his solemn inauguration, 1 Chron. 29.22. Zadok had a double right to be high priest, one by birth, another by special promise. By birth he was in a lineal descent the surviving heir to Aaron, as appears by the genealogy mentioned, 1 Chr. 6.3, &c. The special promise was made to Phinehas and his seed, Numb. 25.13. But Zadok was the eldest surviving seed, or son of Phinehas. Abiathar came from Eli; as v. 27. How Eli came to be chief priest is not recorded in Scripture. Solomon therefore had just cause to take the advantage that was given him by Abiathar’s conspiracy with Adonijah, to put him down, and to place Zadok in the priest’s office.

**V. 36.** *{And the king sent, and called for Shimei}* This is that Shimei of whom David had given to Solomon a strait charge, v. 8. This questionless was the cause that moved Solomon to seek advantage against him. It is the part of a child’s piety to be very careful in observing his parents’ charge; especially when it is his last charge upon his deathbed.

*{and said unto him, Build thee an house in Jerusalem, and dwell there}* Solomon gave this charge, that Shimei might be the more in his eye, and kept within compass. Though Shimei were not among those that conspired with Adonijah, but among those that abode faithful to David, ch. 1.8. Yet Solomon might well restrain him from abiding at his own house, because it was in the tribe of Benjamin, of which Saul was; and thereupon many of that tribe might bear no good will to David’s house, but stir up Shimei against the king. Or the Philistines bordering thereupon, he might by them be stirred up against Solomon, and those that took part with him. Kings use to be jealous over all persons that may in the least degree be suspected.

*{and go not forth thence any whither}* This inhibition was that snare wherewith he was taken.

**V. 37.** *{For it shall be, that on the day thou goest out}* The inhibition is absolute, no limitation or exception granted.

*{and passest over the brook Kidron}* Kidron was a river betwixt Jerusalem and Bahurim, which was the inheritance of Shimei, (See 2 Sam. 16.5) whereunto he could not go but over that river, 2 Sam. 15.23. Jer. 31.40. Jo. 18.1. This river Kidron was by that place where good kings did use to burn idolatrous monuments, 2 Chron. 15.16. & 29.16. 2 K. 23.6. It is said to be about a mile from Jerusalem. So as Shimei had space enough to walk abroad for his recreation and health: He was not confined to close imprisonment.

*{thou shalt know for certain}* Heb. *knowing thou shalt know*.

*{that thou shalt surely die}* Heb. *dying thou shalt die*. Of this Hebraism in doubling words see ch. 8.13.

*{thy blood shall be upon thine own head}* None but yourself shall be accounted guilty of your death; you will be the cause thereof, Josh. 2.19. Mat. 27.25. Ezek. 18.13.

**V. 38.** *{And Shimei said unto the king, The saying is good}* It is just and equal, 2 King. 20.19. He being conscious of the wrong he had done to David, could not but acknowledge his confinement to be meet in itself, and less than his desert.

*{as my lord the king hath said, so will thy servant do}* Little did he think that he should have occasion to transgress the foresaid charge. He binds himself thereto by oath, v. 34.

*{And Shimei dwelt in Jerusalem many days}* He carefully observed the charge, till an occasion was offered of breaking it.

**V. 39.** *{And it came to pass at the end of three years}* So long Shimei had kept himself free from danger.

*{that two of the servants of Shimei ran away}* Those two servants had agreed in some evil, that made them to fly away.

*{unto Achish}* It is probable that this Achish was he to whom David went, 1 Sam. 21.10, and with whom he abode a year and four months, 1 Sam. 27.7. Or he might be some other king of Gath. For kings use to hold their predecessors’ names. Thus kings of Egypt, one after another, were called Pharaoh; of the Amalekites, Agag; of Tyre, Hiram; of Syria, Antiochus; and of Pontus, Mithridates: and emperors of Rome, Caesar. See ch. 11.40. & 15.18.

*{son of Maacha king of Gath}* See on 1 Sam. 5.8. There was then peace betwixt the Israelites and the Philistines; so as one might freely pass to the other.

*{and they told Shimei, saying, Behold, thy servants be in Gath}* This might be told by such as knew the danger of Shimei’s passing over Kidron; and so it was an act of envy: or else by such as were willing to help Shimei to his servants again; & so it was an act of goodwill.

**V. 40.** *{And Shimei arose}* This implies his diligence and speed in following after his servants.

*{and saddled his ass}* This was a means of greater speed in going and returning.

*{and went to Gath to Achish to seek his servants}* His covetous mind made him venture his life: at least much rashness and unadvisedness was manifested herein. Shimei might well have suspected that Solomon had some cause or other to confine him; and that upon so great a penalty as death: and withal he himself had cause to fear that some or other observing his passing over the river Kidron, might inform Solomon thereof. They of whom a king has any suspicion, should be very watchful over themselves lest they give him any advantage.

*{and Shimei went and brought his servants from Gath}* He had what he went for, but it cost him dear, even his own life.
