**V. 41.** *{And it was told Solomon, that Shimei was gone from Jerusalem to Gath, and was come again}* Kings have more eyes than their own. This should have made Shimei the more circumspect. But surely all this was ordered by a divine providence: For howsoever David, upon Shimei’s acknowledgment of his fault, did not only spare him for the present, but also swore that he should not die; namely, by him at that time, 2 Sam. 19.16, &c. Yet God so brought it to pass, that so heinous a fact as Shimei committed, 2 Sam. 16.5, &c. should not remain unrevenged. Though notorious offenders may for some time escape, yet vengeance will follow and overtake them, sooner or later.

**V. 42.** *{And the king sent and called for Shimei, and said unto him}* The justice and prudence of Solomon does clearly appear in this, that he sends for Shimei, and so reasons and expostulates the case with him, as he makes him discern the equity of the king’s proceeding against him: so as he is thereby put to silence, and has nothing to object against what Solomon said.

*{Did not I make thee to swear by the LORD}* See ch. 1.17.

*{and protested unto thee, saying}* This phrase implies a serious carriage of the matter.

*{Know for a certain, that on the day, &c.}* This was the very charge that Solomon gave him, v. 37.

*{And thou saidst unto me, The word that I have heard is good}* This was Shimei’s express answer, v. 38. Solomon produces three evidences against Shimei, the more to convince him of his fault, and of the justness of his punishment. One was his oath whereby he bound himself to God, for performing what Solomon enjoined. Another, the king’s strait charge and solemn protestation thereabout. A third, his own confession of the equity of the case.

**V. 43.** *{why then hast thou not kept the oath of the LORD}* He calls his swearing by the Lord the oath of the Lord, because it was wholly in the power of the Lord to bind him to it, or to loose him from it. Nor Solomon, nor Shimei, nor any other creature could free him from that oath. Thus men’s vows are said to be God’s, Ps. 56.12. See God’s indignation and vengeance on the breaking of an oath, Ezek. 17.18,19. Compare Josh. 9.15,19 with 2 Sam. 21.1, &c. Saul in such a case thought himself bound to put his son to death, 1 Sam. 14.24,27,28,43, &c.

*{and the commandment that I have charged thee with}* Express charges of kings in lawful matters strongly bind subjects.

**V. 44.** *{The king said moreover to Shimei}* The king herein declares the true and just ground of his proceedings against Shimei, from the beginning to the end.

*{Thou knowest all the wickedness which thy heart is privy to}* He implies hereby, that he need no other witness against him than the testimony of his own conscience.

*{that thou didst to David my father}* Pious children are as zealous against injuries done to their parents as to themselves.

*{therefore the LORD shall return}* Solomon puts him in mind of God’s just dealing with him, to justify himself therein, and to bring Shimei to a more serious consideration of the offense, to deeper humiliation for it, and to more thorough repentance.

*{thy wickedness upon thine own head}* i.e. the punishment of his wickedness.

**V. 45.** *{And king Solomon shall be blessed}* Solomon is so far from fearing any curse to fall upon him for executing just judgment on a malefactor, as he rather accounts himself blessed therein. Such as execute vengeance on transgressors, shall in that respect be blessed of the Lord, Numb. 25.11,12.

*{and the throne of David}* That is the kingdom which David for his life time held, and was after continued to his posterity. A throne being the proper seat of a king, is here synecdochically put for the whole kingdom.

*{shall be established}* Execution of justice establishes a kingdom; Psal. 106.30.

*{before the LORD}* i.e. to remain firm and stable in the sight of the Lord, and through his watchful care and providence over it.

*{for ever}* Both in Solomon’s time and in future ages, generation after generation. See ch. 8.13.

**V. 46.** *{And the king commanded Benaiah}* See v. 25.

*{which went out and fell upon him}* For it was not usual to fall upon malefactors in the king’s presence.

*{that he died}* See v. 25. Herein Solomon accomplished his father’s charge v. 9.

*{and the kingdom was established in the hand of Solomon}* 2 Chro. 1.1. For now the contrary faction was destroyed, and there were none left to raise head against him.

# CHAPTER 3

**V. 1.** *{And Solomon made affinity}* This affinity was by Solomon’s marrying Pharaoh’s daughter. See 2 Chr. 18.1. It is more than probable, that Pharaoh’s daughter professed the faith of Israel, was made a proselyte and taken into the church, as Zipporah, Ex. 18.2. Rahab, Josh. 2.9. Heb. 11.31. Ruth, Ruth 1.16. Maachah, 2 Sam. 3.3, and sundry others.

*{with Pharaoh}* See ch. 11.40. & Gen. 12.17.

*{king of Egypt}* Egypt bordered on the south of Israel, Numb. 34.3,4,5, so as the king of Israel and the king of Egypt might have the greater familiarity one with another. And these two being the most renowned kings of all the world at that time, might thereupon be the more forward to make affinity one with the other.

*{and took Pharaoh’s daughter}* He took her to wife and married her; so is this word used 2 Chr. 11.20. Sometimes the word wife is added, as 1 King. 4.15. Gen. 6.2. Ruth 1.4. In this, as in many other things, Solomon was a type of Christ. As Solomon married Pharaoh’s daughter, an alien, so Christ espoused the Gentiles to himself.

*{and brought her into the city of David}* See ch. 2.10. In this city was Solomon’s court. David built this city, and in it his palace, 1 Chr. 11.7.

*{until he had made an end of building his own house}* For this place was too strait for such a prince as he and her retinue. See ch. 7.8. There is intimation of a very fair sumptuous house for the queen, distinct from his own. This he joined to his own, and therefore first finished his own.

*{and the house of the LORD}* Chap. 6.37,38. By the house of the Lord is meant the temple, which was like an house, with foundation, walls, partitions, roof, &c. and it was dedicated to the worship, service, and honor of the Lord: and there the Lord is said to dwell, ch. 8.13, by reason of the manifestation of his presence therein, under sundry types. Such was Solomon’s zeal about building and perfecting this house, as he would not think of a special house for the queen till this were perfected.

*{and the wall of Jerusalem round about}* Chap. 9.15. This wall was of public concernment, tending to the safety and security of the people. This therefore he prefers before the queen’s pomp and royalty. Of Jerusalem see v. 15.

**V. 2.** *{Only}* Or, *as yet*. Then, and before.

*{the people sacrificed in high places}* Of high places see v. 4. The former word only intimates that this was a fault; for it was against the law, Levit. 17.3,4. Custom had made it to be the less regarded: insomuch as Solomon himself (notwithstanding the commendation given him, ver. 3) was brought into that common error, as Jacob, David, and others, into polygamy.

*{because there was no house built, &c.}* This reason (though it did not wholly free from all blame, yet) in part excused their fact: for it intimated that they used the high places for more and better conveniences, as they thought.

*{unto the name of the LORD}* See ch. 5.3.

*{until those days}* This phrase shows that by the house before mentioned was meant the temple which Solomon built, and not the tabernacle made by Moses.

**V. 3.** *{And Solomon loved the LORD}* This is the ground of all duty, and it comprises all duty under it, Matt. 22.36,37. Rom. 13.10. It also puts a man on to all duty, 2 Cor. 5.14. This evidenced the sincerity and ardency of his heart to God.

*{walking in the statutes of David his father}* Those wherein David himself walked, ch. 11.34, and commanded his son to walk in, ch. 2.3. Of this relation of his father see ch. 8.15.

*{only}* See v. 2.

*{he sacrificed}* He offered sacrifices by the ministry of priests.

*{and burnt incense}* See ch. 9.25.

*{in high places}* See v. 4.

**V. 4.** *{And the king went to Gibeon}* Gibeon was that great city which yielded to the Israelites, Josh. 9.3, &c. and fell to Benjamin’s lot, Josh. 18.25, but was given to the Levites to dwell in, Josh. 21.17. There was the tabernacle of the Lord, 1 Chr. 16.39, and the altar of burnt-offering, 1 Chr. 21.29.

*{to sacrifice there}* See v. 2.

*{for that was the great high place}* An high place used to be on an hill, whereon fair spreading trees grew; under which they erected altars for sacrifices, Ezek. 20.28,29. Idolatrous Israelites superstitiously used such high places, and the groves upon them, 1 King. 14.23. 2 Chr. 33.3, and that in imitation of the heathen. But the true worshippers of God used them as convenient places, from the time that the ark and tabernacle were removed from Shiloh. The first high place that we read of to be used by the people of God, is mentioned 1 Sam. 9.12. (See the note there.) This is here said to be the great high place, in sundry respects: 1. By reason of the spaciousness and fairness of the place. 2. In regard of the multitude of people that resorted thereto. 3. Because of the choice persons that frequented it, as the chief priests and Levites, 1 Chr. 16.39. Yea, David and Solomon with their princes and courtiers. 4. In relation to that tabernacle and altar which Moses built, and was there pitched, 1 Chr. 21.29. Of groves see chap. 16.33.

*{a thousand}* See ch. 8.63.

*{burnt-offerings}* See v. 15. See also Gen. 8.20. & Levit. 1.4.

*{did Solomon offer on that altar}* See ch. 8.64.

**V. 5.** *{In Gibeon the LORD appeared to Solomon}* He evidently manifested that it was God himself that spoke to the king.

*{in a dream}* While Solomon was asleep, as Gen. 28.11, &c. See v. 15. Among the manifold ways whereby God of old made known his mind to his people, dreams were one. In dreams sometimes men heard a voice; and conceiving the sense thereof, returned an answer thereto, as ver. 6. Sometimes visions and representations of things were set before their eyes, as Gen. 28.12. Yea, sometimes true, real objects, as Gen. 31.10. Hereby God shows that he can to admiration instruct men, even when they are least capable of instruction, they being asleep, and all their senses bound from executing their functions. Though in dreams men oft fancy and imagine things which are not, as Isa. 29.8, and though the devil makes men in dreams to conceive false things, Zech. 10.2, yet the things made known by God in dreams were most true: and his servants to whom he made them known were assured thereof, Job 33.15,16. So here in this dream.

*{by night}* In that night, 2 Chro. 1.7, after he had offered up his sacrifices. Hereby God showed how acceptable Solomon’s sacrifices were. So Gen. 8.20,21.

*{And God said, Ask what I shall give thee}* God propounded this question to Solomon, to try and manifest his disposition. The like, though not in such an extraordinary manner, is propounded to every believer, Mat. 7.7. Joh. 16.23. Jam. 1.5.

**V. 6.** *{And}* Presently, without further demur upon the matter.

*{Solomon said}* In this sleep and dream, v. 15. For hereupon he had high thoughts when he was awake, and Solomon had oft before desired such things as here he did of God: whereupon the answer was so ready in his dream.

*{thou hast shewed}* Heb. *done*.

*{unto thy servant David}* This title is oft given to David, by reason of his faithfulness to God all the days of his life. See Ps. 78.70,72. The like is oft given to Moses upon the same ground, Numb. 12.7.

*{my father}* See ch. 8.15.

*{great mercy}* Or, *bounty*. That which in the royalty of God’s mind and greatness of the gift may well be called bounty, may also in regard of the freeness of God’s grace, and the need wherein we stand thereof, be called mercy.

*{according as}* This sets out David’s answerable carriage to God’s mercy. God may order his reward according to man’s inward disposition and outward carriage, as Rom. 2.6, and yet in mercy do what he does, Ps. 62.12. For nothing in man, or done by man, can be the proper cause of reward; but it may be a qualification, whereby God fits men for reward.

*{he walked before thee}* See ch. 2.4.

*{in truth}* Truth distinguished from uprightness of heart (as here it is) is opposed to a superstitious performance of external duties, and resting thereon, Josh. 24.14.

*{and in righteousness}* In such courses as were just, equal, and in the matter of them agreeable to God’s word, the rule of righteousness, Isa. 48.1.

*{and in uprightness of heart with thee}* This has respect to an inward sincere manner of performing that which we do, 1 Chro. 29.17. All these must be taken of David’s inward disposition, and of his outward conversation in the general course thereof, so far as human frailty suffered him. For we read of many failings in these.

*{and thou hast kept for him}* The promise was made to David, and continued to Solomon. Now what is done to a man’s child for his father’s sake, may be accounted to be kept for the father, Psal. 89.20,28. & 132.11. 2 Sam. 7.15.

*{this great kindness}* So David accounted it, 2 Sam. 7.19. And as the setting of Solomon upon the throne was great in itself, so the rescuing it from Adonijah’s conspiracy made it appear the greater.

*{that thou hast given him a son to sit on his throne}* This was that great kindness that Solomon meant.

*{as it is this day}* For even then, David being dead, Solomon his beloved son, whom he desired to succeed him, was king.

**V. 7.** *{And now, O LORD my God}* See ch. 8.28.

*{thou hast made thy servant}* This title he ascribes to himself for honor’s sake, (for it is a great honor to be God’s servant) and to bind himself to loyalty and faithfulness unto God.

*{king}* He truly acknowledges that God had conferred that royal dignity upon him.

*{instead of David my father}* See ch. 8.15.

*{and I am but a little child}* He had now a son, ch. 14.21. This therefore is here spoken figuratively, in relation to the great charge which was committed unto him. He was but a little child to sway so great a scepter as was put into his hand: so 1 Chron. 22.5. & 29.1. Jer. 1.6.

*{I know not how to go out or come in}* To order and govern the people. This is a comparison taken from captains or shepherds. See Numb. 27.17. Deut. 31.2. Josh. 14.11. 1 Chro. 1.10.

**V. 8.** *{And thy servant}* See v. 7.

*{is in the midst of thy people}* A supreme governor, about whom they all stand.

*{which thou hast chosen}* To be your church, your peculiar people, Exod. 19.4,5. Deut. 7.6. & 10.14,15. Magistrates set over God’s church need in that respect the more wisdom.

*{a great people}* In number, as the words following show, Nu. 20.20.

*{that cannot be numbered}* This shows the accomplishment of God’s promise, Gen. 15.5. & 32.12. The phrase is hyperbolical: see 2 Chr. 1.9.

*{nor counted for multitude}* A pleonasm. By how much greater one’s charge is, by so much the greater ought his care to be.

**V. 9.** *{Give}* Well he knew that *every good gift is from above, and cometh down from the Father of lights*, Jam. 1.17.

*{therefore}* Because he was a governor over a great people, therefore he desires ability well to govern them: so as governors are set over for people’s good, Psal. 78.71.

*{thy servant}* See v. 7.

*{an understanding}* Heb. *hearing*. For understanding comes by hearing: By an understanding heart he means knowledge and wisdom, 2 Chro. 1.10. See v. 11. By knowledge men understand and conceive matters; by wisdom they well order and dispose them.

*{to judge thy people}* Without understanding a kingdom cannot be well governed; and understanding is given of God, and attained by hearing his word. He had the heart of a governor before, and that makes him pray for the head of a governor.

*{that I may discern between good and bad}* Herein a special part of understanding consists: Thus may governors best know how to carry themselves towards every one.

*{for who is able to judge this thy so great a people?}* Without understanding and wisdom given from above, none can do it.

**V. 10.** *{And the speech pleased the LORD}* Heb. *in the eyes of the Lord*. The greater the people is, the harder it is to govern them.

*{that Solomon had asked this thing}* Desire of ability well to manage the talent that God commits to a man, is very acceptable to God, if it be a true desire of heart. And God knows when a man’s desire is such.

**V. 11.** *{And God said unto him}* God soon manifests his approbation of such things as please him.

*{Because thou hast asked this thing}* That which is mentioned v. 9.

*{and hast not asked for thyself}* Such things as might seem most pleasing and profitable to yourself; such as men’s hearts by nature are set upon, Psal. 4.6. Such particulars as follow.

*{long life}* Heb. *many days*. Long life simply considered in itself, without the understanding here desired, is no blessing, Prov. 16.31.

*{neither hast asked riches for thyself}* Riches without understanding well to use them, may be kept for the owners of them to their hurt, Eccl. 5.13.

*{nor hast asked the life of thine enemies}* Heb. *the soul*. Their ruin and utter destruction. Ruin of enemies may make men too insolent, secure, and careless.

*{but hast asked for thyself understanding}* See v. 9.

*{to discern judgment}* Heb. *to hear*. By hearing causes on both sides men may the better know what judgment to give. By this God shows how he takes distinct notice of that which men most desire.

**V. 12.** *{Behold, I have done according to thy word}* Or, *your desire, *or,* prayer*. His words were uttered in form of a prayer, and thereby the desire of his heart was manifested. God’s doing according to Solomon’s word may move believers to rest on God for obtaining what they desire according to his will, 1 Joh. 5.14.

*{lo, I have given thee}* These two words, *Behold*, *lo*, are the same in the Hebrew. The repeating of them shows that this matter is very remarkable.

*{a wise and an understanding heart}* See v. 9.

*{so that there was none like thee, &c.}* Herein God went beyond Solomon’s express desire; whereby he verifies that he gives above what we ask, Eph. 3.20. See an exemplification hereof ch. 4.19, &c.

**V. 13.** *{And I have also given thee that which thou hast not asked}* Mat. 6.33. I have determined to give you such and such things. You may be assured in due time to have all.

*{both riches and honour}* These are those other things which are included in this promise, *First seek the kingdom of God, and his righteousnesse, and all these things shall be added unto you*. Matt. 6.33. God granted in the former part a greater measure of that thing which Solomon desired than he desired; and in this latter part he grants other things than he desired.

*{so that there shall not be}* Or, *has not been*. The Hebrew word may be applied both to time past and time to come.

*{any among the kings like unto thee all thy days}* Nor before nor since Solomon’s time was there any king in Israel that exceeded him in the things here promised, namely, riches and honor. As for heathen monarchs who may be thought to have exceeded him in riches and honor, they wanted that true wisdom which Solomon had, and whereby his riches and honor were so ordered, as they exceeded theirs.

**V. 14.** *{And if thou wilt walk in my ways}* See ch. 2.3,4. God adds conditions to his promises, that men thereby may know how far the promises do belong unto them.

*{as thy father David did walk}* Ch. 15.5. Approved examples are directions and incitations, which show what must or may be done. They are as guides before us, especially approved examples of parents; which being imitated by children, the memory of worthy parents will remain precious and blessed, and the blessing on children will thereby be multiplied, Exod. 20.6.

*{then will I lengthen thy days}* Long life in the way of righteousness is a blessing, Psal. 91.16.

**V. 15.** *{And Solomon awoke}* God would have him sensibly discern the truth of what was promised.

*{and behold, it was a dream}* A dream worth the heeding more than ordinary, Gen. 41.7.

*{and he came to Jerusalem}* Of Jerusalem see Josh. 15.8. & 18.28. Judg. 1.8. 2 Sam. 5.6. Zion called the city of David ch. 2.10, was within the compass of Jerusalem; so as the ark being in Zion, must also needs be in Jerusalem: in which respect Jerusalem is called *the holy city*, Neh. 11.1,18. Isai. 52.1. And it was an especial type of the church, Psal. 51.18. Isai. 62.1,7. & 66.20. Rev. 21.2. Of the notation of Jerusalem, see 1 Chron. 11.4.

*{and stood before the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See Exod. 40.3,20. Numb. 10.33. In the ark was the law kept, Deut. 10.5, which is styled *the covenant*. See ch. 6.19.

*{and offered up}* In way of thankfulness to God for the kindness of the Lord showed to him.

*{burnt-offerings}* The Hebrew word עלה translated *burnt-offering* signifies *that which ascends*; because it was all lifted up upon the altar, and there being burnt, it ascended in the vapor or smoke thereof unto heaven. There is another word also used for the same thing, כליל *that which is whole*, or, *all of it*. It is in our English translated *whole-burnt-offering*, Psal. 51.19, and *whole-burnt-sacrifice*, Deut. 33.10. The Greek translations turn both words *ὁλοκαύτωμα* *an holocaust*, or, *whole-burnt-offering*: for the whole beast being flayed and cut in pieces, was laid on the altar and burnt, Levit. 1.8,9,13. It was offered up to make atonement for the offerer, Levit. 1.4, and to procure reconciliation for him with God. It typified Christ’s offering up of his whole self, and that to make atonement for us with God, Heb. 10.5, &c. In this respect God is said to smell a sweet savor thereat, Gen. 8.21. Levit. 1.9. It did also morally set out the presenting of our bodies wholly to God’s service, Rom. 12.1. That offering which was styled a *sin-offering*, was also for an atonement and to make reconciliation betwixt God and the sinner, Exod. 30.10. Neh. 10.33. Of freewill-offerings See Ez. 3.5.

*{and offered peace-offerings}* The Hebrew word שלמים is sometimes put for *matters of peace*, as Gen. 34.21, and sometimes for *retributions*, as Psal. 56.12. Isai. 19.21. In both these acceptions may the word, applied to sacrifices, be taken: For on God’s part they did import grace, peace, blessing obtained; on man’s part, thanks and praise returned. In this respect it is styled *a sacrifice of thanksgiving*, Levit. 7.12,13,15. It used to be a voluntary sacrifice; and therefore it might be of male or female, Lev. 3.1. But the burnt-offering was only of the male, Lev. 1.3, and that was all burnt; whereas the fat only of the peace-offering was burnt, Lev. 3.3,4,5. All the flesh was for the priests, Levites, and people, Lev. 7.15,19. The peace-offerings being of beasts slain, typified Christ, who by his death became our peace, Eph. 2.13,14, &c. They did also morally set out our duty of giving thanks and yielding praise to God, Heb. 13.15. With sacrifices which were of living creatures, there used to be joined meat-offerings and drink-offerings, Chap. 8.64. 1 Chro. 29.21. These were so called because they were to be eaten and drunken. For as the priests had a part of the flesh of the sacrifices to eat, so they had also of the meat-offerings for bread, and of the drink-offerings for drink. The meat-offerings were of fine flour and oil kneaded together, and thereof were made loaves, or cakes, or wafers, Ex. 29.23, and being seasoned with salt, were baked, or fried, or broiled, and with incense were offered up to God, Lev. 2.1, &c. The meat-offering was an especial type of Christ, *the bread of life*, Joh. 6.35, anointed with the Spirit, Lu. 4.18, whose intercession is as sweet incense, Rev. 8.3. It also set forth saints, brought as a meat-offering to the Lord, Isa. 66.20, being sifted from the bran of corruptions, and made as fine flour, by faith and repentance; being also anointed and seasoned with the graces of God’s Spirit, offering up unto God the incense of their prayers in the mediation of Christ, Psal. 141.2. Mal. 1.11. Drink-offerings were of wine, or other strong liquor, Ex. 29.40,41. These used to be poured out upon the altar, Numb. 15.5. This was a type of Christ’s blood poured out for us, which was *drink indeed*, Joh. 6.55, in that our dry thirsty souls are through faith refreshed therewith; which is also represented in the sacramental wine of the Lord’s supper. It also showed that saints should be willing to be poured out in Christ’s cause, Phil. 2.17. Of these meat and drink-offerings there was reserved for the priests to eat and drink. For howsoever they were forbidden to drink wine and strong drink when they went about their service, Lev. 10.9. Ez. 44.21, yet at other times they might drink them both. Jewish expositors say that they were not simply forbid to drink wine or strong drink, but rather such a measure of the one or the other as might make them unfit for their service. This provision which the Lord made for the ministers of the law, did show that *they which preached the gospel should live of the gospel*, 1 Cor. 9.7, &c.

*{and made a feast to all his servants}* By servants are here meant subjects, amongst whom officers of several tribes, and all others that came to this solemnity, are comprised. This he did to testify his respect to his subjects, and to quicken their spirits, See ch. 8.65. Solomon’s subjects had the greatest benefit of that wisdom which God bestowed upon him. He does therefore most prudently quicken up their spirits to thankfulness.

**V. 16.** *{Then came there two women}* After the foresaid dream these came, that God might give assurance to Solomon of the accomplishment of his promise, v. 12, and also give evidence to his subjects, yea, and to such of other nations as should hear thereof, of that extraordinary measure of wisdom which he had wrought in him, ch. 4.31,34. See v. 28.

*{that were harlots}* Either the law Deut. 23.17 was neglected, or these were concubines, or victualers, as some take Rahab to have been, Josh. 2.1. If they were wives or concubines, their husbands might be from home about some special affairs. If harlots, they had better affections to their children than for the most part harlots now have: For in these days they use all the means they can to make away their children.

*{unto the king, and stood before him}* The king was now on his throne, ready to hear complaints; they therefore come before him to have their case decided. The persons were mean, but the case very difficult, and they knew not to whom else they might fly for judgment in such a case.

**V. 17.** *{And the one woman said}* Namely, the true mother of the living child.

*{O my lord}* A phrase of complaint: So v. 26. & Gen. 43.20.

*{I and this woman dwell in one house}* This was the ground of the deceit, and of the mistake.

*{and I was delivered of a child, with her in the house}* So as the other woman was with this at her travail, and knew that she had a child.

**V. 18.** *{And it came to pass the third day after}* There were but three days’ difference, so as the children could hardly be distinguished in the face one from another.

*{that this woman was delivered also}* Namely, in safety; the child coming out of her womb alive and well.

*{and we were together}* In the same house, if not in the same chamber.

*{there was no stranger with us in the house}* She means no other woman, at least in the night time, when the one took a child from the other; so as there were no witnesses to prove or disprove the matter in difference.

*{save we two in the house}* This made the case so difficult as it was; and was the reason why Solomon took such an unusual way to find out the truth as he did.

**V. 19.** *{And this woman’s child died in the night}* This circumstance made the case the more difficult; for there was no hope of any ones coming to them in the night.

*{because she overlaid it}* By her arms, or some other part of her body lying upon the mouth of the child, she smothered it. Many children by the carelessness of heavy and drowsy women have in all ages been thus made away.

**V. 20.** *{And she arose at midnight}* The darkest time of the night; and the time of deepest sleep, when fewest are stirring. Thus she takes her fittest opportunity to accomplish her mischievous plot, Job 24.15,16,17.

*{and took my son from beside me}* Though the child might seem safe in the mother’s bosom, yet unawares it was stolen away.

*{while thine handmaid slept}* They against whom mischief is intended, oftentimes sleep securely, while they who work the evil, watch.

*{and laid it in her bosom, and laid her dead child in my bosom}* She had rather have a living child, though another’s, than her own dead.

**V. 21.** *{And when I rose in the morning to give my child suck}* She calls it her child, because she supposed it had been her own, knowing nothing at that time to the contrary. Whether she were an harlot or no, yet to give suck was a mother’s proper duty. See on Gen. 21.7.

*{behold, it was dead}* She means the child that was laid by her.

*{but when I had considered it in the morning}* When the daylight appeared, she thoroughly viewed it. So much does this word consider import, Psal. 119.95. Job 37.14. Isai. 43.18.

*{behold, it was not my son which I did bear}* A mother can soon espy and well discern her own child.

**V. 22.** *{And the other woman said, Nay}* The false mother contradicts the other, because there was no witness to disprove her.

*{but the living is my son, and the dead is thy son}* She is as peremptory in her asseveration as if it had been a truth.

*{And this said, Nay, but the dead is thy son, &c.}* She that had the truth on her side, would not be overcome by the impudency of the liar.

*{Thus they spake before the king}* They contended one against another.

**V. 23.** *{Then said the king, The one saith, &c.}* The king repeats what the one and the other woman had alleged, to show that there was no apparent evidence to discover which party spoke truth, but that there was a necessity of putting it to such a trial as he intended.

**V. 24.** *{And the king said, Bring me a sword}* This he called for merely for trial, as Gen. 22.2. See v. 27.

*{And they brought a sword before the king}* They could not know the king’s end in calling for a sword, yet they yield obedience to his command, which subjects may and ought to do in civil matters not unlawful.

**V. 25.** *{And the king said, Divide the living child in two}* The sequel shows that he intended not to slay the living child, but to discover the true mother, as he did.

*{and give half to the one and half to the other}* His charge being so punctual, all that heard it thought that he meant as he spoke: Whence arose another contention betwixt the two women.

**V. 26.** *{Then spake the woman whose the living child was unto the king}* She knowing the child to be her own, could not in this case hold her peace.

*{for her bowels yearned upon her son}* Heb. *were hot*. Such compassion in such a phrase is attributed to God, Jer. 31.20.

*{and she said, O my lord}* See v. 17.

*{give her the living child}* She had rather her adversary should have the comfort of her child than it should be slain. This shows her motherly affection.

*{and in no wise slay it}* See ch. 8.13.

*{but the other said, Let it neither be mine nor thine, but divide it}* This betrayed an envious disposition: for all that she cared for, was, that her neighbor should not enjoy what she wanted.

**V. 27.** *{Then the king answered and said}* By the different affection of the two women he well discerned the truth of the case, and answerably gives judgment.

*{Give her the living child}* Her that would not have it slain, and thereby declared herself to be the true mother.

*{and in no wise slay it}* Heb. *slaying do not slay it*. See ch. 8.13. This shows that he intended not to slay the child when he called for a sword.

*{she is the mother thereof}* She has plainly declared by her affection to the child, that she is the true mother thereof.

**V. 28.** *{And all Israel}* All sorts of people in that nation, great and mean, good and bad.

*{heard of the judgment which the king had judged}* For it was a just and righteous judgment, given upon good evidence.

*{and they feared the king}* Heb. *before the king*. Or, *at the face of the king*. Good and just men reverenced the king, upon expectation of righteous judgment to be executed by him; but wrongdoers feared lest their crafty devices should be discovered by him. Thus respectively all feared him.

*{for they saw that the wisdom of God}* Most excellent and extraordinary wisdom is styled *the wisdom of God*, as Gen. 23.6. & 30.8. Psal. 36.6. & 80.10. 1 Chron. 12.22. Ezek. 10.5. It was also called *the wisdom of God*, because it was given and inspired by God, Jam. 1.5.

*{was in him}* Heb. *in the midst of him*. Job 20.14.

*{to do judgment}* This is the most eminent grace that can be in a governor as he is a governor, Deut. 1.16. 2 Sam. 8.15. Jer. 21.12.

# CHAPTER 4

**V. 1.** *{So king Solomon was king over all Israel}* i.e. all the twelve tribes; over which no king after him did reign; no, nor David his father in the beginning of his reign, 2 Sam. 5.5. At first only the men of Judah anointed David over the house of Judah, 2 Sam. 2.4. And there was long war betwixt the house of David and the house of Saul, 2 Sam. 3.1, whereby David was kept from reigning over other tribes. When Abner and Ishbosheth were both slain, then, and not before, came all the tribes, and anointed David king over Israel. 2 Sam. 5.1,3. Solomon’s son, who succeeded him in the throne, lost ten tribes, ch. 12.16, so as he and all his posterity reigned over two tribes only, ch. 12.21.

**V. 2.** *{And these were the princes which he had}* By whose ministry he governed the kingdom, according to Jethro’s advice, Ex. 18.21. The number and order of the following governors are recorded, not only to set out Solomon’s riches and glory, but also his wisdom. See ch. 10.4,5. If the variety of distinct functions and benefits which redound to king and state thereby be duly observed, admirable wisdom in ordering them may clearly be discerned.

*{Azariah the son of Zadok}* If Zadok the priest, ch. 2.35, be here meant, Azariah was his grandchild, 1 Chron. 6.8,9. But the Hebrew word כהן signifies *a secular prince*, as well as *an ecclesiastical priest*. This title is given to David’s sons, 2 Sam. 8.18, who are said to be *chief about the king*, 1 Chro. 18.17. It is also given to Ira David’s chief officer, 2 Sam. 20.26. And so Zabud Solomon’s principal officer, v. 5, and to Joseph’s father-in-law, Gen. 41.45, and to Moses his father-in-law, Exod. 2.16. It is therefore most probable that another Zadok is here meant; and that this Azariah was a viceroy under Solomon, as Dan. 6.2,3. & Gen. 41.44.

*{the priest}* Or, *the chief officer*.

**V. 3.** *{Elihoreph and Ahiah, the sons of Shisha}* Shisha the father of these two is supposed to be the same that is styled Sheva, 2 Sam. 20.25, and that he trained up his two sons in his own calling, and made them so expert therein, as moved Solomon to choose them to be his scribes. As Solomon retained many of his father’s officers in their places, so might he choose such sons as were fit to succeed in their father’s room.

*{Scribes}* Or, *secretaries*, 2 Sam. 8.17. Esth. 3.12. Such as our king’s secretaries are. Three sorts of scribes are mentioned in Scripture: 1. King’s secretaries, as here; 2. Public notaries, Jer. 32.26. 3. Teachers of the law of God, Ezr. 7.6. Matt. 23.2. David had but one scribe, 2 Sam. 20.25: Solomon, whose dominion was larger, had two.

*{Jehoshaphat the son of Ahilud}* This man held the same office all David’s time, 2 Sam. 8.16. & 20.24, so as he must needs be an old man. See v. 17.

*{the recorder}* Or, *remembrancer*, 2 Sam. 8.16. 2 King. 18.18. According to the derivation of the Hebrew word, it signifies *a rememberer or remembrancer*, as Jer. 41.9. Isai. 62.6. & 66.3. Such are masters of requests, whose office is to put the king in mind of petitions, and other like things. Some style them *chroniclers*, such as Esth. 2.23. & 6.1.

**V. 4.** *{And Benaiah the son of Jehoiada}* See ch. 1.8.

*{was over the host}* That is, as now such an one is styled, *generalissimo*. This place Joab held all David’s time, 2 Sam. 8.16. But Solomon putting Joab to death, placed Benaiah in his room, ch. 2.35; Benaiah was a colonel over certain troops in David’s time, 2 Sam. 8.18.

*{and Zadok and Abiathar were the priests}* Abiathar was a chief priest when Solomon came first to the crown; and so is he here joined with Zadok: for David so joined him, 2 Sam. 20.25, because he was an occasion of the death of Abiathar’s father’s house, 1 Sam. 22.22,23, and because Abiathar still followed him, ch. 2.26. Besides, though Abiathar was removed from the superior place, yet he remained a priest. See Mark 2.26. He might retain the title, though he executed not the function; from which he was removed, ch. 2.27.

**V. 5.** *{And Azariah the son of Nathan was over the officers}* i.e. those that are mentioned v. 7, & 2 Chron. 8.10. The Hebrew word נצבים translated *officers*, according to the notation of it, signifies such as are set apart or deputed to set employments, ch. 5.16. It is translated *a deputy* ch. 22.47.

*{and Zabud the son of Nathan was principal officer}* This is the same word that is translated *priest* v. 2.

*{and the king’s friend}* So was Hushai, 2 Sam. 15.37: He was the king’s favorite, who had on all occasions free access unto him. By this he is distinguished from Azariah; for the other title is common to them both. It appears that Solomon had Nathan in high esteem, in that he put two of his sons in the highest offices of his kingdom, next to himself, and accounted one of them his favorite. Well might he so do; for Nathan was to David a faithful prophet of the Lord, 2 Sam. 7.1, &c. & 12.7,15,25, and the first that discovered Adonijah’s conspiracy, and gave advice for settling Solomon on the throne, ch. 1.11. And it is probable that Nathan’s sons were trained up with Solomon, under their father’s tuition.

**V. 6.** *{And Ahishar was over the household}* He was treasurer of the king’s house, or governor thereof, ch. 16.9. & 18.3. Gen. 39.4.

*{and Adoniram the son of Abda}* Ch. 5.14.

*{was over the tribute}* Or, *levy*. He was chief customer or receiver of the king’s revenues, ch. 5.14. & 12.18. Such an office had one called Adoram in David’s reign, 2 Sam. 20.24, and another of that name in Rehoboam’s, ch. 12.18. These three might be grandfather, father, and son. There were questionless other inferior customers under Adoniram: for the king had tribute, not only from his own subjects, ch. 9.15, but also from strangers that dwelt in his kingdom, ch. 9.21, and from others out of other countries, v. 21. ch. 10.25.

**V. 7.** *{And Solomon had twelve officers}* These were for the twelve months; such as might oversee the inferior purveyors, so as neither the people should be oppressed, nor the king defrauded.

*{over all Israel}* Thus all that did partake of the benefit of Solomon’s prudent government, did afford of their commodities to the maintenance of his royal estate; yet not so, but that they had some consideration for their commodities, at least at the king’s price. Had the great store provided been gathered only out of the places near the court, the people thereabout might have been overburdened, and yet the king have wanted.

*{which provided victuals for the king and his household}* The store of provision mentioned v. 22,23 shows that Solomon’s household was very great. Under it may be comprised his wives’ household, and such companies of soldiers as attended about the court; yea, and such foreign princes and ambassadors as came to him from other countries, and their retinue, v. 21,27. See v. 6.

*{each man his month in a year made provision}* These had their treasure-houses and other places which at all times they replenished with commodities. But in their several months they caused their store to be brought for the king’s use.

**V. 8.** *{And these are their names}* They were men of note, and therefore their names are kept in memory.

*{the son of Hur}* Or, *Benhur*. So are the rest described by their fathers’ names; because their fathers were men famous in Israel.

*{in mount Ephraim}* The divisions are not simply made according to the tribes (for so might some have been overpressed, in that some tribes were neither so large nor so fertile as others) but according to the commodities of the soil.

**V. 9.** *{The son of Dekar}* Or, *Ben-dekar*.

*{in Makaz, &c.}* These were cities in the tribe of Dan, Josh. 19.40, &c.

**V. 10.** *{The son of Hesed}* Or, *Ben-hesed*.

*{in Aruboth}* This is in the tribe of Judah.

*{to him pertained Sochoh, &c.}* These also were in Judah, Josh. 15.35, &c.

**V. 11.** *{The son of Abinadab}* Or, *Ben-Abinadab*.

*{in all the region of Dor}* In Manasseh, Josh. 17.11. Judg. 1.27.

*{which had Taphath the daughter of Solomon to wife}* This is here set down by anticipation: For mention being made of the son of Abinadab, occasion is thence taken to name the wife which he married many years after; to show that he was a man of some worth, in that the king would bestow his daughter upon him. See v. 15. Solomon at this time could not have daughters marriageable.

**V. 12.** *{Baana the son of Ahilud, to him pertained Taanach and Megiddo}* These were in Manasseh too, Josh. 17.11. Judg. 1.27.

*{and all Bethshean}* Josh. 17.11.

*{which is by Zartanah}* Josh. 3.16.

*{beneath Jezreel}* In Issachar, Josh. 19.18.

*{from Bethshean to Abel-Meholah}* Judg. 7.22.

*{even unto the place that is beyond Jokneam}* In Zebulon, Josh. 19.11. & 21.34.

**V. 13.** *{The son of Geber}* Or, *Ben-Geber*.

*{in Ramoth-Gilead}* In Gad. This was in Gilead, Deut. 4.43. Josh. 20.8. 1 King. 22.3. By Gilead added to it it is distinguished from Ramoth in Issachar, 1 Chron. 6.73.

*{to him pertained the towns of Jair the son of Manasseh}* These towns were in the half-tribe of Manasseh, on the other side Jordan, Numb. 32.41.

*{which are in Gilead}* See ch. 17.1.

*{to him also pertained the region of Argob}* Deut. 3.4,14.

*{which is in Bashan}* See 2 King. 10.33. & 1 Chro. 5.11.

*{threescore great cities with walls and brasen bars}* The land of Israel was compassed about with many enemies, and thereupon in every tribe they had cities very well fenced.

**V. 14.** *{Ahinadab the son of Iddo had Mahanaim}* Or, *to Mahanaim*. This was in Gad, Josh. 13.26. See Gen. 32.2.

**V. 15.** *{Ahimaaz was in Naphtali}* He was some great man, as that which follows shows.

*{he also took Basmath the daughter of Solomon to wife}* This was another daughter, besides the former mentioned v. 11.

**V. 16.** *{Baanah the son of Hushai was in Asher and in Aloth}* Aloth taken appellatively signifies *upper parts of places*, and implies high hilly grounds. This place lay by the seaside in the land of Edom, 2 Chron. 8.17. It is supposed to be the same that is called Elath, Deut. 2.8.

**V. 17.** *{Jehoshaphat the son of Paruah in Issachar}* This his parentage does distinguish him from that Jehoshaphat which is mentioned v. 3, who was the son of Ahilud.

**V. 18.** *{Shimei the son of Elah in Benjamin}* This distinguishes him from that Shimei who cursed David: both were of the tribe of Benjamin, but of different parents. That other was the son of Gera, 2 Sam. 16.5.

**V. 19.** *{Geber the son of Uri was in the country of Gilead}* This was another part than that mentioned v. 13.

*{in the country of Sihon}* Of Sihon and Og, and their countries, see Numb. 21.21, &c. Deut. 3.8, &c.

*{he was the only officer which was in the land}* Over so much of that land taken from Sihon and Og as was not mentioned before, namely, over all the residue.

**V. 20.** *{Judah and Israel}* Though Israel consisted of ten tribes (in which respect it is set for the most part before Judah) yet here, and v. 25, Judah is set before Israel; because the king was of that tribe: And in Judah was Jerusalem the chiefest city of all the tribes; and it was the place where the king had his most frequent residency.

*{were many, as the sand which is by the sea in multitude}* This is a proverbial phrase and hyperbolical, and is not too strictly to be taken. It implies an innumerable number, Jer. 33.22. Gen. 15.5. It has respect to the promise, Gen. 22.17. & 32.12. Note, Prov. 14.28. See v. 29.

*{eating and drinking}* This is a description of plenty and peace: Men that have but little may eat and drink; but to be eating and drinking implies abundance; 1 Chron. 12.39. Job 1.18. Isai. 22.13.

*{and making merry}* This phrase shows that they were freed from fear of troubles, oppressions, incursions of enemies, and other like matters, that might make them eat and drink with sad hearts and troubled spirits. See 1 Sam. 30.16.

**V. 21.** *{And Solomon reigned over all kingdoms, &c.}* 2 Chron. 9.26. He means all the kingdoms round about Israel.

*{from the river}* Euphrates, as it was promised, Gen. 15.18. Exod. 23.31. Deut. 11.24. Josh. 1.4. This river was their coast northward. See on Ps. 72.8, &c.

*{unto the land of the Philistines}* Their coast on the west.

*{and unto the border of Egypt}* This was their coast on the south, Numb. 34.3,4,5. See ch. 8.65.

*{they brought presents}* In testimony of fealty. See 2 King. 17.3. 2 Chron. 17.5. & 32.23.

*{and served Solomon}* They did this as tributary nations; in which respect Solomon is said to reign over them.

*{all the days of his life}* As the truth of God’s promise ch. 3.13 was herein verified, so his patience and long-suffering was exceedingly magnified, in that notwithstanding Solomon’s apostasy God continued his goodness to him all his days. Indeed God stirred up some adversaries against him, ch. 11.14,23,26, but such as did more mischief to his posterity than to himself. The like blessing was granted to no king in Israel before him or after him. David his father had many troubles; so had Asa, Jehoshaphat, Hezekiah and Josiah, the most flourishing kings that succeeded him.

**V. 22.** *{And Solomon’s provision}* Heb. *bread*. The bread and other provision which Solomon had for himself and his court, was so much, as the like is not read of in any other king’s time. Much was that which was provided for Nehemiah, Neh. 5.18, but it was not comparable to this. See v. 7.

*{for one day}* Every day new provision was brought.

*{was thirty measures of fine flour, and threescore measures of meal}* The word translated *measure* is in Hebrew *Cor*. One *Cor* contained ten *Ephahs*: for a *Cor* and an *Homer* are the same measure, and an *Ephah* and a *Bath* are also the same. But an *Ephah* or *Bath* is the tenth part of an *Homer*, Ezek. 45.11.14. An *Homer* of barley was valued at fifty shekels of silver, Lev. 27.16. Whence it may be gathered, that an *Homer* or *Cor* was a very great measure, the greatest that is mentioned in Scripture. Some take it to be an ass’s load: as we count a seam of wheat (which holds eight bushels) an horse-load. An *Ephah* was about the measure of our bushel: by which account, one *Cor* was about ten bushels: so as Solomon had every day three hundred bushels of flour, and six hundred of meal. See Exod. 16.16. & Isai. 5.10.

**V. 23.** *{Ten fat oxen}* Such as were fatted in stalls.

*{and twenty oxen out of the pastures}* Such choice grounds as made them very fat.

*{and an hundred sheep, &c.}* Under the particulars here mentioned all manner of common and choice, ordinary and extraordinary meats, are comprised. They show, that his provision of flesh was answerable to that of bread, v. 22.

**V. 24.** *{For he had dominion over all the region on this side the river}* This is such a description of Solomon’s dominion as was before set down v. 21.

*{from Tiphsah}* 2 King. 15.16. Jewish expositors take Tiphsah and Azzah for two towns on the borders of Euphrates.

*{even to Azzah}* Azzah is oft put for Gaza, as Gen. 10.19. Deut. 2.23. Gaza is one of the principal cities of the Philistines, 1 Sam. 6.17, and it is called Azzah Jer. 25.20, so as those two, Tiphsah and Azzah, might set out the east and west borders of Solomon’s dominions.

*{over all the kings on this side the river}* See v. 21.

*{and he had peace on all sides round about him}* For there were no enemies that durst oppose him.

**V. 25.** *{And Judah and Israel}* See v. 20.

*{dwelt safely}* Heb. *confidently*. Or, *securely*: without fear of invasion from enemies abroad, or tumults and oppressions of any among them, or over them.

*{every man under his vine, and under his figtree}* In his own possession, Zech. 3.10. This proverbial speech is taken from such as have inheritances or farms in the countries, where they use to plant vineyards and orchards, with vines, fig trees and other fruit trees, and in times of peace quietly enjoy them, receive the fruit of them, and refresh themselves under the shadow of them, 2 King. 18.31. Mich. 4.4.

*{from Dan even to Beer-sheba}* From north to south: from one coast to another, Judg. 20.1. 2 Sam. 3.10. 1 Chr. 10.2.

*{all the days of Solomon}* See v. 21.

**V. 26.** *{And Solomon had forty thousand stalls of horses for his chariots}* By stalls he means divisions in stables: whereof there being ten in every of the stables, this number of forty thousand may stand with that of four thousand, 2 Chron. 9.25. Solomon is the first among the judges and kings of Israel that furnished himself with warhorses and chariots; and that as an evidence of God’s promise, ch. 3.13, and also of his own wisdom in securing his kingdom. In what respect God forbid a king to multiply horses, See on Deut. 17.16.

**V. 27.** *{And those officers}* Mentioned v. 7, &c.

*{provided victual for king Solomon}* For himself, and all that are mentioned v. 7.

*{And for all that came unto king Solomon’s table}* Many came out of the several tribes of Israel, and also out of other nations to Solomon’s table, v. 34. ch. 10.24, and sufficient provision was made for them all.

*{every man his month}* See v. 7.

*{they lacked nothing}* By reason of the diligence and faithfulness of the many officers that were appointed in their courses to make provisions.

**V. 28.** *{Barley also and straw for the horses}* Nor men, nor beasts were neglected.

*{and Dromedaries}* Or, *mules*. Post-horses, Esth. 8.10,14. Swift beasts, or beasts of price, Mich. 1.13.

*{brought they unto the place where the officers were}* To those several storehouses that the officers had appointed for the provisions to be brought unto.

*{every man according to his charge}* This is the ready way for great matters to be well accomplished, that every man look to his own charge.

**V. 29.** *{And God gave Solomon wisdom and understanding}* Herein is accomplished God’s promise, ch. 3.12.

*{exceeding much}* See this exemplified, v. 31.

*{and largeness of heart}* i.e. an heart or mind capable of whatsoever might be known by man. The heart is here put for the whole soul: it comprises under it, mind, will, and affections. He that has small understanding, or is not capable of hearing much, is in common speech said to have a short understanding, and a narrow heart.

*{even as the sand that is upon the sea-shore}* See v. 20. This comparison may be applied either to the large capacity of his understanding, as the seashore is very large; or else to the innumerable matter which he understood, as the sands of the sea are innumerable.

**V. 30.** *{And Solomon’s wisdom excelled the wisdom of all the children of the east-country}* Men of the east were commonly reputed the wisest in the world, Mat. 2.1. Such were the Chaldeans (Dan. 2.2) and other philosophers and astronomers. They of the east country were the first that found out the courses of the stars, and rules of astronomy. Job and his three friends were of the east country, Job 1.3, and they are noted to be wise men.

*{and all the wisdom of Egypt}* Egyptians were also famous for wisdom and knowledge in all sciences, Isai. 19.11,12. Act. 17.22. Heathen philosophers accounted them very wise men. The wisest among the Grecians, professed they had their grounds of philosophy from them. Many famous philosophers went to Egypt for increase of learning and wisdom.

**V. 31.** *{For he was wiser than all men}* In Israel, as the following instances show. Much more, than all men in other nations.

*{than Ethan the Ezrahite, and Heman}* See on 1 Sam. 8.2. These two are mentioned 1 Chron. 2.6, and said to be the sons of Zerah, and so may be Ezrahites. These were of the tribe of Judah, and were long before Solomon’s time; for they were the grandchildren of Judah, the fourth son of Israel, 1 Chron. 2.3,6. Their name might continue famous for wisdom, generation after generation. There were also two Levites of these names, 1 Chron. 15.17,19, expressly styled Ezrahites, Psal. 88.1. & 89.1, whom some take to be the penmen of those two Psalms. At least these being very skillful musicians, had some Psalms committed to their composing or tuning. Of Heman a singular commendation is given 1 Chron. 25.5. They lived in David’s time, and had a great name for wisdom. I take these to be here meant.

*{and Chalcol, and Darda, the sons of Mahol}* Two of the like names are mentioned, 1 Chron. 2.6, and set down as brothers of Ethan and Heman. But another name is here given to the father of Chalcol and Darda: so as it seems they were several persons, yea, and lived in several ages: namely, Chalcol and Darda, whose father was Zerah. But the father of these here named was Mahol: And it is probable that they lived in Solomon’s time, or not long before him. I find no mention of them in any other place.

*{and his fame was in all nations round about him}* Remarkable matters, whether good or bad, are soon divulged far and near.

**V. 32.** *{And he spake three thousand proverbs}* Of these some may be in the books of Solomon now extant: others, by continuance of time, lost. Proverbs, according to the derivation of the word, signifies *excellent, eminent speeches*; which for the approved and useful matter of them are in high account, Prov. 1.1.2,3. &c. See the preface before the Annotations on the Proverbs.

*{and his songs were a thousand and five}* Songs were divine sentences, accurately penned, as poems, and fitted to a melodious tune, as the Psalms, and Canticles.

**V. 33.** *{And he spake of trees}* Of all manner of plants that grow out of the earth.

*{from the cedar-tree that is in Lebanon, even unto the hyssop that springeth out of the wall}* A proverbial speech comprises all kind of plants under it. See v. 25. Exod. 11.5. Isai. 1.6. The cedar is the tallest of trees: See ch. 5.6. The herb intended under this word hyssop is the least and lowest of plants. It may be an herb not known to us. By the wall may be meant rocky and stony places.

*{he spake also of the beasts, &c.}* The particulars here specified show that Solomon was a great philosopher. His parables manifested his divine learning; these his human.

**V. 34.** *{And there came of all people to hear the wisdom of Solomon}* See v. 21. Report of Solomon’s wisdom filled all the world. Solomon’s wisdom is thus set forth 1. To commend the greatness and excellency of it; 2. To show the virtue and beauty of wisdom, which allures all of all sorts unto it; 3. To aggravate their perverse disposition, who regarded not the wisdom of him that was wiser than Solomon, Matt. 12.42. Herein was Solomon a type of Christ’s drawing in all nations to the gospel.

*{from all kings of the earth which had heard of his wisdom}* Namely, by common report, or by their ambassadors: for kings themselves use not to go out of their own country. Yet the queen of Sheba was so taken with the report of his wisdom, that she herself came to Solomon, ch. 10.1.

# CHAPTER 5

**V. 1.** *{And Hiram}* 2 Chron. 2.3, he is styled Huram. A name common to the kings of Tyre.

*{king}* This is added to distinguish him from another Hiram, ch. 7.13,14.

*{of Tyre}* And Sidon too. For the Sidonians were his subjects, v. 9. Tyre and Sidon are oft joined together, as Jer. 47.4. Zach. 9.2. Mat. 11.21. & 15.21. These were two seatowns, Isai. 23.2, &c. in Phoenicia, bordering upon Galilee, towards Lebanon. The people of these countries came in multitudes to Jesus, Mar. 3.8, and Jesus also went into their borders, Mar. 7.24.

*{sent}* To congratulate the king, and renew a league, v. 11.

*{his servants}* Ambassadors, which kings and states use to send one to another, 2 Chron. 32.31.

*{unto Solomon}* It has been usual in all ages for neighboring princes to congratulate each other, especially at their first coming to the crown. David did the like to Hanun, 2 Sam. 10.2. Hiram did this the rather to Solomon, because he had notice of that wisdom which God had given him, and of the many other blessings which God had bestowed on him, v. 7.

*{for he had heard that they had anointed him king in the room of his father}* The great affairs of a kingdom, especially about settling of a new king, are soon made known to the neighboring kingdoms.

*{for Hiram was ever}* Heb. *all days*. All the time that they lived together.

*{a lover of David}* Ready to do all good offices to him, 2 Sam. 5.11. Their amity was firm and stable; not like the league betwixt Benhadad and Baasha, 2 Chron. 16.3,4. Or betwixt Sennacherib and Hezekiah, 2 King. 18.14,17. It was settled on surer grounds. For it is more than probable that Hiram believed in the God of Israel, v. 7, and that upon an entire, inward affection he continued his league with David; and that piety helped on the same.

**V. 2.** *{And Solomon sent to Hiram, saying}* 2 Chron. 2.3. Solomon was many ways moved to send to Hiram for those things he did: 1. By that love and league which had been betwixt David and Hiram; 2. By Hiram’s congratulatory message to him; 3. By that ability which Hiram had to gratify him in his suit; 4. By the like courtesies which Hiram had formerly done to David, 2 Sam. 5.11; 5. By that need which Solomon had of those things he sent for, and wherewith he could not otherwise be supplied; 6. By that use whereunto those things should be put, namely, to build an house to the name of the Lord. A greater and better use there could not be.

**V. 3.** *{Thou knowest how that David my father}* See ch. 8.17.

*{could not build an house}* By common report Hiram might know that which Solomon says he here knew. Beside, there being entire amity betwixt David and Hiram, David might communicate the matter to him; the rather, that Hiram might help Solomon with cedars, and such other things as being requisite for Solomon’s work, were in Hiram’s nation.

*{unto the name of the LORD}* To his glory, Psal. 76.1. The name of God is oft put for God himself, Deut. 28.58. And because God did manifest his presence by sundry types in the temple, it is called *the house of the Lord*, ch. 6.1. See ch. 8.16.

*{his God}* See ch. 15.3.

*{for the wars which were about him on every side}* For, first, they afforded him not time enough to build so magnificent a temple as Solomon did. Secondly, they occasioned him to shed much blood, 1 Chron. 22.8. David was willing and very desirous to build an house to God: but God would not have him do it, 2 Sam. 7.5, &c. Solomon therefore says that his father could not build the house, in way of apology and defense of his father.

*{until the LORD put them}* The enemies with whom he had war.

*{under the soles of his feet}* This phrase implies a full conquest over enemies, Josh. 10.24. Psal. 110.1. Mal. 4.3. As they who are overcome but live, patiently suffer the conquerors to put their feet upon their necks; so they who are slain in the field, lie on the ground to be trampled on by man and horse, Isa. 10.6. & 63.3. Zach. 10.5. Rev. 11.2.

**V. 4.** *{But now the LORD my God}* See ch. 8.28.

*{hath given me}* He ascribes not his crown and dignity to the victory which his father David got, nor to his own prudence, nor to the faithfulness of those that stuck close to him; but to God from whom all promotion comes, Psal. 75.6,7, and by whom kings reign, Pro. 8.15.

*{rest on every side}* From all enemies: a settled firm peace. By reason hereof he had more leisure to attend this great work than his father had: and he would not let slip so fair an opportunity. How he was herein a type of Christ, See 1 Chron. 22.8,9.

*{so that there is neither adversary}* Who might detain me from this work, or hinder me therein.

*{nor evil occurrent}* The word turned *occurrent* is translated *chance*, Eccl. 9.11. It signifies anything that a man meets with, standing in his way, or hindering him in that which he is about. He calls it *evil occurrent*, because it could be nothing but evil that should hinder him; yea, and that it hindered him it was evil.

**V. 5.** *{And behold I purpose}* Heb. *say*. See 2 Chron. 2.1. & 28.10. A purpose arises from an inward discourse of the mind, Mar. 2.6, in which respect it is turned a saying. This phrase, *they said in their heart*, is frequent in sacred Scripture, Psal. 14.1. & 74.8. Thus his purpose was grounded on God’s will and purpose, expressed indefinitely, without designing any particular person, Deut. 12.5,11, and also determinately, expressing that son of David, whom God would have succeed him, 2 Sam. 7.12,13: yea also expressing Solomon by name, 1 Chron. 22.9.

*{to build an house unto the name of the LORD my God}* See v. 3.

*{as the LORD spake unto David my father, saying}* God’s word and promise is a good ground and a great encouragement for one to attempt a thing.

*{thy son whom I will set upon thy throne in thy room, he shall build, &c.}* Solomon’s establishment upon the throne of David, was an evidence that he was the man designed to build the temple.

*{an house unto my name}* See v. 3.

**V. 6.** *{Now therefore command thou}* For kings have a power in their own dominions to command their subjects and servants to do what lawful thing they require.

*{that they}* Namely the servants of Hiram, fit for the work here intended.

*{hew me cedar-trees}* These were tall, straight, large trees: the wood of them odoriferous, very durable, not knotty, nor subject to worms, every way fit for building fair. Both long & large beams, and also fair broad boards were made out of them. These therefore, as a royal present, does one king send unto another, to build him a palace, 2 Sam. 5.11. And for these does one king here send to another, to build the first temple therewith. And the Jews after their captivity, by a grant from Cyrus the Persian monarch, procured cedars from Lebanon, to build the second temple. God implies, that his house was to be built by these, in this phrase, *why build ye not me an house of cedars?* 2 Sam. 7.7.

*{out of Lebanon}* An high, large and goodly mountain, Deut. 3.25, whereon cedars grew, Psal. 92.12. On the north of Judea, bordering on Sidon. Geographers place this Lebanon in Syria, but so as it lies on the confines of the land of Israel, and reaches from Sidon to Damascus in Syria. It appears to have been in those days in the dominion of the king of Tyre: because he from thence sent cedars to David, 2 Sam. 5.11, and Solomon sent to him for cedars. There were also fir trees and algum trees in Lebanon, 2 Chron. 2.8.

*{and my servants shall be with thy servants}* This he promises for help; that all the burden might not lie upon the Sidonians. Though Hiram’s servants had best skill about the season and manner of felling, hewing, squaring, and ordering the trees of Lebanon, yet might Solomon’s servants be very helpful in removing, carrying, and doing other services about these trees.

*{and unto thee will I give hire for thy servants}* The more to encourage them, and to make them the more diligent, careful, and painful.

*{according to all that thou shalt appoint}* Heb. *say*. Solomon hereby showed that he was as ready to give as to receive, to recompense as to require a kindness. Thus he kept his friend from being weary of showing kindness.

*{for thou knowest that there is not among us}* Neighboring kingdoms commonly know what their neighbors can well do, or not.

*{any that can skill to hew timber like unto the Sidonians}* The Sidonians were continually exercised in that work, and so retained their skill from generation to generation. Their help herein was procured after the captivity, Ezr. 3.7. Several countries have their several abilities.

**V. 7.** *{And it came to pass when Hiram heard the words of Solomon}* Not out of the mouth of Solomon himself (for he spoke not unto him mouth to mouth) but sent to him, v. 2, as 2 King. 18.19. Or he spoke by letter, as 2 King. 19.14. This is the more probable, because Hiram answered in writing, 2 Chron. 2.11.

*{that he rejoiced greatly}* Hiram was far from an envious disposition, seeing he rejoiced in another’s good.

*{and said, blessed be}* See ch. 10.8,9. Such a stamp did God set upon the gifts which he gave to Solomon, as made this king, the queen of Sheba, and sundry others to admire the same, and to acknowledge that such gifts must needs come from God; and thereupon to give the praise to the Lord. So did Jethro, Exod. 18.9,10. And Nebuchadnezzar, Dan. 3.28.

*{the LORD}* Jehovah, who had by such wonders made himself known to be the true God, as the heathen had knowledge thereof, Deut. 32.31. Josh. 2.11. 2 King. 5.15. Dan. 3.28. & 6.16.26. Besides Hiram had this benefit by his amity with David, to know the true God. See ch. 8.41.

*{this day}* His heart was even then at that present filled with the praises of God.

*{which hath given unto David}* The love that he did bear to his old friend thus quickened his spirit.

*{a wise son over this great people}* The blessing that the church of God received by Solomon did the more enlarge his heart with rejoicing.

**V. 8.** *{And Hiram sent to Solomon, saying}* He manifests the reality of his entire affection, by granting Solomon’s request.

*{I have considered}* Heb. *heard*. See ch. 3.21. Upon hearing a matter follows consideration. It is the property of a wise man to consider what he hears.

*{the things which thou sentest to me for}* Those mentioned, v. 6.

*{and I will do all thy desire concerning timber of cedar}* See v. 6.

*{and concerning timber of fir}* Fir trees also grew in Lebanon, 2 King. 19.23. Musical instruments were made of fir, 2 Sam. 6.5, and shipboards, Ezek. 27.5. Fir was much used in the temple, ch. 6.15,34. 2 Chr. 3.5. It was put to such uses as cedar was, Cant. 1.17. & Zech. 11.2.

**V. 9.** *{My servants shall bring them down from Lebanon unto the sea}* For it was too far to bring such store of timber as Solomon had out of Lebanon, by land-carriages.

*{and I will convey them by sea in flotes}* He means flat-bottomed vessels, or boards fastened together and towed with ropes. Word for word, *I will put them in flotes*, i.e. I will so fasten them together as they shall float upon the water, and be rowed.

*{unto the place}* He means Joppa, 2 Chron. 2.16. Ezra 3.7. This was an haven-town, Jon. 1.3. Josh. 19.46. Act. 9.43.

*{that thou shalt appoint me}* Heb. *send*. i.e. by message or letter declare the particular place.

*{and will cause them to be discharged there}* There my men shall leave them with your servants, that they may be discharged of them.

*{and thou shalt receive them}* By your servants.

*{and thou shalt accomplish my desire}* He desires a recompense, as for the commodities, so especially for his servants’ pains.

*{in giving food}* Heb. *bread*. i.e. all manner of provision, as v. 11. See ch. 4.22. Although Tyrus was stored with all manner of merchandise brought from other countries, Ezek. 27.3, &c. Yet the land was but barren, and brought not forth provision enough for itself. It was nourished by Herod’s country, Acts 12.20. Therefore Hiram here desired food. The like recompense was given to them many hundred years after this, Ezra 3.7. The ordinary merchandise which from Israel was carried to Tyre, was wheat, honey, oil, Ezek. 27.17. Which shows that Tyre wanted these commodities, and that the Jews were able to furnish them therewith.

*{for my household}* Both himself, and his whole court. See ch. 4.7.

**V. 10.** *{So Hiram gave Solomon cedar-trees, and Fir-trees}* Things just and equal in themselves, needful and useful to him that desires them, being in the power of him of whom they are desired, upon valuable consideration and recompense, are soon granted, and that to the full, betwixt friends. Of cedar trees see v. 6, and of fir trees, v. 8.

*{according to all his desire}* So much he expressly promised, v. 8, and in performing his promise he shows himself faithful.

**V. 11.** *{And Solomon gave Hiram}* Solomon shows himself friendly and faithful to Hiram, as well as Hiram did to him. Friends will fairly deal one with another.

*{twenty thousand measures}* Heb. *Cors*. See ch. 4.22.

*{of wheat, for food to his household}* There is a far greater quantity promised 2 Chron. 2.10. But that might be for his household and workmen too, and this for his household only. Or here synecdochically one kind may be put for the rest. There are promised twenty thousand measures of beaten wheat. By beaten wheat he means such wheat as was beaten out of the ear, and severed from the chaff: We call it clean wheat. This made it to be a better present and a lighter carriage. Just so much wheat is here given. But there is also promised twenty thousand measures of barley: Here he may comprise the barley under the wheat, and intend twenty thousand measures of each. Or otherwise, the twenty thousand measures of barley might be for the workmen, and the wheat for the household. The like may be said of the twenty thousand baths of wine promised 2 Chron. 2.10, but not here mentioned. There also mention is made of twenty thousand baths of oil, and here of but twenty measures. Now one measure (which in Hebrew is *Cor*) contained ten baths. The twenty measures then make but two hundred baths. These twenty measures or two hundred baths were of pure oil, and for Hiram’s household: The rest might be of ordinary oil, and for the workmen.

*{and twenty measures of pure oil}* Heb. *beaten oil*. i.e. the oil of olives well beaten and first coming out from the press, which uses to be the clearest and freest from dregs or lees. These two epithets, beaten, pure, are put together, as Exod. 27.20. Such kind of oil was used in sacrifices, Exod. 29.40. Numb. 28.5.

*{thus gave Solomon to Hiram year by year}* So long as Hiram’s men continued to labor for Solomon.

**V. 12.** *{And the LORD gave Solomon wisdom}* Ch. 3.12. See ch. 4.29.

*{as he promised him}* This is an instance of God’s faithfulness; for *faithful is he that promises*, Heb. 10.23.

*{and there was peace between Hiram and Solomon}* This shows that that which the Psalmist mentions, Psal. 83.7, of the inhabitants of Tyre being enemies of Israel, is not to be taken of David’s or Solomon’s time, but of future time. For Tyrus afterwards remembered not the brotherly covenant, Amos 1.9, and she said against Jerusalem, *Aha, she is broken*, &c. Ezek. 26.2, &c.

*{and they two made a league together}* They renewed that league that had been betwixt David and Hiram.

**V. 13.** *{And king Solomon raised a levy}* Heb. *tribute of men*. For the men yielded up themselves as a kind of tribute to the king, to be disposed of by him.

*{out of all Israel}* If true Israelites were here meant, then they might be artificers, such as had skill in squaring timber and stones, and might be joined with the Sidonians in such works as they did.

*{and the levy was thirty thousand men}* Two thousand and five hundred might be chosen out of each of the twelve tribes: Or else out of some tribes more might be chosen, out of others fewer. Or this may be taken of aliens that were dispersed up and down in Israel, ch. 9.21.

**V. 14.** *{And he sent them to Lebanon ten thousand a month by courses}* That they might have some time to be at their own houses, and not be overwearied with continual labor.

*{a month they were in Lebanon, and two months at home}* The time of their being at home was double to their being abroad at work: for they might have wives, children and families to tend. Of those that joined with the Sidonians, there were but an hundred together at once.

*{And Adoniram was over the levy}* Of this Adoniram see ch. 4.6. He had the charge of making this levy of men, of providing for them, and ordering their places and tasks.

**V. 15.** *{And Solomon had threescore and ten thousand that bare burdens}* These were aliens, 2 Chron. 2.17,18. Thus servile and toilsome works were laid on them.

*{and fourscore thousand hewers in the mountains}* They were hewers both of timber and stone. Of hewers of timber see Isa. 10.15. Of hewers of stone 1 Chron. 22.2.

**V. 16.** *{Beside the chief of Solomon’s officers, which were over the work, three thousand and three hundred}* There is mention of three thousand and six hundred 2 Chr. 2.2. The three hundred overreckoned were overseers of the officers. Or else there might be three thousand and three hundred over hewers of stone, and three hundred over hewers of timber. Or there might be three hundred extraordinary, in case any of the ordinary should fail by sickness, death, or any other way.

*{which ruled over the people that wrought in the work}* Where there are multitudes of workmen there is great need of many overseers to keep them to their work, and in good order.

**V. 17.** *{And the king commanded}* Solomon himself had his eyes and hands about this work to order the weighty things therein.

*{and they brought great stones, costly stones, and hewed stones}* Heb. *stones of cutting*. Such as were fit to be cut, hewed and squared. So 1 Chron. 22.2. Three sorts of stone are mentioned; 1. Great, that they might lie more sure. For small stones are ready to fall one from another. 2. Costly, or *precious*: for the Hebrew word signifies *precious*, and so is translated Isai. 28.16. 3. Hewed, i.e. squared, and every way fitted to lie close and firm. And hence it is that *neither hammer, nor axe, nor any tool of Iron was heard in the house while it was in building*, ch. 6.7.

*{to lay the foundation of the house}* Though these stones were hid in the earth, yet Solomon would spare no cost, but have them every way of the best sort, & every way fit for such a glorious fabric as was to be erected. Thus as the whole temple did set out the whole mystical body of Christ, which is his church; so the foundation here mentioned did fitly typify the foundation of the church, which is Christ, 1 Cor. 3.10,11, yea and that foundation also which the apostle sets out in these words, Eph. 2.20. *Ye are built upon the foundation of the Apostles and Prophets, Jesus Christ himself being the chief cornerstone*. This is the foundation whereof the prophet speaks, Isai. 28.16.

**V. 18.** *{And Solomon’s builders, and Hiram’s builders did hew them}* Though they were of two nations, yet willingly they joined together in this great work, which was in special manner the Lord’s. Thus shall Jews and Gentiles build up the church of God.

*{and the stone-squarers}* Or, *Giblites*. These were inhabitants of Gebal, Ez. 27.9, near to Sidon, and were artificial stonecutters. The Hebrew word from whence this title *Giblite* is derived, signifies *to terminate or limit a thing*. Now masons use to limit their stones to such a proportion as they may be even one with another in a building. The translators therefore taking the word appellatively, translate it *stone-squarers*: Others, *masons*. In the Syriac the word signifies *to form or fashion*; which in general makes to the same purpose. These Giblites, as the Sidonians before mentioned, were heathen: Yet because they were skillful in their workmanship, they were used about the holy temple. Thus abilities of men out of the church may lawfully be used for helping on the good of the church. See on Psal. 83.7. Josh. 13.5.

*{and they prepared timber and stones to build the house}* Preparation is of singular use before any great and weighty work.

# CHAPTER 6

**Vers. 1.** *{And it came to pass}* 2 Chron. 3.1. By divine providence it fell out that there should be so fit a season for building such an house as the temple was. Till Solomon’s time Israel was subject to many changes: David had subdued all enemies round about, and settled peace in Israel. Thus that which is here set down came to pass.

*{in the four hundred and fourscore year}* Forty of these were spent in the wilderness, Numb. 14.33. Seventeen under Joshua, forty under Othniel, Judg. 3.11. Fourscore under Ehud and Shamgar, Judg. 3.30. Forty under Deborah, Judg. 5.31. Forty under Gideon, Judg. 8.28. Three under Abimelech, Judg. 9.22. Three and twenty under Tola, two and twenty under Jair, Judg. 10.2,3. Six under Jephthah, seven under Ibzan, ten under Elon, eight under Abdon, Judg. 12.7,9,11,14. Twenty under Samson, Judg. 15.20. Forty under Eli, 1 Sam. 4.18. Forty under Samuel and Saul, Act. 13.21. Forty under David, 2 Sam. 5.4. Four under Solomon.

*{after the children of Israel were come out of the land of Egypt}* Then the Israelites began to be a free people, and a nation of themselves; therefore the computation of times in that state began from thence.

*{in the fourth year of Solomon’s reign over Israel}* In the former years of his reign he prepared and fitted all manner of materials for the work.

*{in the month Zif}* The Jews began the year about the spring equinoctial. Their first month was called *Abib*, Exod. 12.2. & 13.4. This name signified *the spring*. Their second month was called *Zif*, as here in this verse, & v. 37. *Zif* signifies *brightness*: Then are plants in their chiefest splendor. Their seventh month was called *Ethanim*, ch. 8.2, which signifies *ripeness or strength*. Then fruits are at their full ripeness & strength. Their eighth month they called *Bul*, v. 38. *Bul*, signifies *fading*. Then is the fall of leaves. These four are without question Hebrew names. There are two other which have an Hebrew notation: but because there is no mention of them till after the return of the Jews from their captivity, they are supposed to be Chaldee names. The one is *Chisleu*, which is counted the ninth month, Zech. 7.1. Neh. 1.1. This title signifies *variable or tempestuous*. So uses the weather to be in that month. Besides *Chesil* is put for *Orion* Job 9.9. & 38.31. Amos 5.8, a star which rising in this month causes great tempest. The other is *Sebat*, Zech. 1.7, which signifies *a dry stick, or staff*. And in this month are trees very dry. There are five other names of months used in the history of the Jews after their captivity, as *Nisan*, Neh. 2.1. *Sivan*, Esth. 8.9. *Elul*, Neh. 6.15. *Tebeth*, Esth. 2.16. *Adar*, Ezr. 6.15. Of all these see those distinct places. These are all the names of months which are mentioned in sacred Scripture. To the seven Chaldee or Persian names before mentioned five more are added in other authors, as *Ajar*, *Tamuz*, *Ab*, *Tisri*, *Marchesvan*. The order of these Persian names was this: 1 *Nisan*, 2 *Ajar*, 3 *Sivan*, 4 *Tamuz*, 5 *Ab*, 6 *Elul*, 7 *Tisri*, 8 *Marchesvan*; 9 *Chisleu*, 10 *Tebeth*, 11 *Sebat*, 12 *Adar*. The ordinary reckoning of months in sacred Scripture was by numeral distinctions; as the first, the second, the third, &c.
