*{which is the second month}* In the first month the Passover was celebrated, Ex. 12.3. In the very beginning of this second month even the second day Solomon set upon this great work, 2 Chr. 3.2.

*{that he began to build}* Heb. *built*. He laid the foundation.

*{the house of the LORD}* i.e. the temple. See on v. 2. Of the place where this house was built, see 2 Chron. 3.1.

**V. 2.** *{And the house which king Solomon built for the LORD}* For the worship of the Lord, and the honor of his name, in which respect it is called *the house of the Lord*, v. 1.

*{the length thereof}* From east to west. Of the difference betwixt the temple and the tabernacle, see 1 Chron. 22.5.

*{was threescore cubits}* A cubit is the space from the elbow to the top of the middle finger, which of an ordinary man’s stature is eighteen inches. It is supposed that the sacred cubit, styled *a great cubit*, Ezek. 41.8, after which the tabernacle and temple were built, was one and twenty inches, or two foot long at the most: For it was *a cubit and an hand breadth*, Ezek. 43.13. See on Deut. 3.11.

*{and the breadth thereof twenty cubits}* Which was a third part of the length.

*{and the height thereof thirty cubits}* This is to be reckoned from the pavement to the upper ceiling. The measures here set down show that this was a very large edifice, an hundred and twenty foot long, and forty foot broad, taking a cubit in the largest size, for two foot. But larger edifices have in sundry ages and places been erected. We read of a temple built at Ephesus 425 foot long, and 220 foot broad. Yet for costly and choice materials, for curious and exact workmanship, for spiritual employments, and for mystical signification, never was there the like edifice in the world to this of Solomon’s.

**V. 3.** *{And the porch before the temple of the house}* This was no part of the forementioned house, but added to the open end thereof for a more stately entering into it.

*{twenty cubits was the length thereof}* From north to south, all along the end of the temple.

*{according to the breadth of the house}* See v. 2.

*{and ten cubits was the breadth thereof before the house}* Thorough this porch was the passage into the temple. The height of it far exceeded the height of the temple; for it was ninety cubits higher, 2 Chr. 3.4. It has been the manner of building fair edifices to make the entrance much higher than the rest of the building.

**V. 4.** *{And for the house he made windows of narrow lights}* Or, *windows broad within and narrow without*; or *skewed and closed*. The windows were narrow without, to keep out the weather; and broad within, to diffuse the light.

**V. 5.** *{And against the wall of the house}* Or, *upon*; or, *joining to*. There were two thick walls that compassed the house about, for the strength thereof. Betwixt these walls were chambers built for the priests and Levites’ use, and for the treasure of the temple, which was, of firstfruits, tithes, freewill-offerings, and the like, 1 Chr. 28.11. 2 Chr. 31.11,12. Ezr. 8.29. Neh. 10.37,38,39. & 13.5. Ezek. 42.13. The tabernacle afforded no such chambers, yet the priests and Levites had tents about the tabernacle which served them for like purposes, Numb. 3.38.

*{he built chambers}* Heb. *floors*. The floors were under-part of the chambers, and by a synecdoche are put for the whole.

*{round about}* The temple.

*{against the walls of the house}* These latter words are added as a more full expression of the former.

*{both of the temple}* The outward and larger part of the house, where all sorts of priests did their service, v. 17.

*{and of the oracle}* The innermost part: whereinto the high priest only, and that but once a year, might enter. It was called *the oracle* because there God used to deliver divine answers or oracles, Levit. 16.2. Exod. 30.6. This Oracle was at the west end of the temple.

*{and he made chambers}* Heb. *ribs*. For as ribs compass a body, so did these chambers the temple.

**V. 6.** *{The nethermost chamber was five cubits broad, &c.}* The chambers were three stories high, one over another. And as they were higher, they were also wider; for the walls were thickest at the bottom: the higher they ascended, the thinner they were, and the space betwixt them the wider. Every chamber was of one height, namely five cubits, v. 10.

*{for without in the walls of the house he made narrowed rests}* Heb. *narrowings, *or,* rebatements*. At the floor of the middle chambers there was a rebatement in the wall, a cubit thick; and at the floor of the highest chambers such another rebatement. On these rebatements the beams of the chambers were laid.

*{round about}* See v. 5.

*{that the beams should not be fastened in the walls of the house}* That no holes should be made in the wall for the ends of the beams to be put into.

**V. 7.** *{And the house when it was in building was built of stone}* Namely the walls thereof round about were only of stone. But wood also was used for beams and other like purposes, ch. 5.18.

*{made ready before it was brought thither}* This is premised as a reason of that which follows.

*{so that there was neither hammer, nor ax, nor any tool of iron heard in the house, while it was in building}* These are all such instruments as in their use make a noise. The mentioning of these does not necessarily import that such instruments as were useful to make the stones and timber lie firm, might not be used. That no instrument was heard in the building, was for the honor of the temple, and also for typical signification, in relation to the church both on earth and in heaven: To the church on earth, to show that schisms, contentions and all manner of jars (which are as the knocking of iron instruments) must be avoided: To the church in heaven, to set out the quiet and peaceable state thereof. As the stones and timber were hewn in the mountains, and fitted for the temple, to be there laid without knocking; so saints are here in this world prepared for heaven, that there they may enjoy rest and glory.

**V. 8.** *{The door for the middle chamber}* Or, *middle story*.

*{was in the right side of the house}* Heb. *right shoulder*. For the side of the house was as a shoulder to support it. As they went into the temple, the right side was the north; for the entrance was at the east end. In Ezek. 41.11, mention is made of two doors, one towards the north, and another towards the south.

*{and they went up with winding stairs}* These stairs went up from the bottom to the uppermost story; and so by them they had a passage into all the three stories.

*{into the middle chamber}* Or, *story*.

*{and out of the middle into the third}* Thus they went from one story to another.

**V. 9.** *{So he built the house and finished it}* He ceased not to go on with his work till he had perfected the same.

*{and covered the house}* The top or roof of it.

*{with beams and boards of cedar}* Or, *the vault-beams, and the ceilings with cedar*. This must needs be meant of the inward part of the roof. For boards of cedar could not long keep off the weather. The temple was questionless covered otherwise with lead. Of cedar see ch. 5.6.

**V. 10.** *{And then he built chambers against all the house}* On both sides thereof: yea and about the oracle, v. 5, whereinto there was no entrance from without.

*{five cubits high}* Thus three stories of chambers made fifteen cubits in height: so as above these chambers there was room enough for windows to let light into the temple, which was thirty cubits high, v. 2.

*{and they rested on the house with timber of cedar}* The beams whereon those chambers were built, did lie on the narrowed rests mentioned v. 6.

**V. 11.** *{And the word of the LORD came to Solomon, saying}* It came to him by the message of some prophet: For God himself appeared to him but twice, ch. 9.2. & 11.9.

**V. 12.** *{Concerning this house which thou art in building}* This phrase shows that God made this promise to Solomon before the temple was fully finished, and that it is not here inserted by way of anticipation, as if it were the same promise that is mentioned ch. 9.4,5. God would hereby encourage him, even while he was in the work, to go on therein cheerfully.

*{if thou wilt walk in my statutes, &c.}* See c. 2.3. God’s promises have their provisos. See ch. 8.25. By this proviso God draws Solomon’s mind from a superstitious confidence in the external work, and intimates that he had more respect unto his faithful obedience to his word than unto that glorious building, ch. 9.6,7,8,9.

*{then will I perform my word unto thee, which I spake unto David thy father}* 2 Sam. 7.13. 1 Chr. 22.10. It much lies in children themselves, to partake of the promises made to their parents for the good of their posterity.

**V. 13.** *{And I will dwell among the children of Israel}* As a tender father and provident master I will abide among them; and they shall have assured evidence of my gracious presence, Lev. 26.11,12. 2 Cor. 6.16.

*{and will not forsake}* This negative, as it adds a confirmation to the former affirmative, so it implies a perpetual continuation of God’s grace and favor to his people.

*{my people Israel}* Those whom before God styled *children of Israel* in relation to their old father Jacob, he here styles *his people* in relation to himself, because he had chosen them to be his church, Exod. 19.5,6.

**V. 14.** *{So Solomon built, &c.}* See v. 9. This repeating of the same matter twice shows that God himself took special notice thereof, and would have others also so to do.

**V. 15.** *{And he built the walls of the house within}* He ceiled the walls, or, as we use to speak, he wainscoted them: for this was the innermost visible building of the house. The building of stone could not be seen within, all being covered over with the ceiling.

*{with boards of cedar}* See ch. 5.6.

*{both the floor of the house}* This was laid with cedar boards, as now most use to lay their floors with fir boards.

*{and the walls}* Or, *from the floor of the house unto the walls, &c.* and so v. 16. The meaning is, that the house was ceiled with cedar boards on the walls from the bottom to the top.

*{and he covered them on the inside with wood}* Namely, with cedar. This he did, because he intended to overlay all with gold, v. 22, and to carve knops thereon, v. 18, neither of which could so well have been done on the stone wall.

*{and covered the floor of the house with planks of fir}* It was requisite that the boards of the floor should be much thicker than the ceiling of the walls: wherefore he provided planks for the floor. Of fir trees see ch. 5.8.

**V. 16.** *{And he built twenty cubits on the sides of the house}* The house here meant was the most holy place, which being twenty foot square every way, he ceiled it on all the walls from bottom to top: for so much he intends by building, as v. 15.

*{both the floor and the walls with boards of cedar}* The floor of the most holy place was of better boards than the floor of the holy place. That was floored with cedar, this with fir, to show that the glory of the triumphant church is greater than of the militant.

*{he even built them for it within}* He prepared and fitted them for it.

*{even for the oracle}* See v. 5.

*{even for the most holy place}* Heb. *holy of holies*. By this phrase he shows what he meant by the oracle. This doubling of the word does much amplify the excellency of the place. So *heaven of heavens*, ch. 8.27. Psal. 148.4. See ch. 8.13. This holy of holies was a type of the *heaven of heavens*, Heb. 9.11,24, which is the most holy place that ever was. The innermost part of the temple was so styled for distinction from that other part which was styled *the holy place*, ch. 8.8.

**V. 17.** *{And the house, that is, the temple before it}* Both these words are synecdochically used. For house, being a general word, is here put for that particular house which is called *the house of God*; and temple, comprising under it sundry parts, is here put for one of them, namely *the holy place*, ch. 8.8.

*{was forty cubits long}* The house which Solomon built is said ver. 2 to be threescore cubits in length, but there the house is put for both the parts, the holy, and most holy place; here only, for the holy place.

**V. 18.** *{And the cedar of the house within}* i.e. of the inner part of the house.

*{was carved with knops}* Or, *gourds*. So is this word translated 2 King. 4.39.

*{and open flowers}* Heb. *openings of flowers*. Such knops and open flowers as were here carved in wood, were also cast in brass, ch. 7.24. When flowers are open they are the larger and fairer; and therefore they were carved open.

*{all was cedar}* i.e. all the wall everywhere was covered with cedar.

*{there was no stone seen}* This shows the reason of the former phrase.

**V. 19.** *{And the oracle}* See v. 5.

*{he prepared in the house within}* In the uppermost and innermost part of the temple, which was at the west end, contrary to our chancels, which use to be in the east end of the church.

*{to set there the ark}* Other things also were in the most holy place, Heb. 9.4,5. But this was the most principal end for which the oracle was made. Some of the other things appertained to the ark, and so may be comprised under it, as the propitiatory or mercy-seat, which was the cover of the ark, Ex. 25.17. See 1 Chr. 28.11. and the glorious cherubims: they shadowed it over, Ex. 25.18. The rings and staves were in former times to bear the ark, Ex. 25.12, &c. The pot of manna and Aaron’s rod were monuments of God’s special providence. The ark itself was the chiefest evidence of God’s presence, 1 Sam. 4.3,5,7,22, and the most principal type of Christ: Among and above other types the Jews had this in highest account. The word translated Ark, in Hebrew is ארון *Aron*, which is put for a *coffin*, *coffer*, or *chest*. The coffin whereinto Joseph’s dead body was put, is so styled Gen. 50.26. The chest whereinto that money was put which was gathered for repairing the temple, is also so styled 2 King. 12.9. This showed that all the counsels of God, all the love and favor of God, all that God accounts precious, are treasured up in Christ; even as in a man’s coffer or chest his jewels, plate, coin, treasure, and whatsoever he has in special esteem, uses to be put. Such a coffer or chest shall be in the house where he dwells, in the chamber where he lies, even by his bedside. For *where a mans treasure is, there will his heart be also*, Matth. 6.21. Thus *in Christ are hid all the treasures of God*, Col. 2.3. Christ is *the son of his love*, Col. 1.13. Christ is *his elect in whom his soul delighteth*, Isa. 42.1. Christ is ever *at Gods right hand*, Heb. 10.12. Therefore the ark, that type of Christ, is here in God’s house in the innermost part thereof.

*{of the covenant of the LORD}* See ch. 3.15.

**V. 20.** *{And the oracle in the fore part}* Namely in the fore part of the temple, which was the uppermost part thereof.

*{was twenty cubits in length}* This was half the length of the holy place, v. 17.

*{and twenty cubits in breadth}* This was the full breadth of the temple.

*{and twenty cubits in the height thereof}* Though the whole temple were thirty cubits high, v. 2, from the foundation to the top, yet there being steps whereby they ascended out of the court into the porch, and out of the porch into the holy place, and out of the holy place into the most holy place, there might remain but twenty cubits betwixt the floor and top of the oracle: Or there might be an inner roof covering the oracle below the common roof which was over all the house; and thus betwixt the floor of the oracle and that inner roof thereof there might be but twenty cubits in height.

*{and he overlaid it with pure gold}* Heb. *shut up*. That which was thoroughly refined and laid up especially for that purpose, 1 Chr. 18.18. & 29.3,4. 2 Chron. 3.6. See ch. 9.28. This was not gilt, but overlaid with plates of gold.

*{and so covered}* In like manner the altar was covered, being overlaid with plates of gold.

*{the altar}* For incense, v. 22.

*{which was of cedar}* It was first made of cedar board, then plates of gold laid over it. In this respect it is called *the Altar of gold*, ch. 7.48. The altar which Moses made was of *shittim* wood, Ex. 30.1, which in largeness, smoothness, lastingness, and other such properties was like cedar.

**V. 21.** *{So Solomon overlaid the house within}* Namely the most holy place.

*{with pure gold}* See v. 20.

*{and he made a partition}* This partition divided the most holy place from the holy place.

*{by the chains of gold before the oracle}* These chains were to hold up the veil whereby those two places were divided, 2 Chron. 3.14,16.

*{and he overlaid it with gold}* There were cedar boards wherewith also the foresaid partition was made, and these were overlaid with plates of gold.

**V. 22.** *{And the whole house he overlaid with gold}* He means both the holy and most holy place.

*{until he had finished all the house}* He left no part thereof uncovered with gold.

*{also the whole altar, that was by the oracle, he overlaid with gold}* The altar of incense is here meant, as v. 20. This was set in the holy place, Exod. 40.26, close by the entrance into the most holy; and it was overlaid with gold on the top, sides, horns, and everywhere else. So was the altar of incense which Moses caused to be made, Exod. 37.26. This altar typified the person of Christ: The wood whereof it was made, his humanity; the gold, his deity; the incense offered thereon, his intercession. See ch. 9.25.

**V. 23.** *{And within the oracle he made two cherubims}* A cherub was a representation of a young man with wings. The image of a man sets out their understanding; the wings, their speed and swiftness. Cherubims were angels, Gen. 3.24. These here were carved statues. As in the tabernacle there were two cherubims of massive gold, and sundry others wrought in the curtains and veil, Ex. 36.8,35, so in the temple there were these two great cherubims more, and many other graven on the walls, on the doors, v. 29,32,35, and on the bases, chap. 7.29,36. All these set out the multitudes of angels in churches, as Eph. 3.10. See Exod. 25.18.

*{of olive-tree}* Or, *oily*. Heb. *trees of oil*. This kind of wood was hard, firm and lasting, and so fit to make such statues as these cherubims were, and to be for posts and doors, ver. 31,33. These cherubims were very great, like giants, and thereupon the substance of them was of wood, whereas those which Moses made, being far less might, better be of beaten gold, Exod. 25.18. These cherubims covered the cherubims of Moses, 2 Chron. 3.10,11,12,13.

*{and ten cubits high}* This measure shows that they far exceeded the cherubims of Moses.

**V. 24.** *{And five cubits was the one wing of the cherub, &c.}* The breadth from the uttermost part of their two wings was as much as their height. The body of a cherub was comprehended under the wings, so as the two wings together, with the thickness of the body made ten full cubits.

**V. 25.** *{And the other cherub was ten cubits}* So as each of them being ten cubits apiece, they reached from one wall of the oracle to the other, which was twice ten cubits.

*{both the cherubims were of one measure}* One as tall as the other, and one as broad in bulk and wings as the other.

*{and of one size}* Thus were they every way uniform, which is a great grace in things standing together, and put to the same use. Thus are the two ears, eyes, arms, hands, thighs, legs, and feet in a man’s body. The angels are every way unanimous: so what a comely thing would it be for ministers of Christ to be unanimous and uniform, 2 Cor. 12.18, and for other Christians also, Psal. 133.1. Act. 2.46.

**V. 26.** *{The height of one cherub was ten cubits}* See v. 23. It reached up but to half the height of the oracle; which was twenty cubits high.

*{and so was it of the other cherub}* See v. 25.

**V. 27.** *{And he set the cherubims within the inner house}* The most holy place, which being within the holy place, is therefore called *the inner house*, 2 Chr. 3.12.

*{and they stretched forth the wings of the cherubims}* The workmen made them so far to stretch. Or, the cherubims stretched forth their wings, Ex. 25.20.

*{so that the wing of the one touched one wall, &c.}* Thus they reached from one end of the oracle, to the other.

**V. 28.** *{And he overlaid the cherubims with gold}* As he had done the incense altar before, v. 22.

**V. 29.** *{And he carved all the walls of the house round about with carved figures of cherubims}* See v. 23.

*{and palm-trees}* These kinds of trees, at least the boughs of them, were used to set out peace; and therefore were set on the walls of the temple.

*{and open flowers}* See v. 18.

*{within and without}* If this be applied to the partition betwixt the most holy and holy place, it signifies both sides thereof: or otherwise it may be taken for the walls within and without the oracle.

**V. 30.** *{And the floor of the house}* See v. 15.

*{he overlaid with gold}* See v. 20.

*{within and without}* See v. 29. Here this phrase is taken of the floor within and without the veil of the oracle.

**V. 31.** *{And for the entering of the oracle he made doors of olive-trees}* See v. 23.

*{the lintel and side-posts}* He means the two side posts for the door to hang and fall upon, and the transom over them.

*{were a fifth part of the wall}* Or, *fifth-square*. These were four cubits, which is the fifth part of twenty cubits. See v. 20. Each side was four cubits. The door had two leaves, and each leaf had two foldings, as v. 34.

**V. 32.** *{The two doors}* Or, *leaves of the doors*.

*{also}* As well as the posts.

*{were of olive tree}* See v. 23.

*{and he carved upon them carvings of cherubims, &c.}* See v. 29.

*{and open flowers}* Heb. *openings of flowers*.

*{and overlaid them with gold}* See v. 20.

*{and spread gold, &c.}* There was nothing in either part of the temple which was not overlaid with gold. And the same curious carvings which were on the sides of the house, were also on the doors: they were every way uniform, v. 29.

**V. 33.** *{So also made he for the doors of the temple posts of olive-tree}* The temple here is taken for the holy place: and the matter of the doors hereof was such as of the doors of the oracle.

*{a fourth part of the wall}* Or, *four-square*. If this be taken for the fourth part, the doors of the temple were larger than the doors of the oracle. These being the fifth part v. 31 of twenty cubits, were four cubits; those being the fourth part, were five cubits.

**V. 34.** *{And the two doors were of fir-tree}* See ch. 5.8. Fir trees not being every way so excellent as olive trees, the olive tree was used about the most holy place.

*{the two leaves of one door were folding, &c.}* By this means the passage was the larger.

**V. 35.** *{And he carved thereon, &c.}* See v. 29.

*{fitted upon the carved work}* i.e. beaten so close to the carved work as every part of the engraven was distinctly discerned through the gold.

**V. 36.** *{And he built the inner court}* Solomon built the temple answerable to the tabernacle, as in sundry other respects, so in adjoining a court thereunto. Of the court which Moses added to the tabernacle, see Exod. 27.9, &c. That court was far greater than the tabernacle; so was the court which Solomon here built, far larger than the temple. The word court in the singular number may seem to imply that there was only one court: but by a synecdoche the singular number is put for the plural; or else that court was divided into two or more courts. Courts in the plural number are in other places mentioned, as 1 Chr. 23.28. & 28.6. There is express mention made of two courts, 2 King. 21.5. & 23.12. So much also is here implied under this note of distinction inner: For it is a relative word, and has relation to *an utter*, Ezek. 10.3,5. Or *outward*, Ezek. 40.19,20. This inner court was next to the temple. It was also called *the higher court*, Jer. 36.10. For the temple and all the courts appertaining thereunto were built on the side of an hill. The temple on an higher part of the hill than the courts, and the inner court on an higher part than the utter; that so they that were in the first entrance, or lowest place, might see what was done in the upper places. That which here and in sundry other places is called the inner court is styled *the court of the priests*, 2 Chron. 4.9. For therein priests and Levites did their ordinary services. The other court is called *the great court*, 2 Chron. 4.9. For as the inner court was greater than the holy place, so the utter court was greater than the inner. Into the great court came the people; thither they brought all their offerings; and there the priest took them at their hands, Deut. 12.11. Thither they assembled to be instructed in the law of God, Ezr. 10.9, and there they stood while the priests were offering their sacrifices, yea and while they offered incense in the holy place: and there they used to pray, Lu. 1.10. There also the word was usually preached to them, Act. 3.12,11. & 5.12. In this court was the great brazen altar, whereof see 2 Chr. 4.1. This was placed by the entrance into that court out of the people’s court; that so the people might the better see the sacrifices offered thereon. For when a solemn sacrifice was offered, the people in a semicircle stood round in their own court next to the priests, and so were said to *compass the Altar*, Psal. 26.6. In this court also was the great sea, the ten lavers, and all other holy utensils that were of brass. Many Jewish and Christian expositors make three courts, namely the priests’ court, the people’s court, and the women’s court. Some there be that make the people’s and the women’s court to be but one and the same, yet so divided as the men were in one part thereof, and the women in the other; by reason of which division they might be accounted two courts. Another court they make to be for such as were unclean, and for strangers or Gentiles; yet so as there was a division therein, so as the unclean were in one part, and strangers in another. All these divisions may stand with that distinction of two courts which is expressly set down 2 Chr. 4.9. Namely the priests’ court and the great court. The great court compassed the temple and the priests’ court round about. In the former part of the great court were such Israelites as were accounted clean, and they both men and women, but divided one from another, as we heard before. In the other part were unclean persons and aliens, and these also divided one from another. This was it for which Paul was accused, that *he had brought Greeks into the Temple*, Act. 21.28. They supposed he had brought strangers into the court of the Israelites. For not only the most holy and holy place were called the temple, but also the porch before the holy place, yea and the priests’ court and the great court are all comprised, in a general acception, under these phrases, temple and house of God. In which respect that place where oxen and sheep were sold, and where changers of money sat, is called the temple and house of God, Joh. 2.14,16. In the court of the people Solomon erected that scaffold whereon he stood when by prayer and blessing he consecrated the temple, 2 Chr. 6.13, and wherein Joash was set when he was first anointed king, 2 Chr. 23.13, yet this place is called the house of the Lord, 2 Chr. 23.12,20.

*{with three rows of hewed stone, and a row of cedar-beams}* This is a description of a wall. Now there were walls whereby the several courts were severed one from another, and whereby also the courts were compassed round about and enclosed. The former sort were but three cubits high, and made so low that all that were in any of the utter courts might over those walls see the manner of the priests’ offering their sacrifices, and other things done in the priests’ court. In relation to these walls by the three rows of stones may be meant three sorts of choice stones laid even together. These are said to be hewed, because by hewing they were made square and even. Over these were a row of cedar-beams, that the stones might lie the faster under them, and that people might the better lean on them, and look over them. Or these cedar-beams might be for the gates, thorough which there were passages out of one court into another. The uttermost wall was a very high one; and to it were adjoined chambers and galleries. The wall being built with three courses of squared and polished stones, cedar-beams were laid under those rooms to bear them up.

**V. 37.** *{In the fourth year}* Of Solomon’s reign. See v. 1.

*{was the foundation of the house of the LORD laid}* That house was begun to be built. See v. 1.

*{in the month Zif}* See v. 1.

**V. 38.** *{And in the eleventh year}* Of Solomon’s reign. This was the seventh year after the house was begun to be built. If the stateliness of the building, the curious workmanship therein, and the many things appertaining thereunto be duly weighed, we may conclude that Solomon used great speed and diligence in absolving the same.

*{in the month Bul}* See v. 1.

*{which is the eighth month}* This answered in part to our November.

*{was the house finished throughout all the parts thereof, and according to all the fashion of it}* Or, *with all the appurtenances thereof, and with all the ordinances thereof*. The house itself, the several divisions thereof, all the courts, walls, chambers, galleries, instruments, utensils, carvings, deckings, and all things else appertaining thereunto, were finished.

*{So was he seven years in building it}* This is a round number, and therefore thus set down alone. The like is used in sundry other cases. Six months are in a full and just account to be added hereunto: for Solomon began to build the house in the second month of his fourth year, and finished it in the eighth month of his eleventh year. Compare the first and last verses of this chapter together.

# CHAPTER 7

**Ver. 1.** *{But}* This that is here noted is set down by way of opposition to the last clause in the former chapter: and the opposition is in the difference of years.

*{Solomon was building his own house}* That which he intended for himself and his followers, commonly called the king’s court.

*{thirteen years}* Ch. 9.10. These thirteen years began after the temple was finished. He was six years longer in building his own house, than the house of the Lord; not that his own was larger or more curiously built than the Lord’s, but because he neither had such materials prepared for it, nor employed so many workmen about it. The house of Lebanon, v. 2, the porch for the throne, v. 7, and the house for the queen, v. 8, were all built in this time.

**V. 2.** *{He built also the house of the forest of Lebanon}* This house was so called, because great store of trees, as in Lebanon, were planted about it, and gardens, orchards, groves, walks and all manner of delightful things were added thereto, Eccl. 2.4,5,6. It had also in the several stories of it above a hundred pillars of cedar. The beams, boards, and all the timber whereof it was made, were of the cedars of Lebanon: and as some gather out of ch. 9.19, it was framed in Lebanon.

*{the length thereof was an hundred cubits}* Whereas from this measure it may be objected that this house was longer than the house of God, which is said to be but sixty cubits long, and twenty broad, ch. 6.2, I answer, 1. That the cubits after which the temple was measured were sacred, of four and twenty inches; but these common, of eighteen inches. See ch. 6.2. 2. The measures of the temple are reckoned from inside to inside, the thickness of the two walls, and the space of the porch at the entry, and of the chambers round about, being left out. 3. It is supposed that the three houses, one for the king, another for the queen; a third for delight, are all comprised under the one hundred cubits; yea, all the king’s treasury, 2 Chr. 9.16.

*{and the breadth thereof fifty cubits}* It was half as broad as long.

*{and the height thereof thirty cubits}* This was every way larger than the temple. See ch. 6. v. 2.

*{upon four rows of cedar-pillars}* These pillars were to support it, by reason of the breadth thereof.

*{with cedar-beams upon the pillars}* These walls were of stone, v. 9, but the pillars stood in their ranks between the stone walls. The first story was supported with four rows of pillars for the greater strength. The ends of the beams rested upon the heads of the pillars.

**V. 3.** *{And it was covered with cedar above}* He means cedar boards with which it was boarded.

*{upon the beams}* Heb. *ribs*. For beams in a house are as ribs in a living creature.

*{that lay on forty five pillars}* This is to be taken of the higher story: For the first story was supported by four rows of pillars, v. 2.

*{fifteen in a row}* These three times multiplied make up the forty-five.

**V. 4.** *{And there were windows in three rows}* These three rows here intended were in three stories one over another.

*{and light was against light}* Heb. *sight against sight*. By windows light is let into rooms, and thereby men see both in the room, and thorough those windows out of the room: By light against light he means so many windows on one side of the room as on the other, directly opposite, and that for more light and delight.

*{in three ranks}* One over another. By this it appears that this fair edifice was three stories high, there being three rooms one over another.

**V. 5.** *{And all the doors and posts were square}* Or, *spaces and pillars were square in prospect*. They were all alike, all square; and they were so framed as the doors fell more evenly and closely upon the posts.

*{with the windows}* The windows also were made like unto the doors.

*{and light was against light in three ranks}* See v. 4.

**V. 6.** *{And he made a porch}* This porch was before the house of Lebanon, and that for the greater grace thereof. Through it they entered into the house. So had he made a porch before the temple, chap. 6.3.

*{of pillars}* This porch had many pillars in it, and therein was answerable to the house itself, v. 2. It is probable that there were upper stories also in this porch, which were supported by the pillars.

*{the length thereof was fifty cubits}* This measure was answerable to the breadth of the house, v. 2, before which it stood: even so the porch before the temple was as long as the temple was broad, ch. 6.3.

*{and the breadth thereof thirty cubits}* This measure shows it to be a spacious entrance.

*{and the porch was before them}* Or, *according to them*. Everything in the porch was answerable to the house before which it was.

*{and the other pillars}* Namely, those which were in the porch, and in the several stories thereof.

*{and the thick beam}* Or, *the main great summer whereon the floors of the upper rooms were laid*.

*{were before them}* Or, *according to them*. As the porch itself, so the pillars and beams therein, were in fashion answerable to the pillars and beams of the house.

**V. 7.** *{Then he made a porch}* The porch here meant was before the king’s palace. This therefore was distinct from the former porch of Lebanon.

*{for the throne}* That a throne might be set therein. Questionless he means hereby that glorious throne which is described ch. 10.18, &c.

*{where he might judge}* Here the end of a throne is declared; namely, to be for a judgment-seat. This porch was very convenient for such as should come to the king sitting on his throne.

*{even the porch of judgment}* This is added for explanation’s sake. Kings of old were wont to sit on their thrones to hear and determine causes, ch. 3.16,28. Prov. 20.8.

*{and it was covered with cedar}* With cedar boards, as v. 3.

*{from one side of the altar to the other}* Heb. *from floor to floor*. The words most properly set out the whole porch to be boarded every way round about. Some take it for the ceiling of the walls from the floor to the roof.

**V. 8.** *{And his house where he dwelt}* His palace, where he had his residency for the most part.

*{had another court within the porch}* This court was betwixt the foresaid place of judgment and the other rooms of the king’s house. It is called *the middle court*, 2 Kings 20.4.

*{which was of the like work}* Of such work as the king’s palace was.

*{Solomon made also an house for Pharaoh’s daughter}* This likewise was a fair spacious palace for the queen and her retinue. Of Pharaoh’s daughter, and of the reasons why a special house was made for her, see ch. 3.1.

*{whom he had taken to wife}* Of this also see ch. 3.1.

*{like unto this porch}* He means hereby the porch and other edifices before mentioned; whereunto the queen’s house was like in the materials and workmanship specified in the verses following.

**V. 9.** *{All these}* i.e. the forementioned buildings.

*{were of costly stone}* See ch. 5.17.

*{according to the measure of hewed stones}* Though they were in their kind very choice and costly stones, yet were they also as great as other ordinary stones, and every way squared and fitted as other stones.

*{of hewed}* Heb. *of cutting*.

*{sawed with saws}* Masons use these instruments for fitting their stones, as well as carpenters for cutting their timber.

*{within and without}* Every way, on all sides.

*{even from the foundation unto the coping}* By the coping is meant the top of the wall whereon the beams were laid.

*{and so on the out-side toward the great court}* Thus he shows what he meant by the former phrase within and without. The great court was the place where all sorts of people used to assemble. It was without the porch of judgment. For there was another court within it, betwixt it and the king’s house, v. 8. And very fitly was that place of judgment set betwixt two courts; one for such as came out of the king’s house, another for all sorts of people that came out of other places.

**V. 10.** *{And the foundation was of costly stones}* Not only so much of the building as might be seen above ground, but also that which was buried in the earth, was of costly stones. See ch. 5.17. Costly stones were lasting stones, and by such as were well able to purchase them, fittest to lay for a foundation.

*{even great stones}* Being costly and great, they must needs be of the more worth and better price.

*{stones of ten cubits}* This measure shows that they were very massive stones. They made the foundation the surer.

*{and stones of eight cubits}* Though not so big as the other, yet even these were of a great quantity.

**V. 11.** *{And above were costly stones}* Upon or over the foundation. Underground and above ground they were alike.

*{after the measure of hewed stones}* See v. 9.

*{and cedars}* These cedars were for beams, boards, ceiling, and other like uses.

**V. 12.** *{And the great court round about}* The context implies that the great court here meant was that which compassed the king’s house round about, and not that great court which compassed the house of the Lord, 2 Chr. 4.9, whereof see ch. 36.

*{was with three rows of hewed stones, and a row of cedar-beams}* See ch. 6.36.

*{both for the inward court of the house of the LORD}* i.e. the walls of this court were like the walls of the inner court appertaining to the temple: whereof see chap. 6.36.

*{and for the porch of the house}* As there was a porch erected before the house of the Lord, ch. 6.3, so also was there before the king’s house.

**V. 13.** *{And king Solomon sent and fet Hiram out of Tyre}* This Hiram here meant was not king Hiram, ch. 5.1, but a workman described v. 14. Solomon had desired the king of Tyre to send him a skillful workman, and the king commended this man to him, 2 Chr. 2.7.13. Thereupon Solomon sent and fetched him.

**V. 14.** *{He was a widow’s son}* Heb. *the son of a widow woman*. This is noted to give evidence of God’s good providence towards a widow. For questionless such a son was a great stay to her widowhood.

*{of the tribe of Naphtali}* The mention of this tribe here has relation to the son, who was of Naphtali by his father: his mother was of the tribe of Dan, 2 Chron. 2.14. So as he was an Israelite both by father and mother.

*{and his father was a man of Tyre}* This is to be taken of his foster-father, who was an inhabitant in Tyre. This Hiram is so called of him who taught him his art, and adopted him to be his son. See 2 Chr. 4.16. He was educated in Tyre when he learned his art. There also he dwelt, and thereupon it is said that he was sent for, and fetched from thence, v. 13.

*{a worker in brass}* This may be applied to him that is here said to be Hiram’s father: for much more is said of Hiram himself, 2 Chr. 2.14. If it be applied to Hiram himself, it is to be taken synecdochically, skillfulness in one thing being put for skillfulness in many things.

*{he was filled with wisdom and understanding}* These gifts are very requisite to make men skillful in workmanship; yea, also to direct such as are skillful, well to order their skill and workmanship. The phrase of being filled may be taken for a great measure of gifts, and of such a measure as was attained by education, instruction, or a man’s own industry and diligence: But most properly it is taken for such a work of the Spirit as enables men after an extraordinary manner to do great things, as Exod. 35.35. & 36.1. Dan. 17. Luk. 2.40. Neither of these are here to be excluded. For questionless this Hiram by others’ instructions and his own industry attained to more than ordinary skill, yet withal the Spirit of God worked in him the more understanding for this very work’s sake.

*{and cunning to work}* This is added as an effect of his wisdom: For it implies, as a curious, so a well ordering of such things as he did, by making them square, long, round or otherwise fashioned, as the kind of thing which he made required; yea, and by engraving any figures or representations thereupon.

*{all works in brass}* As he showed his skillfulness on this particular metal, so did he the like also on other metals, as was noted before.

*{and he came to king Solomon}* This has relation to Solomon’s sending for him, v. 13, and it shows his readiness to come.

*{and wrought all his work}* If the particular works hereafter mentioned be duly observed, it will appear that he showed great skillfulness and faithfulness.

**V. 15.** *{For he cast}* Heb. *fashioned*. Brass by casting uses to be fashioned, and fashioning of brass presupposes casting; therefore the one may well be put for the other.

*{two pillars of brass}* Pillars are to support things laid upon them, v. 2, and two pillars are needful, to bear beams and such other things as are laid upon them; the one end upon one pillar, the other end upon another. They were of brass, for strength and continuance. They continued till the time of the captivity; for then they were carried into Babylon, 2 King. 25.13.

*{of eighteen cubits high a piece}* This measure is to be taken of the height of a pillar betwixt the foot whereon it stood, and the chapiter on the head of it: for from the ground to the top of the chapiter the pillar was thirty and five cubits high, 2 Chron. 3.15. Besides, the eighteen cubits here mentioned were sacred, but the thirty and five might be common cubits, and so shorter by a third part, as is noted v. 2. Or else, here the height of one single pillar may be set down, but there the length of them both.

*{and a line of twelve cubits did compass either of them about}* They were hollow, as appears Jer. 52.21: and in that respect did bear the greater compass.

**V. 16.** *{And he made two chapiters}* Or, *heads*. They were so called because they were on the tops of the pillars for grace and ornament.

*{to set upon the tops of the pillars}* This confirms the reason given for this title chapiter.

*{the height of the one chapiter was five cubits}* This measure is to be taken of the whole chapiter from the bottom to the top: for so much of the chapiter as was worked upon with curious works, was but four cubits, v. 19, and the round compass or pommel of the chapiter was but three cubits, 2 King. 25.17.

*{and the height of the other chapiter was five cubits}* This is added to show that they were both of an equal height.

**V. 17.** *{And nets of checker-work, and wreaths of chain-work}* These phrases set out a very artificial kind of work, whereby sundry sprigs, leaves, flowers and fruits were curiously enlaced and wreathed together. This was very delightsome to the eye.

*{for the chapiters which were upon the top of the pillars}* The body of the pillars was plain and smooth. The curious workmanship was upon their tops.

*{seven for the one chapiter}* Seven chain-works checkerwise wreathed one into another compassed a chapiter round about.

*{and seven for the other chapiter}* As the two chapiters were uniform in their height and bigness, so also in their workmanship.

**V. 18.** *{And he made the pillars}* He adorned and decked them in such manner as follows.

*{and two rows round about upon the one net-work, &c.}* These two rows were of pomegranates, which hanging upon the net-works, are said to cover the chapiters; yet not so, but that the curious works on the chapiters were discerned between the pomegranates.

*{and so did he for the other chapiter}* They were both alike.

**V. 19.** *{And the chapiters that were upon the top of the pillars were of lily-work}* The lily was a glorious flower, Mat. 6.29, and odoriferous, Cant. 5.13. Fitly therefore were lilies added to beautify the chapiters. For this end they were also set upon the brim of the great sea, 2 Chron. 4.5.

*{in the porch}* i.e. the porch before the holy place. See ch. 6.3. There the pillars were set, v. 21.

*{four cubits}* This measure of four cubits is to be applied to this lily-work, and reached from the square whereon the belly and crown of the chapiter stood. For the chapiter consisted of these three parts, a square, a belly, a crown. All these were in height five cubits, v. 16. The three cubits mentioned 2 King. 25.17 are meant of the net-work thereupon; and the four cubits are to be taken of the lily-work.

**V. 20.** *{And the chapiters upon the two pillars had pomegranates also above}* See v. 18.

*{over against the belly}* Round about the middle part.

*{which was by the net-work}* The net-work being upon the said belly.

*{and the pomegranates were two hundred}* On both sides. For on the one side were but a hundred pomegranates, 2 Chron. 3.16. Where the prophet Jer. 52.23 names but ninety-six, he speaks of the four parts of the border, there being twenty-four on each part, which make up ninety-six. But there were four more on each corner, which four made up a hundred, expressed by the prophet himself in that place.

*{in rows round about the other chapiter}* Heb. *second*. i.e. the one as well as the other; both alike, Jer. 52.21.

**V. 21.** *{And he set up the pillars in the porch of the temple}* See ch. 6.3. 2 Chron. 3.17. This shows that they were not in the holy place. For therein was nothing but things of gold, as the altar of incense, the candlesticks, and tables, whereon were laid sundry utensils of gold, whereof see v. 48, &c. In this porch we read of nothing else to be set but these two pillars, which stood close by the entrance into the holy place. If the height of these pillars were, as 2 Chron. 3.15, thirty-five cubits, they would not have stood in the temple, which was but thirty cubits high, ch. 6.2.

*{and he set up the right pillar}* i.e. that on the right hand.

*{and called the name thereof Jachin}* i.e. *he shall establish*. This name may also set forth *rectitude or uprightness*: For the Hebrew word whence this name is derived signifies *rectitude*, Gen. 42.11. Psal. 5.9. & 51.10. Jer. 16.6, as well as *firmness*.

*{and he set up the left pillar}* Namely, that on the left hand.

*{and called the name thereof Boaz}* i.e. *In it is strength*. Take the former name for rectitude, and the latter for strength, and these two names will set out two special properties of a good pillar, straightness and strength: For so ought pillars to be, straight and strong. So ought they to be who are as pillars in church or state. But these two pillars had especial respect to Christ, whose continual power and faithfulness in his church was typified thereby.

**V. 22.** *{And upon the top of the pillars was lily-work}* See v. 19.

*{so was the work of the pillars finished}* Though this phrase in general be used of the whole house of the Lord, ch. 6.38, and of the king’s house, and of all the works appertaining to the temple, which Hiram did, 2 Chron. 4.11, yet it is not applied to any other of the particulars made by Solomon’s appointment: not to the cherubims, nor to the sea, nor to the candlesticks, nor to the tables, but only to these pillars; which shows that they were remarkable matters. So they were in sundry respects: as 1. In their height, 2 Chron. 3.15. 2. In their thickness and compass, Jer. 52.21. 3. In the curious workmanship about them, v. 17. 4. In their special use: for whereas other pillars are used to support edifices, v. 2, or to direct men in their way, Isai. 19.19, or to be a witness, Gen. 31.51, or to be a memorial, 2 Sam. 18.18, or to be a monument, Gen. 35.20, or to hang such things thereon as were to be taken notice of, 1 Tim. 3.15. 1. These pillars were for an evidence of God’s constant abiding among his people. 2. Of the continuance of the temple and the ordinances thereof. 3. Of the stability of God’s church, Matt. 16.18.

**V. 23.** *{And he made a molten sea}* 2 Chron. 4.2. This vessel was called a sea in regard of the largeness thereof, and of the great quantity of water which it contained. See v. 26. It is called a molten sea, because it was first molten, and then cast, v. 46. See on Gen. 1.10.

*{ten cubits from the one brim to the other}* Heb. *from his brim to his brim*. This shows that it was exactly round. For a diameter, i.e. a straight line through the midst of a center is about the third part of a perfect circle.

*{it was round all about}* Which shows that the compass round about it was three times as much as a straight line that directly struck it through.

*{and his height was five cubits}* So as according to the measure of the sacred cubit it was ten foot deep.

*{and a line of thirty cubits did compass it round about}* This shows a diameter to be the third part of a circumference, as ten is to thirty.

**V. 24.** *{And under the brim of it round about there were knops compassing it}* Of such knops mention is made ch. 6.18. Oxen are named, 2 Chron. 4.3. For the Hebrew word may import any figure. Or it may be that there were the shapes, both of knops, and also of oxen or bull-heads, cast thereon.

*{ten in a cubit}* Ten in every cubit, three hundred in all: for it was thirty cubits in compass.

*{compassing the sea round about}* 2 Chron. 4.3. It was that workmanship of knops, or oxen, which were round about the sea.

*{the knops were cast in two rows when it was cast}* At the first casting of the vessel itself their workmanship was cast therewith; for it was made in the mold; it was not added after the vessel was cast.

**V. 25.** *{It stood upon twelve oxen}* These oxen were not so small as those mentioned 2 Chron. 4.3, but they were huge massy ones, not hollow, but solid throughout, that they might bear the burden of so great a vessel full of water.

*{three looking toward the north, and three &c.}* They were so placed as their faces were set toward every part of the world. The twelve apostles (who by reason of their pains were as oxen) were sent into all nations everywhere, Mat. 28.27, some towards the north, some towards the west, some toward the south, and some towards the east. There have also been ministers of the gospel in all parts of the world since their time.

*{and the sea was set above upon them}* The sea was so placed on them as they did bear it up from the ground.

*{and all their hinder parts were inward}* Thus that great vessel was the better borne; though the former parts of the oxen were without, for the greater grace and ornament. And it may be that passages were made thorough the heads of the oxen to let water out of that sea. See v. 26.

**V. 26.** *{And it was an hand breadth thick}* i.e. about four inches. It was not hollow, but solid throughout, as the oxen were, v. 25.

*{and the brim thereof was wrought like the brim of a cup}* It was round and wider at the brim than anywhere else.

*{with flowers of lilies}* See v. 19.

*{it contained two thousand baths}* So much was ordinarily put thereinto: but being filled up to the brim, it might contain three thousand baths, 2 Chr. 4.5. A bath contained about eight gallons; by which account four baths make a barrel: so as this sea could contain seven hundred and fifty barrels of water; and five hundred barrels-full were ordinarily put into it. This was for the priests to wash in, 2 Chr. 4.6. Not that the priests went with their whole bodies naked into that brazen sea full of water; but, that with the water, which by cocks, or other ways, was let out of it, they washed their hands, arms, feet, legs, other parts of their body, yea and other things also which they had occasion to wash. See v. 38.

**V. 27.** *{And he made ten bases of brass}* These were made to bear so many lavers or great vessels to hold water in, v. 38, besides the great sea before mentioned.

*{four cubits was the length of one base, and four cubits the breadth thereof}* They were every one of them foursquare.

*{and three cubits the height of it}* The bottom of these lavers was about two yards from the ground, though the breadth of the wheels joined to these bases be comprised under these three cubits.

**V. 28.** *{And the work of the bases was on this manner: They had borders}* These borders were broad plates compassing the vessel round about; whereon were sundry images of lions, v. 29.

*{and the borders were between the ledges}* These ledges were plain places round about the vessel, standing out further than the side of the vessel.

**V. 29.** *{And on the borders that were between the ledges, were lions, oxen, and cherubims}* i.e. figures of these several creatures, which were cast with the vessel, as v. 24. The lions put the priests in mind of courage; the oxen, of pains and industry; the cherubims of prudence and speed.

*{and upon the ledges there was a base above}* He means by this base an under-propper. See v. 34.

*{and beneath the lions and oxen were certain additions made of thin work}* By these additions are meant certain curious works which were engraven on the vessel after it was cast.

**V. 30.** *{And every base had four brasen wheels}* These wheels were to remove the vessel from place to place, as occasion should require.

*{and the four corners thereof had under-setters}* Heb. *shoulders*. These were as shoulders to uphold the vessel, and to keep it from shaking or moving out of its place when it should be removed.

*{under the laver were under-setters molten}* They were not by beating fashioned to this or that proportion, but cast. See v. 23.

*{at the side of every addition}* See v. 29.

**V. 31.** *{And the mouth of it within the chapiter, and above, was a cubit, &c.}* The hollow place of the base, into which the lower part of the laver (called the chapiter) was put to the upper part of it, was a cubit; but the round compass of it was a cubit and a half: and though there were a round hollow place in the base, yet the base itself was square, and had sundry gravings upon it.

**V. 32.** *{And under the borders were four wheels}* See v. 30.

*{and the axle-trees of the wheels were joined to the base}* Heb. *in the base*. The base lying on the axle-tree, and the laver on the bases, the laver was removed from place to place by help of the wheels into which the axle-tree was put, and the height of a wheel was a cubit and half a cubit. This was the measure of the wheel from one side to another in a direct line.

**V. 33.** *{And the work of the wheels was like the work of a chariot-wheel}* In such particulars as follow.

*{their axle-trees, and their naves, and their felloes, and their spokes}* These are those particulars wherein these wheels of brass were like chariot wheels.

*{were all molten}* The brass of them was not first beaten out in their several parts, and after fastened together: but they were all cast together, and fashioned like wheels in their molds.

**V. 34.** *{And there were four under-setters to the four corners of one base}* See v. 30.

*{and the under-setters were of the very base itself}* Of the same metal molten and cast altogether.

**V. 35.** *{And in the top of the base was there a round compass half a cubit high}* This is to be taken of a lesser base than that which was mentioned v. 30,31. This was within that, so as this might be but half a cubit, and the other half a cubit more.

*{and on the top of the base, the ledges thereof, and the borders thereof}* Of these ledges and borders see v. 28.

*{were of the same}* Of the same metal cast together.

**V. 36.** *{For on the plates of the ledges thereof he graved cherubims, lions}* See v. 29,30.

*{and palm-trees}* See ch. 6.29.

*{according to the proportion}* Heb. *nakedness*. The proportion of everything is best discerned when it is naked. In this place is meant a plain, clear, fit resemblance.

*{and additions round about}* See v. 29.

**V. 37.** *{After this manner he made the ten bases: all of them had one casting, one measure, and one size}* Frequent mention is made of uniformity in such things of the temple as were of one kind. Uniformity about the things of God is duly to be observed: For all have one and the same rule.

**V. 38.** *{Then made he ten}* Yet there was but one in the tabernacle, Exod. 30.18. Besides that the sea, v. 23, far exceeded in quantity the laver in the tabernacle, there were ten other lavers in the temple: for the temple was bigger than the tabernacle, and had need of a greater quantity of water: Yet further, God would thereby show the increase of his goodness. The like may be noted of the candlesticks and tables, whereof there were ten in the temple, v. 49. 2 Chron. 4.8, to one in the tabernacle. There was also the like in other things. As the holy things in Solomon’s temple exceeded the holy things in Moses’ tabernacle, in their number, measures, weight, and other respects; so the holy things of the Christian church, in a spiritual consideration, far excelled Solomon’s, Hag. 2.9. 2 Cor. 3.11. Heb. 8.1,2. & 9.23.

*{lavers of brass, one laver contained forty baths}* i.e. ten barrels. See v. 26. These lavers were to wash the sacrifices, and other like things in them, 2 Chron. 4.6.

*{and every laver was four cubits}* This shows that they were very broad, being four cubits in a diameter from side to side. Thus they were twelve cubits in compass on the brim; and in that respect the more fit for the priests and Levites to put such things as they were to wash, into them. That great store of water which was in the foresaid sea and the ten lavers, showed the great need that man has to be washed and cleansed; and that in regard of his manifold pollutions and sins: yea, it showed also that all things he uses need cleansing, for he taints and defiles all. That which mystically and effectually cleanses man, in regard of his pollution by sin, is the blood of Christ, 1 Joh. 1.7, which to that purpose can do much more than such a sea, and many more lavers full of water.

*{and upon every one of the ten bases one laver}* Thus were the several lavers set by themselves, one from another.

**V. 39.** *{And he put five bases on the right side}* Heb. *shoulder*. See v. 30.

*{of the house}* He here means the court of the priests, whereof see ch. 6.36. For in the holy place were nothing but things of gold. These vessels were of brass.

*{and five on the left side of the house}* As the golden candlesticks and golden tables were set in the holy place, 2 Chro. 4.7,8, five on the one side, and five on the other, so were these brazen vessels in this house.

*{the sea on the right side of the house eastward}* i.e. on the north, at the entrance into the priests’ court. They entering at the east, the right side must needs be the north.

*{over against the south}* This is mentioned because five lavers stood on that side. There was store of water on both sides, but most on the north: for the sea held much more than the ten bases. See v. 26.38.

**V. 40.** *{And Hiram made the lavers}* See v. 13,14.

*{and the shovels}* These were to take away the ashes and other things from the altar and other places.

*{and the basons}* These were to hold the sprinkling water, or blood. There is mention made of a hundred of these, 2 Chro. 4.8. Under the Hebrew word *bowls* also may be comprised. See v. 50.

*{So Hiram made an end of doing all the work, &c.}* He continued with Solomon, and left not to work till he had finished what was required of him.

*{that he made king Solomon}* By Solomon’s appointment, and at Solomon’s charge.

*{for the house of the LORD}* The most holy place, holy place, porch, and all the courts are comprised under this phrase.

**V. 41.** *{The two pillars}* See v. 15.

*{and the two bowls of the chapiters that were on the top of the two pillars}* These bowls were the bellies mentioned v. 20. For each of the pillars had a belly or bowl.

*{and the two net-works, &c.}* See v. 17.

**V. 42.** *{And four hundred pomegranates, &c.}* Each had two hundred, v. 20. So as these four hundred were upon the two chapiters.

*{that were upon the pillars}* Heb. *upon the face of the pillars*.

**V. 43.** *{And the ten bases}* See v. 27.

*{and ten lavers on the bases}* See v. 38.

**V. 44.** *{and one sea}* See v. 23.

*{and twelve oxen under the sea}* See v. 25.

**V. 45.** *{And the pots}* These were to boil flesh and other things in them: Levit. 2.7. & 8.31. 1 Sam. 2.13, &c.

*{and the shovels, and the basons}* See v. 41.

*{and all these vessels which Hiram made to king Solomon for the house of the LORD}* See v. 40.

*{were of bright brass}* Heb. *made bright, or scoured*. This sets out the excellency of the metal whereof they were made.

**V. 46.** *{In the plain of Jordan}* An even piece of ground that lay along by the river.

*{did the king cast them}* Or, *caused them to be cast*.

*{in the clay ground}* Heb. *in the thickness of the ground*. Clay uses to be thick and stiff, and in that respect the fitter to make molds for brass to be cast into, 2 Chron. 4.17. Hilly, gravelly, stony grounds are rough, nor fit to cast great vessels of brass in. Plain moist grounds, & thick, tough, slimy clay are fittest for that purpose.

*{between Succoth}* Gen. 33.17. This place was in the tribe of Gad, Josh. 13.27.

*{and Zarthan}* In the tribe of Ephraim, Josh. 3.16. It is also called Zeredathah, 2 Chron. 4.17.

**V. 47.** *{And Solomon left all the vessels unweighed}* See 1 Chro. 22.3,14.

*{because they were exceeding many}* Heb. *for the exceeding multitude*.

*{neither was the weight of the brass found out}* Heb. *searched*. By searching, weighing, or other usual means, they could not find out the weight of the brass, by reason of the multitude of vessels, and greatness of some of them. How could they weigh such a vessel as the molten sea was? See v. 23, &c.

**V. 48.** *{And Solomon made}* He caused to be made.

*{all the vessels}* Such as Moses had caused to be made before, Exod. 37.10, &c. but far bigger in quantity, and more in number, because the temple was far larger than the tabernacle. See v. 38.

*{that pertained unto the house of the LORD}* By the house of the Lord is especially meant the holy place, together with the most holy; as the particular vessels following do show.

*{the altar of gold}* So called because overlaid with gold, Exod. 37.25,26, but itself was of cedar wood, ch. 6.20.

*{and the table of gold}* In the same sense that the altar, is the table said to be of gold. There were ten of these tables, 2 Chron. 4.8.

*{whereupon the shew-bread was}* Heb. *bread of faces*. So called because it was continually set before the Lord, Levit. 24.6. i.e. before the ark, the most principal representation of the Lord that they had in the temple. See Exod. 25.30. There was a continual supply made of this bread. When stale loaves were taken away, new ones were put in their room, Levit. 24.8. In this respect it was called *continual shew-bread*, 2 Chr. 2.4. Numb. 4.7. This was a type of Christ, who was *the bread of life*, Joh. 6.35. Among the ten tables there was one more excellent than the rest, because on it the showbread was set: and this may here in special be meant. The other were for cups, bowls, basins, spoons, and other like things; whereof there was a great multitude: See Ezr. 1.9,10. Or the word table may synecdochically be used, the singular number for the plural.

**V. 49.** *{And the candlesticks of pure gold}* This phrase is here used as ch. 5.10. These candlesticks were all massy gold, as that of the tabernacle was, Exod. 37.17, &c.

*{five on the right side, and five on the left}* See v. 39.

*{before the oracle}* i.e. the most holy place. See ch. 6.5. The oracle was as wide as the holy place; so as the candlesticks being on either side of the holy place, might all of them be before the oracle.

*{with the flowers}* i.e. ornaments to set out the candlesticks, Exod. 37.17,18. The manifold deckings and ornaments about the pillars, sea, lavers, and other sacred things in and about the temple, did prefigure the manifold gifts and graces which, as deckings of the inward man, are conferred on saints; yea and the beauty also of Christ's ordinances in his church.

*{and the lamps}* Vessels whereinto oil was put, together with wicks, to give light, being put into the branches of the candlesticks.

*{and the tongs of gold}* These were to take coals to burn the incense on the golden altar.

**V. 50.** *{And the bowls}* For wine or beer, or such other liquor used in the temple. Some take them to be vessels for oil.

*{and the snuffers}* To snuff the lamps, that they might burn more clearly.

*{and the basons}* The basins here mentioned were of gold. Of these there were a hundred, 2 Chron. 4.8. There are also other basins mentioned, v. 45. which were of brass.

*{and the spoons}* For broth or other liquid things.

*{and the censers}* Heb. *Ash-pans*. A censer was an instrument of enduring metal, that would not easily melt, as of gold, or brass, fit to hold fire in, with a steal or handle to hold it by. The use of it was to hold live burning coals, that incense being cast thereon might send out a perfume. The priest carrying this from place to place caused the sweet smell to disperse itself; so as it was smelt wherever he came. Some of these were of brass, Num. 16.39. for the ordinary priests to carry incense on up and down through the tabernacle or temple: Others were of gold; and these are here meant. There was one especial one, with which the high priest went into the most holy place, Heb. 9.4. Of incense see ch. 9.25.

*{of pure gold}* See ch. 6.20. This phrase has relation to all the particulars mentioned v. 48, &c.

*{and the hinges of gold both for the doors of the inner house}* The oracle is so called, because it was within the holy place. It was the innermost of all the places appertaining to the temple.

*{the most holy place}* This is added to show what was meant by the inner house.

*{and for the doors of the house}* This word house is here synecdochically put for the holy place; The whole for a part.

*{to wit of the temple}* The like synecdoche is here used.

**V. 51.** *{So was ended all the work that king Solomon made}* Or, *caused to be made*.

*{for the house of the LORD}* See v. 40.

*{and Solomon brought in the things which David}* Heb. *things of David*.

*{his father}* See ch. 8.15.

*{had dedicated, even the silver and the gold, and the vessels}* 2 Chro. 5.1. It was usual for kings, princes, priests and other great ones to dedicate silver, gold, and other things of price to the house of the Lord, 2 Sam. 8.11. 1 Chron. 26.16,27,28. 2 Chron. 15.18. 2 Kin. 12.18. See 1 Chron. 18.11.

*{did he put among the treasures of the house of the LORD}* There was in the temple a holy treasury. Thither did Solomon bring the forementioned precious things, that in future times they might be, as need should require, for the use of the Lord's house: for the present they had sufficient. Such store of treasure they had for the house of God, as they had sundry treasuries or chambers to lay up the same in, and sundry treasurers. See ch. 15.15. 2 King. 12.4. 1 Chron. 26.20,26.

# CHAPTER 8

**Ver. 1.** *{Then}* This has reference to the beginning of the last verse of the former chapter. See 2 Chron. 5.1,2.

*{Solomon assembled}* By messengers or letters sent to them, 2 Chr. 30.16. This was for the greater solemnity, 1 Chron. 13.1, &c.

*{the elders of Israel}* Such as for age or prudence were rulers over the Israelites, 1 Tim. 5.17. *multitude of years teach wisdom*, Job. 12.12. & 32.7. Old men by experience attain the more prudence, ch. 12.6. Therefore ancient men used to be put in office, Jer. 19.1. They who were in office, though young men, were called elders.

*{and all the heads of the tribes}* All Israel was divided into twelve tribes, according to the number of the sons of Israel; and the highest in rule and chiefest in dignity of those tribes were called heads, Numb. 1.16.

*{the chief of the fathers of the children of Israel}* Every tribe was divided into families, the chief men whereof were called fathers, Exo. 6.14. And because in one house there might be many fathers, the chief of these were sent for.

*{chief}* Heb. *Princes*. i.e. principal governors.

*{unto king Solomon}* That is, unto himself; a phrase proper to the Hebrew tongue. So ch. 2.19. & 12.21.

*{in Jerusalem}* For there the temple was erected.

*{that they might bring up}* For the temple was built on a mount, 2 Chr. 3.1. And the temple was a worthier place.

*{the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See ch. 3.15.

*{out of the city of David}* Where David had set it; 2 Sam. 6.12,17. see on ch. 3.1.

*{which is Zion}* See ch. 2.10. Here Zion is properly taken for that particular city that David built: it is sometimes put by a synecdoche for all Jerusalem. God is said to build his Sanctuary in Sion, Psal. 78.68,69.

**V. 2.** *{And all the men of Israel}* Under this phrase not only the elders are comprised, but also multitudes of the common people, who usually came up to the feasts at Jerusalem.

*{assembled themselves}* Voluntarily and cheerfully came together.

*{unto king Solomon}* To expect what he would have them do.

*{at the feast}* Of tabernacles, 2 Chr. 5.3. This was one of the great feasts in which all the males were to go to the house of the Lord, Deut. 16.13,16.

*{in the month Ethanim}* See on ch. 6.1. Lev. 23.34.

*{which is the seventh month}* Answering in part to our September. It was about the autumnal equinoctial, the most fit season of the year to travel in. It was the month of gathering in their fruits. In this month God's blessing was most manifested unto them, & herein they had more solemnities than in any other: For beside the new moon, common to all months, on the first day of this month was the blowing of trumpets; on the tenth, the day of reconciliation; on the fifteenth the feast of tabernacles, which continued seven days at least. In this month there used to be the greatest concourse of people at Jerusalem: Solomon therefore prudently dedicated the temple in this month. True it is, the house with all the appurtenances thereof and with all the ordinances thereof was not finished till the month Bul, which was the eighth month, ch. 6.38. but the whole edifice and the most material things appertaining to the temple, were finished before. But what if that which is mentioned of the eighth month were in one year, and this dedication in the seventh month of the next year?

**V. 3.** *{And all the elders of Israel came}* Under elders of Israel are comprised the heads and fathers mentioned v. 1. These being sent for accordingly came to the king.

*{and the priests took up the ark}* Indeed Levites are appointed to bear the ark, Deut. 31.25. but they were such Levites as were priests, Deut. 31.9. 1 Chr. 15.2,14,15. Besides the priests may be said to take up the ark, because they were to cover it, and to set it on the Levites' shoulders, Numb. 4.5.

**V. 4.** *{And they brought up}* From Zion, ver. 1. where it was before.

*{the ark of the LORD}* So was it called, because it was an especial evidence of God's presence: See ch. 6.19.

*{and the tabernacle}* That which Moses made, Exod. 36.8. As a sacred monument it was now laid up in the temple, never to be removed again, as formerly it had been. For I. In the wilderness it was carried up and down for the space of forty years, Ex. 40.2. II. It abode in Gilgal, Josh. 4.18,19. about fourteen years. III. It remained in Shiloh till Samuel's time, Josh. 18.1. 1 Sam. 4.4. Psal. 78.60. Jer. 7.12. IV. It was in Nob, 1 Sam. 21.1. till Saul destroyed that place, 1 Sam. 22.19. V. It was in Gibeon all David's time, 1 Chr. 16.39. VI. From thence it was brought into Zion, to be brought with the ark into the temple, See further 2 Macc. 2.5. See on 2 Sam. 6.17.

*{of the congregation}* Where the priests, Levites, and people met together about matters of the Lord. See Exod. 40.2. Levit. 1.1.

*{and all the holy vessels that were in the tabernacle}* Those which Moses had appointed to be made before Solomon made those mentioned ch. 7.23, &c. Both the one and the other were used in the temple.

*{even those did the priests and the Levites bring up}* Priests and Levites in their function were counted holy, and none but such persons might meddle with the holy vessels.

**V. 5.** *{And king Solomon and all the congregation of Israel that were assembled unto him, were with him before the ark}* There they presented themselves and their service to the Lord.

*{sacrificing sheep and oxen}* As had been done when David brought the ark to Zion, 2 Sam. 6.13. Oxen and sheep were the ordinary clean creatures used for great sacrifices.

*{that could not be told nor numbered for multitude}* This is a hyperbolical phrase importing an exceeding great number. And it puts a difference between Solomon's solemnity here, and David's, 2 Sam. 6.13. See on v. 63.

*{and the priests}* To whom that office belonged, Deut. 31.9.

*{brought in the ark of the covenant of the LORD}* See ch. 3.15.

*{unto his place}* i.e. the place appointed for it. See Exod. 26.33,34. This place in general was that which is called *the most holy*; but in particular, that part of the most holy where the cherubims were set, even the midst of it, ch. 6.19.

*{into the oracle of the house}* See on ch. 6.5.

*{to the most holy place}* See ch. 6.16.

*{even under the wings of the cherubims}* i.e. the great cherubims which Solomon made, ch. 6.23,24. Those which Moses made were fastened to the mercy seat wherewith the ark was covered, Ex. 37.7, &c. so as those cherubims with the mercy seat and ark stood by these cherubims. See on 2 King. 19.15.

**V. 7.** *{For the cherubims spread forth their two wings over the place of the ark, and the cherubims covered the ark}* This is added as a reason of the ark's being under the wings of the cherubims reaching through the midst of the most holy place from the one side to the other, must needs be over the middle part of the most holy place where the ark was set, and so cover it.

*{and the staves thereof}* The ark had two staves or bars on each side, put into rings, whereby it might be borne on men's shoulders, Exod. 37.4,5. These staves were never to be taken clean away, Exod. 25.15. They were therefore here set with the ark, and covered with the cherubims, as the ark was.

*{above}* Over the ark and staves thereof.

**V. 8.** *{And they drew out the staves}* They did not clean pluck the staves out from the ark, and set them away from it (for that had been against the law, Exod. 25.15.) but whereas they were so much longer than the ark, as might be convenient to lie upon the shoulders of the bearers; they were pulled out towards the veil further than at the other end.

*{that the ends}* Heb. *heads*. For the upper end of a thing is called the head.

*{of the staves were seen out in the holy place}* Or, *Ark*, as 2 Chr. 5.9. where it is thus expressed, *the ends of the staves were seen from the ark before the Oracle*. i.e. The staves being drawn further out than the ark, were seen in that part of the most holy place which was immediately before or joined next to the holy place.

*{before the oracle}* At the entrance betwixt the holy and most holy place; so as they who stood at the entrance that was before the oracle, might see the staves.

*{and}* Or, *but*, 2 Chr. 5.9. For this is added in opposition to or distinction from that which went immediately before.

*{they were not seen without}* Namely, by such as were without the most holy place, and not at the entrance thereinto.

*{and there they are unto this day}* Namely, the day or time wherein this story was penned. But this is a Hebrew phrase implying a perpetuity, ch. 12.19. Gen. 26.33. & 32.33. So long as the temple stood, they so remained therein. This distinct setting down of the covering the ark and drawing out the staves has, as other rites, a mystery couched under it; which I take to be this. The ark was in fashion as a little chest; and in chests or cabinets men's precious things use to be laid up: So the ark was an especial type of Christ in whom are treasured up God's most precious things. The cover of the ark was the mercy seat, Exod. 25.21. and into the ark the testimony, i.e. the two tables, was put, v. 9. Exod. 25.16. That testimony bore witness against us for our sins, and held out a curse; but the mercy seat kept this testimony in the ark, so as it could not give evidence against us. Again the ark was covered with the cherubims, to show the unsearchable mysteries that are in Christ: But the staves were so far pulled out as they might be seen, to give evidence that there was an ark. Thus the types and prophecies and promises under the law did give evidence of such mysteries as are clearly revealed under the gospel.

**V. 9.** *{There was nothing in the ark save the two tables of stone}* Deut. 10.5. This without all question was most true at that time when Solomon set the ark in his place. We read of other things set by the ark, and before it. As the book of the law (not the moral law written in tables of stone, but other ordinances written by Moses, especially the book of Deuteronomy) was put in the side of the ark, namely in some coffer set by it, Deut. 31.26. This might be it which Hilkiah found 2 King. 22.8. The pot of manna was also appointed to be set before it, Exod. 16.33. and Aaron's rod which budded, Numb. 17.10. But nothing is said to be in it save the two tables of stone. Many suppose the apostle, Hebr. 9.4. places the golden pot that had manna and Aaron's rod in the ark as well as the tables of the covenant. His words are these, *The holiest of all had the golden censer, and the ark of the covenant overlaid round about with gold, wherein was the golden pot that had manna, and Aarons rod that budded, and the tables of the covenant*.
