*{and his ascent by which he went up unto the house of the LORD}* Hereby is set out a stately gallery, or causeway, or terrace, whereby he ascended out of his royal palace into the temple: whereof see more v. 12. A like thing is mentioned Ezek. 44.3. & 46.2. This is called the Kings entry, 2 King. 6.18. And the entrance out of it into the temple is called the Kings gate, 1 Chr. 9.18.

*{there was no more spirit in her}* She was so astonished as if she had been in a swoon. For high admiration makes one stand so amazed as if he had no life in him.

**V. 6.** *{And she said unto the king}* Having somewhat recollected her spirits, she plainly expressed her mind.

*{It was a true report}* Heb. *word*. She had both heard and seen as much as was reported, and thereupon could not but give testimony to the truth thereof, Josh. 2.10. &c. Joh. 4.43.

*{that I heard in my own land}* The truth of famous matters spreads far and near.

*{of thy acts}* Or, *sayings*.

*{and of thy wisdom}* True wisdom will show itself forth in famous acts.

**V. 7.** *{Howbeit I believed not the words}* 2 Chr. 9.6. So much was reported of Solomon's wisdom as she thought all could not be true.

*{until I came and mine eyes had seen it}* By sensible evidences men are brought to believe what otherwise seems incredible, Joh. 20.29.

*{and behold, the half was not told me}* In God's gifts the reality and truth of them oft exceeds report.

*{thy wisdom and prosperity exceedeth the fame which I heard}* Heb. *thou hast added wisdom and goodness to the fame*. Under the word prosperity he comprises all that good which Solomon by his wisdom had done.

**V. 8.** *{Happy are thy men}* She means in general all his subjects. For these here are to be taken in a larger extent than those which follow.

*{happy are these thy servants, which stand continually before thee}* By these servants are meant such as were of his privy council, or of his bedchamber, or had any other place in his court that gave them occasions of being oft in his presence, whether at meals, or at any manner of conference, or any kind of affairs. Such servants are nearer to a king than other subjects. See Deut. 1.38. Pro. 22.29.

*{and that hear thy wisdom}* This is added as the reason of their happiness. Men are made wise by hearing wise men, Pro. 1.5. & 5.1,2. & 13.20.

**V. 9.** *{Blessed be the LORD thy God}* See ch. 8.15. This queen, though she dwelt among the heathen, was convinced that such a king as Solomon was given of God, as Pro. 8.15,16. and thereupon she praises God for him, as ch. 1.48. & 5.7.

*{which delighted in thee}* Or *set his favour upon thee*. For it was God's free grace that advanced Solomon to the throne, and endued him with such wisdom.

*{to set thee on the throne of Israel}* Or *over the whole Israel*. Israel was then the only church of God. It appears therefore to be an evidence of God's delight in one, and favor to him, to commit unto him a trust over his church, 2 Chr. 9.8. This is called the throne of God. See there more.

*{because the LORD loved Israel}* It is an evidence of God's grace to a people, to set a good and prudent governor over them, Psal. 78.70.

*{for ever}* To establish that nation forever, 2 Chr. 9.8. This is true of Israel after the flesh, so long as it remained a true church; and of Israel after the spirit, everlastingly without end. See 1 Chr. 17.22.

*{therefore made he thee king to do judgment and justice}* In executing judgment to deal justly, 2 Sam. 8.15. Or to dispose the affairs of the kingdom with judgment, prudently; and in righteousness, justly.

**V. 10.** *{And she gave to the king}* Freely, as a royal queen. This was an evidence of the high esteem she had of Solomon, and of his wisdom. And this was one means of accomplishing God's promise, ch. 3.12. and of fulfilling David's prophecy, Psal. 72.10. That which was here begun as in a type, was further fulfilled Mat. 2.11. and in the progress of the Christian church.

*{an hundred and twenty talents of gold}* See on ch. 9.14.

*{and of spices very great store}* Sweet spices were in high account among the Jews. Their kings laid them up among their treasures, 2 King. 20.13.

*{and precious stones}* These have at all times and in all places been much esteemed. All the presents here mentioned were native commodities of this queen's country: see v. 2. Kings use to give and send such presents as are most proper to their own dominions: And such use to be most rare to others.

*{there came no more such abundance of spices as these which the queen of Sheba gave to king Solomon}* A high commendation of the presents given by this queen. See the like, v. 12.

**V. 11.** *{And the navy also of Hiram}* This seems to be a distinct navy from that of Solomon, v. 22. See ch. 9.27.

*{that brought gold from Ophir}* See ch. 9.28.

*{brought in from Ophir great plenty of almug-trees}* These were other than the algum trees mentioned 2 Chron. 2.8. though that name be applied to these, 2 Chron. 9.10. for the algum trees grew in Lebanon, the almug in India. These algum trees were to be had again, these not, v. 12. This almug wood was such as is mentioned Rev. 18.12. if not the same. As the rareness of the present, so the plenty thereof does much set it forth.

*{and precious stones}* See v. 10.

**V. 12.** *{And the king made of the almug-trees, pillars}* Or *rails*, Heb. *a prop*. Supporters on each side of the gallery that led from the king's palace to the temple, v. 5. Certainly he speaks of an addition of pillars to beautify that gallery the more; for it was made before this queen came.

*{for the house of the LORD, and for the king’s house}* Pillars of this wood might also be added to these and other houses built by Solomon, to beautify the same.

*{Harps also and psalteries for singers}* That almug wood is here further commended, in that musical instruments were made thereof, See 1 Chr. 13.8.

*{there came no such almug-trees}* See v. 10.

*{nor were seen}* Namely in Judah, 2 Chr. 9.11. For in India they might continue.

*{unto this day}* See ch. 9.13.

**V. 13.** *{And king Solomon gave unto the queen of Sheba}* Royal and bountiful minds are as forward to give as to receive. Giving, receiving, and recompensing are three bonds of affection, which continue firm amity betwixt men.

*{all her desire whatsoever she asked}* There were commodities in Israel which were not in Sheba, and in that respect might well be desired by that queen.

*{besides that which Solomon gave her}* Solomon would not be behind her in bounty.

*{of his royal bounty}* Heb. *according to the hand of king Solomon*. According to his power and ability, yea, and royal mind also. This he did in recompense of her bounty to him, v. 10. See 2 Chr. 9.12.

*{so she turned and went to her own country}* Being satisfied, and that to the full, in that for which she came, she tarries no longer from her own place, but returned thither.

*{she and her servants}* The great train mentioned v. 2.

**V. 14.** *{Now the weight of gold that came to Solomon}* Hitherto Solomon's wisdom has been set forth. His wealth and pomp is further declared.

*{in one year}* Not only, but divers years one after another. But so much was brought at one time.

*{was six hundred threescore and six talents of gold}* See on ch. 9.14. Some ships went to Ophir, ch. 9.28. some to Tarshish, v. 22. Thus, though there were brought but four hundred and fifty talents of gold from Ophir, 2 Chron. 8.18. yet from Ophir, Tarshish, and other places he might receive six hundred sixty-six talents in a year, which amounts to two millions four hundred ninety-seven thousand five hundred pounds.

**V. 15.** *{Besides that he had of the merchant-men}* Or, *of factors, or chapmen*. The Hebrew word signifies such as *search after*, or, *spy out matters*, as Numb. 13.2,25. Deut. 1.33. Now merchants and their factors use to search diligently where they may for their best advantage buy or sell commodities. Such therefore may here be set out by this word. Yea, customers also use to search ships, packs, and other places. So as they also may be implied.

*{and the traffick of the spice-merchants}* Spices were precious commodities, v. 10. Therefore there were special merchants for them, and it may be a company of such merchants, as the East India Company amongst us. They might pay great custom to the king, for liberty to bring in some and carry out other commodities.

*{and of all the kings}* That paid him tribute, being subdued by David. Many also gave gifts on goodwill and gratitude, 2 Chron. 9.24. See Ps. 72.10.

*{of Arabia}* For from thence in particular tribute was sent to kings of Judah, 2 Chr. 17.11.

*{and of the governours}* Or, *captains*.

*{of the country}* Namely, of that country of Arabia. They all brought gold and silver, 2 Chron. 9.14.

**V. 16.** *{And king Solomon made}* The targets and shields hereafter mentioned were rather for pomp than use. For gold is not so fit to keep a man safe from the danger of arrows, darts, javelins, and other like piercing weapons, as steel, iron, or brass; but yet it is far more glorious for show. Howsoever Solomon himself had the glory of these golden targets and shields for the time, yet they were soon taken away from his son, 2 Chron. 12.9. Some hence infer that God was not well pleased with this pomp.

*{two hundred targets}* Targets were such as captains were wont to have carried before them, that, as occasion served, they might take them for their defense.

*{of beaten gold}* This phrase implies that they were all of massy gold, as the candlesticks, Numb. 8.4. and not only some wood laid over with plates of gold, as the ark, Exod. 25.10,11. It is called beaten gold in sundry respects, 1. To commend the excellency of the gold: for the better the gold is, the more pliable it is to be worked. 2. To set out the workman's skill, which is more manifested in fashioning a thing by beating out the metal, than by casting it. 3. To amplify the value and worth of them. Things of beaten gold (the quantity being alike) must needs be more worth than things of wood laid over with plates of gold.

*{six hundred shekels of gold went to one target}* The root from whence the Hebrew word comes signifies *to weigh or poise*. Where mention is made of silver or gold, and no word of quantity expressed, a shekel used to be understood, as here, so oft Judg. 16.5. A shekel of silver was in weight half an ounce; in worth, two shillings and sixpence. The Jews prized gold at ten times more than silver, so as a shekel of gold was twenty and five shillings. By this account every target weighed twenty-five pounds troy, and was worth seven hundred and fifty pound sterling. See on 2 Chr. 22.14.

**V. 17.** *{And he made three hundred shields}* Or, *bucklers*. A shield was less than a target. Men did use to carry their shield on their left arm for defense.

*{of beaten gold}* See v. 16.

*{Three pound of gold went to one shield}* A pound is twelve ounces. An ounce of gold was by the Jews esteemed worth fifty shillings sterling; one pound weight worth thirty pound. Thus every shield worth ninety pound.

*{And the king put them in the house of the forest of Lebanon}* See ch. 7.2. This house was for civil state the most sumptuous work that Solomon made, and his magnificence and royalty was therein most manifested: Therefore those glorious targets and shields were there set. It is probable that they were oft carried before Solomon, as the like of brass before his son, ch. 14.28.

**V. 18.** *{Moreover the king made a great throne}* A throne is a seat proper and peculiar to a king, Gen. 41.40. thereon to sit in state, ch. 12.10. and to exercise judgment, ch. 7.7. This throne is said to be great, because it exceeded ordinary thrones. It was answerable in the kind thereof to his other works.

*{of Ivory}* Heb. *tooth*. Meaning elephant's tooth, v. 22. which is ivory. Herewith stately things were wont to be garnished; as houses, ch. 22.39. Amos 3.15. palaces, Psal. 45.8. benches, Ezek. 27.6. beds, Amos 6.4. vessels, Rev. 18.12.

*{and over-laid it}* Not, as the sacred things of the oracle, all over, but with such curious and artificial work, somewhat checkerwise as the white ivory and yellow gold gave a fair luster one to the other, 2 Chron. 9.17. and made the throne appear the more glorious. Had the white ivory been covered all over with plates of gold, it had not been seen, and so there had been little use of it.

**V. 19.** *{The throne had six steps}* Those six steps show it was about a yard and half from the ground. This ascent by steps, as it was convenient for the king's sitting thereon to be seen and heard, so it added much stateliness and glory thereto.

*{and the top of the throne was round behind}* Heb. *On the hinder part thereof*. The cover over the throne, together with the back part thereof, was round. Thus did it cover the king's head round about, and was more ease for him.

*{and there were stays}* Heb. *hands*. He means such short rails on each side, as being fastened to a chair we call elbows, which are for him that sits to rest his arms on.

*{on either side}* Heb. *here and there*.

*{on the place of the seat}* Fastened to the seat whereon he sat.

*{and two lions stood beside the stays}* Those lions stood on the outside of the stays, both for support and ornament.

**V. 20.** *{And twelve lions stood there on the one side and on the other upon the six steps}* There were two lions, one at the one end, another at the other end of each step. There being a rest or short rail, reaching from one step to another, on both ends a lion was placed at the fore-end of each rest to support it. They stood in an equal distance one from another, and so were the more glorious to behold. There is mention besides of a footstool of gold, 2 Chr. 9.18. which was for the king, sitting on his throne, to set his feet upon.

*{there was not the like}* Heb. *so*.

*{in any kingdom}* This adds much to the glory of this throne. See v. 10,12. As this throne excelled other thrones in costly matter, in quantity, and in curious workmanship, so also in the divine and moral mysteries signified thereby, which were such as these, 1. The throne itself signified stability in that which is good; 2. The matter of it, being gold and ivory, an incorrupt and sound judgment; 3. The interweaving of yellow gold and white ivory, a mixture of justice and mercy; 4. The height thereof above the ground where the people stood, both the eminency of a king's place, and also the excellency of parts which he should have above others; 5. The round top over the throne, God's protection of good kings and their kingdoms; 6. The six steps ascending to the throne, a deliberate and mature proceeding to judgment; 7. The stays on each side, the assistance which the king should have from counselors; 8. The two lions on each side, a king's vigilancy and courage; 9. The twice six lions on the six steps, vigilancy and courage in such subjects and servants as attend him; 10. The footstool of gold whereon the king set his feet, a king's trampling on all the precious things of this world.

**V. 21.** *{And all king Solomon’s drinking-vessels were of gold}* They were not gilt, as much of our plate is, but all gold, as v. 16.

*{and all the vessels}* The word vessel is here more largely taken than in the former clause. It is used for instruments, ornaments, and utensils of any kind: as for hurtful things, Numb. 35.22. hunters' instruments, Gen. 27.3. warlike weapons, 1 Sam. 17.54. musical instruments, Neh. 12.36. jewels, Gen. 24.53. It may here comprise under it not only pots, cups, platters, basins, spoons, and such like vessels; but also candlesticks, andirons, hooks, yea, and the targets and shields mentioned v. 16,17. See 2 King. 7.15.

*{of the house of the forest of Lebanon}* See ch. 7.2, &c.

*{were of pure gold}* The house being made for delight, and for the glory of the kingdom, the things therein were of the choicest gold. See ch. 6.20.

*{none were of silver}* Or, *there was no silver in them*.

*{it was nothing accounted of in the days of Solomon}* This is a hyperbolical speech. For silver was brought to Solomon, time after time, by ship, v. 22. and given him as an acceptable present, v. 25. But by this phrase the great abundance which he had both of silver and gold is amplified. See v. 27.

**V. 22.** *{For the king had at sea a navy of Tarshish}* Or, *that went to Tarshish*, 2 Chron. 20.36. Jer. 10.9. Ezek. 27.12. This navy was one means of Solomon's getting so much gold and silver. Tarshish might then be taken in as large an extent as India is now.

*{with the navy of Hiram}* See v. 11.

*{once in three years came the navy of Tarshish}* This shows that from Judea to Tarshish was a long voyage.

*{bringing gold and silver}* See v. 11. & ch. 9.28.

*{Ivory}* Or, *elephants' teeth*. See v. 18.

*{and apes and peacocks}* Or, *monkeys and parrots*. Beasts and birds of remote countries have in all ages been accounted precious commodities for rarities' sake.

**V. 23.** *{So king Solomon exceeded all the kings of the earth}* All about him. Or, all throughout the world at that time.

*{for riches}* God had promised as much, ch. 3.13.

*{and for wisdom}* God had also promised this grace in that measure, ch. 3.12. See ch. 4.30,31. Take these two, riches and wisdom, together, and it will appear that Solomon did indeed excel therein all kings in all places, throughout all ages of the world. Though some might exceed him in riches, yet in riches and wisdom both never any exceeded him.

**V. 24.** *{And all the earth}* All sorts of men, especially kings, prophets, princes, nobles, and other great ones of the nations round about. Here is a metonymy, earth put for the inhabitants thereon. See v. 2. 2 Chron. 9.23.

*{sought to Solomon}* Heb. *sought the face of Solomon*. They thought it not enough to send messengers and ambassadors, but came themselves to see him and confer with him. See v. 7.

*{to hear his wisdom}* They would be both eyewitnesses and earwitnesses of his wisdom.

*{which God had put in his heart}* This is to be taken of the opinion of the very heathen. Even they discerned that Solomon's wisdom was not attained by human means, but extraordinarily given of God.

**V. 25.** *{And they brought every man his present}* See v. 15.

*{vessels of silver and vessels of gold}* Of the extent of this word vessels, see v. 21.

*{and garments}* See 2 King. 5.5.

*{and armour}* Wise statesmen have armor in high account, even in time of peace. See ch. 4.26. & 9.19.

*{and spices}* See v. 2.

*{horses}* See ch. 9.19.

*{and mules}* There are two words in Hebrew translated mules. One is, ימן *Jemin*, used only Gen. 36.24. and it is supposed that those mules were engendered by the mixture of an ass and a mare. The other is, פרד *Pered*, which comes from a root signifying *to part or separate*; because that kind of creature never come to one another to engender. The Greeks style this creature *ἡμίονος*, *half an ass*, because it is engendered as aforesaid. Mules were in high account of old, being for kings, kings' sons, and great princes to ride upon, ch. 1.33. & 18.5. 2 Sam. 13.29. & 18.9. They were beasts of price, being swift of pace, Est. 8.10. and likewise able to bear great burdens, 2 King. 5.17.

*{a rate year by year}* Or, *the thing of the year in the year*. A like phrase is used ch. 8.59. Every year they brought their presents, and those seasonable.

**V. 26.** *{And Solomon gathered together chariots and horsemen}* See ch. 4.26. & 9.19. 2 Chr. 1.14.

*{And he had a thousand and four hundred chariots, and twelve thousand horsemen}* Whereas 2 Chron. 9.25. mention is made of four thousand stalls for horses and chariots, I conceive that the number of four thousand has especial relation to stalls. Now there being in each stall a horse, and four horses for every chariot, the four thousand stalls will well stand with a thousand chariots. As for the four hundred chariots overplus, they were to be in a readiness when any of the other were out of repair.

*{whom he bestowed in the cities for chariots}* See ch. 9.19.

*{and with the king at Jerusalem}* He ordered that some of them should be in his own court, or near it.

**V. 27.** *{And the king made silver}* Heb. *gave*. Solomon gave silver so freely and liberally as if he had accounted it no more worth than stones: yea, he caused others so to esteem it. The like is said of gold 2 Chr. 1.15.

*{to be in Jerusalem}* In this city he most manifested his magnificence.

*{as stones}* This is a hyperbole. See v. 21.

*{and cedars made he}* See ch. 4.33. & 5.8.

*{to be as sycamore-trees}* Sycamores grew in every field and hedgerow, as oaks and elms with us, 2 Chron. 2.27.

**V. 28.** *{And Solomon had horses brought out of Egypt}* Heb. *and the going forth of the horses was Solomons*. 2 Chron. 1.16. & 9.28. Egypt had great plenty of choice horses, Deut. 17.16. Isai. 31.1. Ezek. 17.15.

*{and linen yarn}* Or, *fine flax*. Hereof there was great store in Egypt, Gen. 41.42. Prov. 7.16. Ezek. 27.7. See 1 Chron. 4.21.

*{the king’s merchants received the linen yarn at a price}* There was a compact or agreement with the king of Egypt for a set price.

**V. 29.** *{And a chariot came up and went out of Egypt}* i.e. a chariot drawn with four horses: or so many horses as use to draw a chariot, i.e. four.

*{for six hundred shekels of silver}* This was the price of four horses, or of the linen drawn in a chariot.

*{and an horse for an hundred and fifty}* This is the fourth part of the six hundred shekels before mentioned.

*{and so for all the kings of the Hittites, and for all the kings of Syria}* These kings had their kingdoms bordering upon Solomon's, and were in amity with him: so as they and other kingdoms thereabouts had horses and other commodities by Solomon's means.

*{did they bring them out by their means}* Heb. *by their hands*. For hands are the chief members whereby men use to act anything.

# CHAPTER 11

**Ver. 1.** *{But king Solomon}* Hitherto excellent things have been spoken of Solomon. His foul revolt is now related, and God's judgments following thereupon.

*{loved}* Unduly, against law; and immoderately against temperance.

*{many}* So many as the like has not been heard of in any other. This was directly contrary to the law given in special to kings of Israel, Deut. 17.17.

*{strange women}* Not Israelites but of other nations, out of the covenant, by law forbidden. See v. 2.

*{together with the daughter of Pharaoh}* Or, *besides*. He was not content with her that had been long his wife. See ch. 3.1.

*{women of the Moabites, Ammonites, Edomites, Zidonians and Hittites}* These were the nations round about him. Under these others also may be comprised.

**V. 2.** *{Of the nations concerning which the LORD had said unto the children of Israel}* Exod. 34.16. Deut. 7.3. Josh. 23.12. The children of Israel were a peculiar treasure unto God above all people, Exod. 19.5. therefore he would not have them mixed with other nations. Marriage with other nations than those mentioned in the former verse was forbidden: therefore there must needs be a synecdoche in that account.

*{Ye shall not go in unto them, neither shall they come in to you}* The phrases of going in to, and coming in to, are modest marriage phrases, Gen. 6.4. & 38.2,18.

*{for surely they will turn away your heart}* It is violent lust that puts men on to affect women against law; and it makes them so to dote on them as they care not to adventure body and soul for them, Judg. 16.16.

*{after their gods}* Idolatrous women use to be very violent in drawing others to their idols, Numb. 25.1,2.

*{Solomon clave unto these in love}* The love here mentioned was filthy lust. See v. 1.

**V. 3.** *{And he had seven hundred wives}* These he solemnly married according to their custom.

*{princesses}* Such were some of them before he married them, as Pharaoh's daughter: others he made such.

*{and three hundred concubines}* These were neither lawful wives, nor plain whores; but in a kind of middle condition. They were not wives, because not solemnly married, nor endowed with dowry or jointure; nor had their children any right of inheritance, Gen. 21.10. & 25.6. They were not whores, because taken by a man to be his alone, as wives, and none others: and in this respect they are oft styled wives, as Gen. 25.1,6. & 35.22. 1 Chr. 1.31. Concubines therefore were a kind of wives in an inferior degree. See on Judg. 19.1,2. & on Gen. 22.24. & 30.4. It is probable that Solomon dealt with many of these as Ahasuerosh did with many of the maidens that went in to him, *she came in to the king no more, except the king delighted in her*, Esth. 2.14.

*{and his wives turned away his heart}* It appears his wives were more bold and prevalent with him than his concubines, who were of an inferior rank. His wives turned his heart from the Lord to their idols, v. 4.

**V. 4.** *{For it came to pass when Solomon was old}* He was (as by circumstances it may be gathered) betwixt fifty and threescore years old. Take him to be about twenty when he began to reign; for he then had a son of a year old, ch. 14.21. He had reigned more than three years before he began to build, ch. 6.1. He was twenty years in building, ch. 9.10. These summed up make forty-three. Some years must be added to all these for spreading his fame throughout the world; and for the queen of Sheba's and other kings coming to him to hear his wisdom, and for their departing from him; and for his continuing in the services of the temple; yea, and for gathering together a thousand wives and concubines; and for that boldness they took to turn away his heart. Howsoever this circumstance of age may seem to extenuate his sin, in regard of that weakness which old age brings upon a man, yet if circumstances be duly weighed, it does more aggravate the same: For if old age weakened him, his lust was not so strong, nor the inward temptation so violent, as to make him hunt after so many women as he did. But his age was not so great as to weaken him; for he was not sixty years old. Besides, the elder he was, the wiser he should have been, Job 32.7. And sure it is that he had thereby the greater experience of God's goodness to him. So as his age is an aggravation of his sin.

*{that his wives turned away his heart after other gods, and his heart was not perfect with the LORD his God}* Of a perfect heart see ch. 8.61. His heart is perfect with the Lord who does cleave wholly and only to him. So did not Solomon, who clave to so many wives and concubines, v. 2.

*{as was the heart of David his father}* See ch. 3.14. & 9.4. David did indeed commit many gross sins, 2 Sam. 12.9. yet he never yielded to any idolatry, but kept the worship and service of the Lord pure all his days. This is here added to show that swerving from the approved pattern of a pious parent is a great aggravation of a child's wickedness.

**V. 5.** *{For Solomon went after Ashtoreth}* 2 Kings 23.13. *Ashtoreth* being a word of the feminine gender, is by most taken for the goddess Venus. And because there were sundry idols styled Venus, the plural number is oft used, *Ashtaroth*. As *Baalim* is taken for *all manner of gods*, so may *Ashtaroth* for *all manner of goddesses*. In this large extent are these two words oft joined together, as Judg. 2.13. & 10.6. 1 Sam. 7.4. & 12.10. The Hebrew notation implies that this idol was set out in the shape of a sheep. See more on Judg. 2.13.

*{the goddess of the Zidonians}* Every nation had their own gods, whom they especially worshipped. And the Zidonians chose Ashtaroth for their goddess, and worshipped her above all: They were also worshippers of Baal, ch. 16.31.

*{and after Milcom}* Styled *Molech*, v. 7. It is probable this was the idol to which children were sacrificed. See on Lev. 18.21. & 20.2. Jer. 32.35. According to the notation of the word *Molech* signifies *a king*. And by the heathen this was accounted one of the chief of their idols: Most think him to be Saturn. Such idols were Adrammelech and Anammelech, 2 King. 17.31.

*{the abomination}* This, as other idols v. 7. was abominable; yet this was the more abominable by reason of sacrificing children to it.

*{of the Ammonites}* See v. 7,33.

**V. 6.** *{And Solomon did evil in the sight of the LORD}* This he did many ways: 1. in doting on so many wives and concubines; 2. in marrying idolatrous strangers; 3. in suffering them to practice their idolatry; 4. in appointing places for their idols; 5. in conforming himself to their desires. See ch. 14.22. & 21.20.

*{and went not fully after the LORD}* Heb. *fulfilled not after the Lord*. He did not so forsake the Lord as utterly to renounce his worship and service, as Joash, 2 Chron. 24.17,18. and Ahaz, 2 Chron. 28.24. For the book of Ecclesiastes shows that such a root of grace remained in him as brought him to repentance. No word in this history does expressly manifest an utter forsaking of the Lord, or a final apostasy. He brought no idols into God's house, but he observed God's ordinances. His heart is said to turn after other gods, v. 2. in that he suffered his wives to worship them, and suffered temples and altars to be erected for them: And therein Solomon went not fully after the Lord.

*{as did David his father}* See v. 4.

**V. 7.** *{Then did Solomon build an high place}* i.e. he permitted such to be built. Of a high place see ch. 3.2.

*{for Chemosh}* This idol is taken for Bacchus, and is said to be worshipped in secret and dark places, as Ezek. 8.12.

*{the abomination of Moab}* See Num. 21.29. Jer. 48.13. If it be objected that Chemosh is said to be the god of the Ammonites, Judg. 11.24. I answer that neighboring countries did oft worship one another's god; and in this respect the god of one country may also be called the god of another.

*{in the hill}* He means the Mount of Olives, 2 Sam. 15.30. styled the mount of corruption 2 King. 23.13. because it was a place of corrupting the Jews.

*{that is before Jerusalem}* In the sight of that holy city where the temple was; so as the true worshippers of God, and idolaters, were in the face of one another, and might outface one another. See ch. 3.15.

*{and for Molech the abomination of the children of Ammon}* See v. 5.

**V. 8.** *{And likewise did he for all his strange wives}* All his idolatrous wives, when they saw how he had gratified some, solicited him to do the like for them. Floodgates of sin being opened for some, make all to rush and press after.

*{which burnt incense, and sacrificed to their gods}* Idolaters yield that to their idols which is due to the Lord, Exod. 3.18. & 30.8. Of incense see ch. 9.25. Of sacrifices, chap. 8.63,64.

**V. 9.** *{And the LORD was angry with Solomon}* God's anger is oft incensed against his own children for their sin. Solomon was also called Jedidiah 2 Sam. 12.25. that is, *beloved of the Lord*. See Ex. 4.14. 2 Sam. 6.7. 1 Chron. 21.7.

*{because his heart was turned}* This is made the ground of his great sin: for when the heart disesteems God, liberty will be taken for any sin.

*{from the LORD God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15.

*{which had appeared unto him twice}* Chap. 3.5. & 9.2. The extraordinary manner of God's appearing unto him was an evidence of his great favor to him, and of much honor done to him. This therefore was a great aggravation of his backsliding.

**V. 10.** *{And had commanded him concerning this thing, that he should not go after other gods}* Chap. 6.12. & 9.6. To violate express particular commandments is a great aggravation, Gen. 2.17.

*{but he kept not that which the LORD commanded}* See 2 Sam. 12.9.

**V. 11.** *{Wherefore the LORD said unto Solomon}* God here declares the equity of his proceeding against him.

*{For as much as this is done of thee}* Heb. *is with thee*. Man's own transgressions pull God's judgments on him.

*{and thou hast not kept my covenant and my statutes which I have commanded thee}* Of statutes see ch. 6.12. A covenant, when applied to God, signifies a law appointed or enjoined to be kept, and that upon promise of reward to such as keep it, and of penalty to such as transgress it, Deut. 29.9,25. Herein lies a difference betwixt statutes and covenants, that these have respect to the sovereignty of him that ordains them; those to his goodwill to whom they are made.

*{I will surely rend}* Heb. *rending I will rend*. See ch. 8.13.

*{the kingdom}* i.e. the greatest part of it, v. 31,35.

*{from thee}* Though this judgment were not executed while Solomon lived, yet because he was the cause of it, and it was done in his son's time, the kingdom was said to be rent from him.

*{and I will give it}* It was done by the rebellion of the Israelites, yet God ordered it so. See ch. 12.24.

*{to thy servant}* i.e. Jeroboam. See v. 26. It aggravates the judgment, that a servant was preferred before the son.

**V. 12.** *{Notwithstanding in thy days I will not do it}* This is an allay of the judgment, and a mixture of mercy therewith. So 2 King. 20.19. See ch. 21.29.

*{for David thy father’s sake}* Namely, by reason of that promise made with David, 2 Sam. 7.16. Such an argument does Moses plead, Exod. 32.13. See 2 King. 13.23. This therefore is not to be taken of any worth or merit in David, but of God's promise made to him, and of his care and conscience in observing the condition annexed.

*{but I will rend it out of the hand of thy son}* A judgment on a child occasioned by the father's sin is a judgment on the father. The son here meant was Rehoboam, ch. 16.17. We read not of any other son that Solomon had, though he had seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines: and this son was born long before he had so many wives, ch. 14.21. See Hos. 4.10.

**V. 13.** *{Howbeit, I will not rend away all the kingdom}* This is another point of mitigation. See v. 12.

*{but I will give one tribe to thy son}* He means especially Judah, but withal Benjamin, ch. 12.21,23. For Benjamin bordered upon Judah, Josh. 18.11. And in it was Jerusalem, Judg. 1.21. So much also of Simeon as lay within Judah, Josh. 19.1,9. was comprised under Judah: and the Levites likewise; for they were accounted no distinct tribe, but dispersed among the other tribes, Deut. 18.1. Josh. 13,14,33. And because their chief service was in the temple, they left their cities in the other tribes, and came to Jerusalem, 2 Chron. 11.13,14. See chap. 12.21. & on 1 Sam. 9.21.

*{for David my servant’s sake}* See v. 12. & ch. 3.6.

*{and for Jerusalem’s sake}* For there was a special promise made to Jerusalem. See v. 36. 2 Chr. 6.6.

*{which I have chosen}* See chap. 8.44. Of Jerusalem see chap. 3.15.

**V. 14.** *{And the LORD stirred up}* Some instances of God's anger mentioned v. 9. are here in order set down. Herein, though Solomon's enemies had their own ends, and sought to revenge their own quarrel, yet God used them as instruments of his justice, to punish Solomon's revolt, Isai. 10.5,6. See ch. 12.15.

*{an adversary unto Solomon}* He was such a one as would not submit himself to Solomon, nor pay tribute, as others, 2 King. 8.20. but did much mischief to Israel, v. 25.

*{Hadad}* There was a former king of Edom of this name, Gen. 36.35.

*{the Edomite}* As Esau, called Edom, Gen. 25.30. & 36.9. had an implacable hatred against Jacob his brother, Gen. 27.41. so the Edomites his posterity, generation after generation, had against the Israelites, Amos 1.11.

*{he was of the king’s seed in Edom}* One of the king's sons, and in probability his only son and heir surviving. See v. 17.

**V. 15.** *{For it came to pass}* The particular circumstances following are not registered in that history of David which is in the sacred Scripture, yet are they to be received as an undoubted truth, because here mentioned.

*{when David was in Edom}* This has relation to that brief story of subduing Edom set down 2 Sam. 8.14.

*{and Joab the captain of the host}* See 1 Chron. 11.6.

*{was gone up to bury the slain}* Such of the Israelites as were slain in the battle, or at the siege, may be here meant. Or in respect to the princes and other chief persons of the Edomites, he might afford them burial, as 2 King. 9.34. Or for avoiding of noisome savors he might cause all the slain to be buried, of what rank or degree soever, as Ezek. 39.11.

*{after he had smitten every male in Edom}* He means especially such as were taken in the city that had been besieged, because they would not yield to reasonable conditions of peace, Deut. 20.13.

**V. 16.** *{For six months did Joab remain there with all Israel, until he had cut off every male in Edom}* These six months must be accounted from Joab's first setting on the Edomites till his full conquest of them.

**V. 17.** *{That Hadad fled}* This depends on the first clause of v. 15. thus, *It came to passe that Hadad fled, &c.*

*{He and certain Edomites}* These made an escape and so fled together, as 1 Sam. 30.17. Jer. 41.15.

*{of his father’s servants with him}* These were of the king's court, and waited on the king, and in their grateful respect to him took his son with them.

*{to go into Egypt}* Egypt was an ancient country and a safe place; thither they therefore went for shelter. See v. 40.

*{Hadad being yet a little child}* It is supposed he was then about twelve years old; in which respect he might well be accounted a little child, especially to run so great a hazard. See Gen. 44.20.

**V. 18.** *{And they arose out of Midian}* This phrase shows that they first fled to Midian, and there abode some time; Midian bordering upon Edom, Numb. 22.4.

*{and came to Paran}* Paran is betwixt Midian and Egypt, Gen. 14.6. & 21.21.

*{and they took men with them out of Paran}* This they did that the king's son might come the better attended, and so get the more respect in Egypt.

*{and they came to Egypt}* The place they first intended to come unto, v. 17.

*{unto Pharaoh king of Egypt}* See v. 40.

*{which gave him an house}* Fit for himself and his followers, that they might have a place to rest in.

*{and appointed him victuals}* For the sustenance of them all, and that from time to time.

*{and gave him land}* That he might have yearly revenues for all needful expenses. These phrases show that Pharaoh courteously entertained him, and afforded him all things fit for present and future maintenance, and for abode with him.

**V. 19.** *{And Hadad found great favour in the sight of Pharaoh}* This has relation to the continuance of Pharaoh's good respect to him.

*{so that he gave him to wife the sister of his own wife}* Thus the king of Egypt made affinity with him. This gives evidence of Pharaoh's hospital and gracious disposition, and also of Hadad's grateful and comely carriage towards Pharaoh.

*{the sister of Tahpenes the queen}* This is an express exposition of the former sentence: for Tahpenes was wife to Pharaoh.

**V. 20.** *{And the sister of Tahpenes bare him Genubath his son}* This is noted as a continuance or succession of such as should be adversaries to Solomon and his race.

*{whom Tahpenes weaned in Pharaoh’s house}* Though she were a princess, yet she gave suck to her child, as Gen. 21.7,8.

*{and Genubath was in Pharaoh’s household among the sons of Pharaoh}* Trained up in the court, and had such education as the king's own children.

**V. 21.** *{And when Hadad heard in Egypt that David slept with his fathers, and that Joab the captain of the host was dead}* The very names of David and Joab were a terror to the Edomites: But when Hadad heard of their death, he conceived some hope of freeing his nation from bondage under the king of Israel.

*{Hadad said to Pharaoh, Let me depart}* Heb. *Send me away*.

*{that I may go to mine own country}* Though he found good entertainment in Egypt, yet his mind was set upon the freedom of his country; which could not be, but by doing some annoyance to Israel.

**V. 22.** *{Then Pharaoh said unto him}* Pharaoh was not weary of giving him entertainment, but would have continued him still with him. It is probable that Pharaoh was ignorant of Hadad's end in seeking to depart.

*{But what hast thou lacked with me, that behold thou seekest to go to thine own country}* Pharaoh implies, that if Hadad had wanted anything that Egypt could have afforded him, his want should have been supplied. This much amplifies his courtesy and bounty.

*{and he answered, Nothing}* Heb. *Not*. Hadad shows that it was not any discontent he found in Egypt that moved him to leave it.

*{Howbeit, let me go in any wise}* Heb. *letting go let me go*. See ch. 8.13. Nothing could detain him from prosecuting his purpose of freeing his country. What at first he could not effect, was in time brought to pass. Hadad laid the foundation of Edom's utter revolt from Israel, 2 King. 8.20,22. whereby Isaac's prophecy Gen. 27.40. was accomplished.

**V. 23.** *{And God stirred him up another adversary}* See v. 14. God can stir up many to execute his judgments.

*{Rezon the son of Eliadah}* Long after this one Rezin, a king of Syria, did much mischief to Judah, 2 King. 16.5, &c. It may be that that Rezin descended from this Rezon.

*{which fled from his lord Hadadezer king of Zobah}* When Rezon saw that his master Hadadezer was like to be utterly overthrown, David having gotten the better of him, 2 Sam. 8.3,4. & 10.18. he fled and left his master. The title of Psal. 60. has relation to that conquest which David by Joab had over Hadadezer. Of Hadadezer see ch. 15.18.

**V. 24.** *{And he gathered men unto him, and became captain over a band when David slew them of Zobah}* Rezon gathered together such as were put to flight in that battle; and in time got such an army together as might be able to stand against Israel, and to annoy them.

*{and they went to Damascus}* A royal city in Syria, Isai. 7.8. Here David had put a garrison, 2 Sam. 8.6.

*{and dwelt therein}* They thrust out David's garrison, and took possession themselves.

*{and reigned in Damascus}* They whom Rezon had gathered established him king in Damascus.

**V. 25.** *{And he was an adversary to Israel}* It is supposed that he was stirred up by Hadad.

*{all the days of Solomon}* He ever bore ill will to Israel, but most manifested the same after Solomon's apostasy. For till then Solomon had no adversary that showed himself, ch. 5.4.

*{beside the mischief that Hadad did}* See v. 14, &c.

*{and he abhorred Israel}* In his heart he hated Israel, and took all occasions of doing what mischief he could against them; like as Hadad did.

*{and reigned over Syria}* Heb. *Aram*. That country whereof Damascus was the chief city, and which was always at enmity with Israel.

**V. 26.** *{And Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* 2 Chron. 13.6. This does distinguish him from Jeroboam the son of Joash, 2 King. 14.23,24.

*{an Ephrathite}* Of the tribe of Ephraim, ch. 12.25. Elkanah was so called, though a Levite, because he dwelt in Ephraim, 1 Sam. 1.1. See on Ruth 1.2.

*{of Zereda}* This was in the tribe of Ephraim. It is taken to be the same that is called Zererath, Judg. 7.22.

*{Solomon’s servant}* One of his chief officers and counselors, being by Solomon deputed to some select employment. See v. 11. & ch. 9.22. The judgment against Solomon was the greater, in that such a one, even his servant, was against him, and not strangers only.

*{whose mother’s name was Zeruah a widow-woman}* This is noted to show that, his father being taken away, he was educated by his mother: And women are most likely to put a high spirit into their sons.

*{even he lift up his hand against the king}* He did that which offended the king, and made him suspect his loyalty. But this phrase setting out plain rebellion 2 Sam. 20.21. may have relation to that which is noted ch. 12.12, &c.

**V. 27.** *{And this was the cause that he lift up his hand against the king}* This was one cause. Another is rendered, v. 40.

*{Solomon built Millo}* See ch. 9.15.

*{and repaired}* Heb. *closed, *or,* made up*.

*{the breaches of the city of David his father}* These might be the breaches which were made in the assault against Zion, 2 Sam. 5.6,7. Some dilapidations might remain therein till Solomon's time.

**V. 28.** *{And the man Jeroboam was a mighty man of valour}* He was in this respect the more fit to be the head of a faction.

*{And Solomon seeing the young man}* It appears that Solomon oft went out to his works, and observed the diligence or slothfulness of those whom he set on work; which was one reason of his bringing his great works to such perfection as he did. For the eye of the master makes the horse fat, and the servant sweat.

*{that he was industrious}* Heb. *did work*. It appears that Jeroboam at first was employed in laborious services, and being faithful therein, and diligent, was preferred to a place of trust and charge over others. For being employed about building Millo and repairing Zion while he was a young man, King Solomon took special notice of his wit, strength, industry, and other commendable parts, and preferred him. Hereupon this youth waxed insolent, and conceived high thoughts of himself, and was ready to enterprise such attempts as opportunity should afford. Solomon had good reason to prefer him: it was just, equal and commendable so to do: But Jeroboam's abuse of preferment occasioned the mischief which followed. Besides herein was a secret work of divine providence which Solomon did not discern. Thus we may see why the building of Millo and repairing Zion are brought in as a cause, or rather an occasion of Jeroboam's lifting up his hand against the king.

*{he made him ruler}* Solomon ignorantly advanced Jeroboam to his own and his posterity's great damage. He being in place of authority might the more easily hear the complaints of people, and more readily draw multitudes after him.

*{over all the charge}* Heb. *burden*. Jeroboam had a charge about gathering in taxes, which the subjects found a heavy burden unto them, ch. 12.4.

*{of the house of Joseph}* Under Joseph are comprised two tribes, namely Ephraim and Manasseh. It is probable that Solomon to maintain the state of his seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines, laid great taxes on his people, which made them complain; and that Jeroboam upon their complaint expostulating the case with the king, Solomon thereupon eyed him as Saul eyed David, 1 Sam. 18.9.

*{29. And it came to pass}* Here another cause of Jeroboam's high thoughts against the king, and of the king's jealousy over Jeroboam, is set down. See v. 27.

*{at that time when Jeroboam went out of Jerusalem}* Divine providence so orders matters as they fall out most opportunely for the accomplishment of God's purpose, 1 Sam. 9.14,15. ch. 13.24. Gen. 37.25.

*{that the prophet Ahijah}* The function of Ahijah is here set down, to distinguish him from other Ahijahs; as from the priest, 1 Sam. 14.3. from the Levite, 1 Chr. 26.20. from the scribe, ch. 4.3. and from Baasha's father, ch. 15.27. This is he, who with some others, penned the Acts of Solomon, 2 Chr. 9.29.

*{the Shilonite}* He is so called, because he dwelt in Shiloh, ch. 14.2. See 2.17.

*{found him in the way}* In that God sent this prophet on his errand, the prophet may well be said to find Jeroboam. For he went to seek him. See ch. 21.18,20.

*{and he}* i.e. the prophet. He might more boldly rend his own garment than another's: And having a charge from God to do what he did, he might purposely for that end put on a new garment.

*{had clad himself with a new garment}* To show that though the kingdom were as a new garment, fresh and strong, yet it should be rent.

*{and they two were alone in the field}* This was, that the fact might not be too much divulged, 1 Sam. 9.27. 2 Kings 9.6.

**V. 30.** *{And Ahijah caught the new garment that was on him}* He hastily pulled it off from his own body.

*{and rent it}* This he did for a sign to make Jeroboam the more confidently believe that what he said should come to pass. So Isaiah, 20.2. Ezekiel, 24.27. This act of the prophet could not but astonish Jeroboam, and make him attentively heed it.

*{in twelve pieces}* According to the number of the tribes of Israel, ch. 18.31. Exod. 24.4.

**V. 31.** *{And he said to Jeroboam}* Here the prophet expressly declares the meaning of the sign.

*{Take thee ten pieces}* He makes Jeroboam a party in this sign, to stir him up the more seriously to ponder it, and the more confidently to believe it.

*{for thus saith the LORD}* This shows the warrant of his message.

*{the God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15.

*{Behold}* His message was strange and remarkable. So ch. 13.2. & 14.10.

*{I will}* See ch. 12.15. & 2 Sam. 12.22.

*{rent the kingdom}* Violently take it away, and sever it asunder. It was Jeroboam with the people of Israel that did instrumentally and immediately rend the kingdom from Solomon's son: But because it was purposed and ordered by God, he assumes it to himself. So 1 Sam. 15.28.

*{out of the hand of Solomon}* See v. 11.

*{and will give ten tribes to thee}* God proportions out to everyone what and how much he shall have, Deut. 2.5,9,19. Jud. 11.21, &c.

**V. 32.** *{But he shall have}* This was verified in and upon Solomon's son. As that which was taken from Rehoboam is said to be taken from Solomon, v. 31. so that which he retained, Solomon is said to retain: For a father lives in his son.

*{one tribe for my servant David’s sake, and for Jerusalem’s sake, the city which I have chosen out of the tribes of Israel}* See v. 13.

**V. 33.** *{Because that they}* Not only Solomon alone, but many also of the people with him forsook the Lord. For people are ready to follow their governors, ch. 12.30. 2 Chron. 24.18. & 33.9.

*{have forsaken me}* This intends as much as was implied under these phrases, went not fully after the Lord, v. 6. His heart was turned from the Lord, v. 9. See ch. 18.18.

*{and have worshipped Ashtoreth the goddess of the Zidonians}* See v. 5.

*{Chemosh the god of the Moabites}* See v. 7.

*{and Milcom the god of the children of Ammon}* See v. 5.

*{and have not walked in my ways}* See ch. 3.14.

*{to do that which is right in mine eyes}* i.e. to do what is warranted by God's word, and that uprightly, as before him whose eyes are every where, Prov. 15.3. and discern all things, even the thoughts of the heart. See ch. 15.5. & 22.43.

*{and to keep my statutes and my judgments}* See ch. 2.3.

*{as did David his father}* See v. 4.

**V. 34.** *{Howbeit I will not take}* See v. 12.

*{the whole kingdom out of his hand}* Or, *any of the kingdom*. No part thereof was taken from Solomon. See a like phrase Gen. 39.23. Psal. 103.2.

*{but I will make him prince all the days of his life for David my servant’s sake}* See v. 12.

*{whom I chose}* 1 Sam. 13.14. & 15.28.

*{because he kept my commandments and my statutes}* See ch. 2.3. This is not alleged as a proper, procuring, and meritorious cause, but as an accomplishment of that condition which God prescribed for an evidence of his good pleasure.

**V. 35.** *{But I will take the kingdom out of his son’s hand, and will give it unto thee}* See the accomplishment, ch. 12.15, &c.

*{even ten tribes}* See v. 34.

**V. 36.** *{And unto his son will I give one tribe}* See v. 13.

*{that David my servant may have a light}* Heb. *lamp, *or,* candle*. By this metaphor he understands a royal glory in a successor, that as a light may shine before his people, and govern them, ch. 15.4. 2 Sam. 21.17. 2 Kings 8.19. Psal. 132.17.

*{always}* See ch. 8.13. In the Messiah this is most properly accomplished.

*{before me}* See 1 Chron. 17.24.

*{in Jerusalem, the city which I have chosen me to put my name there}* In that God appointed Jerusalem to be the place where his temple should be, 2 Chron. 3.1. & 6.6. Psal. 68.29. & 132.13,14. & 135.21. he is said to choose it to put his name there. See on ch. 8.16,44. & 2 Chron. 6.6. of Jerusalem see ch. 3.15.

**V. 37.** *{And I will take thee, and thou shalt reign according to all that thy soul desireth}* He means that Jeroboam should be a supreme king, under no superior on earth, so as he might do and command what himself desired.

*{and shalt be king over Israel}* Israel is here taken for the ten tribes distinguished from Judah and Benjamin.

**V. 38.** *{And it shall be if thou}* God uses to confer honor and other blessings upon conditions, which are duties to be observed by them who desire comfortably to enjoy those blessings, and to have them continued to their posterity, Exod. 19.5. Deut. 4.1.

*{wilt hearken unto all that I command thee}* Deut. 15.5.

*{and wilt walk in my ways}* See ch. 3.14.

*{and do that is right in my sight}* See v. 33.

*{to keep my statutes and my commandments}* See ch. 2.3.

*{as David my servant did}* See v. 4. He does the rather put Jeroboam in mind of David's pattern, because David used no indirect means to come to the kingdom, though it were promised him in Saul's time. See 1 Sam. 26.10,11.

*{that I will be with thee}* This phrase imports an especial presence of God with one, to protect, direct, assist, and every way bless him, Deut. 31.8. Josh. 1.5. Heb. 13.5.

*{and build thee a sure house}* i.e. a durable stock or posterity to Jeroboam. See ch. 9.5. 1 Sam. 2.35. 2 Sam. 7.16.

*{as I built for David}* 2 Sam. 7.27,28,29. David and Solomon his son were all their days established on the throne over all the tribes, and their posterity over Judah, and Christ over all the Israel of God everlastingly, Luk. 1.33. Thus did God make David's house sure indeed. It pleased the Lord to send this gracious message mixed with such precious promises to such a one as Jeroboam was; 1. Indefinitely, to show his own gracious disposition to the children of men. 2. To allure him to whom it was sent unto obedience. 3. To justify himself in inflicting judgments. 4. To warn others to whom in special like promises might be made. 5. To aggravate the condemnation of the rebellious.

*{and will give Israel unto thee}* See v. 37.

**V. 39.** *{And I will for this}* For this cause, which was Solomon's idolatry; or, by this division of the kingdom.

*{afflict the seed of David}* i.e. molest, vex, and lay judgments upon the posterity of David. For after this division there were perpetual wars betwixt Judah and Israel, ch. 14.30. & 15.16. 2 Kings 14.11, &c. 2 Chron. 28.6, &c.

*{but not for ever}* For sometimes there were good kings of David's line who prospered very well. And though the posterity of David were carried into captivity, yet were they again brought back. And finally, the Messiah, of the seed of David, still continues to reign most gloriously, and so shall forever, Luke 1.33.

**V. 40.** *{Solomon sought therefore}* He discerned Jeroboam's stout stomach, and it may be, some secret conspiracies of his. And this inference shows that he heard of Ahijah's message. For though the prophet concealed it from all but Jeroboam, yet might Jeroboam make it known, partly out of pride, that he should be so highly advanced; and partly out of policy, to draw people's hearts to him, and to make a party for him.

*{to kill Jeroboam}* Kings cannot endure corivals, 1 Sam. 20.31. But Solomon, though otherwise a wise man, herein had but a foolish conceit of crossing the determinate and revealed purpose of God, Prov. 21.30.

*{and Jeroboam arose}* This word arose implies diligence and speed, Gen. 24.10. Judg. 13.11. 1 Sam. 26.9.

*{and fled}* Fear of death makes men shift for themselves, Exod. 2.15. 1 Sam. 19.12. ch. 19.3. Matth. 2.14.

*{into Egypt}* See v. 17.

*{unto Shishak king of Egypt}* Shishak was that king's proper name. Pharaoh was a name common to all the kings of Egypt; See ch. 2.39. Particular kings had other titles added, for distinctions' sake, as Pharaoh-Hophra, Jer. 44.30. Pharaoh-Necho, Jer. 46.2. It is probable that this Shishak was brother to Solomon's wife, and son and heir to Pharaoh her father, ch. 3.1. But he might be ignorant of Jeroboam's case, and in common courtesy afford hospitality to him: Or he might be offended with Solomon for taking so many wives as he did besides his sister, and in that respect entertain Jeroboam.

*{and was in Egypt until the death of Solomon}* For it was expressly told him that Solomon should be Prince all the dayes of his life, v. 34. Of Jeroboam's return see ch. 12.1,2.

**V. 41.** *{And the rest of the acts}* Or, *words, *or,* things*.

*{of Solomon}* Though Solomon were a wise man, yet the Holy Ghost thought it not meet to set down all his acts in Scripture. See John 21.25. ch. 14.19. Among other memorable acts his repentance is omitted; whence some infer that he repented not at all, but died in his sin, as Saul, 1 Chron. 10.13. But there is even in this particular an express difference put betwixt Solomon and Saul, 2 Sam. 7.15. Besides, there is such apparent evidence of Solomon's public repentance in the book called Ecclesiastes as the Holy Ghost thought it not necessary to make any other mention thereof. To prove the point, That Solomon repented, let these reasons be well weighed. 1. Solomon penned certain books of sacred Scripture, as Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Canticles. And all the penmen of Scripture were holy men of God, 2 Pet. 1.21. 2. The subject matter of Ecclesiastes shows that it was penned by Solomon after his fall; and the book savors of a penitent spirit. 3. His name, Jedidiah, that is, Beloved of the Lord, 2 Sam. 12.25. implies that he was not utterly cast off. For whom God loves, he loves to the end, John 13.1. 4. God promises to be his Father, 2 Sam. 7.14. But a father will not utterly forsake his son, Psal. 27.10. 5. God also promises that his mercy shall not depart away from him, 2 Sam. 7.15. 6. A probable conjecture may be made that Solomon before his death had forsaken his idolatrous courses, and restrained his wives from theirs, and abated his wickedness thereabout, in that the people who set themselves to defame his government complained of no such matter to Rehoboam, ch. 12.4, &c. 7. Solomon was an especial type of Christ, and that in sundry respects: as I. In his royal dignity, ch. 2.9, &c. Psal. 2.6. II. In the presents all nations brought to him, ch. 10.24. Psal. 72.10. III. In his prophetical function, Eccl. 1.1. Act. 3.25. IV. In his wisdom, ch. 3.12. V. In the peace he worked, ch. 4.25. 1 Chr. 22.9. Eph. 2.14,15. VI. In building God's house, ch. 6.1. 2 Chr. 3.3,6. VII. In the relation betwixt God and him, 2 Sam. 7.14. Heb. 1.5. VIII. In the stability of his throne, 1 Chr. 17.12. Luk. 1.32,33.

*{and all that he did, and his wisdom, are they not written in the book of the acts of Solomon?}* He means large records and chronicles, which served for the use of those times and future ages, but were no part of canonical Scripture. For there were other chronicles of the affairs of Judah and Israel than those now extant. See 2 Chr. 9.29.

**V. 42.** *{And the time}* Heb. *days*. Man's time being very uncertain, may well be reckoned by days. See Gen. 47.9.

*{that Solomon reigned in Jerusalem over all Israel}* In Jerusalem was the king's throne and palace, and there he most resided, but his dominion extended throughout all Israel. Israel is here collectively to be taken for the twelve tribes; and therefore this general particle all is added thereunto.

*{was forty years}* 2 Chr. 9.30. See v. 4.

**V. 43.** *{And Solomon slept with his fathers}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and was buried in the city of David his father}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and Rehoboam}* Math. 1.7. called Roboam.

*{his son reigned in his stead}* Rehoboam was born before Solomon was king. See ch. 14.21.

# CHAPTER 12

**Ver. 1.** *{And Rehoboam}* 2 Chr. 10.1. The three first kings, Saul, David, Solomon, reigned over all the twelve tribes, but Rehoboam was the first that reigned over two only.

*{went to Shechem}* Shechem is a city of Ephraim, belonging to the Levites, Josh. 20.7. in the midst of the land, and so most convenient for all the tribes to come unto. It is called also Sichem. Thither Abraham first came when he entered into Canaan, Gen. 12.6. There was Dinah defiled, Gen. 34. There Joseph was buried, Josh. 24.32. Thither Joshua gathered all Israel, Josh. 24.1. There Abimelech consulted about the kingdom, Judg. 9.1. Indeed Abimelech beat down the city, and sowed it with salt, Judg. 9.45. But by this it appears that it was built again, v. 25.

*{for all Israel were come to Shechem}* He means the heads and officers of Israel. This was the reason why Rehoboam went to Shechem, because all Israel was assembled there: And it is probable the people desired he would come thither; but had he been a prudent man, he might well have suspected some danger.

*{to make him king}* To crown him, and perform such solemnities as were used at a king's coronation, they pretended they would set him over all the twelve tribes; if at least he would have yielded to their conditions, v. 4.

**V. 2.** *{And it came to pass when Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* See ch. 11.26.

*{who was yet in Egypt}* It appears by the particle yet that Jeroboam continued in Egypt till this time. See on ch. 11.40.

*{heard of it}* He might first hear of Solomon's death by some private intelligence; but afterwards the people sent to him by common consent, as v. 3.

*{for he was fled from the presence of king Solomon}* This is a brief of what is more amply set down, ch. 11.40.

*{and Jeroboam dwelt in Egypt}* There he abode from the time that he fled out of Israel, till Solomon was dead.

**V. 3.** *{That they sent and called him}* This is meant of a public sending for him by the whole congregation of Israel. While Rehoboam was solemnizing his father's funeral rites, Jeroboam had time to return to Israel, and to plot with his complices about the kingdom.

*{and Jeroboam and all the congregation of Israel came and spake unto Rehoboam saying}* Having consulted together, and concluded what they should say to the king, Jeroboam was the mouth of all Israel. For he was a prime officer of Solomon's council, and had suffered in the people's cause, and was also a man of valor. See ch. 11.26,28.

**V. 4.** *{Thy father made our yoke}* A yoke properly is that whereby oxen are coupled together, and which lies upon their neck as a means to draw loads after them. Service and subjection and hard tasks is oft set out under this metaphor, Jer. 27.8,11,12. Lev. 26.13. and are counted grievous yokes.

*{grievous}* Yokes, by reason of the heavy weight which beasts draw, do much pinch them. It appears hereby that Solomon put the people to hard services, and laid great taxes upon them. This may have reference to that large provision which was taken out of all parts of the land to maintain the royalty of his court, ch. 4.7,22,23. and to the great pains that multitudes of people took about his buildings and other affairs, ch. 5.18. & 9.22. But questionless there were other far more grievous burdens laid on them after his apostasy, to maintain the royalty and idolatry of his seven hundred wives, all princesses, and three hundred concubines. It may be also that Hadad's and Rezon's enmity (of whom ch. 11.14,23.) occasioned some impositions. See on Ps. 72.12.

*{now therefore make thou the grievous service}* This may be applied to the labor and pains whereunto the people were put about Solomon's works and wars.

*{of thy father}* Which your father exacted of us.

*{and his heavy yoke which he put upon us}* This may be applied to the taxes that were laid on them.

*{lighter}* Or, *easier*. They refused not all services, subjection and taxes, but only oppressions by them. Here we see how sedulous and solicitous they were about their civil affairs, but no care at all taken about true religion, or preventing idolatry.

*{and we will serve thee}* Their meaning is, they would accept Rehoboam for their king, and be subject unto him, v. 7.

**V. 5.** *{and he said unto them, Depart yet}* This he said to gain time for advice and counsel, which in a case of so great importance was a part of prudence. Had he not preferred the arrogant advice of unexperienced young men before the grave counsel of old and well-staid statesmen, it might have proved advantageous unto him.

*{for three days}* Where there are many counselors, and the matter weighty, there needs much time for conferring counsels together: But great multitudes being assembled from many remote places could hardly admit a longer time.

*{then come again to me}* He does not utterly refuse to answer their demand.

*{And the people departed}* This they did, as to testify their willingness to accept him for their king on good terms, so to consult about rejecting him in case his answer should not satisfy their desire.

**V. 6.** *{And king Rehoboam consulted with the old men}* Such as were ancient and experienced counselors of state, called by us privy counselors, Gen. 50.7. they are called elders of the land. See Job 12.12. & 32.7. Psal. 119.100. See ch. 8.1.

*{that stood before Solomon his father while he yet lived}* Such as these were on all occasions called to advise with the king. See Esth. 1.13,14. Solomon being a very wise man, this their standing before him could not but be a means to them of learning much wisdom. Prov. 13.20. Here are two reasons rendered of asking their advice. 1. because they were mature and experienced counselors; 2. because they had been so much in Solomon's company.

*{and said, How do you advise that I may answer this people?}* That which follows shows that he propounded this question in hope that they would satisfy his humor; but not with a resolution to follow their counsel if it should cross his mind. Compare Jer. 42.2,5. with 43.2.

**V. 7.** *{And they spake unto him, saying, If thou wilt be a servant unto this people this day}* i.e. if you will as a servant yield unto them, and grant their demand for this once.

*{and wilt serve them, and answer them, and speak good words unto them}* 2 Chron. 10.7. thus, *If thou be kind to this people, and please them, and speak good words unto them*, i.e. gentle and meek words, such as may seem good to them. See ch. 2.7. Prov. 15.1. & 25.15.

*{then they will be thy servants for ever}* i.e. obedient subjects. See v. 4. This they added to meet with a secret conceit. It might be conceived that if the king should once submit himself to the will of his subjects, they might prove lords over him: But here they show the contrary.

**V. 8.** *{But he forsook the counsel of the old men which they had given him}* Herein he showed himself a child in understanding, though in age old enough. See 2 Chron. 12.2.

*{and consulted with the young men}* Such as were unexperienced; styled young in opposition to the old men, v. 6.

*{that were grown up with him}* Who had been with him time after time, and well knew his humor and disposition.

*{and which stood before him}* They did attend upon him, and also on other occasions gave counsel to him.

**V. 9.** *{And he said unto them, What counsel give ye}* They who like not good counsel are ready to seek for other counsel, that they may have some pretext to justify their folly.

*{that we may answer this people who have spoken to me?}* He supposed they would satisfy his humor more than the old men had done.

*{saying, Make the yoke which thy father did put upon us lighter}* He leaves out this promise of the people, *and we will serve thee*, v. 4. to make their demands seem the more unreasonable.

**V. 10.** *{And the young men that were grown up with him spake unto him, saying, Thus shalt thou speak unto this people that spake unto thee, saying}* They are as ready and bold to put an answer into his mouth as if they had been the most prudent counselors that could be.

*{Thy father made our yoke heavy, but make thou it lighter unto us}* See v. 4.

*{thus shalt thou say unto them, My little finger shall be thicker than my father’s loins}* A proverbial and hyperbolical speech. By *little* (for the word *finger* is not in the Hebrew, but yet fitly supplied) is meant the least and weakest member of the body; by *loins* the side, or thigh, or back, which are the greatest and strongest parts. And hereby he implies that his power and courage far surpassed his father's. It is a speech full of great pride, boldness, folly, and falsehood. He alludes to his father's youth when the crown was first set on his head, ch. 3.7. 1 Chron. 22.5, and to his own man-age, ch. 14.21.

**V. 11.** *{And now whereas my father did lade you with an heavy yoke}* See v. 4.

*{I will add to your yoke}* *q. d.* I will exact more taxes of you, put you to harder services, and every way more burden you than ever my father did. O foolish king that followed such counsels! Was this the way to gain a mutinous people? Or rather was it not a course to have alienated a willing people?

*{my father hath chastised you with whips: but I will chastise you with scorpions}* This is another proverbial speech. Whips use to be made of hard cord knotted, and soon fetch blood, and tear skin and flesh. Scorpions are venomous creatures with stings in their tails, wherewith they that are stung are intolerably tortured, ofttimes to death. There are also certain scourges of wire, with knobs of lead and sharp hooks or pricks at the ends of them, which are called *scorpions*. Some write of kinds of briars and thorns called *scorpions*. All these senses tend to one and the same end, that Rehoboam would handle his subjects much more roughly and hardly than his father had done.

**V. 12.** *{So Jeroboam and all the people came to Rehoboam the third day, as the king had appointed}* Their longing desire to know Rehoboam's mind and his affection to them, makes them keep touch, and not fail in their attending upon him at the time set by himself.

*{saying, Come to me again the third day}* See v. 5.

**V. 13.** *{And the king answered the people roughly}* Heb. *hardly*. Certainly he neither foresaw nor feared what the issue of exasperating a tumultuous people might be.

*{and forsook the old men’s counsel that they gave him}* This does much aggravate his folly, and shows that willfulness and obstinacy was added thereto. So Jer. 43.4.

**V. 14.** *{And spake to them after the counsel of the young men, saying}* Hearkening to flattering and unadvised counselors is the ready way to ruin, 2 Chron. 22.4, 5.

*{My father made your yoke heavy, and I will add to your yoke: my father also chastised you with whips, but I will chastise you with scorpions}* Because the counsel of the young men pleased him, though it were pernicious, yet he holds close to the very words thereof, v. 11.

**V. 15.** *{wherefore the king hearkened not unto the people}* He regarded not their motion, v. 4, but utterly rejected their desire of having their heavy burdens made lighter.

*{for the cause}* Heb. *circuit, *or,* turning about*. i. e. The alteration of the state, and turning the greatest part of the kingdom to another. This has relation to that which followed v. 16.

*{was from the LORD}* This passage or event was ordained and ordered from the Lord: I. In that the Lord withheld his spirit of wisdom from Rehoboam, whereby he might have discerned what in probability would be the issue, Deut. 29.4. II. In that he gave him over to hearken to rash and evil counselors, as ch. 22.23. III. In that he alienated the hearts of the people from him, as Judg. 9.23. This God did to punish Solomon's idolatry, ch. 11.11, and to discover Rehoboam's folly, insolency, and cruelty, 2 Chron. 32.31. Whatsoever in such cases is attributed to God, is to be taken as an act of justice done by the supreme and righteous judge: thus Amos 3.6. See 2 Chron. 25.20.

*{that he might perform}* God had foretold as much ch. 11.11, and to show his truth, he now brings to pass what he threatened. See 1 Sam. 15.29.

*{his saying which the LORD spake}* The word which God's ministers deliver from God, is God's word, ch. 16.7, 12.

*{by Ahijah the Shilonite unto Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* ch. 11.29, etc. This shows that God in accomplishing his word has respect unto his servants, that they might not be found false witnesses, 2 King. 9.36. & 10.10.

**V. 16.** *{So when all Israel saw that the king hearkened not to them, the people answered the king, saying}* The king's over-sharp and rigorous answer gave such a general distaste, as they would no longer stay to advise further thereupon, but unanimously manifest a present and peremptory resolution.

*{what portion}* Or, *what jurisdiction, or command*. Saul propounds such a question 1 Sam. 22.7.

*{have we in David}* In David's lineage or posterity. What good can we expect from David's stock? Or, what right has it to us?

*{neither have we inheritance}* i. e. fields, and vineyards, as 1 Sam. 22.7, or, as we say, lands and tenements.

*{in the son of Jesse}* This is a phrase of contempt: for Jesse was but a private man. But the Holy Ghost uses this title *Jesse* with much honor, 1 Chron. 12.18. 2 Sam. 23.1. Psal. 72.20. Act. 13.22, and that because he was the father of David, and in that respect the stem or root out of which Christ sprouted, Isa. 11.1, 10. The people therefore here manifested great impiety and ingratitude against God, who raised David to do so much good for Israel: they learned the phrase of that king whom God rejected, 1 Sam. 22.7, and of an arch-rebel, 2 Sam. 20.1.

*{to your tents, O Israel}* This is a phrase of apostasy from their former government. They imply they would no longer depend on David's stock, but every one look to his own house, v. 24. Because of old they dwelt in tents, this phrase continued to be in use among them, Deut. 5.30. Josh. 22.4. See v. 24.

*{now see to thine own house, David}* They direct their speech to Rehoboam, and call him David, because he descended from him; and by *his own house* they mean Judah, that tribe whereof David was. Hereby it appears that the heads of the tribe of Judah did not conspire with them, nor consent to their treason.

*{So Israel}* He means the ten tribes. See v. 20.

*{departed}* With a resolution never to subject themselves to Rehoboam, or to any of his posterity.

*{unto their tents}* To their own houses or homes.

**V. 17.** *{But as for the children of Israel}* *Israel* is here put for *Judah*, as 2 Chron. 12.1. Or that part of Simeon which was within the tribe of Judah is here meant: or such in the ten tribes as set their hearts to seek the Lord, 2 Chron. 11.16.

*{which dwelt in the cities of Judah}* And Benjamin: see ch. 11.13.

*{Rehoboam reigned over them}* For all they voluntarily subjected themselves to him, because the temple of the Lord was within his jurisdiction.

**V. 18.** *{Then king Rehoboam sent Adoram}* Some take this man to be that Adoniram mentioned ch. 4.6. & 5.14, because of the same office here and there mentioned. If this were the same, he was an ancient man and had been in office most part of Solomon's reign, and so more fit to appease the people, being one of the old men, v. 6. When by woeful event (which is the fool's teacher) Rehoboam discerned his folly, he sought redress, but too late.

*{who was over the tribute}* By virtue of this office Adoram had the more acquaintance with the people, and might the better assure them they should be eased in their tribute, and thereby pacify them. But Rehoboam's mention of adding to their yoke, i. e. of increasing their taxes, made them detest him that was over the tribute.

*{and all Israel stoned him with stones, that he died}* This they did, without any legal proceeding, tumultuously, and in a rage; as Acts 7.57, 58.

*{therefore king Rehoboam made speed}* Heb. *strengthened himself*.

*{to get him up to his chariot}* For by this evidence of the people's rage he saw there was great cause for him to look to himself.

*{to flee to Jerusalem}* This city was in that tribe which stuck to him: and he could have no safe abiding in any of the cities of Israel. Of *Jerusalem* see ch. 3.15.

**V. 19.** *{So Israel rebelled}* Or, *fell away, *or,* dealt treacherously*. The Hebrew word is attributed to such as transgress a covenant, Ho. 8.1. 2 King. 1.1. & 3.4, 5.

*{against the house of David}* i. e. David's royal posterity. Though this were decreed by God, and by God's prophet revealed beforehand, yet because they did it not on that ground, but upon a rebellious disposition, their falling off is justly styled *rebellion*. So is this word used 2 King. 1.1.

*{unto this day}* See ch. 9.13.

**V. 20.** *{And it came to pass when all Israel}* *All Israel* is here to be taken as v. 1.

*{heard that Jeroboam was come again, that they sent and called him unto the congregation}* It is said v. 3 that *they sent and called Jeroboam*; how then are they said to send for him again? R. 1. This may have relation to that time, or that time to this; or, 2. the elders might send for him then, and now all the congregation; 3. Jeroboam, after he saw fair way made for him to be king, might withdraw himself, as Saul did, 1 Sam. 10.21, but when Rehoboam was actually cast off, he might return again, and so the people send for him the second time.

*{and made him king over all Israel}* They actually chose him, openly proclaimed him, and solemnly settled him on the throne.

*{there was none that followed the house of David but the tribe of Judah only}* See ch. 11.13.

**V. 21.** *{And when Rehoboam was come to Jerusalem}* This was the securest place that he had.

*{he assembled all the house of Judah, with the tribe of Benjamin}* Herein is verified that that was promised ch. 11.13. Under *all the house of Judah* is comprised not only that tribe, but also all that took part with them.

*{an hundred and fourscore thousand chosen men, which were warriors}* Here is a great army suddenly raised out of two tribes; yet his son raised a far greater, 2 Chron. 13.3.

*{to fight against the house of Israel}* He sought by human means to recover what by his father's idolatry and his own folly was forfeited and lost. But his purpose was in vain.

*{to bring the kingdom again to Rehoboam the son of Solomon}* A Hebraism like that which is noted ch. 8.1.

**V. 22.** *{But the word of God came}* 2 Chr. 11.2. The message of true prophets was God's own word.

*{unto Shemaiah the man of God, saying}* The *man of God* is the title of a true prophet, ch. 13.1. 2 Chr. 15.1, 8. By it this prophet is distinguished from two false prophets, Shemaiah the Nehelamite, Jer. 29.24, and Shemaiah the son of Delaiah, Neh. 6.10.

**V. 23.** *{Speak unto Rehoboam the son of Solomon king of Judah}* God's word must be delivered even unto kings.

*{and unto all the house of Judah and Benjamin}* See v. 20.

*{and to the remnant of the people, saying}* Under this word *remnant* are comprised such of the tribe of Simeon as dwelt within the tribe of Judah, together with the priests, Levites, and many of other tribes that came into Rehoboam's jurisdiction, 2 Chr. 11.13, 16. See ch. 11.13.

**V. 24.** *{Thus saith the LORD}* A prophet's usual preface, showing his warrant to gain the better attention.

*{Ye shall not go up, nor fight against your brethren the children of Israel}* Their near relation, hinted in this word *brethren*, is urged as a ground of restraint, to keep them from over-much forwardness to fight against the Israelites. So Deut. 2.4, 8. But it did not make such a war simply unlawful. See 2 Chr. 13.15. & 28.8. Judg. 20.28.

*{return every man to his house}* i. e. Be quiet, and follow every one his own affairs. What was called *tents* v. 16, here is titled *house*.

*{for this thing is from me}* See v. 15.

*{They hearkened therefore to the word of the LORD}* The mind of the Lord being so plainly made known to them, they submitted themselves thereto, though it were against their own mind. See 2 Chr. 25.10. & 28.10.
