*{and returned to depart}* A Hebraism, implying that they were come from their own houses, and assembled in arms, and prepared to fight.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* Another Hebraism in redundancy. It shows God's word much prevailed with them, and was the only cause that moved them to desist.

**V. 25.** *{Then Jeroboam built Shechem in mount Ephraim, and dwelt therein}* Of *Shechem* see v. 1. It was built before, but now he made it a kind of new city, a royal city. See ch. 9.15.

*{and went out from thence}* To the other side of Jordan, where Penuel was.

*{and built Penuel}* This was the place where Jacob wrestled with the angel, Gen. 32.30. Here had been a strong tower, long before beat down by Gideon, Judg. 8.9, 17. In Shechem Jeroboam made a palace for himself to dwell in: in Penuel he placed a garrison. And as Jeroboam fortified places for the strength of his kingdom, so did Rehoboam for his, 2 Chr. 11.5, etc.

**V. 26.** *{And Jeroboam said in his heart}* He thought and imagined such things as follow. See Psal. 14.1. That which he projects as a means to preserve his kingdom, proved the ruin thereof, ch. 15.30.

*{Now shall the kingdom return to the house of David}* He thought the children of Israel within his kingdom, if some course were not taken to prevent it, might revolt from him and own David's heir for their king, as is more plainly expressed v. 27. Behold his small or no faith, his high misconfidence in God's promise; for he had no care of God's charge ch. 11.37, 38.

**V. 27.** *{If this people go up to do sacrifice in the house of the LORD at Jerusalem}* To do sacrifice was the principal and proper service of the temple, Deut. 12.6, and is here put for the whole worship of God. This usurper feared that piety to God might draw away the people's heart from him, and thereupon he draws them to idolatry. He might also fear that the magnificence of the temple, and the glorious things appertaining thereunto, yea, and the priests making known God's promise to David's house, would cause them to join with the men of Judah.

*{then shall the heart of this people turn again unto their lord}* He was not ignorant that God's ordinances might be a means to turn their hearts to obedience and loyalty, especially if the priests and Levites, the ministers thereto, should press them upon the consciences of those that came to the temple, as he had cause to think they would.

*{even unto Rehoboam king of Judah}* Here he acknowledges that Rehoboam, against whom he and Israel had rebelled, was their lord, and so is a witness against himself and those that took part with him.

*{and they shall kill me}* Usurpers live in continual fear, and suspect the worst.

*{and go again to Rehoboam king of Judah}* He makes himself the principal cause of the rebellion, and concludes that if he were taken away, all might be brought to be subject again to their proper king.

**V. 28.** *{Whereupon the king took counsel}* Wicked men in their impious acts can take advice, and use more heads than their own.

*{and made two calves}* In imitation of those idolaters Exod. 32.4, who themselves imitated the Egyptians, among whom they had long lived. Yea, and Jeroboam himself having lately lived in Egypt ch. 11.40, might thereby be induced to make his idols like unto those of the Egyptians.

*{of gold}* Idolaters can spare their most precious metals to make their idols of, Dan. 3.1, supposing to honor their gods thereby. Concerning which folly see Isa. 46.6. Jer. 10.4, 8. Hos. 8.4. Hab. 2.19. Acts 17.29. These idols were not gilded over, but were of massy gold, molten, and cast into the fashion of calves, ch. 14.9. 2 King. 17.16. Neh. 9.18.

*{and said unto them, It is too much for you to go up to Jerusalem}* He pretends the people's accommodation and ease, as a cloak to cover his own diffidence and cursed policy. He would herein show himself more prudent for the people's good, and more merciful than God.

*{behold thy gods O Israel}* He means a representation of the God of Israel: and he takes the phrase from Exod. 32.4.

*{which brought thee up out of the land of Egypt}* Hereby he would imply that he drew them to worship no other God than the true God, the God of their fathers, that chose them to be his people, and redeemed them from bondage. By this description does the Lord God of Israel oft set forth himself, as Exod. 20.2. Lev. 19.36. Ezek. 20.5, 6. Amos 2.10. Mic. 6.4.

**V. 29.** *{And he set the one in Bethel}* A city of Benjamin, in the uttermost coast of Israel on the south, Josh. 18.22. It signifies *the house of God*, because there God appeared to Jacob, Gen. 28.11, 19, and there they worshiped God, Gen. 12.8. & 35.7. But by reason of Jeroboam's impiety, the prophet calls it *Beth-aven*, Hos. 10.5. The inhabitants of this city severed themselves from others of their tribe, and revolted to Jeroboam; and thereby he had the opportunity to set up one of his calves there. It was recovered, together with divers others even in Jeroboam's time, 2 Chr. 13.19.

*{and the other put he in Dan}* A city in the utmost coast of Israel on the north, Josh. 19.47. See on Judg. 18.29.

**V. 30.** *{And this thing became a sin}* A most heinous sin, most dishonorable to God, and which caused such a divorce betwixt him and his people, being a spiritual adultery, Jer. 13.27. Ezek. 23.43. Hos. 2.2, a sin whereby he drew all Israel from God, and made them to sin, 2 King. 17.21. This therefore is put into Jeroboam's title, 1 King. 15.31. See on ch. 13.34. & on Gen. 36.43. Many circumstances much aggravate this sin. I. It was against that charge which God had most expressly, frequently and earnestly inculcated, Exod. 20.4, 23. & 32.7, etc. II. His image was one of the basest, Psal. 106.20. III. One image contented him not, v. 29, 32. IV. He performed such service to devils as was due to God himself, 2 Chr. 11.15. V. He ordained divine worship to be performed in another place, v. 29, at another time, v. 32, after another manner, v. 28, and by other priests, v. 31, than God had appointed. VI. He himself usurped the priest's office, v. 33.

*{For the people went to worship}* By offering sacrifices, v. 32.

*{before the one, even unto Dan}* Dan was more remote from sundry inland towns and cities of Israel than the temple; yet thither did the idolatrous Israelites go, rather than to the house of God. Men can take more pains to do evil than to do good. To make up the full sense of this verse, thus much may be added, *and to worship before the other they went unto Bethel*.

**V. 31.** *{And he made an house of high places}* Jeroboam made a fair temple on a high hill, wherein were many altars and other idolatrous monuments, which use to be in high places; so as this temple was instead of many high places. Mention is made of *houses* in the plural number ch. 13.32. In other places the word *house* is left out, and only *high places* mentioned, as ch. 13.32. 2 King. 17.32. 2 Chr. 11.15. He had otherwhere places for idolatry besides Dan and Bethel, ch. 13.32.

*{and made priests of the lowest of the people}* i. e. the meanest sort, ch. 13.33. These were fit enough for services of man's invention, and for such gods. Yet this proclaims his impious and profane mind; for he professed to serve the true God.

*{which were not of the sons of Levi}* He feared lest the Levites, being instructed in the law, should dissuade the people from his idols, and persuade them to go to the temple, v. 27. His practice was against the express law, Num. 3.6, 10.

**V. 32.** *{And Jeroboam ordained a feast}* The Israelites from their coming out of Egypt, had been accustomed to solemn and sacred feasts: and people use to be much taken with feasts.

*{in the eighth month, on the fifteenth day of the month}* This month was a time of his own invention: none of the Lord's feasts were therein, v. 33. He might fear lest the people might be moved to go up to Jerusalem, if his feast had been then when the Lord commanded all the males to go up to his house, Deut. 16.16.

*{like unto the feast that is in Judah}* This is meant of such solemnity as was used in the feasts at Jerusalem. Or in particular it may have reference to the feast of Tabernacles, at which time Solomon dedicated the temple, ch. 8.65.

*{and he offered upon the altar}* Or, *went up unto the altar*, namely, to that which was in Dan, v. 29.

*{(so did he in Bethel)}* These were the two places where he set his idols, v. 29.

*{sacrificing}* Or, *to sacrifice*.

*{unto the calves that he had made}* He does that to his idols which was due only to the true God.

*{and he placed in Bethel}* So also did he in Dan.

*{the priests of the high places which he had made}* Of *high places* see ch. 3.4. This phrase *Priests of the high places*, distinguishes them from the Lord's priests.

**V. 33.** *{So he offered upon the altar}* Or, *went up to the altar*.

*{which he had made in Bethel}* Jeroboam's offerings at Bethel are repeated, to show the time and occasion of the prophet's reproving him in the next chapter.

*{the fifteenth day of the eighth month}* See v. 32.

*{even in the month which he had devised of his own heart}* For God had nowhere enjoined it.

*{and ordained a feast unto the children of Israel}* See v. 32.

*{and he offered upon the altar}* Jeroboam most presumptuously, as Uzziah 2 Chron. 26.16, intruded himself into the priest's office, the rather to work in his people's mind a high esteem of his idols.

*{and burnt incense}* Heb. *to burn incense*. This was another part of the priest's function, Lev. 16.12, 13.

# CHAPTER 13

**V. 1.** *{And behold}* This preface gives us to understand that a remarkable history follows.

*{there came a man of God}* See on 2 Pet. 2.21. This man of God is said to be a prophet, v. 18, one inspired and sent by God: such an one as Shemaiah, ch. 12.22. He had his commission from God, both for the message itself, and for confirmation thereof with miracles. Seeing the Holy Ghost has not expressed his name, it is too great curiosity to search after it. It is not probable that he should be any of them who are mentioned 2 Chron. 9.29. & 12.15. For some of them died before this *man of God*, the other lived after him. He died the same day wherein he delivered his message. Tertullian says this *man of God* was Shemaiah, l. *De jejunio*, c. 16.

*{out of Judah}* To wit, one of them that detesting Jeroboam's idolatry had fled to Judah, 2 Chron. 11.16. By sending one from Judah, God showed that his true worship was there to be found.

*{by the word of the LORD}* By God's appointment and command. Or, *with the word of the Lord*.

*{unto Bethel}* At that time there was a great assembly and solemnity, chap. 12.33. Though it were a place of idolaters, yet God sends a prophet to reclaim them, before he would utterly cast them off; or, in case they were obstinate, to aggravate their sin, and justify God's proceedings against them. See chap. 16.1. The ten tribes even after their revolt, had many prophets, ch. 18.4. 2 King. 17.13.

*{and Jeroboam stood by the altar to burn incense}* Or, *to offer, namely with his own hand*, as chap. 12.33. Of *incense* see 1 King. 9.25.

**V. 2.** *{And he cried}* With earnestness and boldness the man of God declared his message, Isa. 58.1.

*{against the altar}* *Altar* is here (synecdochically) put for all their idolatrous service; for their chief worship and service was upon and about the altar. In this extent is this word oft used, as chap. 19.10. 2 Kings 18.22. Isa. 19.19. 1 Cor. 9.13.

*{in the word of the LORD}* Inventing nothing of himself.

*{and said, O altar, altar}* This is an elegant and emphatical expression of his message: as Isa. 29.1. Thus he orders his speech, to astonish the king and people the more, and to make his word be the better heeded. So Jer. 22.29.

*{thus saith the LORD}* See ch. 12.24.

*{Behold}* A most remarkable prophecy is here recorded. For though the accomplishment was three hundred and thirty years after this, yet all particular circumstances are as punctually set down, as if it had been a narration of things past rather than a prediction of things to come. Read 2 King. 22.1, 2. & 23.15, 16, 17.

*{a child shall be born unto the house of David}* Because Jeroboam had revolted from the house of David, God will make one of that house an executioner of his justice on Jeroboam and his successors. Hereby the judgment is aggravated.

*{Josiah by name}* 2 King. 23.16. This shows that particular matters are foreordained and after ordered by God. See the like Isa. 44.28. & 45.1.

*{and upon thee}* An elegant figure called *Prosopopœia*, whereby he speaks to the senseless altar as if it had understanding, to make man, who has understanding, the more to heed it.

*{shall he offer the priests}* Priests are named to aggravate the terror. He means by the priests so much as was remaining of their bodies in Josiah's time, which was their bones.

*{of the high places}* See ch. 3.4.

*{that burn incense}* See ch. 11.8.

*{upon thee}* The place of sin was the place of judgment, Luk. 13.1. This prophecy compared with the accomplishment thereof 2 King. 23.16, shows that priests had a common place of burial together.

*{and men’s bones shall be burnt upon thee}* This sets out the extent of the judgment: for other men's bones are here meant besides the priests. And this is added to terrify others, as well as the priests. The idolaters, priests, and others, who offered sacrifices of beasts to their idols, shall themselves be made sacrifices: and as they turned God into the image of beasts, so they were to be made as sacrificed beasts. Thus judgment answered the sin.

**V. 3.** *{And he gave a sign}* To assure them that heard him of the truth of his prophecy, Jer. 28.9. See Joh. 4.48. This he did the rather, because of the long time that was to pass betwixt this prediction and the execution thereof.

*{the same day}* Present signs immediately following upon the word spoken make the deeper impression.

*{saying, This is the sign which the LORD hath spoken}* In that the Lord commanded him to threaten the sign before it fell out, the Lord is said to *speak the sign*. Predictions of things by men sent from God show that they fall not out by chance, nor any other way, but by the Lord, Isa. 48.5.

*{Behold, the altar shall be rent}* Presently, of itself, it shall cleave asunder. This was a fit sign to prefigure the abolishing of their idolatry; as was the rending of the veil, Matth. 27.51, to foreshow the ending of the legal services.

*{and the ashes that are upon it shall be poured out}* They shall with such violence fall out as water poured down.

**V. 4.** *{And it came to pass, when king Jeroboam heard the saying of the man of God}* That which should have abated his passion, did enrage it, namely *the saying of a man of God*. See v. 1.

*{which had cried against the altar in Bethel}* See v. 1, 2.

*{that he put forth his hand}* This he did in anger to have apprehended him; or at least to show his eagerness to have the standers by to do it.

*{from the altar}* Where he was burning incense, v. 1.

*{saying, Lay hold on him}* The mischief which tyrants cannot themselves effect, they will have others to do for them.

*{And his hand which he put forth against him dried up}* The flesh withered and sinews shrunk, and that without any external cause.

*{so that he could not pull it in again to him}* The use of sinews and joints, which are instruments of motion, was clean taken away. God can restrain the rage of the fiercest, so as they shall not be able to hurt his servants.

**V. 5.** *{The altar also was rent, and the ashes poured out from the altar}* An event answerable to the threatening, ver. 3. So chap. 14.12, 18.

*{according to the sign which the man of God had given}* God verified his word delivered by his servant. See v. 26.

*{by the word of the LORD}* See ver. 4. Here are two signs, to make the deeper impression in the people's hearts; 1. the drying up of the king's hand, 2. the rending of the altar. The former being on the king himself made the latter to be the more heeded. Yea, a third is added v. 6, namely, the restoring of the king's hand. Thus they had *line upon line, line upon line*, as Isa. 28.10. Whereby grace and indulgency were manifested, and their incredulity and obstinacy aggravated.

**V. 6.** *{And the king answered}* This has relation to God's speaking in and by the signs, v. 4, 5. Or, the word *answered* may simply be taken for *spake*: so it is translated Deut. 26.5. Job 3.2. Yea, it is oft used as a pleonasm, especially when the word *said* is joined with it, as here, & 2 Chron. 29.31. & 34.15. Mar. 11.14.

*{and said unto the man of God, Entreat now the face of the LORD}* This word *face* is attributed to the Lord after the manner of man. The face manifests either displeasure and anger, or good liking and favor. To *entreat the face* of one that is offended is earnestly to desire a change of his countenance, that his anger may be turned into favor, Prov. 19.6. Jeroboam's meaning then is, that the prophet would beseech the Lord to turn away his anger, and then pray for his favor. This phrase is used Exod. 32.11, 12. 2 Chron. 33.12.

*{thy God}* He adds this in opposition to his own idols. He had no heart to acknowledge the Lord to be his God. See Jon. 1.6. Dan. 6.20. Exod. 10.17.

*{and pray for me, that my hand may be restored me again}* Judgments so work even on idolaters as they are moved to seek redress of the Lord.

*{And the man of God besought the LORD}* Heb. *the face of the Lord, as before*. This he did in Jeroboam's idolatrous temple. For having at this time just cause to be there, he might there lawfully pray. Men of God are soon brought to pray even for persecutors, Ex. 8.8, 9.

*{and the king’s hand was restored again}* Righteous men's prayers avail much, Jam. 5.16, even for the wicked, Exod. 8.12, 13. Gen. 20.7.

*{and became as it was before}* God can perfectly cure the wounds he makes, Hos. 6.1.

**V. 7.** *{And the king said to the man of God, Come home to me}* Wonders work on the stoutest, and mollify the hardest hearts, Exod. 8.8, especially such wonders as bring good to men, Acts 28.8, 9, 10.

*{and refresh thyself}* Heb. *strengthen*. For eating and drinking repair a man's strength, which is weakened by want thereof, Gen. 18.5. 1 Sam. 30.12.

*{and I will give thee a reward}* This he offers in gratitude for the benefit he received by his prayers, as 2 King. 5.15. By the light of nature men discern the equity of gratefulness.

**V. 9.** *{And the man of God said unto the king, If thou wilt give me half thine house, I will not go in with thee}* This phrase *half thine house*, is a proverbial speech, Esth. 5.3. Mar. 6.23, like to that, Numb. 22.18. His meaning is, that by no reward at all (were it as great as that which the devil offered to Christ, Mat. 4.8, 9) he would be induced to abide with the king.

*{neither will I eat bread nor drink water}* *Bread* and *water* are synecdochically put for all sustenance, Isa. 3.1.

*{in this place}* i. e. the city Bethel. For he denied, on this ground, to eat in the old prophet's house, v. 16.

**V. 9.** *{For so was it charged me by the word of the LORD, saying}* God's charge was more to him than refreshing, or reward. He *esteemed the words of Gods mouth more then his necessary food*, Job 23.12.

*{Eat no bread nor drink water}* God would have him show his detestation of idolatry by avoiding all communion with idolaters, Rom. 16.17. 2 Joh. 10.

*{nor turn again by the same way that thou camest}* For so to do might make show of some shame, or fear, or failing of his purpose. In such a sense this phrase is used 2 Kings 19.28.

**V. 10.** *{So he went another way, and returned not by the way that he came}* Herein he did as became a man of God; though afterwards he failed, through too much credulity, against God's express word, v. 19.

*{to Bethel}* See v. 1. & ch. 12.29.

**V. 11.** *{Now there dwelt an old prophet}* He is called *a prophet*, because he assumed that function, as ch. 18.20, and because God used his ministry, v. 20, as he used Balaam's, Numb. 23.5. And this title is oft given to false prophets, Deut. 13.1. Jer. 2.8. Ezek. 13.2. He is said to be *an old prophet* in regard of his age, being an old man, and of his office, having been many years a prophet, yet not very faithful, v. 18. There are different opinions about him, whether he were a true or a false prophet. There is nothing recorded of him, which might not be done or spoken by a false prophet: Balaam went as far as he did: but there are many things recorded of him which cannot well become a true prophet.

*{in Bethel}* There he had his habitation; but at that time he came from Samaria, 2 King. 23.18. Or, he had formerly dwelt in Samaria, but now in Bethel.

*{and his sons}* Heb. *his son*. First one son might tell the news, then other of his sons confirm the same. For in the end of this verse it is said, *they told*. Or, by a synecdoche the singular number may be put for the plural.

*{came and told him all the works that the man of God had done}* i. e. the miracles before mentioned. They wrought great astonishment in the beholders; and that moved these sons to relate them to their father.

*{that day in Bethel}* This shows that the sons of this prophet were present at the idolatrous sacrifices.

*{the words which he had spoken unto the king}* Those threatenings which he had denounced against that place.

*{them they told also to their father}* They concealed nothing from him.

**V. 12.** *{And their father said unto them, what way went he?}* The judgments threatened, and miracles accompanying the same, made that old prophet inquisitive after the man of God.

*{for his sons had seen what way the man of God went}* This is brought in as a reason of the old prophet's inquiry. For his sons having observed which way the man of God went, told their father. This they might well do, because the man of God made no stay, but immediately went away.

*{which came from Judah}* See v. 1.

**V. 13.** *{And he said unto his sons}* Fathers have power to require service of their children, Gen. 30.35. Exod. 2.16.

*{Saddle me the ass}* Asses in those countries were used as horses are now with us, Numb. 22.21.

*{So they saddled him the ass}* Children must be ready to do service to their parents, Exod. 20.12.

*{and he rode thereon}* He was old, and knew not how far he might go before he overtook the man of God, and therefore used this help.

**V. 14.** *{And went after the man of God, and found him sitting under an oak}* Weary and hungry; as Christ, Joh. 4.6.

*{and he said unto him, Art thou the man of God that camest from Judah?}* Though the old prophet by his sons' relation might suppose that he whom he found under the oak was the man whom he sought, yet he propounds this question to him, to be more assured thereof.

*{And he said, I am}* The man of God is not ashamed to profess who he was.

**V. 15.** *{Then he said unto him, Come home with me, and eat bread}* This might be a point of courtesy. But it is probable that his sons had told him how the man of God had refused to accept the king's invitation, and withal the reason of his refusal: which if so, he could not mean any good by this invitation. His answer v. 18 shows, that he had heard how God had forbid him to eat in that place.

**V. 16.** *{And he said, I may not return with thee, nor go in with thee}* Yet he remains resolute in keeping God's charge, v. 9.

*{neither will I eat bread, nor drink water with thee}* He would not make needful refreshing of his body to be a dispensation for him against God's express command.

*{in this place}* i. e. Bethel, as v. 8.

**V. 17.** *{For it was said}* Heb. *a word was*.

*{to me by the word of the LORD, Thou shalt eat no bread, &c.}* This is a faithful repetition of the charge, v. 9.

**V. 18.** *{He said unto him, I am a prophet also}* Thus he was in his own esteem, and in the esteem of the idolaters among whom he lived. So Balaam, 2 Pet. 2.16.

*{as thou art}* Herein he speaks too much of himself.

*{and an angel spake unto me by the word of the LORD, saying}* This he pretends, because God was wont to declare his mind to men by angels, Numb. 22.35.

*{Bring him back with thee into thine house}* Hereby he would intimate that the man of God had sufficiently obeyed God's charge in refusing the king's invitation, and going so far another way as he had done.

*{that he may eat bread and drink water}* This was a fair bait to a man hungry and thirsty; but a dangerous hook lay covered under it.

*{but he lied unto him}* This was not the part of a true prophet. By this lie, 1. he makes God to thwart himself, by forbidding and commanding the same thing; 2. he dishonors the ministry of angels, by making it a pretext for falsehood; 3. he disgraces the function of a prophet, whereof he professes himself to be; 4. he betrays the man of God.

**V. 19.** *{So he went back with him, and did eat, &c.}* All this was done contrary to the express charge he had received, v. 9, from which no angel should have drawn him, Gal. 1.8. His sudden yielding also to the old prophet's words much aggravated his sin. For being himself a prophet, he might have inquired of the Lord whether that which the old prophet suggested were true or no: at least he might have prayed unto the Lord for direction in such a suspicious case.

**V. 20.** *{And it came to pass as they sat at table}* God soon discovers both the falsehood of the one, and the over-much credulity of the other; and thereby takes away all comfort from the intended refreshment.

*{that the word of the LORD came unto the prophet that brought him back}* God can inspire wicked men, and use their ministry.

**V. 21.** *{And he cried}* Being by an inward divine rapture in a manner forced, though it were an evidence against his own former word, he plainly and audibly with vehemency declares God's mind.

*{unto the man of God that came from Judah}* See v. 1.

*{saying, Thus saith the LORD}* See ch. 12.24.

*{Forasmuch as thou hast disobeyed the mouth of the LORD, &c.}* Temptations alter not the nature of sin. Disobedience is laid to the charge of this man of God, though he were beguiled.

**V. 22.** *{But camest back, and hast eaten bread and drunk water in the place of which the LORD did say unto thee, Eat no bread, and drink no water}* His disobedience is here particularly exemplified, to convince him the more thoroughly. His sin was like Adam's, Gen. 2.17. & 3.6.

*{thy carcase shall not come unto the sepulchre of thy fathers}* You shall not die among your kindred, nor be buried with your progenitors. This was accounted a curse, 2 Chron. 21.20. Isa. 14.19, 20. Jer. 22.19, for people very much desired to be buried near their ancestors, Gen. 47.30. & 49.29. This judgment is aforehand denounced to bring him to repentance, and make him the more seriously prepare for death. The kind of judgment might give him to understand that he should be taken away in his journey before he came to his own country.

**V. 23.** *{And it came to pass after he had eaten bread, and after he had drunk}* God suffered him to refresh himself.

*{that he saddled for him the ass}* The old prophet caused the ass to be saddled, or he saddled it himself. He was now the more officious and courteous to the man of God, because he discerned how injurious he had been unto him by seducing him to do otherwise than God had charged him.

*{to wit, for the prophet whom he had brought back}* This is added to show distinctly to whom the courtesy was offered. But the old prophet himself did not accompany the man of God, lest thereby he might partake of the judgment.

**V. 24.** *{And when he was gone}* From the old prophet's house.

*{a lion}* One of the most ravenous and insatiable beasts that be. They oft do much mischief in those parts, chap. 20.36. Judg. 14.5. 1 Sam. 17.34. 2 Sam. 23.20. 2 King. 17.25.

*{met him by the way}* The event so answers the threatening before mentioned, as it apparently shows that it was ordered by God, ver. 26. See chap. 11.29. & 20.36. 2 King. 2.24.

*{and slew him}* Judgment begins at the house of God, 1 Pet. 4.17, and saints are oft made signs to the wicked, Ezek. 24.24. By this judgment inflicted on this man of God it was manifested that the judgment threatened against the idolaters would surely be executed.

*{and his carcase was cast in the way}* Hence it appears that the lion did not devour him. God would herein show a mixture of mercy with judgment. For, 1. by this means his body was buried, ver. 30, which was an outward blessing, ch. 14.13; 2. God gave hereby a visible evidence of his soul's salvation, as in the case of Aaron's two sons, who were destroyed with fire, and yet their bodies not consumed, nor their coats, Lev. 10.2, 3. Read 1 Cor. 11.32.

*{and the ass stood by it}* Lions use to devour asses: yet the Lord would here show how he can restrain them from that which most moves hunger after. This is more fully exemplified Dan. 6.22. See v. 29.

*{the lion also stood by the carcase}* He had time to have devoured both the man and the ass; but having done that for which the Lord sent him, he could do no more. Thus persecutors are permitted and restrained, Gal. 1.23. Job 38.11. Again, by the lion's standing by the carcass it was kept from being devoured by other beasts or fowls. Thus the destroyer proved a preserver, as Judg. 14.14.

**V. 25.** *{And behold men passed by, and saw the carcase cast in the way}* God would have many witnesses of so remarkable a matter.

*{and the lion standing by the carcase}* Lions use in the daytime to *lay themselves down in their dens*, Psal. 104.22, for there is in them a dread of men: but this lion neither fears the passengers going to and fro, nor is so fierce as to fly upon any of them, but stands still by the corpse. Surely this also was of the Lord.

*{and they came and told it in the city where the old prophet dwelt}* This did the more increase the respect thereof. The city here meant was Bethel, v. 11, where many idolaters were assembled together, ch. 12.33.

**V. 26.** *{And when the prophet that brought him back from the way, heard thereof}* This description shows that the old prophet mentioned v. 18 is here meant. Certainly he did wait for and hearken after the event, and thereupon quickly heard thereof.

*{he said, It is the man of God, who was disobedient unto the word of the LORD}* Though the man of God were deceived, yet is he not thereby excused. See v. 21.

*{therefore the LORD hath delivered him unto the lion}* God spares not his own children's disobedience. See v. 24.

*{which hath torn him}* Heb. *broken him*. The lion might by biting him break some of his bones.

*{and slain him according to the word of the LORD which he spake unto him}* This has reference to that judgment which the old prophet inspired by the Spirit of God denounced v. 21, 22. See v. 5.

**V. 27.** *{And he spake to his sons, saying, Saddle me the ass. And they saddled him}* See v. 13.

**V. 28.** *{And he went}* Conscience of former wrong made him the more careful of future kindness.

*{and found his carcase cast in the way}* If the several passages between the lion's killing the man of God and the prophet's finding it to be so be duly weighed, it will appear that the lion had time enough to have devoured the carcass: so as this must needs be an extraordinary and divine restraining of the lion.

*{and the ass and the lion standing by the carcase}* When the lion had done what God appointed he could do no more, neither to the dead corpse, nor to the living ass.

*{the lion had not eaten the carcase, nor torn the ass}* Heb. *broken*. See v. 24.

**V. 29.** *{And the prophet took up the carcase of the man of God}* Either the lion upon the prophet's approach went away, or the prophet observing how the lion was restrained, took courage and feared him not.

*{and laid it upon the ass, and brought it back}* Here we see one end why the ass was preserved, namely to be a means of carrying back the corpse.

*{and the old prophet came to the city to mourn}* So was the custom for such as were respected, ch. 14.13.

*{and to bury him}* By this we see that the man of God's carcass came not to the sepulcher of his fathers, v. 22. For that was in Judah, and he is here buried in Bethel. Yet decent burial was not denied him.

**V. 30.** *{And he laid his carcase in his own grave}* These relatives, *he, his own*, have reference to the old prophet, who, it seems, had prepared a grave for himself, as Joseph of Arimathea, Matth. 27.60.

*{and they}* The old prophet himself, his sons, and others in the city who took him to be a man of God.

*{mourned over him}* See v. 29. They might mourn, 1. for his death, that such a man of God should be so soon taken away; 2. for the manner of it, that it should be by such a judgment; 3. for the occasion of it, a false suggestion; 4. for the consequence of it, the execution of the judgment he had denounced.

*{saying, Alas my brother}* This might be the beginning of a funeral song. See Jer. 22.18. They call him brother, 1. as he was a man, of the same flesh coming from Adam; 2. as he was an Israelite of the same lineage descending from Jacob; 3. the old prophet might so call him in regard of his function; for the other was a man of God, a prophet.

**V. 31.** *{And it came to pass after he had buried him, that he spake to his sons}* He hoped his sons should survive him, and thereupon gives them this charge. It belongs as a duty to surviving children to take special care of their parents' burial, Gen. 25.9. & 35.29. & 50.7, 8.

*{saying, when I am dead, then bury me in the sepulchre wherein the man of God is buried}* He believing that whatsoever the man of God had foretold should be accomplished, thought his bones if known, might be spared: and therefore appoints his body to be there laid, and a superscription to be engraven on the tombstone to show whose bones lay there. And what in this kind he believed, answerably fell out, 2 King. 23.17.

*{lay my bones beside his bones}* Though their flesh might in time be dust, yet he knew their bones would last long: therefore he makes mention of bones, and the rather because they are expressly mentioned in the judgment, v. 2.

**V. 32.** *{For the saying which he cried by the word of the LORD against the altar in Bethel}* This has relation to the judgment denounced, v. 2.

*{and against all the houses of the high places}* See ch. 13.31.

*{which are in the cities of Samaria}* There was a very large mountain called *Samaria*, and there might be many cities thereon, or appertaining thereto. After this the chief city of Israel, where the king had his royal palace, was called *Samaria*, ch. 16.24. So as this word *Samaria* may here be used by anticipation. For this story might be published after that Samaria was built.

*{shall surely come to pass}* Heb. *being shall be*. The Hebrews imply much emphasis under the doubling of their words. See ch. 8.13. By this it appears that he believed the truth of what was foretold v. 1, 2.

**V. 33.** *{After this thing}* i. e. the passages set down before in this chapter. And this is brought in as an aggravation of Jeroboam's sin, in that he continued in his idolatrous courses notwithstanding that fair warning.

*{Jeroboam returned not from his evil way}* Nothing works upon a man resolved to do evil, especially if the leaving of that evil may seem to endanger a kingdom, as this did to Jeroboam, ch. 12.26, 27, 28.

*{but made again}* Heb. *returned and made*. This has reference to that show of repentance which he made, v. 6, 7.

*{of the lowest of the people priests of the high places}* See chap. 12.31.

*{whosoever would}* Offer himself to be a priest.

*{he consecrated him}* Heb. *filled his hand*. Exod. 28.41. The priests at their consecration laid their hands on the sacrifice, and in their hands took some part thereof, when it was cut asunder; together with bread and oil: thus were their hands filled, Exod. 29.9, 10, 23, 24. They also who brought gifts to the Lord, by such a filling of their hands are said to be consecrated, 1 Chr. 29.5. And they who executed the Lord's just revenge, Exod. 32.29. Idolaters, in imitation of the Lord's ordinances, were wont to fill their hands with some sacrifice or gifts, and thereby thought themselves sufficiently consecrated to their priestly function. Some apply this to the filling of Jeroboam's hands with gifts, as if every one that was made a priest first gave him a great bribe. See on Judg. 17.5.

*{and he became one of the priests of the high places}* The foresaid idolatrous course of consecrating priests seemed to them every way as sufficient as God's own ordinance.

**V. 34.** *{And this thing}* Jeroboam persisting in his idolatry.

*{became sin unto the house of Jeroboam}* An indelible sin, that could never be removed from his house, no nor from any of the kings or people of Israel after him, till they were all carried away captive out of their land. See on ch. 12.30.

*{even to cut it off, and to destroy it from off the face of the earth}* See this executed ch. 15.29, 30.

# CHAPTER 14

**V. 1.** *{At that time}* While Jeroboam persisted in his idolatrous courses. For it has relation to the two last verses of the former chapter rather than to the history going before them.

*{Abijah}* This was also the name of Rehoboam's son, who succeeded him in the throne, 2 Chr. 13.3.

*{the son of Jeroboam}* It is supposed he was his eldest son; heir to the crown: which aggravated the judgment.

*{fell sick}* God had before punished Jeroboam in his own person by drying up his hand, ch. 13.4. Here he punishes him by striking his dear son.

**V. 2.** *{And Jeroboam said to his wife}* He dared trust none else, lest the matter should be divulged. Besides, she might inquire as about her own child, and being disguised not to be known. If another had been sent, inquiry might have been made whose child it was.

*{Arise, I pray thee}* This is a word of quickening, Gen. 13.9. See ch. 11.40.

*{and disguise thyself}* Every way, in attire, attendance, speech, behavior.

*{that thou be not known to be the wife of Jeroboam}* Neither by Ahijah, nor by the people. If the prophet should know her, he might think he would not vouchsafe her an answer, unless such an one as Elisha did Jehoram 2 King. 3.13. If the people should know it, he might imagine they would go on all occasions to the true prophets and priests of the Lord, as he himself did.

*{and get thee to Shiloh}* See ch. 11.29.

*{Behold, there is Ahijah the prophet}* God suffers his true prophets to have their abode among idolaters for their greater conviction, ch. 17.1. & 22.8. 2 King. 5.3. Amos 7.10.

*{which told me that I should be king over this people}* Chap. 11.31. He believed him to be a true prophet; and that as he had foretold one thing which came to pass, so he could also foretell another.

**V. 3.** *{And take with thee}* Heb. *in thy hand*. 2 King. 5.5. It was usual among the Jews to carry presents to prophets. See 1 Sam. 9.7, 8. 2 King. 8.8. This was done not to bribe them, but to testify gratefulness to them.

*{ten loaves}* Loaves of bread.

*{and cracknels}* Or, *cakes*. The notation of the Hebrew word implies that they were round cakes full of holes.

*{and a cruse of honey}* Or, *a bottle*. These were not such presents as Naaman intended to Elisha, 2 King. 5.5, 15, but country commodities, to make Ahijah take her for some countrywoman.

*{and go to him}* He would have his wife herself go to the prophet.

*{he shall tell thee}* See v. 2.

*{what shall become}* Whether he shall recover or no, as 2 King. 8.9. He does not inquire after the cause of his son's sickness, nor after any remedy or means how he might be cured, but only after the event. His obstinate heart could hope for no favor from God.

*{of the child}* It appears v. 13 that he was of such age and discretion as he could discern between good and evil courses, and follow the one, and leave the other. The Hebrew word translated *child* is oft applied to such as we call *youth*, or *young men*, Exod. 24.5. Numb. 11.27. 1 Sam. 2.17.

**V. 4.** *{And Jeroboam’s wife did so}* Though she were a queen, yet desire and hope of her son's recovery made her ready to do what was required.

*{and arose}* She made no delay. See v. 2.

*{and went to Shiloh, and came to the house of Ahijah}* See v. 2.

*{but Ahijah could not see}* Prophets are sons of men, Ezek. 2.1, and are subject to human infirmities and casualties, Act. 14.15. Eccl. 9.2.

*{for his eyes were set by reason of his age}* Heb. *stood for hoariness*. This was by reason of the withering and shrinking the eye-strings or nerves. Ahijah was now a very old man: but a little before Jeroboam came to the kingdom, he seemed to be a strong man, ch. 11.30. The sickness therefore of Jeroboam's son was after he had reigned many years.

**V. 5.** *{And the LORD said unto Ahijah}* The secret contrivings of men are known to the Lord, and he can and will make them known when and to whom he pleases.

*{Behold}* That which God is pleased to make known is as true and sure as that which a man beholds with his eyes.

*{the wife of Jeroboam}* She is thus in particular named, to discover the folly of her disguising herself.

*{cometh to ask a thing of thee for her son}* Namely, what shall become of him, v. 3.

*{for he is sick}* Sicknesses make men inquisitive after the issue.

*{thus and thus shalt thou say unto her}* Even as is expressed v. 7, and God, who revealed that dissimulation, directed his servant how to frame his answer.

*{for it shall be when she cometh in, that she shall feign her self to be another woman}* This she did by disguising herself as v. 2.

**V. 6.** *{And it was so when Ahijah heard the sound of her feet}* i. e. her treading or trampling. This was the first evidence he had of her approach to his house.

*{as she came in at the door}* Namely the street-door, or first door of the house, before she entered the room where he was.

*{that he said, Come in thou wife of Jeroboam}* He named her, that she might know he well perceived her dissimulation, and also knew what would be the issue of her son's sickness.

*{why feignest thou thyself to be another?}* This interrogation is a sharp reproof. It implies, that they thought he knew what would become of their son, and that thereupon he might know who it was that came to him.

*{for I am sent to thee with heavy tidings}* Heb. *hard*. The tidings indeed were heavy and hard. For besides the death of Jeroboam's son, the loss of his kingdom, and the utter destruction of him and all his posterity was foretold. *I am sent*, says Ahijah. The same prophet that foretold his advancement, ch. 11.29, etc., foretells his ruin. For true prophets declare what God gives them in charge, ch. 22.14. Jer. 42.4.

**V. 7.** *{Go, tell Jeroboam}* Though she were his wife, yet she was to carry from the Lord this doleful message to him.

*{Thus saith the LORD God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15. & 12.24.

*{Forasmuch as I exalted thee}* God has an hand in ordering and turning to his own purposes the acts of wicked men; 1 Sam. 15.17. See ch. 12.24.

*{from among the people}* Jeroboam had been a subject, and one of the people, yea one of the meaner sort, ch. 11.28. So ch. 16.2.

*{and made thee prince}* See ch. 11.37.

*{over my people Israel}* Israel was God's people by covenant, Exod. 19.6. And when Jeroboam was first made prince, they were not such idolaters as afterwards: and after he had established his idolatry, many without question kept themselves pure, as in Ahab's time, ch. 19.18. Yea, though most had cast off the Lord, yet the Lord had not cast off them. Besides, they still retained the external notes of a church, as circumcision, the laws of Moses, etc., as testimonies that Jehovah was their God.

**V. 8.** *{And rent the kingdom away from the house of David, and gave it thee}* See ch. 11.31.

*{and yet thou hast not been as my servant, David}* David's pattern was set before Jeroboam when the kingdom was first promised him, and that as a condition to be observed for the establishing thereof, ch. 11.38; therefore his swerving from that pattern is here set down as a just ground of taking away the kingdom from him. For it could not be thought meet that the kingdom taken from David's house should be continued in his house that was worse than David's.

*{who kept my commandments}* See ch. 11.34.

*{and who followed me}* As a servant his master, and never forsook me.

*{with all his heart}* See ch. 2.4.

*{to do that only which was right in mine eyes}* This is to be taken in respect I. to the general course of David's life. See ch. 15.5. II. to his repentance for the particular sins he committed; in which respect God laid them not to his charge, 2 Sam. 12.13. Psal. 32.1, 2. III. to the worship of God; against which was the great sin here laid to Jeroboam's charge. In all these respects David may be said to have done *that only which was right in God's eyes*, approved by God, and accepted of him; being so indeed and in truth as they appeared to be. For God sees things as they are indeed.

**V. 9.** *{But hast done evil above all that were before thee}* i. e. all the kings. Saul made not such idols as the calves: though he were otherwise very wicked, yet he was not an idolater. David was the best of all kings. Solomon, though by his wives' instigation he permitted idolatry, yet made not all Israel to sin. Rehoboam kept not God's people from God's house. But all these things did Jeroboam, ch. 12.27, 28, 29, 30.

*{for thou hast gone and made thee other gods}* Though it may be said that he only made representations of God, yet such are accounted to be as gods.

*{and molten images}* See ch. 12.28.

*{to provoke me to anger}* To *provoke* is to stir up one that would be quiet, and make him do what otherwise he would not. Moses was a very meek man, Numb. 12.3, yet the Israelites by their murmurings are said to *provoke his spirit*, Psal. 106.33. God himself is said to be *provoked by his sons and daughters*, Deut. 32.19. Surely God is willing to show all favor to his children; and it is no small matter that stirs him up against them. Observingly read the Scripture, and you shall find words of terror added to this term of *provoking*; as *to provoke to anger*, Deut. 4.25, *to wrath*, Deut. 9.8, *to jealousy*. No sin more incenses God's anger than idolatry, v. 22. ch. 11.9. & 12.30. Deut. 32.21.

*{and hast cast me behind thy back}* i. e. you have scornfully rejected me as one not to be regarded, Ezek. 23.35. Neh. 9.26. Psal. 50.17, set idols before your face, and cast me behind your back. So Ezek. 8.16. Jer. 2.27. This must needs be a great despite.

**V. 10.** *{Therefore behold}* God's judgments are remarkable matters.

*{I will bring evil}* Jeroboam's sin had before been declared; the punishment thereof now follows. See ch. 15.29.

*{upon the house of Jeroboam}* i. e. the stock and all that belonged to Jeroboam, ch. 16.3.

*{and will cut off from Jeroboam him that pisseth against the wall}* An usual phrase, whereby an utter destruction of all that belong to a man is set forth, even of all that breathe, ch. 15.29. The child that can stand against a wall, or a dog that lifts up his leg against a wall, are said *to piss against a wall*. See on 1 Sam. 25.22.

*{and him that is shut up and left in Israel}* Or, *left out*. Another proverbial speech, whereby is meant whatsoever a man has at home or abroad, whether precious and much esteemed, as things which men use to shut up, or less esteemed, as things left without. Or it may be applied to such as have shut themselves up in strongholds, and such as are left in other places. All shall be destroyed, none escape, Deut. 32.36. See on 2 King. 14.26.

*{and will take away the remnant of the house of Jeroboam, as a man taketh away dung, till it be all gone}* A third proverbial phrase. Because dung is filthy and noisome, men use to rake every whit away. A very fit comparison, like to that 2 Kings 21.13. Isa. 14.23. Ezek. 26.4.

**V. 11.** *{Him that dieth of Jeroboam in the city, shall the dogs eat; and him that dieth in the field shall the fowls of the air eat}* This also is a proverbial speech, whereby is implied that no care at all should be had of their dead corpse, but they should be left to dogs and ravens. Dogs use to be in cities and towns, ravenous fowls in open fields, ch. 16.4. & 21.24. Jer. 15.3. See on Eccles. 6.3.

*{for the LORD hath spoken it}* This is added to ratify the certainty of the judgment.

**V. 12.** *{Arise thou therefore, get thee to thine own house}* It is not probable that she lay down or sat. The phrase then imports speed without delay. See v. 2, 4.

*{and when thy feet enter}* So soon as you come.

*{into the city}* Tirzah. It is probable by that which is expressed v. 17 that he means her entering into her own house. But whether the phrase be taken properly for her entrance into the city, or tropically for her entrance into the king's house in the city, is no great matter: both tend to the same end, to signify that she should not see her son alive. This surely could not be but a very sad message: for a mother cannot but much desire her son's life; and if that cannot be, then to be present at his departure, to close his eyes.

*{the child}* See v. 3.

*{shall die}* This was the reason why he would have her make no delay. This judgment was the beginning or first part of the heavy tidings, v. 6.

**V. 13.** *{And all Israel shall mourn for him}* Some in respect to their king, because he had lost a son; others in their love to the child, because there was goodness in him; and others for the loss that the kingdom had of him.

*{and bury him}* They who accompany one's corpse to the grave, as Luk. 7.12, may be said to bury him; and thus all Israel might bury Abijah, which they did for honor's sake. See ch. 2.31. & 13.30. See the contrary, v. 11.

*{for he only of Jeroboam shall come to the grave}* Jeroboam himself might be buried, as may be gathered out of v. 20. But Ahijah speaks of such as appertained to him.

*{because in him there is found some good thing}* Some seeds of true piety, and fear of God.

*{toward the LORD God of Israel}* i. e. in regard of the worship of God. So as this young man might be such an one as the seven thousand are said to be ch. 19.18.

*{in the house of Jeroboam}* This is added for his greater commendation, that he was godly in Jeroboam's house. He was in this like Lot, 2 Pet. 2.8, and Joseph, Gen. 42.18.

**V. 14.** *{Moreover the LORD shall raise him up a king over Israel}* i. e. Baasha, ch. 15.27, etc. As Jeroboam was exalted by God, v. 7, so was Baasha raised by him.

*{who shall cut off the house of Jeroboam}* i. e. utterly destroy it, ch. 15.29.

*{that day}* In the very beginning of his reign, ch. 15.28, 30.

*{but what?}* This is an elegant communication, to work a more thorough consideration of the point. For men were wont to put off divine prophecies of judgment to future times, and those far off, Ezek. 12.22.

*{even now}* This is a direct answer to the former question, like to Ezek. 12.25. For now was Baasha, the king spoken of, among the people.

**V. 15.** *{For the LORD shall smite Israel}* Divine vengeance ceased not with cutting off Jeroboam's posterity, but extended itself to all the ten tribes, till they were all rooted out of the land, 2 King. 17.6, etc.

*{as a reed is shaken in the water}* A reed with the continual running of waters, and blasts of wind, is time after time bowed down, and much shaken: so was Israel with civil wars and different factions continually troubled. For ten several families in succeeding ages got the crown, and that by destroying each other's house: see the Argument. There were also many wars betwixt Israel and Judah, and betwixt Israel and foreign nations.

*{and he shall root up Israel}* By causing the nation to be carried out into strange countries.

*{out of this good land}* See Deut. 8.7, 8, etc.

*{which he gave to their fathers}* As that good land was promised the ancient patriarchs, so the Lord brought their children thereinto, by giving success to the means which they used.

*{and shall scatter them}* i. e. disperse them up and down in several places.

*{beyond the river}* Euphrates, which was betwixt the land of Israel and Assyria; whither the Israelites were carried captive. See all that is here foretold executed, 2 King. 15.29. & 17.6, etc.

*{because they have made their groves}* For idols. See ch. 3.4. & 16.33.

*{provoking the LORD to anger}* See v. 9.

**V. 16.** *{And he shall give Israel up}* Into the hands of their enemies. See v. 15.

*{because of the sins of Jeroboam}* For they consented to his evil counsels, and walked in his idolatrous courses, and made themselves many ways accessory to his sin.

*{who did sin}* See ch. 12.29.

*{and who made Israel to sin}* By causing them to leave the temple of the Lord, and to worship the calves. See ch. 12.30.

**V. 17.** *{And Jeroboam’s wife arose, and departed}* For she saw cause enough to make all the speed she could.

*{and came to Tirzah}* An ancient city held by one of the kings that Joshua destroyed, Josh. 12.24. It seems to have been a very fair and pleasant city, in that the church, which is beautiful in Christ's eyes, is resembled thereunto, Cant. 6.4. Though Jeroboam at first dwelt in Shechem, yet he might after that erect a palace in Tirzah, and make that the royal city of the kingdom. Other kings of Israel after him had their royal palace in this city, till Omri built Samaria, ch. 16.24. See ch. 15.33. & 16.17, 18.

*{and when she came to the threshold of the door}* See v. 12.

*{the child died}* See ch. 13.5.

**V. 18.** *{And they buried him, and all Israel mourned for him}* See v. 13.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See ch. 13.5, 26, 32.

*{which he spake by the hand}* Or, *ministry*. See ch. 8.53.

*{of his servant Ahijah the prophet}* See ch. 13.6.

**V. 19.** *{And the rest of the acts of Jeroboam}* This phrase implies that many more acts of his were registered in some public chronicle: but the Holy Ghost culled out some choice ones, most needful for the church, and put them into a perpetual sacred record.

*{how he warred}* With Rehoboam, v. 30, and Abijah, 2 Chr. 13.2, etc.

*{and how he reigned}* In other things not mentioned in Scripture.

*{behold they are written in the book of the chronicles of the kings of Israel}* Such as are mentioned ch. 11.41.

**V. 20.** *{And the days which Jeroboam reigned were two and twenty years}* He reigned seventeen years in Rehoboam's time, v. 21, three years in Abijah's, ch. 15.1, 2, and two in Asa's, ch. 15.9.

*{and he slept with his fathers}* Heb. *lay down*. See ch. 2.10.

*{and Nadab his son reigned in his stead}* Aaron's eldest son had also this name *Nadab*, Exod. 6.23. It is probable this Nadab was Jeroboam's eldest son living.

**V. 21.** *{And Rehoboam the son of Solomon reigned in Judah}* Here the Holy Ghost returns to the history of Rehoboam, interrupted ch. 12.25 by inserting that of Jeroboam. For in the book of the Kings, from the division, ch. 12.1, etc., till the captivity, 2 King. 17.6, etc., the history of both kingdoms are interwoven, now the acts of one, and then the acts of the other being spoken of.

*{Rehoboam was forty and one years old when he began to reign}* 2 Chr. 12.13. By this it appears that he was born before his father began to reign, ch. 11.42.

*{and he reigned seventeen years in Jerusalem}* These years were complete. For he and Jeroboam began to reign about the same time, ch. 12.20, and Abijam succeeded Rehoboam in the eighteenth year of Jeroboam. Yet if any long time were spent about Jeroboam's sending for out of Egypt, ch. 12.3, then so many days or months might be betwixt Solomon's death and Rehoboam's crowning, as Rehoboam might die in the seventeenth year of his own reign, and in the eighteenth of Jeroboam's, ch. 15.1.

*{the city which the LORD did choose out of all the tribes of Israel, to put his name there}* See ch. 11.36.

*{and his mother’s name was Naamah}* *Naamah* was the name of the daughter of Lamech the first polygamist, Gen. 4.22. Of mentioning the mothers of kings see ch. 15.10.

*{an Ammonitess}* It is probable that at the time of the marriage Naamah made profession of the true religion, as Pharaoh's daughter did, ch. 3.1, the rather, because this marriage was made in David's time: but when Solomon's other wives drew him to permit idolatry, she then might follow her country gods: and be a means to seduce her son, as 2 Chron. 22.3. And it is commonly seen that morally, as well as naturally and civilly, the birth follows the belly, the mother by her tenderness most working on the inclination.

**V. 22.** *{And Judah did evil}* The people following the example of their king, or by him being made to sin, as v. 16, committed the evils following, v. 23, 24. This was three years after Rehoboam began to reign, 2 Chron. 11.17. And it is here noted to show that none of the twelve tribes remained faithful with their God. For ten having revolted with Jeroboam, the other fell away from the Lord with Rehoboam; yet so as some particular persons might here and there remain faithful. See ch. 19.10, 18.

*{in the sight of the LORD}* This may be taken either simply; in such things as God could not endure to see, v. 23, 24, or comparatively; though in the sight of the king and of one another they offended not, yet in the sight of God they sinned, 1 Sam. 15.9. ch. 16.19. See ch. 11.6. & 21.20.

*{and they provoked him to jealousy with their sins which they had committed}* Jealousy is the wrath of an husband incensed against his wife for disloyalty. God being as an husband to his people, Isa. 54.5. Jer. 31.32, and idolatry a spiritual adultery, Jer. 13.27. Ezek. 23.37, 43, in this case jealousy is attributed to him, Deut. 29.28. & 32.16, 21. It imports the highest displeasure and greatest degree of anger, Prov. 6.34, 35. See v. 9.

*{above all that their fathers had done}* This being restrained to the tribe of Judah, is in this respect true, that in none of their fathers' days there was such a general apostasy: but if it be extended to all Israel, then it is to be taken comparatively, in relation to God's mercies. For never any that had received such mercies, provoked God. Abuse of mercies is a great aggravation of sin, 1 Sam. 12.7, etc.

**V. 23.** *{For they also}* They of Judah as well as the ten tribes.

*{built them high places}* To serve their idols in, as the heathen did, Deut. 12.2. See ch. 3.2.

*{and images}* Or, *standing images, or statues*. These were expressly forbidden, Exod. 20.4. See 2 King. 10.26.

*{and groves on every high hill, and under every green tree}* See ch. 3.4. 16.33. Under the broad, fair, flourishing trees which were on their hills, the idolaters used to build altars, 2 Chr. 28.4. Isa. 57.5. The things which being erected by others God commanded his people to destroy, Deut. 7.5, they presumptuously erected.

**V. 24.** *{And there were also sodomites in the land}* Sodomites had their name from the people that inhabited Sodom at that time when God destroyed it with fire and brimstone from heaven, Gen. 19. They were such as against nature *burned in their lust one towards another*, being of the same sex, and committed unnatural and abominable uncleanness, Rom. 1.26, 27, as did the Sodomites, Gen. 19.5, and the men of Gibeah, Judg. 19.22. The notation of the Hebrew word is taken from the contrary; for it signifies *an holy one*; which title unclean persons, being of all others farthest from holiness, have given them in scorn. Thus the word that properly signifies *blessing* is put for *cursing*, ch. 21.10. Or this sin here mentioned may be taken for obscene and filthy idolatry, such as is mentioned Ezek. 8.14. Under the word *Sodomites* are comprised not only the actors of that filthiness, but also the fomenters thereof. For there were such as kept Sodomites in their houses, 2 King. 23.7, and prostituted them for hire. So is the word taken Deut. 23.17.

*{and they did all according to all the abominations of the nations}* Such as are specified Lev. 18.24. Deut. 18.10, 11, 12. Such as leave the Lord, being left of him, give themselves over to all abominable filthiness, Rom. 1.24.

*{which the LORD cast out before the children of Israel}* Joshua and the Israelites with him, who destroyed the Canaanites, were but God's instruments. He appointed them to do it; he directed them how; he put courage into them; he made the hearts of the Canaanites to faint; he gave the success.

**V. 25.** *{And it came to pass in the fifth year of king Rehoboam}* This was two years after that he began to fall from the Lord. Because in Judah was the temple and the holy services, and they most especially remained God's people, he does sooner punish them, and that as the event manifested, in love and good respect to them: for this was an occasion of their repentance and turning again to the Lord, 2 Chr. 12.6.

*{that Shishak king of Egypt came up against Jerusalem}* This Shishak was he to whom Jeroboam fled. See ch. 11.40. It is probable he was put on by Jeroboam, and did the rather hearken unto him, because ten tribes had fallen from Rehoboam, and because of that treasure he knew was laid up by David and Solomon in Jerusalem. See more of Shishak's coming against Jerusalem, 2 Chr. 12.2.

**V. 26.** *{And he took away the treasures of the house of the LORD, and the treasures of the king’s house}* This shows that Rehoboam did not much stand against him: for he was afraid of that mighty army, 2 Chr. 12.3. And it is no marvel therefore that Shishak so far prevailed as to come into the midst of Jerusalem, even to the temple of the Lord and the palace of the king, he being so well prepared, and the prince and people against whom he came being forsaken of the Lord, 2 Chr. 12.5.

*{he even took away all}* All he could find and finger. Yea, he also had done more spoil, but that upon their humiliation the Lord delivered them, 2 Chr. 12.7.

*{and he took away all the shields of gold which Solomon had made}* See ch. 10.16.

**V. 27.** *{And king Rehoboam made in their stead brazen shields}* It appears these shields were for some good use, at least an ensign of glory. But in that he made them of brass, it was evident that the land was much impoverished, and that there was as great difference betwixt Solomon's pomp and Rehoboam's as is betwixt gold and brass.

*{and committed them into the hands of the chief of the guard}* Heb. *runners*. Such as used to run, or go before the king, or wait on him. 1 Sam. 22.17.

*{which kept the door of the king’s house}* This was a special office of the king's guard; and the forementioned shields were in a readiness for them to use, as they had occasion.

**V. 28.** *{And it was so, when the king went into the house of the LORD}* From his own palace to the temple.

*{that the guard bare them}* That he might be the better fenced against all insurrections.

*{and brought them back into the guard-chamber}* That there they might be safely kept.

**V. 29.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Rehoboam, and all that he did}* See v. 19.

*{are they not written}* This interrogation implies a strong asseveration, intimated v. 19 under this word *behold*.

*{in the book of the chronicles of the kings of Judah}* These were such chronicles as are mentioned v. 19, not the sacred Chronicles inserted in the Bible. Judah had their special chronicles as well [sentence incomplete in the scan].

**V. 30.** *{And there was war between Rehoboam and Jeroboam all their days}* i. e. skirmishes were now and then made on the borders, each king maintaining his own. It is not probable there was any set battle betwixt the two kingdoms all Rehoboam's time, because God expressly forbade the men of Judah to fight against their brethren the children of Israel, ch. 12.24.

**V. 31.** *{And Rehoboam slept with his fathers, and was buried with his fathers in the city of David}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and his mother’s name was Naamah an Ammonitess}* See v. 21.

*{and Abijam}* Some copies here read *Abijah*, and so he is called 2 Chr. 12.16. Jeroboam's good son had also this name v. 1, and one of the families of the priests, 1 Chr. 24.10, which continued till Christ's time, Luk. 1.5. It was also a woman's name, 2 Chr. 29.1. In Greek it is *Abia*, Mat. 1.7. *Abijah* signifies, *My father the Lord*.

*{his son reigned in his stead}* The reason why Rehoboam preferred Abijah is set down 2 Chr. 11.21, 22.

# CHAPTER 15

**V. 1.** *{Now in the eighteenth year of king Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* 2 Chr. 13.1. See ch. 14.21. It may be inferred from the title *young man* applied to Jeroboam ch. 11.28, that Rehoboam was much elder than he; for he was one and forty when he began to reign, ch. 14.21, so as Jeroboam might die younger than Rehoboam did, though he reigned four or five years longer, ch. 14.20.

*{reigned}* i. e. began to reign, v. 25.

*{Abijam}* See ch. 14.31.

*{over Judah}* See ch. 12.20.

**V. 2.** *{Three years reigned he}* Three years current, not complete. The eighteenth of Jeroboam was Abijah's first; the nineteenth, his second; the twentieth, his third: and in that twentieth year of Jeroboam began Asa to reign, v. 9. This reckoning of current years in setting down kings' reigns is usual in Scripture.

*{in Jerusalem}* The chief city of the kingdom, where was the king's palace and throne.

*{and his mother’s name}* See v. 10.

*{was Maachah}* Called *Michaiah*, 2 Chr. 13.2. These might be two names of one and the same person.

*{the daughter of Abishalom}* Called *Absalom* 2 Chr. 11.21, and *Uriel of Gibeah*, 2 Chr. 13.2. It is probable this Absalom was another than David's son: for David's son erected a pillar, which he called by his own name, because he had no son to keep his name in remembrance, 2 Sam. 18.18. There was one Maacah Absalom's mother, 2 Sam. 3.3. Whence some infer that Tamar Absalom's daughter, being married to Uriel of Gibeah, had by him a daughter, who was named after her great-grandmother's name, *Maachah*, who is called *Absalom's daughter*, because she was his grandchild. But to draw kindred from a name is a very uncertain collection; for the same name is oft given to persons of several stocks.

**V. 3.** *{And he walked in all the sins of his father}* By this it appears that though Rehoboam upon fear of Shishak humbled himself, 2 Cor. 12.6, yet afterward he returned to his former wicked courses. For this has relation to his latter times. It appears also that Abijam had a corrupt heart, though he made a fair profession, 2 Chr. 13. No marvel therefore that his reign continued so short a time, v. 2.

*{which he had done before him}* Though this phrase *before him* in our English may have reference to the time, as if Rehoboam's sin which he committed before his son began to reign, were in that respect here intended; yet in the Hebrew it has reference to the presence of the son, and so implies the sins which the father did in his son's sight. And thus the phrase aggravates the evil courses both of father and son; of the father, in that he was an ill precedent to his son; of the son in that he was not warned by his father's evil courses.

*{and his heart was not perfect with the LORD}* i. e. not entire. See ch. 8.61. He made a fair show but wanted soundness.

*{his God}* i. e. in regard of his profession; for he professed the Lord to be his God, 2 Chr. 13.10.

*{as the heart of David his father}* See ch. 3.14. & 11.38.

**V. 4.** *{Nevertheless for David’s sake}* See ch. 11.12.

*{did the LORD his God give him a lamp}* Or, *candle*. See ch. 11.32, 36.

*{in Jerusalem}* See ch. 11.36.

*{to set up his son after him}* i. e. David's posterity time after time.

*{and to establish Jerusalem}* See ch. 9.5.

**V. 5.** *{Because David did that which was right in the eyes of the LORD}* This has reference both to the matter or things which David did, styled *right*, in that they were warranted by the word of God, which is a right rule; and also to the manner of doing the same, which was uprightly, as in the presence of God.

*{and turned not aside from any thing that he commanded him, all the days of his life}* This is meant of his inward disposition and intention. He transgressed not in anything deliberately, purposely, obstinately, though through weakness of the flesh and violence of temptations he did many things otherwise than was commanded.

*{save only in the matter of Uriah the Hittite}* 2 Sam. 11.3, 4. & 12.9.

Under this are comprised his adultery, murder, and many aggravating circumstances which accompanied the same. This matter about Uriah is in particular mentioned, because it was the most apparently scandalous that ever he committed.

**V. 6.** *{And there was war between Rehoboam and Jeroboam all the days of his life}* Ch. 14.30. This is mentioned in Abijam's time, because he revived and pursued the wars begun by his father. The revolt of the ten tribes from the house of David lay in the stomach of Abijam, though it happened in the days of Rehoboam. See the Appendix after Genesis.

**V. 7.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Abijam, &c.}* See ch. 14.29.

*{and there was war between Abijam and Jeroboam}* Hereof express mention is made 2 Chron. 13.3.

**V. 8.** *{And Abijam slept, &c.}* See ch. 2.10. More things are written of Abijam, 2 Chron. 13.

*{and Asa his son reigned in his stead}* This was a good change for the kingdom of Judah. God provided a good son to succeed a bad father.

**V. 9.** *{And in the twentieth year of Jeroboam king of Israel reigned Asa over Judah}* Asa began to reign in the time of the first king of Israel, and continued to the time of the eighth. Those eight kings were of five distinct families, one destroying another; I. Jeroboam's, ch. 12.20. II. Baasha's, v. 29. III. Zimri's, ch. 16.10. IV. Tibni's, ch. 16.21. V. Omri's, ch. 16.22.

**V. 10.** *{And forty and one years reigned he in Jerusalem}* A great reformation was he to make, therefore God continued him the longer.

*{and his mother’s name}* i. e. grandmother's. All female ancestors in the right line use to be called *mothers*, as male ancestors are called *fathers*. David, who was father to Asa's great-grandfather, is styled his father, v. 11. Mention uses to be made of the mothers of kings, because in their younger years they were instructed by them, Prov. 31.1. Again, kings then used to have many wives, therefore the proper mother is expressed by name.

*{was Maachah the daughter of Abishalom}* See v. 2. It may be Asa's own mother was dead, and he educated by his grandmother; who though an idolater, yet could not infect him.

**V. 11.** *{And Asa did that which was right in the eyes of the LORD}* See ch. 14.8. This is to be taken of the general course of his life; and particularly, about the worship of God: for otherwise he had many infirmities, and committed sundry gross sins, as 2 Chron. 16.7, 10, 12.

*{as did David his father}* See ch. 9.4. This shows he had an honest and entire heart, which was David's chief excellency.

**V. 12.** *{And he took away the sodomites}* See ch. 14.24.

*{out of the land}* Heb. *from the earth*. i. e. He slew them, as 1 Sam. 28.3, 9. Or, he took them out of his kingdom. He suffered them not to abide there.

*{and removed all the idols}* The Hebrew word signifies *dirty*, or *dungy*; and is used for *man's dung*, Ezek. 4.12, and *dung or dirt in the street*, ch. 14.10. Zeph. 1.17. Sundry judicious interpreters of Scripture translate it *dirty or dungy gods*, when it is applied to idols, as 2 King. 17.12. Ezek. 6.4. & 18.6, 12, 15. & 20.39. This title was given to idols in scorn and contempt, to show that they are base, vile, loathsome things, not fit to be endured above ground. They are styled *abominations*, 2 Chr. 15.8. See ch. 11.5.

*{that his fathers had made}* As Solomon, ch. 11.7, Rehoboam, ch. 14.23, Abijam, v. 3. The evil example of his forefathers was no precedent to him.

**V. 13.** *{And also Maachah his mother}* 2 Chron. 15.16. See v. 2, 10.

*{even her he removed from being queen}* Though the king her husband were dead, yet she retained some royal dignity, yea, and authority too, at least over her own court, in her son's and grandchild's time; and probable it is that she had a command over such as were of her religion. The wives of kings deceased are still called *queen-mothers*. Such an one was Athaliah, 2 Chron. 22.2, 3. If Asa were underage, she might be queen-regent; and he observing her idolatrous courses might in an holy zeal and indignation take the government upon himself, and put her from it; certainly he deprived her of all dignity and authority which she had by custom, or usurped.

*{because she had made an idol}* The word signifies *an horrible thing*. This idol was horrible in the ugliness and filthiness of it: so as the worst of idols were brought in among the Jews. See ch. 11.7.

*{in a grove}* See ch. 16.33.

*{and Asa destroyed}* Heb. *cut off*. He suffered it not to continue. See v. 12.

*{her idol}* That before mentioned.

*{and burnt it}* To show his great indignation, and utterly to abolish it, Deut. 7.5. See the manner 2 Chron. 15.16.

*{by the brook Kidron}* See ch. 2.37. He burnt it by the river, that he might cast the ashes of it thereinto, and so nothing at all thereof be preserved, 2 King. 23.12. See Exod. 32.20.

**V. 14.** *{But the high places were not removed}* People were loath to be tied to one place for their sacrifices; and therefore would here and there have their high places, and that in times of reformation, 2 King. 12.3. & 15.4, 35. Though he took away high places for idols, 2 Chr. 14.3, yet the high places for God's worship, such as are mentioned ch. 3.2, he let stand. He ought not to have permitted these, Deut. 12.5, 6, 11, so as this was his infirmity.

*{nevertheless}* Though the people did so and so, yet the king neither appointed them so to do, nor approved them in so doing.

*{Asa his heart was perfect}* See ch. 8.61.

*{with the LORD}* In regard of the worship of God.

*{all his days}* For we read not that he ever turned from the true worship of the Lord to idolatry, though he sinned otherwise, 2 Chr. 16.10.

**V. 15.** *{And he brought in the things}* Heb. *holy*. For such things as were dedicated to the Lord were counted holy.

*{which his father}* After that famous victory which he got against Rehoboam, 2 Chr. 13.15, it is probable that in way of thankfulness he dedicated some of his treasures to the Lord. Not kings only, but princes, captains, and others used so to do: see 1 Chron. 26.26, 28.

*{had dedicated}* i. e. vowed to the Lord, and prepared for the temple, but had not brought them into it.

*{and the things which himself had dedicated}* For fearing God as he did, he could not but devote of his treasures unto God.

*{into the house of the LORD}* There to remain as part of the sacred treasure.

*{silver and gold}* For repairing the temple, and other the like uses.

*{and vessels}* For the services of the temple. See ch. 7.25, 50.

**V. 16.** *{And there was war between Asa and Baasha king of Israel all their days}* See ch. 14.30. All Baasha's reign was in Asa's time, v. 28.

**V. 17.** *{And Baasha king of Israel went up}* In the thirteenth year of his own reign, and sixteenth of Asa's, 2 Chr. 16.1. This was after that great victory that Asa had against the Ethiopians, 2 Chr. 14.12, etc., whereby he growing very mighty, Baasha was the more afraid of him.

*{against Judah}* To block up the passages, not to invade it, as the words following import.

*{and built}* Made strong with bulwarks and forts. See 2 Chron. 11.6.

*{Ramah}* A city of Benjamin, Josh. 18.25, upon the frontiers of Ephraim, Judg. 4.5.

*{that he might not suffer any}* Of his own subjects especially.

*{to go out or come in to Asa king of Judah}* Lest they should join with the men of Judah in the true worship of God, and turn to Asa's side, as ch. 12.26, 27. This he the rather sought to prevent, because many had formerly turned to him, 2 Chr. 15.9.

**V. 18.** *{Then Asa took all the silver and gold that was left}* After Shishak had pillaged the land, ch. 4.26.

*{in the treasures of the house of the LORD}* Herein he committed sacrilege.

*{and the treasures of the king’s house}* Herein he robbed himself. For these were the treasures which himself and his fathers had laid up for their own use.

*{and delivered them into the hand of his servants}* Ambassadors chosen to carry that present, which was a very great one.

*{and king Asa sent them to Benhadad}* 2 Chron. 16.2. *Benhadad* was a name common to sundry kings of Syria, ch. 20.1. 2 King. 8.7. & 13.3, as *Pharaoh* to the kings of Egypt. See ch. 2.39. 2 King. 23.29. *Benhadad* signifies *son of Hadad*. Hadad is supposed to have been an ancient king of Syria; and that in memorial of him his name was by his posterity compounded, by prefixing something before it, as *Benhadad*: or by adding something to it, as *Haddadezer*, 2 Sam. 8.3.

*{the son of Tabrimon, the son of Hezion king of Syria}* This Hezion is taken to be that Rezon mentioned ch. 11.23, who was an inveterate enemy of the Jews.

*{that dwelt at Damascus}* See ch. 11.24, 25.

**V. 19.** *{Saying, There is a league between me and thee, and between my father and thy father}* It seems there was a former league betwixt the kings of Judah and Syria, so as this was a renewing thereof.

*{behold, I have sent unto thee a present of silver and gold}* See v. 18.

*{come and break thy league with Baasha king of Israel}* Herein Asa adds sin to sin. He I. distrusts God, 2 Chr. 16.7. II. robs God of what was dedicated to him, v. 15. III. spoils himself, v. 18. IV. enriches an infidel and an enemy, v. 18. V. stirs up an enemy against his brethren the Israelites, ch. 12.24. VI. makes a covenant against God's law, Exod. 23.32. VII. causes a covenant made to be broken. See Ezek. 17.18, 19. VIII. trusts too much to the arm of flesh, Isa. 31.1. Thus he that destroyed all outward idols, erects and maintains an idol in his own soul, which was confidence in flesh.

*{that he may depart from me}* Heb. *go up*. Baasha had come down to the very borders of Judah.

**V. 20.** *{So Benhadad hearkened unto king Asa}* For the rich present, v. 18, much wrought with him.

*{and sent the captains of the host, which he had, against the cities of Israel}* Benhadad in breaking league exercised great hostility. The league of a mortal enemy is but for his own advantage.

*{and smote Ijon}* A frontier town, 2 Kin. 15.29. See v. 29.

*{and Dan}* Of the gaining and naming this city, see Judg. 18.27, 28, 29. & Josh. 19.47.

*{and Abel-bethmaachah}* Called *Abel-maim*, 2 Chr. 16.4. It is that strong city whereunto Sheba betook himself, 2 Sam. 20.15.

*{and all Cinneroth}* i. e. all the towns situate by the sea of Cinneroth, Josh. 12.3, called *Genesaret*, Luk. 5.1.

*{with all the land of Naphtali}* i. e. the store-cities therein, 2 Chr. 16.4. The places here mentioned being in several tribes, it appears that this enemy ranged up and down and did great mischief in many of the coasts of Israel.

**V. 21.** *{And it came to pass when Baasha heard thereof}* See the like 1 Sam. 23.27, 28.

*{that he left off building of Ramah}* Lest by abiding there he should suffer the enemy to do more spoil. He chose rather to defend his own country than to annoy Judah.

*{and dwelt in Tirzah}* He had his chief residence there after he had secured his land; and continued quiet, without any revenge against Asa, whom he feared. See ch. 14.17.
