**V. 22.** *{Then king Asa made}* Heb. *made to hear*. It is the end of a king's proclamation to cause his will so to be made known thereby as all his subjects may hear and take notice thereof. See 2 Chron. 24.9. & 30.5. & 36.22.

*{a proclamation throughout all Judah}* To gather all his subjects together to demolish Baasha's works, and to perfect his own, with all expedition.

*{(none was exempted)}* Heb. *free, *from adding their assistance: for the work was very great.

*{and they took away the stones of Ramah, and the timber thereof, wherewith Baasha had builded}* This shows that Baasha had proceeded far in the built-works he intended.

*{and king Asa built with them}* See v. 17. Thus Baasha provided materials for his enemy.

*{Geba of Benjamin}* A strong city given to the Levites, Josh. 21.17. It was the north coast of the kingdom of Judah.

*{and Mizpah}* This was another city in Benjamin, Josh. 18.26. Asa here made a great pit which continued till the captivity. See Jer. 41.9.

**V. 23.** *{The rest of all the acts of Asa}* See ch. 14.19.

*{and all his might, and all that he did}* i. e. his valiant deeds, as 2 King. 14.15, 28. Of these see 2 Chron. 14.9, etc.

*{and the cities which he built}* See v. 22.

*{are they not written in the book of the chronicles of the kings of Judah}* See ch. 14.19.

*{nevertheless in the time of his old age he was diseased in his feet}* His prosperity could not keep away the gout. For his sin provoked God to afflict him, 2 Chron. 16.12.

**V. 24.** *{And Asa slept with his fathers}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and was buried with his fathers}* See 2 Chr. 16.14.

*{in the city of David his father}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and Jehoshaphat}* Mat. 1.8, called *Josaphat*.

*{his son}* Who was better than his father; at least, than his father was in his latter time.

*{reigned in his stead}* Succeeded him.

**V. 25.** *{And Nadab the son of Jeroboam began to reign over Israel}* Heb. *reigned*. Because many kings of Israel reigned in Asa's time (see ver. 9), the penman of this history finished his reign, and now returns to the history of Israel, which he continued to ch. 22.41.

*{in the second year of Asa king of Judah}* Asa's first year ended in the one and twentieth of Jeroboam, v. 9, his second in the two and twentieth, in which Jeroboam died, and Nadab succeeded.

*{and reigned over Israel two years}* His second year was but current, as v. 2. For Nadab's first year was in the second of Asa; his second in Asa's third, wherein he was slain, v. 28.

**V. 26.** *{And he did evil in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.22. The Lord finding the son no better than his father, saw it high time to cut him off, according to the threatening ch. 14.10, etc.; therefore he was slain in his second year.

*{and walked in the way of his father}* So as not only his father's sin but his own also was the cause of his destruction.

*{and in his sin wherewith he made Israel to sin}* i. e. Jeroboam's idolatry about the golden calves. See ch. 12.30. & 14.16.

**V. 27.** *{And Baasha the son of Ahijah}* See on ch. 11.29.

*{of the house of Issachar}* Issachar was one of the meanest of the ten tribes, yet by reason of Baasha's might, it got the principality from Ephraim; which having no such promise as Judah, no marvel that the scepter departed from it. One of the judges was of this tribe, Judg. 10.1.

*{conspired against him}* Traitorously, as ch. 16.9, 20. 2 King. 12.20. His treason is aggravated by the time when, and place where he committed it, namely, in the army, when the king was recovering what of right belonged unto Israel.

*{and Baasha smote him at Gibbethon}* A city of Dan, given to the Levites, Josh. 21.23. There was therefore great and just cause to seek to recover it.

*{which belongeth to the Philistims}* It seems they had got it from the Israelites; so as it belonged to them by right of possession.

*{for Nadab and all Israel laid siege to Gibbethon}* This they did to recover it: which because it was not effected at this time by reason of the slaughter of the king, the people afterwards renewed the siege, ch. 16.15.

**V. 28.** *{Even in the third year of Asa king of Judah}* See v. 25.

*{did Baasha slay him and reigned in his stead}* As if he had lawfully come by the crown.

**V. 29.** *{And it came to pass when he reigned, that he smote all the house of Jeroboam}* See ch. 14.10.

*{he left not to Jeroboam any that breathed}* This phrase is oft restrained to reasonable persons, as Josh. 11.14, though it may be extended so to cattle and other creatures that belonged to him.

*{until he had destroyed him according unto the saying of the LORD}* ch. 14.7, 10, 18.

*{which he spake by his servant Ahijah the Shilonite}* See ch. 11.29. Though this were foretold by the prophet, yet Baasha did it not in obedience to God's word, but to satisfy his own ambitious humor, God in the meantime wisely ordering Baasha's evil intent to bring to pass his own righteous end.

**V. 30.** *{Because of the sins of Jeroboam which he sinned, and which he made Israel sin}* See ch. 12.30. & 13.34. & 14.15.

*{by his provocation wherewith he provoked}* See ch. 14.9, 22.

*{the LORD God of Israel to anger}* See ch. 8.15.

**V. 31.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Nadab, and all that he did, &c.}* See ch. 14.19.

**V. 32.** *{And there was war, &c.}* See v. 16. This was before set down in the life of Asa, and here repeated in the life of Baasha, because it concerned both the one and the other.

**V. 33.** *{In the third year of Asa king of Judah}* See v. 28.

*{began Baasha the son of Ahijah to reign over all Israel}* So soon as Jeroboam's stock was destroyed, all the ten tribes soon turned to his enemy.

*{in Tirzah}* See ch. 14.17.

*{twenty and four years}* Current. See v. 2. For he began his reign in the third year of Asa, and ended it in the twenty-sixth, ch. 16.8.

**V. 34.** *{And he did evil}* Though he were God's instrument to punish him that did evil, v. 27, yet was he not thereby moved to depart from evil.

*{in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.22.

*{and walked in the way of Jeroboam}* Not only Jeroboam's son, but this and all other the kings of Israel cleaved close to Jeroboam's idolatry.

*{and in his sin wherewith he made Israel to sin}* See ch. 14.16.

# CHAPTER 16

**V. 1.** *{Then the word of the LORD came to Jehu the son of Hanani}* This clause, *the son of Hanani*, distinguishes this Jehu from Jehu the king, 2 King. 9.14, from the son of Obed, 1 Chr. 2.38, and from the Anathothite, 1 Chr. 12.3. This Jehu was a prophet, v. 12, long after this sent to Jehoshaphat, 2 Chron. 19.2, who also wrote chronicles, 2 Chron. 20.34. Hanani his father was also a seer, 2 Chr. 16.7. See ch. 13.1. For though prophets were not so born, as priests and Levites, yet God oft bestowed that gift on father and son, yea he therein blessed the desire and endeavor of fathers, who educated their children thereunto. See ch. 18.4.

*{against Baasha, saying}* Jehu's prophecy tended to Baasha's utter ruin, therefore it may well be said to be *against him*.

**V. 2.** *{For as much as I exalted thee}* The prophet brings in God himself speaking, to add the more authority to his prophecy. See ch. 14.7.

*{out of the dust}* From the lowest of the people, ch. 14.7. Psal. 113.7.

*{and made thee prince}* For it was God that by his secret and wise providence brought Baasha's attempt to that issue.

*{over my people Israel}* See ch. 14.7.

*{and thou hast walked in the way of Jeroboam}* See ch. 15.34.

*{and hast made my people Israel to sin}* The evil example of kings uses to draw people to sin. See ch. 1.21.

*{to provoke me to anger with their sins}* See ch. 14.9, 22.

**V. 3.** *{Behold, I will take away the posterity of Baasha}* Baasha himself died in outward peace: therefore the judgment is threatened against his posterity.

*{and the posterity of his house}* Such as were allied, or otherwise appertained to him. Thus the brethren of Ahaziah who were allied to Ahab were slain, 2 Kings 10.13.

*{and will make thy house like the house of Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* See ch. 15.29.

**V. 4.** *{Him that dieth of Baasha in the city, &c.}* See ch. 14.10, 11. The same judgment in the same words is threatened against Baasha that was against Jeroboam, because he persisted in the same sin. The Lord sets before Baasha that which was done to Jeroboam, because histories of judgments executed do more to the life set out God's terror, than predictions of the like to come. Besides, thereby the greater evidence is given of God's truth. For that which he has once done, he can do again; yea, his former fulfilling of a threatening plainly demonstrates that he will not fail to fulfill what afterwards he threatens.

**V. 5.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Baasha, and what he did, and his might, are they not written, &c.}* See ch. 14.19. 15.23.

**V. 6.** *{So Baasha slept with his fathers}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and was buried in Tirzah}* See ch. 14.19.

*{and Elah his son reigned in his stead}* *Elah* was an ancient name, Gen. 36.41.

**V. 7.** *{And also}* This may have reference to the judgment denounced by Ahijah against Jeroboam, ch. 14.6, 7, etc. The word of the Lord came by Ahijah against Jeroboam, *and also* by Jehu against Baasha. Or this particle *also* may have reference to God's charge, v. 1: thus, God charged Jehu to denounce a judgment against Baasha, *and also* Jehu denounced the same.

*{by the hand}* Or, *ministry*. See ch. 8.53.

*{of the prophet Jehu the son of Hanani}* See v. 1.

*{came the word of the LORD against Baasha}* See v. 1.

*{and against his house}* See ch. 14.10.

*{even for all the evil that he did in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.22. & 21.20.

*{in provoking him to anger}* See ch. 14.9.

*{with the work of his hands}* To wit, idols, Psal. 115.4. Isa. 2.8. Jer. 1.16.

*{in being like the house of Jeroboam}* See v. 2.

*{and because he killed him}* i. e. Nadab, Jeroboam's son, whom Baasha slew out of a traitorous and ambitious design, chap. 15.27, neither commanded by God, as Jehu was, 2 King. 9.7, nor inwardly incited by God's Spirit, as Ehud was, Judg. 3.20, 21.

**V. 8.** *{In the twenty and sixth year of Asa king of Judah began Elah the son of Baasha to reign over Israel}* See v. 6.

*{in Tirzah}* See ch. 14.17.

*{two years}* Current, as ch. 15.2. For he was slain in the twenty-seventh year of the reign of Asa, v. 10. See ch. 15.25.

**V. 9.** *{And his servant}* A special officer attendant on the king.

*{Zimri}* This was also the name of an impious prince of Judah, Numb. 25.14, and of one of Saul's posterity, 1 Chron. 8.36, who might be the Zimri here spoken of.

*{Captain of half his chariots}* He had thereby a very great command. Of the use of chariots see 1 Chr. 18.4.

*{conspired against him}* See ch. 15.27.

*{as he was in Tirzah}* See ch. 14.17.

*{drinking himself drunk}* He drank so excessively as he distempered himself, and knew not what to do, and so was the more easily surprised, as Amnon, 2 Sam. 13.28, 29, and Belshazzar, Dan. 5.2, 30.

*{in the house of Arza, steward of his house}* Heb. *which was over*. Or, *governor*, ch. 4.6.

*{in Tirzah}* This showed that he was absent from his army, v. 15, which might have defended him.

**V. 10.** *{And Zimri went in, and smote him}* See ch. 14.17.

*{and killed him in the twenty and seventh year of Asa king of Judah}* The year after Elah began to reign, v. 8.

*{and reigned in his stead}* See ch. 15.28.

**V. 11.** *{And it came to pass when he began to reign}* i. e. as soon as he had slain Elah. See ch. 14.28.

*{as soon as he sat on his throne}* He instantly set himself thereon.

*{that he slew all the house of Baasha}* By the assistance of the soldiers under his command, v. 9.

*{he left him not one that pisseth against a wall}* See ch. 14.10.

*{neither of his kinsfolks, nor of his friends}* Or, *both his kinsmen and his friends*. The word translated *kinsfolks* signifies *redeemers*, such as had a right to redeem the lands of their kindred, Ruth 3.13. & 4.4. It is also put for *revengers of such kindred as were unjustly slain*, Numb. 35.19. Under this word *friends* are comprised all such as might be thought any way to favor Elijah's cause, or be on his side, or seek to revenge his death.

**V. 12.** *{Thus did Zimri destroy all the house of Baasha}* He must needs be very diligent and speedy in his bloody design, that in a week's space could make such a thorough dispatch of them all.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See ch. 13.5, 14, 18.

*{which he spake against Baasha}* See v. 1.

*{by}* Heb. *by the hand of*. Or, *ministry*. See ch. 8.53.

*{Jehu the prophet}* See v. 1.

**V. 13.** *{For all the sins of Baasha}* See v. 2.

*{and the sins of Elah his son}* See ch. 15.26.

*{by which they sinned}* This is a pleonasm, and has this emphasis: for in general it implies an aggravation; and in particular it shows that their posterity so sinned as their sin became exemplary.

*{and by which they made Israel to sin}* By being an example and encouragement unto them. See ch. 14.16.

*{in provoking the LORD God of Israel to anger}* See ch. 14.9.

*{with their vanities}* Idols are here meant, which are mere *vanities*, Deut. 32.21. 2 King. 17.15. Isa. 41.28. 1 Cor. 8.4. False gods have no reality of deity in them, but are vainly imagined to be what they are not, Jer. 2.5. Jon. 2.8. Neither can they do anything at all, Psal. 115.5, etc. Isa. 41.23. So as confidence placed in them is a vain confidence, and they that serve them are bereaved of right understanding and judgment; in which respect they are said to *become vain*, 2 King. 17.15.

**V. 14.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Elah, &c.}* See ch. 14.19. If we compare the state of Jeroboam and his son and Baasha and his son together, we shall find their sins and judgments much alike. Jeroboam's son reigned but two years, no more did Baasha's; and Baasha's stock was as utterly rooted out as Jeroboam's. Baasha the revenger of Jeroboam's impiety was in like manner punished for his own.

**V. 15.** *{In the twenty and seventh year of Asa king of Judah did Zimri reign}* See v. 10.

*{seven days}* All that time he was God's rod to scourge Baasha's stock. So soon as that work was done, the rod was cast into the fire, v. 18.

*{in Tirzah}* See ch. 14.17.

*{and the people were encamped against Gibbethon}* There Nadab was slain, ch. 15.27. Though by Baasha that siege was raised, yet in his son's time it was laid again.

*{which belonged to the Philistims}* See ch. 15.27.

**V. 16.** *{And the people that were encamped heard say}* Such acts could not but be soon divulged far and near, in city and field.

*{Zimri hath conspired}* v. 9.

*{and hath also slain the king}* v. 10.

*{wherefore all Israel}* All but such as took part with Tibni v. 21.

*{made Omri, the captain of the host}* By reason of his office and charge Omri had the hearts of the people the more set on him; and they were moved the more to confide in him: so as he having the army under his command, was the fitter to revenge Zimri's treason.

*{king over Israel that day}* Hereby he had the greater power to settle those distractions.

*{in the camp}* Before they went out of the field, that so he might immediately without delay set upon Zimri.

**V. 17.** *{And Omri went up from Gibbethon}* He accounted a traitorous usurper more dangerous than an open enemy, and the loss of a kingdom worse than the loss of a city. This was the second time that the siege against Gibbethon was raised. See ch. 15.27.

*{and all Israel with him}* See v. 16.

*{and they besieged Tirzah}* Which Zimri it seems had fortified.

**V. 18.** *{And it came to pass, when Zimri saw that the city was taken}* It was taken by storming. For the besiegers rising from another siege, brought all the train of their artillery with them.

*{that he went into the palace of the king’s house}* Probable it is that he carried with him all his precious commodities.

*{and burnt the king’s house over him with fire}* He would not leave his body to be abused by his enemies, nor any commodities to be enjoyed by them.

*{and died}* Thus he showed a most desperate mind, like Saul 1 Sam. 31.4, who would rather destroy himself than fall into his enemy's hand. Tyrants cruel to others are oft given over to be cruel to themselves.

**V. 19.** *{For his sins which he sinned}* His reign being but seven days, the sins here intended are to be extended to the time before he slew his master as well as the time after.

*{in doing evil in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.22.

*{in walking in the way of Jeroboam}* Jeroboam's course of life was an impious and rebellious course against God.

*{and in his sin, which he did to make Israel sin}* This particular sin was worshiping the calves. Herein Zimri caused the people to continue. For he might in that short time not only worship the calves himself, but also make edicts to enjoin all the people so to do. See ch. 12.28, 29, 30. & 14.16.

**V. 20.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Zimri}* See ch. 14.19.

*{and his treason that he wrought}* v. 9, 10.

*{are they not written, &c.}* See ch. 14.19.

**V. 21.** *{Then were the people of Israel divided into two parts}* They that remained at home disdained that the soldiers in the field should without their consent set up a king; they therefore would not submit to Omri, and the soldiers would not depart from their choice, and thereupon they were divided; the people on one side, the soldiers on another. This division continued from the seven and twentieth year of Asa to the one and thirtieth, v. 15, 23.

*{half of the people followed Tibni the son of Ginath}* These were the people that tarried at home.

*{to make him king}* In opposition against Omri.

*{and half followed Omri}* Namely they in the field.

**V. 22.** *{But the people that followed Omri prevailed against}* For these were well armed, and had all things fit to subdue those that were against them.

*{the people that followed Tibni the son of Ginath}* These were such as took part with Tibni, affording their best aid to settle him on the throne.

*{so Tibni died}* Some take this of an untimely death, even while he was in his greatest height.

*{and Omri reigned}* Quietly, without any opposition.

**V. 23.** *{In the thirty and one year of Asa king of Judah}* Then was Tibni destroyed, so as Omri sat upon the throne quietly. The people made Omri king so soon as they heard that Zimri had killed his master, which was in the twenty-seventh year of Asa, v. 15, 16, but because it was uncertain whether he or Tibni should carry the crown away, he is here said to *begin his reign* four years after, *in the one and thirtieth of Asa*.

*{began Omri to reign over Israel twelve years}* Heb. *reigned*. These twelve years must needs be reckoned from the people's first making him king, which was in the twenty-seventh of Asa, because in the thirty-eighth of Asa Ahab the son of Omri began to reign, and that after the reign of his father.

*{six years reigned he in Tirzah}* Even till he had bought Samaria, and built his palace there. Of Tirzah see ch. 14.17. The palace in Tirzah being burnt, v. 18, Omri built it again, or made another house in Tirzah fit for a king.

**V. 24.** *{And he bought the hill Samaria}* *Samaria* is a Greek name, Joh. 4.4. The Hebrew is *Shomron*. Here an hill is called *Samaria*: in the end of this verse a city built thereon is so called: so also was the whole country about that hill, wherein were many cities, ch. 18.2. 2 King. 17.24. Yea, the whole kingdom of Israel was so called; for the king of Israel is styled *King of Samaria*, 2 King. 1.3, because Samaria was his chief city.

*{of Shemer for two talents of silver}* The Jews valued silver ten times less worth than gold: so as a talent of silver was reckoned at three hundred seventy-five pounds sterling, but a talent of gold at three thousand seven hundred and fifty pounds, ch. 9.14. The price then that Omri paid was seven hundred and fifty pound sterling. See on 1 Chro. 22.14.

*{and built on the hill}* A city, which was the chief city of that kingdom.

*{and called the name of the city which he built, after the name of Shemer owner of the hill, Samaria}* The Hebrew word *Shomron* is a derivative of *Shemer*. Shemer was a man of great note, who owned all that land whereon the great city Samaria was built. See on Gen. 10.18.

**V. 25.** *{But Omri wrought evil in the eyes of the LORD}* See chap. 14.22.

*{and did worse than all that were before him}* Herein in special he did worse, in that he made statutes to bind the people to his idolatry, Mic. 6.16. Besides, his continuing in Jeroboam's ancient sin after so many heavy judgments on all the kings betwixt Jeroboam and him made his sin worse than all theirs.

**V. 26.** *{For he walked in the way of Jeroboam, &c.}* See v. 19.

*{in provoking the LORD God of Israel to anger}* See ch. 14.19, 22.

*{with their vanities}* See v. 13.

**V. 27.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Omri which he did, and his might that he shewed}* For he was a captain of the host, v. 16, and prevailed against Zimri, v. 17, 18, and against Tibni, v. 21, 22.

*{are they not written, &c.}* See ch. 14.19.

**V. 28.** *{So Omri slept with his fathers}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and was buried in Samaria}* See v. 29.

*{and Ahab his son reigned in his stead}* For being his son he had a right to his kingdom.

**V. 29.** *{And in the thirty and eighth year of Asa king of Judah}* Omri's twelve years v. 23 are to be accounted complete. For the eight and thirtieth of Asa was the thirteenth current of Omri. Compare v. 15 with this. Or it must be granted that Omri began his reign in the very end of Asa's twenty-seventh, and held it some while in the beginning of Asa's thirty-eighth.

*{began Ahab the son of Omri to reign over Israel}* Heb. *reigned*.

*{and Ahab the son of Omri reigned over Israel in Samaria}* There was his royal palace. See v. 24. This city was to Israel as Jerusalem to Judah, their metropolis or chief city, the seat of their kings, where they kept their chief residence, ch. 20.1. & 22.10, yea, the place of their kings' sepulchers. See v. 28. ch. 22.37. 2 King. 10.35. & 13.9, 13.

*{twenty and two years}* Current, as ch. 15.2. His first year must be reckoned in the last of his father's. Compare this verse with the eighth.

**V. 30.** *{And Ahab the son of Omri did evil in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.22.

*{above all that were before him}* Above his father, and others too that were before his father. See on v. 33.

**V. 31.** *{And it came to pass}* This clause has reference to these words, *that he took to wife Jezebel*. All the words between may be included in a parenthesis.

*{as if it had been a light thing for him to walk in the sins of Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* Heb. *was it a light thing?* etc. The interrogative in the Hebrew implies a very vehement denial; *q. d.* It was certainly no light thing to walk in the sins of Jeroboam; yet Ahab did so and more also; he took to wife Jezebel. This kind of expression does much aggravate Jeroboam's sin, but much more Ahab's.

*{that he took to wife Jezebel}* Jezebel was an heathen, a notorious idolater, extremely arrogant and cruel, as the case of Naboth and destruction of the prophets show, ch. 18.4. & 21.8, etc. In relation to this woman an idolater and countenancer of idolatry is styled *Jezebel*, Rev. 2.20.

*{the daughter of Ethbaal}* As Jezebel's name, so Ethbaal is compounded of *Baal*, which shows he was much addicted to that idol.

*{King of the Zidonians}* The Zidonians were of the cursed brood of Canaan, Gen. 10.15, devoted to destruction, Josh. 13.6, but left to prove Israel, Judg. 3.3, 4, and took what advantages they could to oppress Israel, Judg. 10.12. They were gross idolaters, ch. 11.5.

*{and went and served Baal}* *Baal* signifies *a lord*, Hos. 2.16. In common it is put for any kind of idol which men worship as a lord. In special it signifies *a prime or chief idol*, *a prince of idols*. *Beelzebub* is called *Prince of devils*, Mat. 12.24. Sundry countries, to distinguish their idols from others, had several additions to *Baal*; as Ekron, *Baalzebub*, 2 King. 1.2, *a fly-lord*; the Moabites, *Baal-Peor*, Num. 25.3. Peor is a mount in Moab where their Baal was worshiped: the Israelites, *Baal-Berith*, Judg. 8.33, *a lord of covenant*, an idol to whom by covenant they bound themselves. The chief idol of the Zidonians was *Baal*, supposed to be Hercules, common to Tyrus as well as Zidon, 2 Macc. 4.18, 19. See on Judg. 2.11.

*{and worshipped him}* Ahab worshiped Baal himself, and not Jehovah in that image, as the Israelites did in the golden calves, ch. 12.28. This phrase of Elijah, *if Baal be God*, ch. 18.21, proves that Baal was accounted a very god.

**V. 32.** *{And he reared up an altar for Baal}* To offer sacrifice thereon to Baal. See ch. 12.32. & 13.2.

*{in the house of Baal, which he had built}* He erected a sumptuous temple for Baal in imitation of that temple which was built in Jerusalem for the Lord. See ch. 12.31.

*{in Samaria}* Samaria was the royal city of the kingdom which Omri built, v. 24. Thus he exceeded Jeroboam's idolatry.

**V. 33.** *{And Ahab made a grove}* A grove was a place planted with trees for delight. They dedicated groves to the honor of their idols, and to allure people thereby to idolatry. Therein they used to set idols, to erect altars, and to offer sacrifices, ch. 15.13. Jer. 17.2. See on Judg. 3.7. Gen. 21.33.

*{and Ahab did more}* Heb. *added*.

*{to provoke the LORD God of Israel to anger}* Purposely, as if he would dare God to do his worst. See ch. 14.9.

*{than all the kings of Israel that were before him}* See v. 30. Of his exceeding others these instances may be given. 1. None trod in the steps of such a father, v. 25. 2. None married such a wife, v. 31. 3. None attributed such deity to an idol, v. 31. 4. None erected such an idolatrous temple, v. 32. 5. None so slighted God's word, ver. 34. 6. None so hated God's prophets, chap. 22.8. 7. None so persecuted them, chap. 18.10. 8. None so coveted another's inheritance, chap. 21.4. 9. None so causelessly and unjustly shed blood, chap. 21.19. 10. None so heaped up idolaters and false prophets, chap. 18.22. & 22.6. 11. None so foolishly let go the enemy whom God put into his hand, ch. 20.42. 12. None so sold himself to work wickedness in the sight of God, ch. 21.20, 25.

**V. 34.** *{In his days}* Before his days, for the space of more than five hundred years, none dared attempt that work, upon which there lay so fearful a curse. If it were done with his knowledge and consent, surely he too much slighted God's threatenings; if without his consent, yet was it a judgment, even on him, that such a thing should be done in his days.

*{did Hiel the Bethelite}* He is so called, because he was an inhabitant of Bethel that idolatrous city.

*{build Jericho}* This was an accursed city, the first of all Canaan that was destroyed, and that miraculously, Josh. 6.20. God would have the ruins thereof remain as a monument of his power, and of the curse that lay upon that city.

*{he laid the foundation thereof in Abiram his first-born}* When he first began this forbidden work, his eldest son and heir died; and in the finishing thereof, his youngest son died. It is supposed that he had more children, and that all betwixt the eldest and the youngest died while the city was in building, so as he destroyed his living house by erecting this cursed city.

*{And set up the gates thereof in his youngest son Segub}* The setting up of the gates was one of the last works about the building of that city; so as the beginning and finishing thereof was fatal to his children. It may probably be gathered from the curse mentioned Josh. 6.26, that by some casualty in laying the foundation his eldest son was killed, and then by some other casualty in setting up the gates his youngest son was killed.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See ch. 13.5.

*{which he spake by}* Or, *by the hand, or minister*. See v. 12.

*{Joshua the son of Nun}* Though he were general over an army, yet was he inspired with a prophetical spirit. For this event falling out so fitly according to the prediction, must needs be foretold by a spirit of prophecy.

# CHAPTER 17

**V. 1.** *{And}* This particle joins this chapter with the former, and shows that the judgment of famine here related followed upon the sins of Ahab and Hiel.

*{Elijah}* Heb. *Elijahu*. Luk. 4.25, he is called *Elias*. Sundry things very remarkable are noted of this man. I. His name, which signifies *God the Lord*. That God was the Lord, he brought the idolatrous people of Israel to acknowledge, ch. 18.39. II. His jealousy for the Lord God of hosts, when he supposed that all Israel had forsaken the Lord, ch. 19.10. III. His faithful standing to and for the Lord, when as he knew none to be with him but himself, ch. 18.22. IV. His courage against the king himself, ch. 18.18. & 22.10. Yea, though the cruel queen Jezebel nourished sundry false prophets, yet he feared not to oppose them, ch. 18.19. V. His zeal against idolaters, ch. 18.40. VI. His power in prayer, 2 King. 1.10. Jam. 5.17, 18. VII. The power of his ministry, Luk. 1.17. VIII. The wonderful miracles he wrought, ch. 17.16, 22. 2 King. 2.8. IX. His authority: he was a father and chief governor of the college wherein the children of the prophets were trained up, 2 King. 2.3, 5, 8. X. His fasting forty days and forty nights, ch. 19.8. The like did Moses, Exod. 24.28, who was the lawgiver, and Christ, Mat. 4.2, who was the bringer-in of the gospel. None else ever did so but these three. XI. His rapture into heaven, 2 King. 2.11. None else ever had such a privilege but Enoch, Gen. 5.24. XII. A letter of his brought to a rebellious king after he was translated, 2 Chron. 21.12. XIII. His apparition with Moses at Christ's transfiguration, Mat. 17.3. XIV. His manner of bringing forth into the world, like that of Melchizedek, *without father, or mother, or descent*, Gen. 14.18. Heb. 7.3.

*{the Tishbite}* Of the city of Thisbe, Tob. 1.2. See on Judg. 9.50.

*{who was of the inhabitants of Gilead}* Gilead appertained part to Gad and part to Manasseh, Josh. 13.24, 31. In this respect Elijah is taken to be of the tribe of Manasseh.

*{said}* After he had prayed there might be no rain, Jam. 5.17, and by some evidence from God was assured his prayer was heard. This he did by an extraordinary spirit, as in another case he called for fire from heaven, Luke 9.54, 55.

*{unto Ahab}* In his presence, to his face, undauntedly.

*{As the LORD God of Israel liveth}* See ch. 1.29. & 8.15. He delivers his message after this solemn manner, to make Ahab the more heed it, and to strike the greater terror into him.

*{before whom I stand}* Whose minister I am, and whom I am ready to serve on all occasions, Deut. 10.8. Acts 27.23.

*{there shall not be}* Heb. *if there be*. A form of asseveration which implies an imprecation, and makes a more vehement and certain negation. The judgment following is foretold, to show that it was ordered by God, and happened not by any celestial constellation, or by chance, Isa. 48.5, etc.

*{dew}* He means hereby that there should be no moisture or refreshing at all to fall upon the earth: for dew is the least of that kind. This is the threatening Lev. 26.19 accomplished.

*{nor rain}* Without rain all manner of plants on the earth wither and perish.

*{these years}* Immediately following, till the end of three years and a half, Luk. 4.25. Jam. 5.17.

*{but according to my word}* Unless God by my ministry shall declare otherwise. Or, *but till as I have said*. See ch. 18.1, 41, 44, and on Gen. 7.4. This was the first miracle that is registered of Elijah: there were twelve in all, v. 8, 16, 22. ch. 18.38, 45. & 19.8, 20. 2 King. 1.10, 12. & 2.8, 11. Basil calls Elijah *the bridle of heaven*.

**V. 2.** *{And the word of the LORD came unto him, saying}* It came to him extraordinarily by inspiration, or revelation, or some other like way.

**V. 3.** *{Get thee hence}* God directs him how to provide for himself. Where he was he could continue in safety.

*{and turn thee eastward}* The brook whither he was directed was on the east of Samaria.

*{and hide thyself}* From Ahab's rage. God could have assuaged Ahab's fury, but he directs his prophet to use ordinary means of escaping, for an example to others. See Jer. 36.26. Josh. 8.5, 9. Exod. 2.16.

*{by the brook Cherith}* This brook came from a spring-head, and ran along in a desert place not inhabited.

*{that is before Jordan}* For this brook ran into Jordan.

**V. 4.** *{And it shall be, that thou shalt drink of the brook}* Ponds and wells were sooner dried up than this brook, and therefore he was directed thither.

*{and I have commanded the ravens}* Even unreasonable creatures are at God's command. God sets this forth in the time past, because he had determined beforehand how to provide for Elijah; and also because it was as sure as if it had been done.

*{to feed thee there}* To bring you such food as you must eat.

**V. 5.** *{So he went, and did according to the word of the LORD}* Believers are ready to go at God's word, Luk. 5.5.

*{for he went and dwelt}* He abode there night and day, as in an house. There was questionless some cave there wherein he so dwelt, as ch. 19.9.

*{by the brook Cherith that is before Jordan}* See v. 3.

**V. 6.** *{And the ravens}* Ravens are greedy birds, devouring what they get, scarce sparing anything for their young, as may be collected out of Job 38.41. & Psal. 147.9. It was therefore by an overruling providence that this kind of fowls should be such diligent caterers for the prophet.

*{brought him bread and flesh}* Some ravens brought bread, others flesh, and that seethed or roasted. God by his providence directed them to the places where they might have it, as to some rich men's kitchens.

*{in the morning, and bread and flesh in the evening}* They were as constant in observing times seasonable for refreshing as if they had been reasonable creatures purposely appointed to attend upon the prophet. This was the effect of his prayer and faith; and may be reckoned amongst his miracles: so as it was Elijah's second miracle. See v. 1.

*{and he drank of the brook}* The means which God afforded him to quench his thirst he used thankfully.

**V. 7.** *{And it came to pass after a while}* Heb. *at the end of days*. Namely, at the end of six months. See ch. 18.1.

*{that the brook dried up}* God had other ways to provide for his prophet, and therefore he suffers this brook to be dried up, the rather that Elijah might the more sensibly discern the judgment to be executed according to his prophecy. Questionless as the spring from whence this brook came dried up, so also did other spring-heads, and the streams issuing from them.

*{because there had been no rain}* Springs are maintained by rain; so as where no rain falls, there must needs the springs be dried up. This sets out the greatness of the drought. For if flowing springs were dried up in six months, what water could they have, the drought continuing three years and six months? See ch. 18.1.

*{in the land}* i. e. the land of Israel. No circumstance shows that rain was withheld so long from Judah. Judah might be at this time as Goshen Exod. 8.22. & 9.26. See on Amos 4.7.

**V. 8.** *{And the word of the LORD came unto him, saying}* See v. 2.

**V. 9.** *{Arise}* See ch. 14.2. God would have men diligent, even in providing for themselves.

*{get thee to Zarephath}* Luk. 4.26, called *Sarepta*.

*{which belongeth to Zidon}* It was a city which the Zidonians inhabited, of which country this widow was, Luk. 4.26. It bordered upon the land of Israel: and as Israel was infected with the Zidonians' sin, ch. 16.31, so they did partake of Israel's punishment.

*{and dwell there}* There continue as in your own house, v. 5.

*{behold, I have commanded}* By my overruling providence, I have, as by a command, moved her. Of setting this down in the time past, see v. 4. Here are instances given of senseless creatures, as dew and rain, v. 1, of unreasonable creatures, as ravens, ver. 4, and of the heathen, as here, to be at God's command.

*{a widow woman}* God oft makes choice of the weaker sex to do great matters, yea when they are most helpless, as widows. And again, he here prefers a Gentile before all the widows in Israel, Luk. 4.25, 26, wherein is given an instance of the calling of the Gentiles.

*{there to sustain thee}* With needful food for three years together. See ch. 18.1.

**V. 10.** *{So he arose, and went to Zarephath}* See v. 5.

*{and when he came to the gate of the city}* i. e. to the first entrance thereinto. God so orders matters as the evidence of his divine providence in bringing to pass what he promises may be soon discerned, as Gen. 24.15.

*{behold, the widow woman}* See v. 9.

*{was there gathering of sticks}* This shows that she was but a poor woman, in that she wanted not only food, but fuel also.

*{and he called to her, and said}* By instinct he knew this was the woman of whom God spoke; at least he would make some proof thereof, as Gen. 24.17.

*{Fetch me, I pray thee, a little water}* Water was there more plentiful than bread; thereby therefore he first makes trial of the readiness of her mind to sustain him.

*{in a vessel}* Or, *in this vessel*. For it is very probable that he brought with him the dish or cup with which he took water out of the brook, and offered that to her.

*{that I may drink}* At first he makes show as if he desired no more than a cup of cold water. A fair trial.

**V. 11.** *{And as she was going to fetch it}* She shows herself ready to grant his first request, and thereupon he is encouraged to ask more.

*{he called to her, and said, Bring me, I pray thee, a morsel of bread in thy hand}* The word implies *a little piece of bread*, no more than might somewhat satisfy his present hunger.

**V. 12.** *{And she said}* Here she makes a stop, because she could not so well spare bread as water.

*{As the LORD thy God liveth}* See ch. 1.29. She says, *thy God*, because she believed the God of the Israelites to be the true God; as Rahab Josh. 2.11, and the angel Luk. 1.16. Or she might know Elijah to be a prophet by his attire, 2 King. 1.8, and in that respect say to him, *thy God*.

*{I have not a cake}* Not so much as a little cake baked on the hearth, Gen. 18.6. I have no bread at all baked in the oven, not a piece of a loaf.

*{but an handful of meal in a barrel, and a little oil in a cruse}* In those days they did knead their meal or flour of which they made cakes with oil. And as a barrel was a fit vessel to keep meal in, so a cruse to keep oil in.

*{and behold, I am gathering two sticks}* A synecdochical speech, meaning a few sticks. So we use to say, *two or three*, when we intend a few. See 2 Sam. 13.6.

*{that I may go in, and dress it for me and my son}* See v. 15.

*{that we may eat it, and die}* That we may preserve our lives as long as we have any means, and then yield them to the good pleasure of God. For she saw no means of preserving their lives any longer.

**V. 13.** *{And Elijah said unto her, Fear not}* Fear not such want as death should follow thereupon.

*{go, and do as thou hast said}* This has reference to v. 12, where she said she intended to make a cake for herself and her son.

*{but make me thereof a little cake first, and bring it unto me}* Herein he tries her faith, and her readiness to provide for him.

*{and after make for thee and for thy son}* The prophet was not wholly for himself, but was willing she should also provide for herself and her son.

**V. 14.** *{For thus saith the LORD God of Israel}* See ch. 11.31.

*{The barrel of meal shall not waste, neither shall the cruse of oil fail}* i. e. Neither meal nor oil shall waste but both continue in their proper vessels, and afford sufficient for food. See 2 King. 4.2.

*{until the day that the LORD sendeth}* Heb. *giveth*. For what the Lord by his providence affords us is free gift.

*{rain upon the earth}* i. e. She should have meal and oil enough till God should cause the earth to bring forth corn and oil. See Josh. 5.11, 12.

**V. 15.** *{And she went and did according to the saying of Elijah}* This ready performing what she was required gave good witness of her faith and obedience.

*{and she and he}* The widow and the prophet.

*{and her house}* i. e. her family. So Gen. 7.1. This implies that there were more with her when the prophet came than herself and her son. But there being no more meal and oil left than was for once sufficient for them two, she mentions them only, v. 12.

*{did eat many days}* Or, *a full year*. Yea rather three years. See ch. 18.1. This was a recompense which the widow had for her courtesy to the prophet. She afforded one meal to him; he many to her and hers.

**V. 16.** *{And the barrel of meal wasted not, neither did the cruse of oil fail}* This is a ratification of the promise made v. 14.

*{according to the word of the LORD}* See ch. 13.5.

*{which he spake by Elijah}* Heb. *by the hand of Elijah*. See ch. 13.5. This was Elijah's third miracle. See v. 1.

**V. 17.** *{And it came to pass after these things}* After the woman had had good experience of God's providence.

*{that the son of the woman}* That son mentioned v. 12.

*{the mistress of the house}* This further shows that she had a family under her. See v. 15.

*{fell sick}* This sickness was sent before his death, to make her the more thoroughly to observe the issue thereof.

*{and his sickness was so sore}* Sickness does not necessarily presuppose death; but this youth's sickness was such an one, and so extreme, as death did follow thereupon. God oft mixes great blessings with heavy crosses.

*{that there was no breath left in him}* A description of death. His breath was not only stopped, as it is in a swoon, but clean gone. He was really dead.

**V. 18.** *{And she said unto Elijah}* She knew none else to go unto in this case. Her very going unto the prophet was an evidence of her faith and hope.

*{what have I to do with thee}* Heb. *what to me and thee*. Such a phrase is used John 2.4, *q. d.* What difference is there betwixt you and me? What have I done to you? See Judg. 11.12.

*{O thou man of God}* Though that heavy judgment wrought much passion in her, yet she still retained a reverent and high esteem of Elijah, and also some faith in God. See ch. 13.1.

*{art thou come unto me to call my sin to remembrance}* Or, *to put God in mind of my sins*. Or, *to complain to me before God of my sins*. She penitently acknowledged sin to be the cause of that judgment. See Luk. 5.8. Rev. 18.5.

*{and to slay my son}* Or, *to incense God so against me as to take my son away from me*. These also were passionate speeches, yet they imply that by the judgment she was brought to think of her sins, and to acknowledge that they justly deserved that judgment.

**V. 19.** *{And he said unto her, Give me thy son}* The prophet was moved with her complaint, and instantly endeavors to redress her grief.

*{And he took him out of her bosom}* It seems her son being dead, she hugged him close in her breast, to bring, if it were possible, some heat into him. Thence the prophet took him.

*{and carried him up into a loft where he abode}* That he might be the more private. See Act. 9.39, 40. Privacy is a good help to fervency in prayer.

*{and laid him upon his own bed}* We are not to suppose that he did this in regard of any sanctity in his bed more than in other beds; but going to his own chamber he laid him upon his bed there, that he might the better stretch himself upon the child, v. 21.

**V. 20.** *{And he cried unto the LORD}* i. e. he earnestly prayed unto God. So Exod. 14.15. 1 King. 22.32. This shows miracles are wrought by God's power, not man's.

*{and said, O LORD my God}* He strengthens his faith with a close consideration of the special relation betwixt God and him.

*{hast thou also brought evil upon the widow?}* By *evil* he means *judgment*, as Job 2.10. Amos 3.6. Just judgment is not evil in its nature, but in its effect, causing smart and anguish which seems evil to him that feels it. Elijah's complaint to God is out of his apprehension of the woman's distress, who being a widow had the greater need of a son's support: with the loss therefore of her son he much affected.

*{with whom I sojourn}* This is a third thing whereby his spirit is made the more earnest in his request to God, namely the kindness he had received from the woman.

*{by slaying her son}* His knowledge that God had the power of life and death, and that he did kill and make alive, Deut. 32.39, makes him so to press this prerogative of God, that he might the rather be pleased to restore her son, because he had taken him away.

**V. 21.** *{And he stretched}* Heb. *measured*.

*{himself upon the child}* His stretching or measuring of himself was such a posture as in a like case is noted of Elisha, 2 King. 4.34. *He put his mouth upon the childs mouth, and his eyes upon his eyes, and his hands upon his hands.* He so lay upon the child, that by sense of the child's coldness his own spirit in calling upon God might be the more fervent, and also that he might the better discern heat and life, as it should come into the child. See Act. 20.10. As the hen sits on her eggs to put life into them, so he lay upon the child to do what he could to put life into him.

*{three times}* To show his fervency and constancy in prayer. So Mat. 26.44. 2 Cor. 12.8. We must not give over praying though at first we be not heard.

*{and cried unto the LORD, and said, O LORD my God}* See v. 20.

*{I pray thee}* This express and humble petition shows that the expostulation v. 20 was in faith, and with reverence.

*{let this child’s soul come into him again}* Heb. *into his inward parts*. This phrase expounds that v. 17. And it declares that the child was actually dead; and that by death the soul is separated from the body.

**V. 22.** *{And the LORD heard the voice of Elijah}* By this it appears that the prayer of Elijah was very powerful, Jam. 5.17, and that God is ready to hear those that pray aright, Psal. 65.2, and that it was God himself that restored life and wrought the miracle.

*{and the soul of the child came into him again}* This is a proof of a resurrection from the dead.

*{and he revived}* This is the first that we read of raised from death. And this was Elijah's fourth miracle. See v. 1.

**V. 23.** *{And Elijah took the child and brought him down out of the chamber into the house}* That, such as saw him dead, might be witnesses of his miraculous restitution to life.

*{and delivered him unto his mother}* To show her that she had not entertained a prophet in vain. See Mat. 10.41.

*{and Elijah said, See, thy son liveth}* Due notice is to be taken of miraculous works.

**V. 24.** *{And the woman said to Elijah}* Quickly she manifests her joyful and grateful heart.

*{Now by this I know}* I am further assured. She knew as much before, v. 18, but miracles confirm faith, Joh. 3.2. She believed her son might be raised when first she complained to the prophet, v. 18. So did the Shunammite, 2 King. 4.28. In this respect women are said *to receive their dead raised to life again*, Heb. 11.35.

*{that thou art a man of God}* A true prophet. See ch. 13.1.

*{and that the word of the LORD in thy mouth is truth}* Herein she has not only relation to the raising of her son, but also to other matters foretold by this prophet, as the drought and continuance thereof, v. 1, and the increase of her meal and oil, v. 16. Whatsoever Elijah had said from the Lord she believed to be infallibly true. Here are two effects of a miracle set down; I. To demonstrate those who work it to be sent of God, Joh. 3.2. II. To confirm the truth, Heb. 2.4.

# CHAPTER 18

**V. 1.** *{And it came to pass after many days}* After three years and six months: for so long the famine continued, Luk. 4.25. Jam. 5.17.

*{that the word of the LORD came to Elijah}* ch. 17.1. Elijah had said from the Lord, that there *should be no rain, but according to his word*. Now therefore the time being come, wherein God was purposed to send rain, he sends Elijah to Ahab to tell him that there should be rain, that so the former prophecy, even in this circumstance, might be ratified.

*{in the third year}* Of his abode with the widow. If we should reckon from the beginning of the drought, when Elijah went to the brook Cherith, we should leave out six months. And there is no other remarkable matter from whence we can take the beginning of these three years, than the prophet's going to Zarephath, ch. 17.10.

*{saying, Go shew thyself unto Ahab}* For Ahab had not seen Elijah since he first denounced the judgment: he having hid himself ch. 17.3. See v. 10. As the drought was foretold to Ahab, so God would have the removal thereof foretold to the king himself, that he might thereby know, that the one and the other came from the Lord.

*{and I will send rain}* It is God that withholds and gives rain, Deut. 28.12, 23, 24. Amos 4.7. And it is he that makes good the word of his prophets.

*{upon the earth}* It is by rain that the earth brings forth the fruit thereof, Lev. 26.4. For want of rain that land brought not forth so much as grass. See v. 5.

**V. 2.** *{And Elijah went to shew himself unto Ahab}* He was not ignorant of Ahab's murderous resolution against him, v. 10, but the Lord who sent him, put an undaunted courage into him, v. 15, 18. His confidence was in God's protection, Heb. 13.5, 6.

*{and there was a sore famine}* For food for man and beast had failed divers years, v. 5. ch. 17.7.

*{in Samaria}* In that kingdom whereof Samaria was the head city. See 2 King. 17.24.

**V. 3.** *{And Ahab called Obadiah}* Heb. *Obadijahu*. Some take this to be that Obadiah, whose prophecy is set among the small prophets. But 1. Likeness of names does not evince that one and the same person should be set out thereby. 2. There is a letter and syllable more in this name than in the other. 3. The matter of that prophecy is scarce agreeable to this time. 4. Obadiah's place at court, and a prophet's function, do not well stand together; in that Ahab makes this man a partner with him in that work which himself undertook, it appears he was in great favor, and that the king placed much confidence in him.

*{which was the governour of his house}* Heb. *over his house*. As Joseph was to Potiphar, Gen. 39.4, 5, and afterwards to Pharaoh, Gen. 41.38, 39, 40. See ch. 4.6.

*{(now Obadiah feared the LORD greatly)}* as Gen. 42.18. We may well thence infer, that Obadiah was one of the seven thousand that bowed not their knee to Baal, ch. 19.18. It is not probable that though he feared God, he went to the feast at Jerusalem: for his place and charge permitted him not so to do. Yet we are not to think that he worshiped either the calves or Baal, but the Lord only, as Dan. 6.10. And this integrity the Lord accepted, and passed by other frailties. The time and place wherein he lived, and yet feared God, does show that he did it greatly.

**V. 4.** *{For it was so, when Jezebel}* Heb. *Izebel*. See ch. 16.31.

*{cut off}* Slew, destroyed, v. 13. She cut them off from the earth. She was a bloody persecutor of God's choicest servants, even his prophets. Certainly she executed her cruelty upon many: so as there were more than those whom Obadiah preserved. And we cannot but think that there were others also in Israel, that kept prophets of the Lord from her fury, besides Obadiah.

*{the prophets of the LORD}* They that gave themselves to be thoroughly instructed in the will of God, and to use best endeavors to declare the same to others, were titled *prophets*. Of these there were companies, 1 Sam. 10.5, 10. & 19.20, yea, colleges, 2 King. 22.14, in which the masters and seniors, were called *fathers*, 1 Sam. 10.12. 2 King. 2.12, and they who as auditors or attendants, accompanied the ancient prophets, were called *children*, or *sons of the prophets*, ch. 20.35. 2 King. 2.3. & 4.38. Also all that openly protested against idolatry, and professed the true religion, are comprised under this title *prophets*.

*{that Obadiah took an hundred prophets}* This is set down, as an evidence and proof of his fear of God.

*{and hid them}* See ch. 17.3.

*{by fifty}* More could not conveniently be kept together. It must needs be a large place wherein so many were kept so long: besides, by parting them, if they which were in one cave had been surprised, the other might be provided for more safely. See Gen. 32.8.

*{in a cave}* A cave (as some great hollow place in the earth, or in a rock, Isa. 2.19, 21. Jer. 16.16) made by men, or scraped out by wild beasts in some desolate place, or wilderness, where men or beasts might abide securely. See Josh. 10.16. Judg. 6.2. 1 Sam. 24.3.

*{and fed them}* He did not only keep them safe from danger, suffering them in those hard times and barren places to shift for themselves, but also provided sustenance for them.

*{with bread and water}* In such a famine and drought as then was, bread and water was dainty cheer; and the feeding of an hundred therewith, was a fruit of great liberality. The phrase may also be taken indefinitely for *meat and drink*.

**V. 5.** *{And Ahab said unto Obadiah}* See v. 3.

*{Go into the land}* Or, *throughout all the land*.

*{unto all fountains of water, and unto all brooks}* Though the brook Cherith were dried up, ch. 17.7, three years before this; yet some fountains and brooks might still continue: otherwise nor man nor beast would have been preserved.

*{peradventure we may find grass}* For grass uses to grow in grounds lying by springs, brooks and rivers. Under this word *grass* all manner of fodder growing out of the earth, and fit for beasts, is comprised.

*{to save the horses and mules alive}* This is not to be taken so as if they had more care of beasts than men; but that they could not so well provide out of other countries for beasts as for men. Yet this is evident, that they had more care of beasts than of their own or others' souls; for they searched not after their sins, the cause of that judgment, nor were they by that judgment brought to repentance.

*{that we lose not all the beasts}* Heb. *that we cut not off our selves from the beasts*. This is to be extended to horses, mules, and all other useful beasts.

**V. 6.** *{So they divided the land between them, to pass throughout it}* This they did for more quick dispatch; for they were in a very great strait.

*{Ahab went one way by himself}* That a business of such consequence might be the more thoroughly done, he would not leave it to the trust of others.

*{and Obadiah went another way by himself}* Ahab had found Obadiah as faithful as Potiphar found Joseph, Gen. 39.4.

**V. 7.** *{And as Obadiah was in the way}* This was so ordered by divine providence, ch. 11.29.

*{Behold, Elijah met him}* Before he had gone far from Ahab, v. 15.

*{and he knew him}* Obadiah knew Elijah. For one that feared God, so as Obadiah did, could not but be acquainted with such a prophet as Elijah was.

*{and fell on his face}* A gesture of high esteem, and great reverence; yet civil only. See Gen. 50.18. Ruth 2.10. 1 Sam. 20.41. 2 Sam. 19.18.

*{and said, Art thou that my lord Elijah?}* Obadiah calls Elijah his lord, in regard of the prophet's high calling, and extraordinary gifts; so v. 9, 12, he thus styles himself, *thy servant*.

**V. 8.** *{And he answered him, I am}* This answer has especial relation unto his name *Elijah*, *q. d.* I am Elijah.

*{go, tell thy lord}* He means king Ahab, whose household servant Obadiah was, v. 3. Impiety does not destroy the right of dominion.

*{Behold, Elijah is here}* As this note, *behold*, roused up his attention; so it invites him to go and see, as Joh. 1.26, 46.

**V. 9.** *{And he said, What have I sinned, that thou wouldest deliver thy servant into the hand of Ahab to slay me?}* He does not here excuse himself of all sin, (see ch. 8.46,) but of such heinous sins as should expose him to present destruction. As if he had said, By what sin against God, or wrong to you, have I deserved that my life should be put in such hazard, as it is like to be, by doing as you bid me? See v. 12.

**V. 10.** *{As the LORD thy God liveth}* See ch. 1.29. In speaking to Elijah, Obadiah uses this title, *thy God*, not to exempt himself from this relation; but to work the greater credence in Elijah. Or because Elijah was not only an ordinary servant, but also an especial prophet of God.

*{there is}* Heb. *if there be*. An usual form of an oath wherein an imprecation is included, as Psal. 96.11. Heb. 3.11.

*{no nation or kingdom}* This literally taken is to be applied to nations and kingdoms neighboring by him, and in league with him: or tropically, to such ambassadors, messengers, agents, merchants, factors, or others as came from sundry nations and kingdoms, or to several parts and tribes of Israel, which by a synecdoche may be titled *nations* and *kingdoms*: or, proverbially, the phrase may imply a diligent, narrow, and thorough search; as we use to say, *there is no place or part of the world where I have not inquired: I have sought every where, in city and country*.

*{whither my LORD}* He means Ahab. See v. 8.

*{hath not sent to seek thee}* That he might find you, and get you into his clutches.

*{and when they said, He is not there, he took an oath of the kingdom and nation that they found thee not}* He moved them by all the means he could to swear they knew not where Elijah was.

**V. 11.** *{And now thou sayest}* This inference shows, that he was afraid to make known to Ahab that he had seen Elijah.

*{Go, tell thy LORD, Behold, Elijah is here}* See v. 8. The words following especially in v. 14 show the reason why he was loath to carry that message to Ahab.

**V. 12.** *{And it shall come to pass, as soon as I am gone from thee}* Before Ahab can see you.

*{that the Spirit of the LORD}* By the *Spirit of the Lord*, he means the same extraordinary means that the Lord would use suddenly and secretly to remove him from place to place.

*{shall carry thee}* As Ezek. 3.14. Acts 8.39. & 2 Kings 2.11, 16.

*{whither I know not}* So as I shall not know where to find you.

*{and so when I come and tell Ahab, and he cannot find thee, he shall slay me}* Conceiving me to mock him.

*{but I thy servant}* This is a note of an humble mind, and ready to do him any service. See v. 7.

*{fear the LORD}* This he pleads, not in any boasting manner, much less as a matter of merit, but to move the prophet to forbear the imposing of that perilous task upon him. An upright and humble man may lawfully make profession of that grace that is in him.

*{from my youth}* Continuance from one's youth in grace cannot but work great confidence.

**V. 13.** *{Was it not told my LORD, &c.}* It could not be but that some of those prophets which in time of need received great succor from Obadiah, should make it known to Elijah their father and lord, 2 King. 2.3.

**V. 14.** *{And now thou sayest}* See v. 11. Fear makes him inculcate the same thing again.

*{and he shall slay me}* See v. 9, 12.

**V. 15.** *{And Elijah said, As the LORD of hosts liveth}* God is styled *the Lord of hosts*, because under his command all creatures are as soldiers, ready to execute what he enjoins. Because Obadiah was in great fear, Elijah by this solemn oath puts him out of all fear. See ch. 1.29.

*{before whom I stand}* See ch. 17.1.

*{I will surely shew myself unto him to day}* Heb. *I will be seen, or I will appear to him*. He had such faith in God's protection of him as he feared not to appear before a king incensed against him.

**V. 16.** *{So Obadiah went to meet Ahab}* He believed what Elijah had said and sworn, and knew that such a prophet had good ground to appear before Ahab.

*{and told him}* That he had seen Elijah in such a place.

*{and Ahab went to meet Elijah}* He that had everywhere sought for Elijah, could not but readily go where he heard him to be; and that, either to execute his rage on him, or to know when he might expect rain.

**V. 17.** *{And it came to pass when Ahab saw Elijah, that Ahab said unto him}* At first sight the angry king manifests his mind against him, and accuses him of public sedition, which was a capital crime.

*{Art thou he}* This interrogation is a strong asseveration, whereby he lays the blame of the drought upon the prophet, and considers not that he himself was the proper cause thereof.

*{that troubleth Israel}* That bring such trouble and calamity upon the people as they are forced to seek up and down and to send far and near for ordinary food; whence quarrels and tumults do arise: who also dissuade the people from the religion established, and so disturb the peace of the land. Thus the heathen imputed plagues, and famine, etc., to the Christians. See on Gen. 39.14.

**V. 18.** *{And he answered, I have not troubled Israel}* An innocent party may and ought to put off from himself a crime unjustly laid to his charge, though it be by his superior and sovereign.

*{but thou}* God's Spirit put this undaunted spirit into him, whereby he was stirred up to tell the king his fault, Matth. 14.4. Ezek. 3.8. 2 Sam. 12.7. See on Num. 16.7.

*{and thy father’s house}* i. e. his brothers, kinsfolks and courtiers left him by his father, and observing his father's courses and statutes, ch. 16.25.

*{in that ye have forsaken}* As they who having once professed the law of the Lord revolt from the same, are said to forsake him, as ch. 11.33. 2 Chr. 24.24, so they who should have professed it, but never did, nor yet do, may also be said to forsake him, as Ahab did here, and Ahaz, 2 Chr. 28.6. See 1 Chr. 28.9.

*{the commandments of the LORD}* To forsake the Lord's commandments and to forsake the Lord are one and the same thing. For the Lord's authority is set forth by his commandments; and he that despises them despises him. See 1 Chr. 12.1.

*{and thou hast followed Baalim}* Many false gods. Here is showed, that sinners against God, whether it be by disobeying him, or following false gods, are most properly troublers of the church and such as bring judgments thereupon, Josh. 7.25.

**V. 19.** *{Now therefore send, and gather to me all Israel}* He means elders, heads of tribes, all sorts of governors, and others out of every tribe, that might be eye- and ear-witnesses of that which he intended to do, v. 21, etc.

*{unto mount Carmel}* There were two places called *Carmel*; one in Judah, Josh. 15.55, where Nabal had his possession, 1 Sam. 25.2. The other in Asher, Josh. 19.26. This here and elsewhere is called a *mount*. It is an high mountain, Amos 9.3, by the sea, Jer. 46.18. On it Elisha had his habitation, 2 King. 2.25. & 4.25. *Carmel* signifies *a fair fertile place*, Isa. 35.2, and is by our English translated *a fruitful field*, Isa. 32.15. See on 1 Sam. 25.2.

*{and the prophets of Baal}* Idolaters had such functions as the true church had, but they were false ones; as these prophets were false prophets, such as ch. 22.22. Luk. 6.26. 2 Pet. 2.1.

*{four hundred and fifty}* See v. 20. These were dispersed up and down throughout the ten tribes.

*{and the prophets of the groves}* Heb. *that grove*. See ch. 16.33. These were prophets of the idols in groves; and in those groves they had their residency. These might attend the court.

*{four hundred, which eat at Jezebel’s table}* They were sustained by Jezebel's provision, as if they had sat at her table, or received meat from thence.

**V. 20.** *{So Ahab sent}* Ahab's earnest desire of rain might make him the more readily yield to Elijah, who he thought might be a means to procure it; or fear of a greater judgment might make him yield; or it may be Elijah had told him beforehand of the trial he meant to make for proving Jehovah to be the true God, and Ahab desirous to see the issue might do what Elijah required. Whatsoever the outward motive was, surely God overruled his spirit, and made him willing to send for those Elijah required to be sent for.

*{unto all the children of Israel}* Round about throughout all the tribes. See v. 19.

*{and gathered the prophets together}* i. e. the four hundred and fifty prophets of Baal mentioned v. 19. See v. 22. It is probable that Jezebel kept back the other four hundred, which were those spoken of ch. 22.6. The number of these prophets is set down, to show the courage of Elijah, who being one alone dared oppose so many false prophets.

*{unto mount Carmel}* See v. 19.

**V. 21.** *{And Elijah came unto all the people, and said}* By this it appears that that which follows was not accomplished on that day when Obadiah first told Ahab of Elijah's appearance. For there must needs some days pass betwixt Elijah's advice to Ahab, v. 19, and this accomplishment thereof. All the children of Israel from all parts of the kingdom could not be gathered together in one day.

*{How long halt ye}* A man that halts inclines his body now one way, and now another. This metaphor may be applied two ways: 1. To the body of the people, whereof some inclined their hearts to Jehovah, and others to Baal. 2. To the same persons, who in some things, at some times, inclined to Jehovah; in other things, at other times, to Baal. It is probable that the latter is here especially intended. The like is taxed Matth. 6.24.

*{between two opinions}* Or, *thoughts*. For thoughts cause opinions.

*{If the LORD be God}* This word *Lord* is the interpretation of *Jehovah*, which is the proper name of the only true God, the God of Israel. This title *God*, in Hebrew *Elohim*, is the first that is attributed to him in Scripture, and that when he created all things, Gen. 1.1. It being a word of the plural number, sets out that one God which is distinguished into three persons, the creator, preserver and governor of all things. Now the Israelites Obadiah, and many others like him, did indeed take Jehovah the true Lord for their God, and as a God esteem him, utterly renouncing all idols. But most of the people in Israel being circumcised acknowledged that God which brought them out of Egypt, to wit Jehovah, to be the true God; only they worshiped him under the calves, ch. 12.28. These also worshiped Baal. To these says Elijah, *If the Lord be God*.

*{follow him}* He here advised them to deal with God as a faithful servant does with his master; he follows him whithersoever he goes; he is ready to do whatsoever he requires; he leans to him, and will not follow another. See Matth. 6.24. See also ch. 9.6.

*{but if Baal}* Here we must understand the same title that was before attributed to the Lord, *q. d.* *If Baal be God*. *Baal* in this place is taken for the proper name of that idol which they then worshiped.

*{then follow him}* He grants they had done well in following Baal, and that they might continue so to do, if they could prove that Baal were indeed God. This he grants the rather to move them to bring the case to a trial, but not to give them liberty to be of what religion they pleased.

*{And the people answered him not a word}* They who followed Jehovah dared not openly protest against Baal for fear of Ahab and Jezebel; and they who followed Baal were so convinced as they dared not plead for him: both parties therefore held their peace.

**V. 22.** *{Then said Elijah unto the people}* The prophet having convinced them that it was meet to follow but one God, shows which that God was.

*{I, even I only, remain a prophet of the LORD}* His meaning is, that there were none there present to maintain the Lord's cause but himself. The hundred which Obadiah hid, v. 4, had not yet showed themselves. See ch. 19.10, 28. God oft maintains his own cause by one, or by a few that himself may be the more seen therein.

*{but Baal’s prophets are four hundred and fifty men}* There were other four hundred, v. 19, but they appeared not. The miracle following is the more remarkable, in that one man opposed four hundred and fifty. This shows that multitude is not a certain note of the true church.

**V. 23.** *{Let them therefore give us}* This is meant of the people: or else it is indefinitely to be taken, thus, *Let there be given*; or *Let them that will give*.

*{two bullocks}* One for Elijah, the other for Baal's prophets.

*{and let them choose one bullock for themselves}* The offer is made to Baal's prophets, that the truth of the case might the more evidently appear.

*{and cut it in pieces, and lay it on wood}* All this was to prepare it for a sacrifice.

*{and put no fire under}* That the truth of the miracle might be the more evident.

*{and I will dress the other bullock}* By cutting it in pieces, and observing other rites, to make it a sacrifice.

*{and lay it on wood}* That the fire which should come from heaven, might by setting the wood on fire consume the sacrifice.

*{and put no fire under}* He ties himself to the same condition that he required of the false prophets.

**V. 24.** *{And call ye on the name of your gods}* He uses the plural number, *gods*, to show that they had many idols. And he gives them liberty to call upon all their false gods.

*{and I will call on the name of the LORD}* He holds close to his own God Jehovah, and adds no other to him.

*{and the God that answereth}* It is a note of the true God, to hear such as call upon him, and to grant their desire, Psal. 65.2.

*{by fire}* By sending fire from heaven to consume the sacrifice, as [citations cut off in the scan]. In the tabernacle and temple the fire that came from heaven was preserved, Lev. 6.12: but in Israel there was no such fire: therefore Elijah would have it called for from heaven.

*{let him be God}* Let him be reputed and esteemed as the only true God.

*{And all the people answered, and said, It is well spoken}* Heb. *The word is good*. This is a phrase of approbation and consent. The people were willing to put the case to trial.

**V. 25.** *{And Elijah said to the prophets of Baal}* He puts them upon the proof, to accept the condition.

*{Choose you one bullock for yourselves}* Two bullocks being set before them, he lets them choose which they pleased.

*{and dress it first}* This he permits, that the impotency and vanity of that idol might be first discerned, and thereby way made for acknowledging the Lord.

*{for ye are many}* A multitude, to be preferred before one.

*{and call on the name of your gods}* See v. 24.

*{but put no fire under}* See v. 23.

**V. 26.** *{And they took}* It seems they were very confident of their idol's power.

*{the bullock}* One of the bullocks which they best liked.

*{which was given them}* Heb. *which he gave them*. See the last word of this verse.

*{and they dressed it}* See v. 23.

*{and called on the name of Baal}* No doubt but they expected he would hear them.

*{from morning even until noon}* From the beginning till the farthest time that was used for the morning sacrifice.

*{saying, O Baal, hear us}* Or, *answer us*. They speak to a dumb idol as to the living God.

*{But there was no voice, nor any that answered}* Heb. *heard*. For their idol had neither tongue to utter any voice, nor ear to hear anything.

*{And they leapt upon the altar}* Or, *leaped up and down at the altar*. The word is the same that was used v. 21 and translated *halt*. Idolaters used to have many antic and strange gestures in their idol-services, as leaping upon their altars, as if they themselves were willing to be sacrificed, or as if they would themselves fetch fire from heaven, skipping also on it, and dancing round about it. To all these this phrase may have relation. See v. 30.

*{which was made}* Heb. *he made*. This is an indefinite phrase, *he*, whosoever he was. It is well expounded by the passive, *was made*; as v. 23, *let them give*. Some apply that relative *he* to the chief priest amongst them, and some to Ahab.

**V. 27.** *{And it came to pass at noon}* Elijah was silent till the very end of their time was come, lest they should say he had interrupted them.

*{that Elijah mocked them}* To discover their folly the more.

*{and said, Cry aloud}* Heb. *with a great voice*. As if otherwise he could not hear. But the true God knows the desire of the heart.

*{For he is a god}* This he says in a plain scoff, in that the things which he mentions hereafter do no way agree to the true God.

*{either he is talking}* Or, *he meditates*. So as he could not attend them that were calling on him.

*{or he is pursuing}* Heb. Or* hath a pursuit*. He means a pursuit of enemies, or beasts, or fowls, or other pleasures.

*{or he is in a journey}* As it were about another business.

*{or peradventure he sleepeth, and must be awaked}* The true God is subject to no such things. To be hindered by any of them, is human frailty. All these therefore are manifest scoffs, and great aggravations of their folly.

**V. 28.** *{And they cried aloud}* Not with ardency of affection, as saints, to the searcher of hearts; but with extension of voices, as idolaters, to such as cannot hear.

*{and cut themselves after their manner}* The manner of idolaters, whereunto God's people might not conform themselves, Lev. 19.28. Deut. 14.1.

*{with knives and lancets}* This was worse than Papists whipping of themselves.

*{till the blood gushed out upon them}* Heb. *poured out blood upon them*. The devil, delighting in man's blood, brought idolaters, in whom he rules, therewith to seem to pacify and gratify their idols. In which respect they spared not their own blood, as here; nor their children's, Ezek. 16.36.

**V. 29.** *{And it came to pass when mid-day was past}* This was the time they should have given over: for they began in the morning, v. 26. But because they had no answer from their idol, they still continue to call for one.

*{and they prophesied}* They made themselves to prophesy, or they stirred up themselves thereto. By their strange, affected, passionate gestures they made show of prophetical raptures, and in the vulgar's account prophesied. See 1 Sam. 10.5. & 18.10, etc. Or *prophesying* may here be taken for calling upon their idol to be heard. See 1 Cor. 11.3, 5. Or for singing and playing on musical instruments, 1 Chron. 25.1, 2, 3.

*{until the time of the offering}* Heb. *ascending*. For the sacrifice was laid up upon the altar; and the priest ascended to lay it there; and the smoke thereof, while it was burning, ascended up towards heaven.

*{of the evening sacrifice}* This was the ordinary time when the daily sacrifice was appointed to be offered, Exod. 29.39, which was about three in the afternoon. See v. 36.

*{that there was neither voice, nor any to answer}* No manner of evidence whereby any might discern that their god heard them.

*{nor any that regarded}* Heb. *attention*. For it might be supposed that if any had attended to what they did or spoke, they would so far have regarded them, as to have given some answer.

**V. 30.** *{And Elijah said unto all the people}* It was the people's conversion that Elijah aimed at.

*{Come near unto me}* Be eye-witnesses of my whole carriage, and diligently mark whether I use any fraud or no.

*{And all the people came near unto him}* They were willing to see whether Elijah could do more with the Lord than the false prophets had done with Baal.

*{And he repaired the altar of the LORD}* This altar was either one which of old had been erected by their pious predecessors, or which Elijah himself had beforehand prepared for this purpose.

*{that was broken down}* Either by former idolaters, or by these prophets of Baal, who in their mad leaping and dancing might spitefully break it down.

**V. 31.** *{And Elijah took twelve stones}* As Josh. 4.5, 20. Exod. 24.4.

*{according to the number of the tribes of the sons of Jacob}* To show that all the tribes should be united in the true worship of God. See Ezra 6.17. Hereby he implicitly taxes the revolt of the ten tribes from the other two.

*{unto whom the word of the LORD came, saying}* This has relation to Jacob, the immediate father of the twelve patriarchs who were heads of the twelve tribes.

*{Israel shall be thy name}* Gen. 32.28. See 2 King. 17.34.

**V. 32.** *{And with the stones he built an altar}* i. e. the twelve stones mentioned v. 31, and the altar mentioned v. 30. This altar, in this extraordinary case, justified by God himself, v. 36, 37, was lawful, though otherwise forbidden, Deut. 12.13, 14. See 1 Sam. 7.9. & 9.12. & Judg. 21.4.

*{in the name of the LORD}* At God's appointment, v. 36, to the honor of God, (compare 1 Cor. 10.31 with Col. 3.17,) dedicated to the service of God.

*{and he made a trench about the altar}* i. e. a ditch or a channel which might contain the water hereafter mentioned.

*{as great as would contain two measures of seed}* It was as wide and as deep as a bag of two pecks and a half of seed might lie in; or a furrow so much in compass as men might ordinarily sow two pecks and an half of seed in. The word translated *measure* signifies somewhat more than a peck and a pottle.

**V. 33.** *{And he put the wood in order, and cut the bullock in pieces, and laid him on the wood}* He did all things which used to be done in offering a sacrifice, Lev. 1.6, 7.

*{And said, Fill four barrels with water}* That all suspicion of hiding fire under the wood, or altar, or anywhere else might be taken away, and that the power of God and the truth of the miracle might be the more manifested. So Judg. 6.20.

*{and pour it on the burnt-sacrifice, and on the wood}* This was a likely means to keep fire from burning up the sacrifice and wood, which being wet are not so soon set on fire.

**V. 34.** *{And he said, Do it the second time}* That they might be the more wet.

*{And they did it the second time}* They were ready to do what the prophet required.

*{And he said, Do it the third time: And they did it the third time}* Thus twelve barrels of water were poured on the altar and wood. Of the number twelve see v. 31. Mount Carmel was near the sea; so as thence, notwithstanding that great drought, he might have water enough: yea, the river Kishon also was by the bottom of the mount. See v. 40. Of *Carmel* see v. 19.

**V. 35.** *{And the water ran}* Heb. *went*.

*{round about the altar, and he filled the trench also with water}* See v. 32. The filling of the trench was an evidence of the great store of water which he poured on the altar, that the reality and fervency of the fire might be more manifest.

**V. 36.** *{And it came to pass at the time of the offering of the evening sacrifice}* See v. 29. 2 King. 3.20.

*{that Elijah the prophet came near}* To the altar, where he prayed.

*{and said, LORD God of Abraham, Isaac, and of Israel}* These were the patriarchs from whom by lineal descent all the Israelites came. To each of these three God made and ratified his promises of the good which he did to Israel. God took this style to himself Exod. 3.16, to move the Israelites, that came from those patriarchs, to take him for their God, and often call to mind his promises, Exod. 32.13. See 1 Chron. 29.18.

*{let it be known this day}* He presses in his prayer three motives.

*{that thou art God in Israel}* This is the first motive, God's glory; that he might be known and acknowledged to be what he was. The people before this took Baal to be the god in Israel.

*{and that I am thy servant}* This is the second motive, that his ministry might be confirmed to be of God.

*{and that I have done all these things at thy word}* By your direction. This is the duty of God's servant.
