**V. 37.** *{Hear me O LORD, hear me}* This repetition implies great fervency. He means, that God would give an evidence of hearing him by sending down fire, as v. 24, which Baal could not do, v. 29.

*{that this people may know that thou art the LORD God}* He presses again the first motive, because it was a weighty one.

*{and that thou hast turned their heart back again}* From Baal to you. This is the third motive, the conversion of the people.

**V. 38.** *{Then the fire of the LORD fell}* Fire which came out of heaven from the Lord, as 1 Chron. 21.26. 2 Chron. 7.1. Job 1.16, that the people might better discern whence it came.

*{and consumed the burnt-sacrifice, and the wood}* This shows it was true fire, and not in appearance only.

*{and the stones, and the dust}* This effect shows that this fire had more than ordinary power and efficacy.

*{and licked up the water that was in the trench}* Though the dry earth did not soak up this water, yet this fierce fire licked it up. This was Elijah's fifth miracle. See ch. 17.1.

**V. 39.** *{And when all the people saw it, they fell on their faces}* A gesture as of astonishment, so also of adoration. See 1 Chron. 21.16.

*{and they said, The LORD he is the God, the LORD he is the God}* These words, though few, comprise much, as, 1. An acknowledgment of Jehovah to be the true God, and therein determining the opinion questioned v. 21. 2. A confirmation of him only to be so, which this emphatical phrase, *he is*, intends. 3. A renouncing of Baal. For the mentioned phrase is exclusive, *not Baal*. 4. Their confidence in this their profession implied, by the doubling of the phrase.

**V. 40.** *{And Elijah said unto them}* To the people who acknowledged Jehovah to be God.

*{Take}* Or, *apprehend*.

*{the prophets of Baal}* Elijah knew that the evidence of the miracle had so wrought upon them as they discerned the jugglings and deceits of Baal's prophets, and would not stick to see vengeance executed upon them.

*{let not one of them escape}* There must be no partiality in executing justice.

*{And they took them}* They readily yielded to his directions, whom they observed to be a true prophet of the Lord.

*{and Elijah brought them down}* He caused them to be brought.

*{to the brook Kishon}* Kishon ran along at the bottom of Carmel. This river was made famous by the overthrow of Sisera, Judg. 5.21. Thither these idolatrous prophets were brought, that their bodies, after they were slain, might be cast thereinto.

*{and slew them there}* According to the law, Deut. 13.5. & 18.20. Jehu is commended for the like, 2 King. 10.25. The verses following show that king Ahab was present; so as he did not gainsay, but rather assented. Questionless the prophet took the present advantage of the people's zeal for God, and indignation against Baal, to do what he did; the rather, because he was put on thereunto by a divine Spirit, Luk. 9.55, and because it was agreeable to God's word. The king might be moved at least tacitly to consent thereunto, by reason of that extraordinary sign of fire which was given from heaven, v. 38, and by reason of the people's open profession of the Lord, yea, also upon hope that rain might be given thereupon.

**V. 41.** *{And Elijah said to Ahab}* Now the main work which Elijah intended was accomplished, he shows to Ahab the blessing that followed thereupon, and gives him direction what to do.

*{Get thee up}* For he was in a vale by the river.

*{eat and drink}* It appears that the king as well as others had fasted all that day: so as now he advises him to refresh himself. So Act. 27.34.

*{for there is a sound of abundance of rain}* Or, *a sound of a noise of rain*. Elijah heard some noise in the heavens that foreshowed much rain. This showed his assurance of rain near approaching.

**V. 42.** *{So Ahab went up to eat and to drink}* He went from the foresaid valley to his palace at Jezreel, v. 46.

*{and Elijah went up to the top of Carmel}* He went from the people which were below, that he might be the more retired for prayer. So Christ, Matth. 17.1. Elijah's mind was more upon God, to give him thanks for hearing his prayer, v. 36, and further to call upon him for rain, than upon himself, to refresh his body by eating and drinking. He also went up to the top of the mountain, that he might the sooner see the very beginning of rain. Of *Carmel*, see v. 19.

*{and he cast himself down upon the earth}* In a most humble manner prostrating himself before God.

*{and put his face between his knees}* He bowed his face to the very earth whereon he kneeled, testifying thereby both his deep humiliation and also his fervent devotion. He was assured that rain was coming, yet he is earnest in using means for obtaining it, Jam. 5.18. So 2 Sam. 27.7. Dan. 9.2, 3. Ezek. 36.36, 37.

**V. 43.** *{And said to his servant}* Heb. *young man*. So ch. 19.3. & 20.14. He sends his servant to watch, because he meant himself to continue in prayer.

*{Go up now}* He sends him to the highest part of the mountain, that he might see the first rise of a cloud. For he longed to see some evidence of the return of his prayer.

*{look toward the sea}* Westward, whence clouds of rain first arise. See Luk. 12.55.

*{And he went up, and looked, and said, There is nothing}* God does not always hear prayer instantly; but to stir up fervency and continuance in prayer, puts off for a time, what at length he grants.

*{And said, go again seven times}* He waxed not faint, but would hold out, Luk. 18.1.

**V. 44.** *{And it came to pass at the seventh time that he said}* What at first is not granted, by perseverance may be obtained.

*{Behold, there ariseth a little cloud out of the sea like a man’s hand}* God by degrees gives evidence of his purpose. Small beginnings may have a great increase, Zech. 4.10.

*{And he said, Go up, say unto Ahab}* He gives notice to the king at the very beginning, because he knew it was joyful tidings, and because he would have Ahab prevent all damage that might arise from the shower.

*{Prepare thy chariot}* Heb. *Tie, or Bind*. The meaning is, that he should cause his horses to be brought forth, and the chariot to be fastened to them, that he might speedily be carried away.

*{and get thee down}* Elijah was on the top of the hill, and Ahab remained on a side thereof. See v. 42. Besides, this phrase implies that Jezreel was in a vale, at least lower than this place.

*{that the rain stop thee not}* This implies that it was a very great rain that was now to be given to the earth, such an one as might have hindered the king from going to his palace.

**V. 45.** *{And it came to pass in the mean while}* While Ahab was preparing his chariot, which was no long time.

*{that the heaven was black with clouds and wind}* Those were ordinary means and signs of rain.

*{and there was a great rain}* There had been a long and great drought, and the earth was exceeding dry, so as it stood in need of much rain. God orders his blessings (when he sees cause to bestow them) according to man's need. I. Judgment is God's *strange act*, Isa. 28.21. He delights not always to continue therein, Lam. 3.32, 33, 34. II. There was a mixture of pious ones with the impious, v. 4. ch. 19.18, and God had an especial respect to them. See Gen. 18.26. 2 King. 3.14. III. The people were brought to renounce their idolatry, v. 39. IV. Execution was done on idolaters. See Num. 25.8. Psal. 106.30. This rain being a fruit of Elijah's prayer, Jam. 5.17, 18, may be accounted his sixth miracle. See ch. 17.1.

*{and Ahab rode and went to Jezreel}* A city of Issachar, Josh. 19.18, where one of the king's houses was, ch. 21.1. 2 King. 8.29. & 9.30.

**V. 46.** *{And the hand of the LORD was on Elijah}* The Spirit of the Lord was after an especial manner in him, which made him more than ordinarily nimble and swift. See 2 King. 8.3, 16. Ezek. 1.3.

*{and he girded up his loins}* He tucked up his long side coat, that it might be no hindrance to him, 2 King. 4.19. & 9.1. Jer. 1.17.

*{and ran before Ahab}* So as he outran him, though he were in a chariot.

*{to the entrance of Jezreel}* Heb. *till thou come to Jezreel*. He went not into the city: for he knew Jezebel's hateful and cruel disposition. Thereby he was kept from lodging in Jezreel.

# CHAPTER 19

**V. 1.** *{And Ahab told Jezebel}* A timorous husband. First he tells his imperious wife what was done, that she might first have notice of it in the fairest manner that he could. For by some other it might in such manner have been related as she thereby might have been exasperated, not only against Elijah, but also against her husband that suffered him to do what he did.

*{all that Elijah had done}* All the particulars before mentioned, about the altar, sacrifice, fire, people's acclamation, slaughter of Baal's prophets, prediction of rain, and coming to Jezreel.

*{and withal, how he had slain}* He lays the slaughter of Baal's prophets on Elijah, to put off the blame thereof from himself.

*{all the prophets with the sword}* All that are mentioned ch. 18.22, 40. See ch. 18.20.

**V. 2.** *{Then Jezebel sent a messenger unto Elijah, saying}* She sent this messenger to affright Elijah the more; but it proved to be a warning to him to shift for himself. Certainly she supposed his courage would not suffer him to flee, or else she would not have given him such a warning beforehand: but God can infatuate wicked ones, and disappoint their mischievous intents.

*{So let the gods}* For she was a worshiper of many gods, as all that forsake the true God use to be: ch. 20.10.

*{do to me, and more also}* A form of oath, wherein the vengeance imprecated is implied in this particle *so*: as if more fully she had said, So as I threatened to him, let it be done to me. See ch. 2.23. Wicked ones are forward to bind themselves to do mischief, 2 King. 6.31. Act. 23.12.

*{if I make not thy life as the life of one of them}* By this relative *them* she means the prophets of Baal that were slain ch. 18.40. She threatens his death.

*{by to morrow about this time}* She would not then attempt it, because she knew both the king's and people's minds were filled with admiration of the great miracle, and of the blessing of rain following thereupon.

**V. 3.** *{And when he saw that}* When he perceived by Jezebel's message what she meant to do.

*{he arose and went for his life}* To keep himself safe from her fury. Or, *according to his soul*, even whither his mind carried him. This he might do lawfully. See ch. 17.3. If fear, by reason of the infirmity of his flesh, made him flee, God might in wisdom leave him to himself, lest through the great things done before by him he should be exalted above measure, as 2 Cor. 12.7.

*{and came to Beersheba, which belongeth to Judah}* A city in Simeon, but within the inheritance of Judah, Josh. 19.1, 2. It was at the utmost border, as is evident by this frequent phrase, *from Dan to Beersheba*: it was on the south, opposite to Geba, 2 King. 23.8.

*{and left his servant there}* Heb. *his boy*. See ch. 18.43. Many of the ancients and others suppose this servant of Elijah to have been the widow of Zarephath's son, mentioned ch. 17.23. He left him here to provide for himself, and that the boy might not be any occasion of discovering his master.

**V. 4.** *{But he himself went a days journey into the wilderness}* It seems the prophet was in a great strait, that he dared not trust himself in the land of Israel, nor in the land of Judah, but withdrew himself into the wilderness.

*{and came and sat down under a juniper-tree}* The word translated *a juniper-tree* is used Psal. 120.4. & Job 30.4. It is no broad spreading tree; but it was the best that in that desert he could find. It is said that serpents will not come near it, and therefore he might the more securely lie under it.

*{and he requested for himself}* Heb. *for his life*. He prayed to God that he would be pleased to take him out of this mortal miserable life.

*{that he might die}* This questionless was an effect of his frailty. The like is noted of Jonah, Jon. 4.3.

*{and said, It is enough}* That I have hitherto lived. See Deut. 3.26. Num. 11.15.

*{Now, O LORD, take away my life}* This expresses the former phrase, *he requested for himself*.

*{for I am not better than my fathers}* Of no better stock or constitution than my predecessors that have died before me. It may be he was now very old, elder than most of his ancestors, and thereupon presses this petition. Now that he is out of the clutches of persecuting idolaters, so as they could not triumph in taking him away, he desires fairly to die.

**V. 5.** *{And as he lay and slept under a juniper-tree}* Being so weary as he was, he might well sleep in the open field, as Jacob, Gen. 28.11.

*{behold, then an angel touched him}* As before ch. 17.6, 15, he was provided for by a raven, and a widow extraordinarily, so now by an angel. God has many ways to provide for his.

*{and said unto him, Arise}* See ch. 14.2.

*{and eat}* It appears by this, that he was hungry. God proves his by one trial after another.

**V. 6.** *{And he looked, and behold}* This was the more remarkable, because he knew not how that which was set before him was provided for him.

*{there was a cake baken on the coals}* Heb. *a cake of coals*. Whether the coals whereon this cake was baked were before his eyes, is not certain. An angel could quickly bring it hot from a remote place.

*{and a cruse of water}* For this was as necessary for his thirst as the cake for his hunger.

*{at his head}* Heb. *bolster*. This bolster was certainly no other than such an one as Jacob had, Gen. 28.11.

*{and he did eat and drink}* What God provides for man must accordingly be used.

*{and laid him down again}* Namely, to sleep.

**V. 7.** *{And the angel of the LORD came again the second time}* God follows his servants with his blessings.

*{and touched him}* To awaken him: for he fell asleep again.

*{and said, Arise and eat}* Eat liberally that you may be the stronger, and your strength the longer continue.

*{because the journey is too great for thee}* This he says in relation to the prophet's weakness. He was a man, and subject to human frailties; in which respect the journey would have proved too long, unless God had enabled him to hold out.

**V. 8.** *{And he arose, and did eat and drink}* He obeyed God's charge, and rested upon God's assistance.

*{and went in the strength of that meat}* By that virtue and efficacy which God gave thereunto. It may be that meat and water remained forty days in his body, and continued to nourish him, as the meal in the barrel and oil in the cruse continued three years, ch. 17.14, 15.

*{forty days and forty nights}* So much time was spent in going to and abiding in Horeb. So long time continued Moses without food, Exod. 34.28, and Christ also, Mat. 4.2. This was Elijah's seventh miracle. See ch. 17.1.

*{unto Horeb}* See ch. 8.9.

*{the mount of God}* Where God appeared to Moses, Exod. 3.1, 2, and delivered the law to Israel, Deut. 4.10, 15.

**V. 9.** *{And he came thither unto a cave}* See ch. 18.4. This cave is supposed to be that place where the Lord showed his glory to Moses, Exod. 33.21, 22.

*{and lodged there}* For this was a more convenient place than the open air in a desert.

*{and behold, the word of the LORD came unto him, and he said unto him}* An Hebrew pleonasm, to give the greater assurance that God immediately spoke to him.

*{What dost thou here, Elijah?}* Heb. *what to thee here*. This question is propounded to make way to the discourse following. Some take it to be spoken by way of reproof for Elijah's neglecting his charge. But if God had disliked his traveling in the wilderness, he would not by his extraordinary providence have enabled him to undertake such a journey as he did, and to abide forty days without food, v. 7, 8.

**V. 10.** *{And he said, I have been very jealous}* Heb. *being jealous I am jealous*. See c. 24.22. He shows that God's honor was as tender to him as a wife's honesty to her husband. So Num. 25.11. Psal. 69.7, 8.

*{for the LORD God of hosts}* See ch. 18.15.

*{for the children of Israel}* Three great crimes are alleged as occasions of Elijah's jealousy against Israel. In which respect he is said to *make intercession to God against Israel*, Rom. 11.2.

*{have forsaken thy covenant}* This is the first crime. Of *covenant* see ch. 11.11.

*{thrown down thine altars}* This is the second crime. Such altars are here meant as in former times had been erected to the Lord, which the idolatrous Israelites, as an evidence of their preferring Baal before God, had impiously demolished, ch. 18.26, 30. Some take *altars* to be here put by a trope for the *worship of God*.

*{and slain thy prophets with the sword}* A proverbial speech comprising under it any kind of death, whether by sword, stones, fire, or other instruments. This is the third crime; and it was a crying sin, Gen. 4.10. The first crime argued their folly in rejecting the mercy of God, consisting in his covenant made with them; the second showed their impiety against God; the third, their cruelty against the best men.

*{and I, even I only am left}* Rom. 11.3. This he speaks according to his own apprehension: he was alone for aught he knew to the contrary. None showed themselves in God's cause when he most zealously stood for it, ch. 18.4, 22. Psal. 74.9.

*{and they seek my life to take it away}* Persecutors of saints care not to leave any of them alive: and the more eminent saints are, the more they seek to destroy them.

**V. 11.** *{And he said}* God again said to Elijah.

*{Go forth}* For the prophet had reposed and settled himself in the cave.

*{and stand upon the mount}* Mount Horeb, v. 8.

*{before the LORD}* Where the Lord will manifest his presence.

*{And behold}* See ch. 14.10. God's terror is not to be slighted.

*{the LORD passed by}* In some sensible manifestation of his presence. Such a thing is called *the glory of the Lord*, Exod. 33.18, 19.

*{and a great and strong wind}* Such evidences of terror are here set down as were at the delivery of the law, Exod. 19.18. Heb. 12.18, etc. They were to show the power of God, to humble the prophet, and to prepare him with better heed to hearken to the still voice.

*{rent the mountains, and brake in pieces the rocks}* The wind is in the nature thereof but a vapor; yet how great things can God do thereby?

*{before the LORD}* Terrible is God's presence, when he is pleased to show forth his terror.

*{but the LORD was not in the wind}* God did not manifest his presence by any distinct voice in that terrible wind; for the prophet might have been more affrighted thereby than instructed.

*{and after the wind an earthquake}* An earthquake is made by hot vapors enclosed in the earth. It is more terrible than wind: for by it trees, houses, mountains, and the firmest things on earth have been shaken and overthrown, Isa. 29.6. Zech. 14.5. Matth. 27.51, 54. Act. 16.26. Luk. 11.13. God can add terror to terror.

*{but the LORD was not in the earthquake}* As he was not in the wind.

**V. 12.** *{And after the earthquake a fire}* Fire is more terrible than either of the two former. Nothing more dreadful to man and beast, nothing more destructive.

*{but the LORD was not in the fire}* As he was not in the wind, nor the earthquake. The wind might prefigure Hazael; the earthquake, Jehu; the fire, Elisha, v. 15, 16.

*{and after the fire a still small voice}* A distinct expression of God's mind, that might without any terror be heard and understood. This was after the forementioned terrible things, to raise him up whom before he had cast down.

**V. 13.** *{And it was so when Elijah heard it, that he wrapped his face in his mantle}* This mantle was his outermost garment, as our cloak. See v. 19. He turned it over his head, in testimony of his own unworthiness, and of his reverence to God, Ex. 3.6. Isa. 6.2. See on Gen. 24.65.

*{and went out and stood in the entering in of the cave}* God commanded Elijah to go forth and stand upon the mount: and questionless he in obedience to that charge was going forth; but upon the sight of the terrible wind, earthquake and fire, he stayed in the entering of the cave, till now hearing the still small voice he erected himself, and went out.

*{and behold, there came a voice unto him, and said}* It was the forementioned small still voice that now again spoke to him.

*{What dost thou here, Elijah?}* God here propounds the same question he did v. 9, to produce a further proof of Elijah's zeal. So Christ dealt with Peter for proof of his love, Joh. 21.15, 16, 17.

**V. 14.** *{And he said, I have been very zealous, &c.}* Elijah returns the same answer he did ver. 10, because it was the true reason of his being in that desert.

**V. 15.** *{And the LORD said unto him}* The directions following contain the substance of that which was uttered. The Lord foretells the revenge of idolaters, to stay and uphold the spirit of Elijah.

*{Go, return}* God gives him assurance of his life, whereof he doubted before.

*{on thy way}* Neither to Jezreel ch. 18.46, nor to Beersheba, v. 3, from which places he came, but to that place to which God now directed him. For that is one's way in which God appoints him to go.

*{to the wilderness of Damascus}* Which appertained to Damascus. See ch. 11.24.

*{and when thou comest, anoint Hazael to be king over Syria}* Elijah might himself do what is here enjoined, as 1 Sam. 10.1. And it is the more probable, because he is commanded to go to the place where Hazael had his abode. Or *to anoint* may here intend no more than to take order that Hazael be anointed or declared king. So much was done by the ministry of Elisha, 2 King. 8.13, and that questionless by the appointment of Elijah.

**V. 16.** *{And Jehu the son of Nimshi}* Son, i. e. *grandchild*, 2 King. 9.2. So also 2 King. 8.26. It seems Nimshi was a man of note, because thus expressly named.

*{shalt thou anoint to be king over Israel}* This was done 2 King. 9.6, by one of the children of the prophets sent by Elisha, who certainly received a charge from Elijah so to do.

*{and Elisha}* Luk. 4.27, called *Eliseus*.

*{the son of Shaphat}* An ancient and honorable name, Numb. 13.5.

*{of Abel-meholah}* See ch. 4.12.

*{shalt thou anoint to be prophet}* This may literally be taken, or figuratively, the sign put for the thing signified. And so it implies that Elijah should solemnly ordain or appoint Elisha to be a prophet.

*{in thy room}* To succeed you, and do such things as you, if alive on earth, should do. Though the Israelites were at this time great idolaters, yet would not God leave them without an eminent prophet.

**V. 17.** *{And it shall come to pass, that him that escapeth the sword of Hazael, shall Jehu slay}* The greatest destruction that Hazael wrought was at the end of Jehu's reign 2 King. 10.32, and after, 2 King. 13.2. Yet he began to destroy Israel before Jehu's time, 2 King. 8.28. And many of those who escaped Hazael's hand Jehu slew, as Joram and others, 2 King. 9.24, etc.

*{and him that escapeth from the sword of Jehu shall Elisha slay}* Some apply this to the two and forty children slain by bears upon Elisha's curse, 2 King. 2.24, etc. Others, to the multitude that perished by the famine in Samaria; which was laid upon Elisha, 2 King. 6.31. But both these being before Jehu's time, this *slaying* is to be taken of Elisha's prophetical denouncing divine vengeance against such Israelites as remained idolaters, even after Jehu had destroyed the house of Ahab. So is this word taken, Isa. 11.4. Jer. 1.10. Hos. 6.5. See Amos 5.19.

**V. 18.** *{Yet I have left me}* Or, *I will leave*. In that general apostasy of the Israelites God kept many from the evil of the time: but because these were not openly known, Elijah thought there were none left but himself. In a like case it is said that *all Israel forsook the Lord*, 2 Chron. 12.1. This God declared as an answer to Elijah's complaint, v. 10. So much the apostle affirms, Rom. 11.4, and infers that there may remain many chosen of God in corrupt times, though they be not apparently discerned by men. This God now made known to Elijah, to encourage him against his many fears and dangers, and to make him willing to go on in his ministerial function.

*{seven thousand in Israel}* A set number for an indefinite, as Matth. 18.22. He means a very great number.

*{all the knees which have not bowed unto Baal}* Bowing the knee is an act of external worship, Psal. 95.6. Such therefore as had not worshiped Baal, no not outwardly with their bodies, are here meant.

*{and every mouth which hath not kissed him}* Kissing was used to testify I. Great and entire affection, Gen. 48.12. & 50.1. II. Submissive reverence, Exod. 18.7. 1 Sam. 10.1. III. Willing and ready subjection, Psal. 2.12. In all which respects it was used by idolaters: for they would oft hug, embrace, and kiss their idols, Hos. 13.2, namely their feet, hands, or robes; as subjects do to their sovereigns, and the meaner sort to the greater, Luk. 7.38, to testify all manner of obedience. The denial of bowing the knee and kissing with the mouth shows that they before mentioned were so far from setting their hearts upon Baal as they would not make show by any outward rite of their esteeming him to be a god. They no way conformed themselves to the idolatry of the times.

**V. 19.** *{So he departed thence, and found Elisha}* It appears that he went immediately from the mount of God to the place of Elisha's abode; and after that to Damascus, whither he was directed v. 15. Here is an evidence of God's providence, that he should so seasonably meet with Elisha. See ch. 11.20.

*{the son of Shaphat}* See v. 16.

*{who was plowing with twelve yoke of oxen before him}* There were many plows going in the field where Elisha was, and several persons attending them.

*{and he with the twelfth}* i. e. with the last plow. Thence God called him to be a prophet. See Amos 7.14. Mat. 4.18.

*{and Elijah passed by him, and cast his mantle upon him}* See v. 13. It was that garment whereby he was known to be a prophet. 2 King. 2.8. Zach. 13.4. This he cast upon Elisha as a sign of his calling to be a prophet.

**V. 20.** *{And he left the oxen}* To those others that were in company, plowing with him, or attending upon him.

*{and ran after Elijah}* Hereby he showed that the Spirit of God had entered into him upon Elijah's casting his mantle on him. So Mat. 4.20, 22. & 9.9.

*{and said, Let me, I pray thee, kiss my father and my mother}* He craved leave to take a fair farewell of his friends. Of old, friends were wont at their first meeting to kiss one another, Exod. 18.6, 7, and also at their parting, Gen. 31.28. Elisha was so wrought upon by this call as he would not do an ordinary act of civility to his friends without leave of him whom now he took to be his master.

*{and then I will follow thee}* I will wholly attend upon you. In that upon Elijah's casting his mantle upon him, Elisha left all and followed him, it may be put into the catalogue of his miracles: and so it was his eighth miracle. See ch. 17.1.

*{And he said unto him, Go back again}* Heb. *Go return*. By this liberty which Elijah grants him it appears that Elisha desired to go to his friends with another mind than he that being called of Christ, said, *Let me first go bid them farewel which are at home at my house*, Luk. 9.61. He made that a pretext to depart clean away from Christ, or at least to make delay; but Elisha intends only a friendly and speedy farewell.

*{for what have I done to thee?}* Elijah shows that he no way hindered Elisha from doing what he desired: only the Spirit of God was come upon him, and he might know thereby that he was called to a great and extraordinary work, which was not in any case to be put off, Luk. 9.60.

**V. 21.** *{And he returned back from him}* Elisha for a time went from Elijah to his father's house.

*{and took a yoke of oxen, and slew them}* It is probable that these were the oxen with which he plowed; and hereby he would show that he clean left his former calling.

*{and boiled their flesh with the instruments of the oxen}* i. e. the plow itself, and all the wooden things appertaining thereunto. These he then used, first for greater expedition, they being readiest at hand and fit to burn; and also to show that he willingly parted with his former calling.

*{and gave unto the people}* To his kindred, companions and neighbors, as a farewell feast. So Luk. 5.29.

*{and they did eat}* This shows that he made a feast.

*{then he arose}* So soon as he had entertained his friends, he made no longer delay, but departed from them. See ch. 14.17.

*{and went after Elijah}* According to his promise, v. 20. This he did that he might daily observe Elijah's carriage in his function, and be instructed by him.

*{and ministered unto him}* As a continued attendant, willing and ready to do him any service.

# CHAPTER 20

**V. 1.** *{And Benhadad}* The son of that Benhadad v. 34, who spoiled sundry cities in Israel, ch. 15.20, and he whom Hazael murdered and succeeded, 2 King. 8.15.

*{the king of Syria}* Heb. *Aram*.

*{gathered all his host together}* All sorts of soldiers in his dominion.

*{and there were thirty and two kings with him}* These were kings of cities, counties, or provinces; such as Joshua destroyed in Canaan, Josh. 12.7, etc. The kind of their government, which was by one alone, and not the largeness of their dominion, gave them the title of kings.

*{and horses and chariots}* These were such as belonged both to the king of Syria himself, and also to every of the other kings. All joined together must needs make a very great army.

*{and he went up}* For Samaria was on an hill, ch. 16.24.

*{and besieged Samaria}* See chap. 16.29. This being the head city, Benhadad's besieging it shows he aimed at the whole kingdom, as 2 King. 17.5.

*{and warred against it}* i. e. made batteries and assaults.

**V. 2.** *{And he sent messengers to Ahab king of Israel}* He pretends a mind to article and make conditions.

*{into the city}* By the messengers entering into the city it appears Benhadad and Ahab were upon some treaty.

*{and said unto him, Thus saith Benhadad}* This phrase implies that the enemy would treat upon his own terms only.

**V. 3.** *{Thy silver and thy gold is mine}* This according to the letter signifies the king's treasure only.

*{thy wives also and thy children, even the goodliest are mine}* He would have them to be at his command and disposing.

**V. 4.** *{And the king of Israel answered, and said, My LORD O king}* This is an acknowledgment of fealty and subjection to the king of Syria.

*{according to thy saying}* As I conceive the purport of the message to be.

*{I am thine}* As a tributary king to you.

*{and all that I have}* I hold all in fealty under you. Fear made him take his enemy's demand in the best sense, and so to yield to it.

**V. 5.** *{And the messengers came again, and said, Thus speaketh Benhadad, saying}* Benhadad perceiving how far Ahab yielded, now stands upon higher terms, and sends him another message.

*{Although I have sent unto thee, saying, Thou shalt deliver me thy silver, and thy gold, and thy wives, and thy children}* Though my message be so interpreted by you as if I intended no more than homage and fealty to be yielded by you to me, yet my purpose is to have the possession of all, as v. 6.

**V. 6.** *{Yet I will send my servants unto thee to morrow about this time}* He would not long delay to take possession of what he demanded.

*{and they shall search thine house}* To see what is laid up therein. He would not trust Ahab upon his word.

*{and the houses of thy servants}* This Benhadad adds to his first message. And this Ahab sticks upon: For he supposed he might cause a tumult among his subjects, if without their consents he should grant away what was proper to them.

*{And it shall be, that whatsoever is pleasant}* Heb. *desirable*.

*{in thine eyes}* That which you desire to keep, and are loath to part withal.

*{they shall put it in their hand, and take it away}* They shall take it from you to bring it to me.

**V. 7.** *{Then the king of Israel called all the elders of the land}* It seems he had rashly of his own head returned the former answer: but now observing the enemy to increase more and more upon him, he takes better advice, and that of his ancient grave counselors that were with him in the city, who are here called elders. See ch. 8.1.

*{and said, Mark, I pray you, and see how this man seeketh mischief}* He gives him this title, This man, in contempt; and shows that though he pretended terms of agreement, yet he sought not sovereignty only over them, but their utter ruin and destruction.

*{for he sent unto me for my wives, and for my children, and for my silver, and for my gold}* For me to acknowledge him to have such a right in them as I to hold them under him.

*{and I denied him not}* Heb. *I kept not back from him*. I refused not to yield to that which at first I conceived him to aim at, namely to make me and mine tributary to him.

**V. 8.** *{And all the elders and all the people said unto him}* Ahab propounded his case to the elders in the audience of the people.

*{Hearken not unto him, nor consent}* To his latter demand; stand it out rather to the uttermost.

**V. 9.** *{Wherefore he said unto the messengers of Benhadad, Tell my LORD the king}* Though Ahab could not like Benhadad's imperious demands, yet being afraid to provoke him further, he continues his acknowledgment of subjection unto him.

*{All that thou didst send for to thy servant at the first, I will do}* He means that he would willingly yield himself and all his to be tributary to the king of Syria. See v. 4.

*{but this thing}* To put all presently into your hand.

*{I may not do}* He dared not be over-resolute in saying, *I will not*; but too too timorously he says, *I may not*.

*{And the messengers departed, and brought him word again}* They related to Benhadad the second answer of Ahab.

**V. 10.** *{And Benhadad sent unto him, saying}* The proud enemy, too confident upon the greatness of his army, accepts no mitigation, but stands upon the uttermost of his own demands.

*{The gods do so unto me and more also}* See ch. 19.2.

*{if the dust of Samaria shall suffice for handfuls for all the people}* A proverbial speech full of insolency. To affright his enemy the more, he boasts of the multitude of his soldiers; *q. d.* I swear that I will bring more soldiers into Samaria than there are handfuls of dust therein. Or, I will so beat down the castles, walls, and houses, as every of my soldiers shall not take up a handful of dust from the earth.

*{that follow me}* Heb. *that are of my feet*. Exod. 11.8. Judg. 4.10.

**V. 11.** *{And the king of Israel answered and said}* Now he takes more courage to himself, and forbears that slavish title, my Lord.

*{Tell him, Let not him that girdeth on his harness boast himself as he that putteth it off}* Another proverbial speech, showing that the issue of war is uncertain, and that it is a point of folly to triumph before the battle be ended.

**V. 12.** *{And it came to pass when Benhadad heard this message}* Heb. *word*. For the message was delivered not in writing, but by word of mouth.

*{(as he was drinking, he and the kings in the pavilions)}* Heb. *tents*. It appears v. 16. that he drunk so excessively as he was drunken; in which respect he could not well give good direction.

*{that he said to his servants, Set yourselves in array}* Or, *place the engines*.

*{and they set themselves in array against the city}* They approached nearer to the walls of the city, and set their engines to batter it down.

**V. 13.** *{And behold}* It is very remarkable that God should send a prophet to him under whose government the prophets of the Lord were cut off, ch. 18.4.

*{there came}* Heb. *approached*. He might be in Samaria before, but now, being sent of the Lord, he approaches nearer to the king, and comes into his presence.

*{a prophet}* i. e. A true prophet of the Lord. He might be one of those whom Obadiah hid, ch. 18.4. Some take him to be Micaiah, ch. 22.8.

*{unto Ahab king of Israel, saying, Thus saith the LORD}* Not Baal, but Jehovah the Lord. Herein God's goodness is manifested to an impious king.

*{Hast thou seen all this great multitude?}* From the towers of Samaria they might well discern the whole compass of the enemy's camp. This interrogation served to move him the better to heed the message; as did also the note of attention following.

*{Behold, I will deliver it into thine hand this day}* He sets down the present time of vanquishing that army, the more to pull down the pride and insolency of Benhadad, manifested v. 6,10.

*{and thou shalt know that I am the LORD}* This was the end why God foretold deliverance and victory following, lest Ahab should ascribe it to his idols, or to chance. See v. 28. There was enough done ch. 18.39. to make Ahab and all Israel know that the Lord was God; but wicked ones soon forget the evidences which God gives of himself, though never so clear.

**V. 14.** *{And Ahab said, By whom?}* The most wicked in their distress can seek direction of God.

*{And he said, Thus saith the LORD}* The prophet would not give directions of himself, but what he had received from the Lord.

*{Even by the young men}* Heb. *servants*. The Hebrew word is oft put for attendants or servants; as chap. 18.43. Josh. 1.1. 1 Sam. 2.13,15.

*{of the princes of the provinces}* The young men here meant were either the sons of the princes, or their pages and attendants. In man's opinion such were very unfit for such a work. Experienced captains and commanders in war one would think should have been employed. But God so ordered it that the victory might more manifestly appear to be from him. So Judg. 7.2.

*{Then he said}* Ahab still seeks in his great strait to be more and more instructed by God.

*{Who shall order the battle?}* Heb. *bind, *or,* tie*. i. e. set in array, as 2 Chr. 13.3. and lead out, and set first on the enemy.

*{And he answered, Thou}* i. e. the king himself, that so the young men might the more willingly undertake that task.

**V. 15.** *{Then he numbered the young men of the princes of the provinces}* This is an evidence of Ahab's giving credit to the word of the Lord.

*{and they were two hundred and thirty two}* These were to be captains and leaders unto the rest.

*{and after them he numbered all the people, even all the children of Israel}* He means [words obscured] were fit to bear arms in Samaria.

*{being seven thousand}* This was but a small number to set upon so great an army as lay against Samaria. It is the number of those that remained faithful with the Lord, ch. 19.18. and thereupon some infer that these were those very same persons; but without sufficient ground.

**V. 16.** *{And they went out at noon}* A time wherein it was likely the enemy would be more busy about refreshing his body than preparing for war: and so it fell out, as the words following show.

*{but Benhadad was drinking himself drunk, &c.}* See v. 12.

**V. 17.** *{And the young men of the princes of the provinces went out first}* God put such courage into them, to embolden the rest of the army to follow them. They join together as a forlorn hope, first to set upon the enemy.

*{and Benhadad sent out}* For he had intelligence that some marched out of the city, as the words following show.

*{and they told him, saying}* The scouts, that were to espy what the enemy did, gave him notice of their coming out.

*{There are men come out of Samaria}* It is probable that only the aforesaid forlorn hope had then appeared out of the city.

**V. 18.** *{And he said}* i. e. Benhadad.

*{whether they be come out for peace, take them alive}* It was against the law of arms to surprise ambassadors of peace. Their message should have been heard.

*{or whether they be come out for war}* In his pride he thinks they were not able to make a battle.

*{take them alive}* By this it seems that he feared no opposition; but he would have them so taken as he might put them to death, or imprison them, or otherwise deal with them as he should see cause.

**V. 19.** *{So these young men of the princes of the provinces came out of the city}* See v. 14.

*{and the army which followed them}* Namely, the seven thousand mentioned v. 15.

**V. 20.** *{And they slew every one his man}* i. e. seven thousand two hundred thirty and two, at the least, even as many of the enemies that came to apprehend them as they themselves were in number.

*{and the Syrians fled}* This first success of the Israelites possessed the Syrians with such fear as they dared not stand to it.

*{and Israel pursued them}* They followed the good success which they had in the beginning. See 2 Chr. 13.19.

*{and Benhadad the king of Syria escaped on an horse, with the horsemen}* They which had means of a speedy flight left the rest of the army to shift for themselves.

**V. 21.** *{And the king of Israel went out}* With such troops of horse as he had he pursued the enemy.

*{and smote the horses and chariots}* This shows that all the horsemen escaped not.

*{and slew the Syrians with a great slaughter}* For when fear once possesses an enemy and makes him fly, he is an easy prey to such as pursue him.

**V. 22.** *{And the prophet came to the king of Israel}* That prophet which first foretold this victory, v. 13.

*{and said unto him}* Having by divine revelation knowledge of the enemies' purposes, 2 King. 6.12.

*{Go strengthen thyself}* This may be applied both to external preparations; and also to spiritual duties of turning unto God and seeking his assistance. With outward means, faith, repentance and prayer must be joined.

*{and mark and see what thou doest}* Be not secure and careless, as if you were free from all fear of further danger.

*{for at the return of the year}* When the time is seasonable for war. 1 Chr. 20.1. See 2 Sam. 11.1.

*{the king of Syria will come up against thee}* Enemies once spoiled, if they can recruit their army, will not rest, but try again and again how they may recover their loss. See 1 Chr. 14.13.

**V. 23.** *{And the servants of the king of Syria said unto him}* They are forward to put on their king to another battle, and yet consider not what were the most proper causes of their overthrow, as their insolency, sensuality, intemperancy, security, and the like vices.

*{Their gods are gods of the hills, therefore they were stronger than we}* The heathen conceived there were a multitude of gods; some for the air, some for the waters, some for the earth, some dwelling upon hills, who would therefore protect their dwelling-places. This last they say of Israel's gods, because the Israelites had many high places on hills where they worshiped their gods.

*{but let us fight against them in the plain}* For Samaria, about which they fought before, was on a mountain, ch. 16.24.

*{and surely}* Heb. *if not*. This is a concise speech, ordinarily used in oaths. To make up the sense something must be supplied; as see, or try. So Gen. 14.23.

*{we shall be stronger than they}* Their presumption upon a superstitious conceit implunges them into a greater danger.

**V. 24.** *{And do this thing, Take the kings away, every man out of his place}* Kings use not to be expert and hardy enough for difficult and dangerous exploits, nor so submissive to military discipline as captains. And as for the kings used before by the Syrians, they had been by their intemperancy an ill example to the rest of the army, v. 16.

*{and put captains in their rooms}* They mean captains of Benhadad's own dominions and under his subjection, which were like to stand out more courageously.

**V. 25.** *{And number thee an army like}* Like in number, and prowess and all warlike abilities.

*{the army that thou hast lost}* Heb. *that was fallen*.

*{horse for horse, and chariot for chariot}* It seems they were before as well prepared and furnished as they could be, and therefore they desire but the like.

*{and we will fight against them in the plain, and surely, &c.}* See v. 23.

*{And he hearkened unto their voice, and did so}* Their counsel seemed so good to him as he saw no reason to gainsay it.

**V. 26.** *{And it came to pass at the return of the year}* See v. 22.

*{that Benhadad numbered the Syrians}* Prepared an army of his own subjects.

*{and went up to Aphek}* A city of Asher bordering upon the Sidonians, Josh. 13.4. & 19.30. Here sundry of the Canaanites continued to dwell, Judg. 1.31. At this time the Syrians had some command there. See v. 30. Benhadad might choose the plain near this city, there to set his battle in array, that in case he should be again worsted in the field, he might have the city to flee unto: and so it happened v. 30.

*{to fight against Israel}* Heb. *to war with Israel*. That they might recover their former loss, v. 10,11.

**V. 27.** *{And the children of Israel were numbered}* Were brought into array.

*{and were all present}* Or, *were victualed*. And it may be meant of persons, or things: All that were appointed came to the battle; or, All necessaries were provided for them; or, All that were in the former battle were safe, and none of them slain.

*{and went against them}* To meet them, and enter battle against them.

*{and the children of Israel pitched before them, like two little flocks of kids, but the Syrians filled the country}* The Israelites are said to be like little flocks, because few in number, compared to the huge host of the Syrians: they are said to be flocks of kids, because weak and not furnished with warlike provisions as the Syrians were: So as both in number and warlike preparations the Syrians much exceeded the Israelites. Some conceive that one of the flocks were the two hundred and thirty and two young men, and the other the seven thousand people mentioned v. 15.

**V. 28.** *{And there came a man of God}* The prophet mentioned v. 13,22.

*{and spake unto the king of Israel and said, Thus saith the LORD}* Thus he shows that he came not of his own head, but came from God, and delivered God's word; that so the more credit might be given to him.

*{Because the Syrians have said, The LORD is God of the hills, but he is not God of the valleys}* Herein they derogated much from God, as if he could not manifest his power when and where he please. See v. 23.

*{therefore will I deliver all this great multitude into thine hand}* This inference shows that God had not respect to the Israelites, who were gross idolaters, both king and people, and notoriously wicked, but to his own name, in giving the success which he did. His name being blasphemed by the enemies, he would take just vengeance on them. See Ezek. 36.21,22.

*{and ye shall know that I am the LORD}* See v. 13. Two reasons are here rendered of God's giving the Syrians into the hands of the Israelites; one to discover the vanity of that conceit which the Syrians had of God; the other, to give evidence to the Israelites that the Lord was the only true God.

**V. 29.** *{And they pitched one over against the other}* By this it appears that the two armies stood facing one another, expecting whether should make the first onset.

*{seven days}* So long they continued in battle-array, as 1 Sam. 17.2,3. It must needs be that one of those days was the Sabbath, as Josh. 6.15.

*{and so it was, that in the seventh day the battle was joined}* i. e. the seventh day of their standing in array, not the seventh day of the week, commonly called the Sabbath.

*{and the children of Israel slew of the Syrians an hundred thousand footmen in one day}* These were many more than every one his man, as v. 20.

**V. 30.** *{But the rest fled to Aphek into the city}* This shows that Aphek was now in the Syrians' power. See v. 26.

*{and there a wall fell upon twenty and seven thousand of the men that were left}* i. e. the wall of the city, or at least of some forts and towers. This wall might fall by the Israelites undermining it, or by some earthquake, or by the earth sinking under it, or by God's secret providence, as Josh. 6.20. This place they fled unto for succor, but it proved destructive.

*{and Benhadad fled}* See v. 20.

*{and came into the city into an inner chamber}* Or, *from chamber to chamber*. Heb. *into a chamber within a chamber*. He knew not where securely to abide, but went up and down and sought where he might best hide himself. See ch. 22.25. His pride is now turned into fear.

**V. 31.** *{And his servants said unto him}* Observing him to be perplexed they give him the best advice they could.

*{Behold now we have heard that the kings of the house of Israel are merciful kings}* Heb. *Kings of mercy*. This may be meant of David and Solomon. Or there might be acts of mercy done by kings of Israel to their enemies, which were famous among other nations, though not recorded in Scripture. Acts of great cruelty done by Baasha, Zimri, and others are registered.

*{let us, I pray thee, put sackcloth on our loins}* In token of sorrow for taking up arms against Israel. See on Gen. 37.34.

*{and ropes on our heads}* In token of their deserving death.

*{and go out to the king of Israel}* They are careful to use all means for obtaining mercy of him whom they thought to be merciful. And have not we heard that God is merciful? Why then do we not penitently seek mercy of him?

*{peradventure he will save thy life}* They would make a trial of obtaining that which they were not sure of.

**V. 32.** *{So they girded sackcloth on their loins, and put ropes on their heads}* See ver. 31. They could readily follow good advice for their lives.

*{and came to the king of Israel, and said, Thy servant Benhadad saith}* He that thought to have trampled on the king of Israel, v. 6. now acknowledges himself to be his servant.

*{I pray thee, let me live}* Nothing is now desired but mere quarter, to have his life saved.

*{And he said, is he yet alive?}* He supposed he might have been slain with the multitude.

*{he is my brother}* Of the same calling whereof I am; and one whom I respect as a brother. See ch. 9.13. How foolish a courtesy this was appears v. 42.

**V. 33.** *{Now the men did diligently observe whether any thing would come from him}* Any words that they might work upon, or from which they might raise any ground of hope.

*{and did hastily catch it}* Heb. *hastened and caught*. They presently took the word out of his mouth; or, took occasion to make a present reply.

*{and they said, Thy brother Benhadad}* They use the title brother in relation to that which Ahab had used. Their speech is concise and imperfect; *q. d.* He whom you vouchsafe to call brother yet lives.

*{Then he said}* Ahab was so ready to return his answer as he did not stay till they had made out their full speech.

*{Go ye, and bring him}* He so uttered these words as they perceived he intended good will to their king.

*{Then Benhadad came forth to him}* Certainly he feared no ill.

*{and he caused him to come up into the chariot}* He adds to mercy great honor.

**V. 34.** *{And Benhadad said unto him, The cities which my father took from thy father}* The word father may be indefinitely taken for predecessor; and so this have relation to those cities which Benhadad took from Baasha, ch. 15.20. Or he might also take some cities from Omri which are not mentioned in the sacred history; and so the title father here in both places is literally understood.

*{I will restore}* It appears by Benhadad's holding Ramoth-Gilead, ch. 22.4. that he did not to the full perform his word.

*{and thou shalt make streets for thee in Damascus}* He means places in Damascus for Israelites or others upon Ahab's warrant to walk and trade in, or markets out of which Ahab might as he pleased have toll, or places where Ahab might appoint judges to hear his people's causes, or garrisons of Ahab's soldiers.

*{as my father made in Samaria}* By this it seems that the former king of Syria had great power over the king of Israel.

*{Then said Ahab, I will send thee away with this covenant}* He was too hasty in accepting such a covenant.

*{So he made a covenant with him}* Having made promise, to keep it was a just thing.

*{and sent him away}* Set him free, to go to his own country again, and there to reign as before. Herein he dealt too remissly, as appears v. 42.

**V. 35.** *{And a certain man of the sons of the prophets}* See ch. 18.4. He is called a prophet v. 38.

*{said unto his neighbour}* By neighbor is meant one of the same profession, even another of the sons of the prophets.

*{in the word of the LORD}* By the Lord's command, which he made known to his neighbor, as appears v. 36.

*{Smite me, I pray thee}* So smite me as the stroke may be seen, and they that look on me may perceive that I have been smitten. He was disguised, so as they who saw him wounded might take him to be a soldier. Herein he was a sign to the king of Israel, to show that he also should be smitten, as Isa. 20.2,3. Jer. 27.2. &c. Ezek. 24.24.

*{And the man refused to smite him}* At the first this may seem a just refusal, because there seems no just cause why he should smite him: but if we consider the warrant, in this phrase, *in the word of the Lord*, it will appear a rebellious refusal.

**V. 36.** *{Then said he unto him, because thou hast not obeyed the voice of the LORD}* This shows that what the prophet required of his neighbor was from the Lord, and that he had told him as much.

*{Behold, as soon as thou art departed from me, a lion shall slay thee}* We read of a like judgment, ch. 13.24. This further prefigures the extent of the judgment that should fall upon Ahab, even the taking away of his life, ch. 22.34,37.

*{And as soon as he was departed from him, a lion found him and slew him}* God's threatenings are executed accordingly, and oftentimes speedily. See ch. 13.24.

**V. 37.** *{Then he found another man}* It is probable that this man was also one of the sons of the prophets.

*{and said, Smite me I pray thee}* This charge was also given upon the same warrant that is mentioned v. 35.

*{And the man smote him}* This being well done by this man shows that God's word must be obeyed though it seem unreasonable to man's reason.

*{so that in smiting he wounded him}* Heb. *smiting and wounding*. He so wounded him as blood followed thereupon. This the servant of God was willing to endure at God's command. So Christ, Isa. 53.5.

**V. 38.** *{So the prophet departed, and waited for the king by the way}* This was the main end why he was so wounded, that the king might see it.

*{and disguised himself with ashes upon his face}* These ashes were so sprinkled or laid on his face besmeared with blood as it could not readily be known who he was. Hebrew expositors say that he disguised himself with a cover on his face. See ch. 14.2. & 22.30. At the first he would not be known to the king who he was, that the king might the more impartially give his judgment on the case propounded.

**V. 39.** *{And as the king passed by, he cried unto the king, and he said}* He was the more earnest in calling upon the king that the king might the better heed the case.

*{Thy servant went out in the midst of the battle}* He relates his mind by way of parable, that Ahab not discerning how it concerned himself might be made a judge against himself, as David was 2 Sam. 12.1, &c.

*{and behold, a man turned aside}* By this man he means a commander in war, under whom we might serve.

*{and brought a man to me}* i. e. a prisoner taken in war.

*{and said, Keep this man}* Hold him fast, as a prisoner, that he make not an escape.

*{if by any means he be missing}* Heb. *if by missing he be missed*.

*{then shall thy life be for his life}* He would have it supposed he was a marshal or officer to keep prisoners taken in war; and his letting go of a prisoner was capital. To pay life for life is to be put to death, Exod. 11.23.

*{or else thou shalt pay}* Heb. *weigh*. In those days, and those places they passed money one from another by weighing it. It seems that commanders in war had in the case mentioned power to ransom a capital crime by a sum of money.

*{a talent of silver}* See ch. 16.24. & 1 Chron. 22.14.

**V. 40.** *{And as thy servant was busy here and there, he was gone}* Heb. *he was not*. For he that escapes from another, and cannot be found again, is as if he were not: He means the prisoner slipped away.

*{And the king of Israel said unto him, So shall thy judgment be}* So as you have said; either to render yourself, or a talent of silver.

*{thyself hast decided it}* By testifying that you took the prisoner on the condition expressed. Thus the king also decided his case against himself.

**V. 41.** *{And he hasted}* So soon as that was effected which he intended, the prophet makes known himself.

*{and took the ashes away from his face}* By wiping his face clean that so it might be known who he was. And, if he had a cover on his face, that also he took away.

*{and the king of Israel discerned him that he was of the prophets}* He either knew him before, or else by some gesture or habit proper to the prophets he came to know that he was a prophet.

**V. 42.** *{And he said unto him, Thus saith the LORD}* This he premises to astonish the king the more. See ch. 11.31. & 14.7.

*{Because thou hast let go out of thy hand}* Here the prophet applies the parable mentioned, v. 39. By that full victory which God gave unto Ahab, he had committed Benhadad into his hand, to be destroyed by him, as he gave Agag into Saul's hands, 1 Sam. 15.8. But as Saul, so Ahab let God's prisoner make an escape.

*{a man whom I appointed to utter destruction}* Heb. *a man of my curse*. It is probable that the prophet who foretold the victory v. 18. gave also in charge that the king as well as other Syrians should be destroyed. But if such an express charge were not given, yet the law enjoined as much, Deut. 7.2. The word whereby Achan's sin is set forth, Josh. 7.1. is here used. See 1 Sam. 15.18, &c. Benhadad is styled a man of God's curse in sundry respects. I. His stock was a mortal enemy to God's people, 2 Sam. 8.5. & 10.6. 1 Kings 11.25. Much enmity is hereafter noted of the Syrians against Israel. II. This man's father had done great spoil in Israel, ch. 15.20. III. He himself would have been a lord over all Israel. His besieging of their head city implies as much. See v. 1. IV. He manifested extreme insolency and cruelty, v. 6,10. V. He vilified the God of Israel, v. 28.

*{therefore thy life shall go for his life}* See v. 39. In that you have suffered him to escape, you yourself shall die for it. See this accomplished ch. 22.34, &c.

*{and thy people for his people}* Sins of kings oft prove very dangerous to their people, 2 Sam. 24.1. Ahab's people had made themselves accessory to his sin, and therefore were punished with him.

*{And the king of Israel went to his house heavy and displeased}* At the prophet's words, whereby the joy and glory of the victory was turned away, as 2 Sam. 19. See ch. 21.4.

*{and came to Samaria}* Or, *when he came*. For the king's palace was there. God's threatenings work upon the most stubborn, and dejects them.

# CHAPTER 21

**V. 1.** *{And it came to pass after these things}* The evidences, which the Lord gave of himself to be God, ch. 18.39. the bringing of rain, ch. 18.45. the two victories against the Syrians, ch. 20.21,29. are the things here intended: And they were all great evidences of God's goodness to Ahab and his people. Yet they alter not his corrupt disposition. No mercies will thoroughly work upon impious persons.

*{that Naboth the Jezreelite had a vineyard}* This clause does not so depend upon the forementioned circumstance of time as if Naboth had obtained his vineyard after those things before named; for he had it long before from his father, v. 3. but it sets down the ground of Ahab's coveting it; Naboth having a vineyard, Ahab desired it.

*{which was in Jezreel}* See ch. 18.45.

*{hard by the palace of Ahab king of Samaria}* By this means Ahab's eye was oft set upon the vineyard, which made him the more to long after it. See Josh. 7.21. Covetousness is called *the lust of the eyes*, 1 John 2.16.

**V. 2.** *{And Ahab spake unto Naboth, saying, Give me thy vineyard}* Ahab does not intend that Naboth should freely give him the vineyard, but rather that he might have it by sale or exchange.

*{that I may have it for a garden of herbs}* Vineyards by their usual fruit are profitable: but Ahab's purpose was to make it a pleasant place for him to walk in and to see variety of herbs and flowers.

*{because it is near unto my house}* See v. 1.

*{and I will give thee for it a better vineyard than it}* He means a vineyard better for profit. This was a fair pretense.

*{or if it seem good to thee}* Heb. *good in your eyes*. If you shall conceive that more benefit does accrue to you by money.

*{I will give thee the worth of it in money}* This shows that his aim was not to have it without valuable consideration.

**V. 3.** *{And Naboth said to Ahab, The LORD forbid it me}* Heb. *This be abomination to me from the Lord*. i. e. The Lord keep me from this as from an abominable thing. This phrase sets out an utter detesting of a thing, Gen. 44.7. Josh. 22.29.

*{that I should give the inheritance of my fathers unto thee}* To alienate inheritances was expressly forbidden, Levit. 25.23. Numb. 36.7. Ezek. 46.18.

**V. 4.** *{And Ahab came into his house heavy and displeased}* He was very sad and discontented. v. 5. See ch. 20.43.

*{because of the word which Naboth the Jezreelite had spoken unto him}* Naboth's answer was very reasonable, and grounded upon God's word; but because it crossed Ahab's humor and desires, he was perplexed in his spirit.

*{for he said, I will not give thee, &c.}* See v. 3,7.

*{and he laid him down upon his bed}* A place fit, as for weak bodies, so for disquieted spirits.

*{and turned away his face}* He turned it in discontent from the company about him, as if he would have none to speak to him.

*{and would eat no bread}* He refused to take any sustenance, as if he would starve himself. A man crossed in his ambitious, voluptuous, or covetous humor, is weary of life.

**V. 5.** *{But Jezebel his wife}* See ch. 16.31.

*{came to him, and said unto him}* Herein she seems to perform the duty of a kind wife.

*{Why is thy spirit so sad, that thou eatest no bread?}* This question implies that she yet knew nothing of the case: for Ahab had concealed it in his own breast.

**V. 6.** *{And he said unto her, Because I spake unto Naboth the Jezreelite, &c.}* See v. 2.

*{And he answered, I will not give thee my vineyard}* He does not faithfully relate Naboth's answer, which was this, *The inheritance of my fathers*, v. 3. In which phrase he shows the reason of his refusal, which was, Because it belonged to that family, and might not be alienated.

**V. 7.** *{And Jezebel his wife said unto him}* Herein she shows herself like unto her grandmother Eve, Gen. 3.6.

*{Dost thou now govern the kingdom of Israel?}* By this interrogative she does most impiously and ungraciously imply that it is the part of a supreme governor to take from his subjects what he lists, by fair or foul means: and she checks him for his remissness in not using the power he has to violence.

*{arise}* A phrase of quickening and inciting one to do a thing speedily. See ch. 14.2.

*{and eat bread}* She would not have him any longer to forbear food, lest his abstinence might prove prejudicial unto him.

*{and let thy heart be merry}* Heb. *let your heart be good*. i. e. Cast off this sadness of spirit. So Judg. 16.25. 1 Sam. 25.36.

*{I will give thee the vineyard of Naboth the Jezreelite}* She would take a course to get Naboth's vineyard for her husband. Though he could not accomplish what he desired, yet she would undertake to do it.

**V. 8.** *{So she wrote letters in Ahab's name, and sealed them with his seal}* She used the king's authority, as Haman did, Esth. 3.12. If she did this without the king's consent, he suffered her to have too much power, in that she could at her pleasure use his secretary and signet, yea, and it may be get the king's hand to a blank, and write under it what she pleased. If she did it with his consent, then was he a principal actor in that murder, as well as she.

*{and sent the letters unto the elders}* Under these elders the chief magistrates of that city are comprised. See ch. 20.7.

*{and to the nobles}* Heb. *the white ones*. For great and noble persons were wont to go in white or fine robes, Gen. 41.42. Esth. 8.15. See Neh. 2.16. It may be these were Ahab's or Jezebel's creatures, raised to their dignities by one or both of them; which made them so ready to serve them in what they did command.

*{that were in his city dwelling with Naboth}* The city here meant was Jezreel. It is said to be his city, because Naboth there dwelt, and was a prime man therein. And it is probable that he was a pious man, in that he preferred the law of the eternal God before the will of a mortal king, v. 3.

**V. 9.** *{And she wrote in the letters, saying, Proclaim a fast}* This was done that all might be thought to be done out of zeal to God's glory. This much aggravated her impiety, being such a fault as is condemned Isa. 58.4,5. At their fasts they were wont to inquire after heinous sinners; and execute the law on them, that thereby they might obtain reconciliation with God. Num. 25.7,8. Psal. 106.30. See on Eccl. 4.6.

*{and set Naboth on high among the people}* Heb. *in the top of the people*. Hereby is meant that he was set in a high place according to his dignity, that they might not seem to have plotted what they did against him. Or some scaffold may be here meant, whereon, having caused Naboth to come from among the people, they might set him before the judges.

**V. 10.** *{And set two men}* For the law required that there should be two witnesses at least to condemn a man, Deut. 19.15. So dealt they with Christ, Matth. 26.60. and with Stephen, Act. 6.13.

*{sons of Belial}* This phrase sets out men of a most desperate condition, who care not what they do. Belial signifies *without yoke*, or *without profit*. See on Judg. 19.22. 1 Sam. 2.12. This title is attributed to the devil, 2 Cor. 6.15.

*{before him to bear witness against him}* Otherwise they could have had no pretext for putting him to death.

*{saying, Thou didst blaspheme}* The Hebrew word properly signifies *to bless*. For the Jews so abhorred blasphemy against God as they would not have the sound of blasphemy to be joined with the name of God, but expressed it under a word of the contrary sense. So Job 1.5. See ch. 14.24. & on 1 Cor. 16.22.

*{God and the king}* These two are joined to work in such as heard it a greater detestation of the crime, that they who slighted blasphemy in relation to the king, might be moved with it in relation to God: And they who regarded not God, might be moved at the name of the king; to blaspheme whom also was forbidden, Exod. 22.28.

*{and then carry him out, and stone him that he may die}* Both according to the law, Lev. 24.14. A blasphemer was carried out, that he might not breathe out his spirit in the city; and he was stoned, that there might be many hands in putting him to death. Sundry enormous impieties were in this letter: as 1. Abuse of the king's name and seal. 2. Causing elders and nobles to have their hand in such a fact. 3. Profaning a solemn day of fast. 4. Making a just man an open shame before all the people. 5. Suborning false witnesses. 6. Choosing the worst of men thereunto. 7. Laying the most notorious crime to his charge. 8. Prosecuting him to a most shameful and painful death.

**V. 11.** *{And the men of his city}* Such citizens as had votes in city-businesses. See v. 8.

*{even the elders, and the nobles, who were the inhabitants of his city}* See v. 8.

*{did as Jezebel had sent unto them}* This in general sets out their yielding to her impious and cruel command.

*{and as it was written in the letters which she had sent unto them}* This implies that they did execute her bloody plot in all particulars. Though it were a most horrible act not only against God's word, but also against the light of nature, yet they having forsaken the Lord, and made idols their God, and all this at the king's command, are soon brought to prefer the king's pleasure before God's word. Some, to mitigate the elders' fault, say that Jezebel suggested that the king and Naboth being together without any in their company, Naboth blasphemed; but the king had no witness to testify it, and therefore moved them to get some witnesses, assuring them that it was a truth which they should witness. But certainly Jezebel's imperiousness wrought upon their slavishness.

**V. 12.** *{They proclaimed a fast, &c.}* In this and the next verse is set down how punctually they observed all things in Jezebel's letter.

**V. 13.** *{And there came in two men, &c.}* See v. 10.

*{and stoned him with stones that he died}* His children also were stoned by them, 2 King. 9.26. This in a just cause had been agreeable to some extraordinary practices, both of God, Num. 16.31. and also of men, Josh. 7.24. But it was expressly against the law, Deut. 24.16. Yet these creatures of Jezebel so far proceed, that none might be left to lay claim to the vineyard, and that so it might escheat to the king. See on Num. 15.35,36.

**V. 14.** *{Then they sent to Jezebel, saying, Naboth is stoned and is dead}* Hereby they give evidence of their readiness to execute the queen's cruel design, and that all was made sure, in that Naboth was actually dead. Paul was stoned, but not dead, Act. 14.19.

**V. 15.** *{And it came to pass when Jezebel heard that Naboth was stoned & was dead}* It seems Jezebel was not forward to put on Ahab with violence to enter into the vineyard till he that hindered was taken out of the way.

*{that Jezebel said to Ahab, arise}* See v. 7. Gen. 19.15.

*{take possession of the vineyard of Naboth the Jezreelite}* This was the main end of her plot, and therefore she would have it put in execution.

*{which he refused to give thee for money}* See v. 2,3.

*{for Naboth is not alive but dead}* Some think that Naboth was of kin to Ahab, and that he and his children being dead the right of inheritance fell to the king. This they gather from the near situation of Naboth's vineyard to the palace, v. 1. But Naboth being so cruelly dealt withal, we may well think that none of his kindred dared challenge their right to hinder the king's possession.

**V. 16.** *{And it came to pass when Ahab heard that Naboth was dead, that Ahab rose up}* Ahab could quickly hearken to the counsel his wife gave him, being agreeable to his corrupt humor. His heaviness is now changed.

*{to go down to the vineyard of Naboth the Jezreelite to take possession of it}* That it might remain to him and his forever.

**V. 17.** *{And the word of the LORD came}* Soon can God find out the wicked in their wickedness.

*{to Elijah}* One that feared not the face of Ahab, ch. 18.18.

*{the Tishbite, saying}* See ch. 17.1.

**V. 18.** *{Arise, go down to meet Ahab king of Israel}* Jezebel was not more ready to stir up Ahab to enter upon Naboth's vineyard, v. 15. than God is to hasten his prophet to carry heavy tidings unto him for his unjust and cruel act.

*{which is in Samaria}* This may be applied to the king himself, who at that instant when God spoke to Elijah might be in Samaria. Or he might be said to be there because there was his dwelling-place, or because his throne was there, and there he reigned. And thus he is distinguished from the king of Judah, who was in Jerusalem, and there reigned, ch. 22.42. and yet is called *King of Israel*, 2 Chr. 21.2. So as one king of Israel was in Samaria, another king of Israel was in Jerusalem. This phrase *is in Samaria*, may also have reference to the word Israel, and so signify that part of Israel which belonged to Samaria: for all the twelve tribes were called Israel.

*{behold, he is in the vineyard of Naboth}* It was indeed strange that so soon as he had taken away the life of Naboth he should enter upon his inheritance. He was now going towards the vineyard, and because he would be there by the time that Elijah should come unto him, he is said to be *in the vineyard*, so as there Elijah should find him. Suppose Ahab to be in Samaria when God sent Elijah, and in the vineyard when Elijah met with him, and these two phrases, *which is in Samaria*, and *he is in the vineyard*, may well stand together.

*{whither he is gone down to possess it}* This much aggravated the cruelty done to Naboth.

**V. 19.** *{And thou shalt speak unto him, saying, Thus saith the LORD}* See ch. 11.31. & 14.7.

*{Hast thou killed}* Have you not feared to murder an innocent man? He is said to have killed Naboth, Because through his covetous desire of the vineyard he was the first occasion of the murder. 2. Because (as is probable) he was acquainted with the whole carriage of the matter. 3. Because he punished not the actors thereof. 4. Because he approved and ratified it by taking possession.

*{and also taken possession}* Was it not enough to take away his life, but you must also alienate the inheritance from the family? See v. 3. The interrogation was to convince Ahab the more thoroughly of the heinousness of his sin; and it imports much emphasis.

*{And thou shalt speak unto him, saying, Thus saith the LORD}* The doubling of the prophet's warrant could not but much astonish him.

*{In the place where dogs licked the blood of Naboth, shall dogs lick thy blood}* Or *Like as dogs*. The substance of this threatening was accomplished on Ahab himself, in that dogs licked his blood; and so was the circumstance of place also in part, ch. 22.38. But in Ahab's son both the substance and the circumstance of this doom was expressly fulfilled, 2 King. 9.25,26. Dogs are said to lick Naboth's blood, because he died as a malefactor. For malefactors' blood being spilled on the ground, dogs that came by used to lick it up.

*{even thine}* This repetition shows that the judgment should not wholly be put off to Ahab's wife or son, but should even also be executed upon himself.

**V. 20.** *{And Ahab said to Elijah, Hast thou found me}* Are you so envious against me and audacious as to dog me and meet me wheresoever I am?

*{O mine enemy}* You ever carry yourself as an enemy to me, never speaking good, but evil unto me. Indeed Ahab was an enemy to Elijah, and hated him, as he did Micaiah, ch. 22.8. and thereupon thinks that Elijah was his enemy. Wicked persons count such their enemies as tell them of their faults, and denounce God's judgments against them; Yea, they think that all whom they hate, hate them.

*{And he answered, I have found thee}* Elijah had such a spirit as he was not daunted with the king's opprobrious speech, but plainly tells him to his face, that he had indeed sought him and found him, and that he had cause to come to him, and denounce God's judgments against him.

*{because thou hast sold thyself to work evil}* 2 Kings 17.17. He was not only sold under sin, but he sold himself. A regenerate man may passively be sold under sin, in that by the flesh remaining in him he is so held down in a kind of spiritual bondage as, when he would do good, evil is present with him, Rom. 7.14,15, &c. But Ahab gave himself over wittingly and willfully to evil, and as a slave bound himself thereto. He gave himself wholly to satisfy the mischievous desires of his wife, the suggestions of the devil, and the lusts of his flesh, and wrought all evil with greediness, Eph. 4.19.

*{in the sight of the LORD}* Heb. *in the eyes*. Notwithstanding you know that God sees you, yet you do evil; and so do what you do in despite of God, Num. 32.13.

**V. 21.** *{Behold, I will bring evil upon thee}* See ch. 14.10. 2 Kings 9.8. This is here added as a judgment proportionable to the forementioned iniquity. Because he had *sold himself to work evil*, therefore the *Lord would bring evil upon him*; so as in v. 20. there should be a full point after *found thee*, and a comma after *Lord*.

*{and will take away thy posterity}* See ch. 16.3.

*{and will cut off from Ahab, &c.}* The same judgment that was denounced against Jeroboam, Baasha, and other idolatrous kings of Israel; because Ahab continued in their idolatrous courses. See ch. 14.10. & 1 Sam. 25.22.

**V. 22.** *{And will make thy house like the house of Jeroboam, &c.}* See ch. 16.3.

*{and like the house of Baasha, &c.}* See ch. 15.27. & 16.11. Both those were kings of Israel, as well as Ahab: And the particulars of both are distinctly set down, to show the severity of God's revenge, Heb. 10.31. Yea also to show that that which should befall Ahab, was no new matter, having been done to two kings before him. See 2 Kings 9.9.

*{for the provocation, &c.}* See ch. 14.9.

*{and made Israel to sin}* See ch. 12.30. & 14.16.

**V. 23.** *{And of Jezebel also spake the LORD, saying}* See ch. 16.31. She was the chief plotter of Naboth's murder, and therefore her doom is also expressly set down.

*{The dogs shall eat Jezebel by the wall of Jezreel}* Heb. *ditch*. This judgment was accordingly executed, 2 Kings 9.35,36,37.

**V. 24.** *{Him that dieth of Ahab in the city, shall the dogs eat, &c.}* See ch. 14.11.

**V. 25.** *{But there was none like unto Ahab}* See ch. 16.33.

*{which did sell himself, &c.}* See v. 20.

*{whom Jezebel his wife stirred up}* Or, *incited*. See v. 7,15. Gen. 3.6. Judg. 16.6. Job 2.9. ch. 11.4.

**V. 26.** *{And he did very abominably in following idols}* By idols are meant not only the golden calves, but Baal also, and other false gods which he worshiped. See ch. 16.31. 2 Kings 17.12.

*{according to all things as did the Amorites}* The Amorites were the wickedest even among the heathen, Gen. 15.16. 2 Kings 21.11.

*{whom the LORD cast out before the children of Israel}* God forbade the Israelites to do such things as the nations which were cast out before them had done, Lev. 18.24,25, &c. Yet such was their impudency that neither God's judgments on the Amorites, nor his express charge against conformity with those nations did work upon them.

**V. 27.** *{And it came to pass when Ahab heard those words}* Ahab having seen the word of the Lord spoken by Elijah, in withholding rain for three years and a half, in giving rain at the end of those years, in bringing fire down from heaven, and in sundry other remarkable matters, accomplished, could not but think that this judgment also would come to pass, and thereupon is moved, as the words following show, which set out sundry external signs of penitency, warrantable and commendable when they proceed from an inward answerable devotion.

*{that he rent his clothes}* This sets out contrition of heart; See 2 King. 18.37. & 22.11.

*{and put sackcloth upon his flesh}* By this rite was signified an acknowledgment of one's unworthiness of any soft or comely apparel, Jon. 3.6.

*{and fasted}* Hereby is signified an acknowledgment of one's unworthiness of any sustenance: 2 Sam. 12.16.

*{and lay in sackcloth}* So as he did not only wear sackcloth on him in the daytime, but also lay therein in the nighttime; which was a means to keep from sleep, that the soul might watch unto prayer, Joel 1.13. Gen. 37.34.

*{and went softly}* A sign of mourning, Isa. 38.15. and a testimony of grief for what was done.

**V. 28.** *{And the word of the LORD came to Elijah the Tishbite, saying}* ch. 17.1,2. God makes his prophets messengers of gladsome as well as of heavy tidings.
