**V. 29.** *{Seest thou}* External actions may be discerned by man.

*{how Ahab humbleth himself before me}* This was but in outward appearance, not the humiliation of a true penitent. It is said to be *before God*, not because it was in truth and from his heart, for he (turned from none of his sins:) but because he was moved thereunto by the word of God declared unto him.

*{because he humbleth himself before me, I will not bring the evil in his days}* Temporary humiliation may put off or defer a temporary judgment. And if God be so far moved with external humiliation, how prevalent is the hearty humiliation of a true penitent? 2 King. 22.19.

*{but in his son's days will I bring the evil upon his house}* i. e. his posterity, friends, and others that belonged unto him. See the uttermost of this judgment accomplished 2 King. 9, & 10.

# CHAPTER 22

**V. 1.** *{And they continued three years}* These must be reckoned from the covenant between Ahab and Benhadad, ch. 20.34. which was made in the nineteenth year of Ahab. For Ahab died in this expedition, v. 37. in the two and twentieth year of his reign, ch. 16.22.

*{without war between Syria and Israel}* One would have thought that covenant mentioned ch. 20.34. would have been more lasting. But enemies use not to keep covenant longer than may be for their advantage.

**V. 2.** *{And it came to pass in the third year}* This was in the seventeenth year of Jehoshaphat, v. 51. After he had made peace v. 44. and affinity with Ahab, 2 Chron. 18.1. 2 King. 8.11.

*{that Jehoshaphat the king of Judah came down to the king of Israel}* Jehoshaphat might well be said to come down, both in regard of the situation of the place; for Jerusalem was higher than Samaria: and also in regard of the disposition of his mind. It was too low a descending for a king of Judah, to go down to a king of Israel. None of his predecessors had done it before him. But much more too low was it for a worshiper of the true God to go down to such an idolater as Ahab was. See 2 Chron. 19.2. Surely it was some policy that moved him hereunto. It may be that by a union betwixt the two kingdoms they both might be the better fenced against the king of Syria, who began to wax very great, and was an ill neighbor to them both.

**V. 3.** *{And the king of Israel said unto his servants}* Under servants are here comprised such counselors of state as upon all occasions attended on the king.

*{Know ye that}* This interrogative is a vehement asseveration.

*{Ramoth in Gilead}* See ch. 4.13.

*{is ours}* Ramoth-Gilead was a part of Israel's inheritance, even one of their cities of refuge, Josh. 20.8. And though it were taken away by Benhadad's father, yet it was promised to be restored again by Benhadad himself, ch. 20.34. But some think the Syrians had possession of it before Benhadad's father's time, and that Benhadad thence took occasion to hold it, in that it was not within the letter of his promise, which was this, *The cities which my father took from thy father, I will restore.*

*{and we be still, and take it not}* Heb. *silent from taking it*. The word implies a forbearing to do, as well as to speak. Judg. 18.9.

*{out of the hand of the king of Syria}* The cause of Ahab's war was just, but he was not a fit instrument to manage a just war, because he continued in his idolatry notwithstanding the evidence against it, ch. 18.39. Therefore God left him, v. 34, &c.

**V. 4.** *{And he said to Jehoshaphat, wilt thou go with me to battle to Ramoth-Gilead?}* Ahab had good cause to desire Jehoshaphat's assistance: for he could not but know how wonderfully God had blessed Jehoshaphat, and made him strong, 2 Chr. 17.10,11, &c. Herein therefore he dealt prudently.

*{And Jehoshaphat said to the king of Israel, I am as thou art, my people as thy people, my horses as thy horses}* 2 King. 2.7. His meaning is, that he himself, his people, his beasts, all that he had were ready to go and do as Ahab would order them, even as if they had been all Ahab's own. See v. 44.

**V. 5.** *{And Jehoshaphat said to the king of Israel, enquire I pray thee, at the word of the LORD}* Before he went to battle, Jehoshaphat would have him send for some prophet of the Lord, to know the mind of God, whether he would prosper their design or no. An evidence of his piety and prudence. Thus God's servants used to do, Judg. 1.1. & 20.28. 1 Sam. 23.2.

*{to day}* Presently, before we prepare any further for the war.

**V. 6.** *{Then the king of Israel gathered the prophets together}* He supposed that by a multitude gathered together Jehoshaphat might be the better persuaded of the truth of that which they should deliver.

*{about four hundred men}* It is probable that these were the prophets of the groves mentioned, ch. 18.19.

*{and said unto them, Shall I go against Ramoth-Gilead to battle, or shall I forbear?}* The question is fairly propounded. But question may be made whether he would have stood to their resolution if their answer had been contrary to his purpose. See v. 15,16.

*{And they said, Go up}* They received not this answer from the Lord, but according to Ahab's humor.

*{for the LORD}* They pretend the name of the Lord, whom they served not, to delude such as heard them, that they might think they revealed the mind of the Lord; and to collogue with Jehoshaphat who was a worshiper of the Lord. It was the practice of idolaters to give the title of the true God to their idols, Exod. 32.4,5.

*{shall deliver it into the hand of the king}* The particle it is not in the Hebrew. And if it be left out, their answer will be like the oracles of Apollo, doubtful and ambiguous, so as it may be taken either one way or other; either thus, The Lord shall deliver Ramoth-Gilead into the hand of the king of Israel; which sense they certainly intended: Or thus, The Lord shall deliver you into the hand of the king of Syria.

**V. 7.** *{And Jehoshaphat said, is there not here a prophet of the LORD besides}* i. e. besides these prophets of the groves. Jehoshaphat suspected their word was not from the Lord.

*{that we might enquire of him}* It seems Jehoshaphat had a very earnest desire to know God's mind in this case; yet when it was made known, he was not careful to do accordingly, v. 8.28.

**V. 8.** *{And the king of Israel said to Jehoshaphat, There is yet one man}* Yet, notwithstanding many of them have been slain, ch. 18.4. & 19.10. and some fled, so as I know not where to find them. As for Elijah, he had so oft concealed himself from Ahab, that he knew not where to have him. It may be gathered from this phrase *Carry him back again*, v. 26. that Micaiah was in prison when Ahab sent for him, so as he knew where to have him.

*{Micaiah the son of Imlah}* His parentage is added to distinguish him from another prophet, Mic. 1.1. It is supposed this Micaiah was that prophet who denounced the judgment against Ahab, ch. 20.42. and that therefore he could not endure him.

*{By whom we may enquire of the LORD, but I hate him}* It has always been the lot of such as have been faithful in declaring the truth to be hated, Gen. 37.8. Psal. 38.19. Amos 5.10. Joh. 7.7. Truth gets hatred.

*{for he doth not prophesy good concerning me}* By good he means that which is pleasing unto him, Jer. 38.4. Or that which tended to his honor or profit.

*{but evil}* i. e. terrible things, which tend to my shame, damage, and undoing. This was the reason why he was not sent for before.

*{And Jehoshaphat said, Let not the king say so}* This is a modest check, whereby Jehoshaphat implies that God's word, though it seem unpleasing, is to be hearkened unto.

**V. 9.** *{Then the king of Israel called an officer}* Or, *Eunuch*. The word signifies *one that is gelded*, Isa. 56.3. So the Greek, Matth. 19.12. The Persians, Babylonians and other heathens were wont to appoint such to be chamberlains to queens and great ladies, for the surer guarding of their continency. Such also were attendant upon kings and other great persons: whence it came to be a title given to great officers that attended on royal or honorable persons, whether they were gelded or no, Gen. 39.1. See on Gen. 37.36. Potiphar had a wife, though he be styled an eunuch, Gen. 39.7.

*{and said, hasten hither Micaiah the son of Imlah}* He made the greater haste in sending for him, that he might the more gratify Jehoshaphat.

**V. 10.** *{And the king of Israel and Jehoshaphat king of Judah sat each on his throne}* There were two thrones set for these two kings, as ch. 2.19.

*{having put on their robes}* i. e. their royal robes, which now they put on to set out their majesty to the people, as Act. 12.21. The LXX. set out their kings clad in armor.

*{in a void place}* The word is used to signify a barn or a floor where corn is threshed, as Ruth 3.3. 2 Sam. 24.18. but here and in other places, for a large place or ground for multitude of people to stand on together; Gen. 50.10.

*{in the entrance of the gate of Samaria}* Kings' thrones and seats of justice were in the gates of cities, that so all manner of people passing to and fro, might see and hear.

*{and all the prophets prophesied before them}* See ch. 18.19.

**V. 11.** *{And Zedekiah the son of Chenaanah}* His parentage distinguishes him from that Zedechiah Jer. 20.21. This was the chief among all those prophets, and as a father of them.

*{made him horns of iron}* This he did as a sign of the truth of his prophecy, and in imitation of true prophets, Jer. 27.2. & 28.13. Zech. 1.18,19. By horns, bulls and other horned creatures defend themselves and offend such as stand against them. Chenaanah made his of iron, to set out the strength of the two kings the more.

*{and he said, Thus saith the LORD}* The false prophets express the name Jehovah, the Lord, to please Jehoshaphat, and to gain the more credit to their prophecy. See v. 6.

*{with these shalt thou push the Syrians}* As horned beasts push and gore other beasts till they have wounded and destroyed them, so shall your army deal with the Syrians.

*{until thou have consumed them}* This implies an utter destruction, so as they should not be able to get head again.

**V. 12.** *{And all the prophets prophesied so}* They all agree together in the same song with Zedechiah.

*{saying, Go up to Ramoth-Gilead, and prosper}* This word prosper is added the more fully to express the intent of their prophecy. To prosper is to have good success in an enterprise.

*{for the LORD shall deliver it into the king's hand}* See v. 6.

**V. 13.** *{And the messenger that was gone to call Micaiah}* He that was styled an officer, v. 9. is here called a messenger; for he had a message committed to his charge.

*{spake to him, saying}* That which he spoke was beyond his commission; so as herein he did not show himself a faithful messenger.

*{Behold now, the words of the prophets declare good unto the king}* See v. 8. This messenger knew that the prophecy of the four hundred prophets pleased Ahab and the people well, and therefore he calls it good.

*{with one mouth}* With one consent, as if all had expressed their mind with one mouth.

*{let thy word, I pray thee, be like the word of one of them}* He makes a pretense of kindness to the prophet, because he had heard how hard an opinion the king had of him, v. 8.

*{and speak that which is good}* That which is pleasing to king and people.

**V. 14.** *{And Micaiah said, As the LORD liveth}* This was a usual concise form of an oath. See ch. 1.29. & 17.1.

*{what the LORD saith unto me, that will I speak}* This was a note of a true prophet, Jer. 23.28. & 42.4.

**V. 15.** *{So he came to the king}* Micaiah, though he knew he should bring a displeasing message to the king, did not forbear to come and declare it.

*{and the king said unto him, Micaiah, shall we go against Ramoth-Gilead to battle, or shall we forbear?}* The king fairly propounds the case, as if he intended wholly to refer himself to the pleasure of God which should be made known by this prophet; but the event shows that he was fully settled to go. So dealt the Jews, Jer. 42.5,6, &c.

*{And he answered him, Go, and prosper, &c.}* He uses the very same words that the false prophets had done before, v. 12. but he spoke them ironically, to discover their falsehood more evidently, and uttered the words in such a manner as Ahab discerned them to be a mere mock. For an ironical speech may more plainly be observed by the manner of uttering it than by reading it. This way of discovering the folly of men is frequent in Scripture; as ch. 18.27. Gen. 3.22. 2 Chron. 25.8. Eccl. 11.9. Lam. 4.21. Amos 4.4,5.

**V. 16.** *{And the king said unto him, How many times shall I adjure thee}* The interrogation implies a vehement asseveration, thus, Again, and again, as by a solemn oath, I charge you.

*{that thou tell me nothing, but that which is true}* He that regarded not truth at all made show of regarding nothing but truth.

*{in the name of the LORD}* That truth which is received from the Lord. For Micaiah in his answer v. 15. no way implied that he spoke in the name of the Lord. The king falsely imagined that it was the Lord's will that he should go and recover Ramoth-Gilead: Because he *retained not the love of the truth, God gave him over to believe strong delusions.* 2 Thess. 2.10,11.

**V. 17.** *{And he said, I saw}* This he saw in a vision, as Ezek. 1.4, &c. Act. 10.11,17. A vision was a representation of things to a man's imagination while he was awake, wherein it differed from a dream.

*{all Israel}* All that were drawn forth to war.

*{scattered upon the hills}* There were many hills in Israel full of trees; to them would soldiers put to flight betake themselves.

*{as sheep}* Pursued by wolves.

*{that have not a shepherd}* He implies that the king, who was as a shepherd, should be slain, and thereupon the people fly, v. 34,35.

*{And the LORD said}* To his prophet in the vision.

*{These have no master}* Their king being slain.

*{let them return every man to his house}* To this purpose was a proclamation made, v. 36.

*{in peace}* Peace is here put for safety, and is opposed to death and destruction. They escaped with their lives.

**V. 18.** *{And the king of Israel said unto Jehoshaphat}* Ahab might fear that Micaiah's prophecy would discourage Jehoshaphat: therefore he seeks to impeach the truth thereof.

*{Did I not tell thee that he would prophesy no good concerning me but evil?}* See v. 8. Ahab would make Jehoshaphat believe that Micaiah intended Ahab no good, and declared the former vision to cross and vex them.

**V. 19.** *{And he said}* Micaiah said.

*{Hear thou therefore the word of the LORD}* Because you did adjure me to tell the truth in the name of the Lord, I do now do it plainly and fully.

*{I saw}* As v. 17.

*{the LORD sitting on his throne}* As a judge, Isa. 6.1. Dan. 7.9.

*{and all the host of heaven}* Angels, who as a host or army of soldiers attend the Lord, Psal. 103.20,21. Evil spirits were also mixed among these.

*{standing by him, on his right hand and on his left}* Whether the good spirits were on his right hand only, and the evil on his left, is uncertain. But thus God sets forth in his glorious majesty as a sovereign on his throne, with all sorts of people attending round about him, the more to beat down the sour spirit of Ahab, who was here set in his majesty, v. 10.

**V. 20.** *{And the LORD said}* All the discourse following in this vision is by way of resemblance, and that after the manner of man, applied to God. *For who is his counsellor?* Isa. 40.13.

*{Who shall persuade Ahab}* Or, *deceive*. For he was persuaded to a matter wherein he was much deceived.

*{that he may go up and fall at Ramoth-Gilead}* He went up with assurance of victory, but found destruction.

*{And one said on this manner, and another said on that manner}* This is spoken of the spirits that stood about the throne. And it is not to be taken as if there were diversity of opinions among the heavenly spirits, but rather to show the various passages of divine providence.

**V. 21.** *{And there came forth a spirit, and stood before the LORD}* An evil spirit that is here meant: for he calls himself *a lying spirit*, v. 22. Evil spirits can for their advantage thrust themselves into the company of good spirits, Job 1.6.

*{and said, I will persuade him}* How he persuaded Ahab is manifested v. 22. False prophets are instructed by evil spirits, as true prophets are by the Spirit of God.

**V. 22.** *{And the LORD said unto him, Wherewith?}* God propounds this question, not to be informed, but more clearly to manifest that evil spirits can do nothing without his providence.

*{And he said, I will go forth}* Hereby it is manifest that even evil spirits are ready to execute the vengeance of the Lord, and that by reason both of the power of God overruling them, and also of their own readiness to do hurt unto men.

*{and I will be a lying spirit}* Heb. *a spirit of falsehood, *or,* of lying*. i. e. He would so work on Ahab's prophets as they should declare false things. Herein is verified what Christ says of the devil Joh. 8.44. In this respect he is also called *a Spirit of error*, 1 Joh. 4.6. because he puts on men to err, lie and deceive. Rev. 12.9.

*{in the mouth of all his prophets}* The devil speaks in false teachers.

*{And he said, Thou shalt persuade him, and prevail also}* Because Ahab hated God's true prophets, v. 8. therefore God in justice gave him over to believe lies, 2 Thess. 2.11,12.

*{Go forth, and do so}* God sends forth even evil spirits to do acts of justice. But this implies not a commission, so much as a permission.

**V. 23.** *{Now therefore behold}* The prophet calls the king to a serious consideration of a matter very remarkable in itself, and nearly concerning him.

*{the LORD hath put a lying spirit in the mouth of all these thy prophets}* God did this as a just judge, making an evil spirit his executioner to punish Ahab. So 2 Sam. 16.10. Job 12.16. Ezek. 14.9. Sin may be punished with sin, and that justly, Rom. 1.24. 2 Sam. 12.11,12. See on Judg. 9.23.

*{and the LORD hath spoken evil concerning thee}* He has both determined, and also denounced judgment: for that he here calls evil, Amos 3.6.

**V. 24.** *{But Zedekiah the son of Chenaanah went near, and smote Micaiah on the cheek}* Most impudently before the two kings and the whole assembly he manifested his malice against the Lord's faithful prophet, and that reproachfully too, Job 16.10. Act. 23.2. with blows he answered him because he had no better reasons.

*{and said, Which way went the Spirit of the LORD from me to speak unto thee?}* 2 Chr. 18.23. Zedekiah pretended that he himself had the Spirit of the Lord, v. 11. and thereupon here infers that Micaiah had it not, because it could not be shown how it passed from one to the other.

**V. 25.** *{And Micaiah said}* He returns not blow for blow, but only declares what God had determined.

*{Behold, thou shalt see}* By woeful experience you shall find that I by the Spirit of the Lord have declared a truth. To see is oft put for an experimental proof, as Gen. 26.28. Matth. 2.15.

*{in that day}* The day wherein Micaiah's prophecy should be accomplished, the king being slain, and his army worsted.

*{when thou shalt go into an inner chamber}* Or, *from chamber to chamber*. Heb. *a chamber in a chamber*. See ch. 20.30.

*{to hide thyself}* From Ahab's children and friends, who when they should see the fearful issue of Zedekiah's prophecy, would seek up and down for that false prophet, to revenge the blood of the king and overthrow of the army upon him.

**V. 26.** *{And the king of Israel said}* The king of Judah sat mute, and dared not speak for him whom he knew to be a prophet of God. Ill company mars good men.

*{Take Micaiah, and carry him back unto Amon the governour of the city}* This Amon was as a lord mayor in Samaria. He had the power to cast into prison. See v. 8.

*{and to Joash the king's son}* Joash might be appointed governor of the kingdom while the king his father went to war; as Jotham was when his father was a leper, 2 Chr. 26.21. Some take this Joash to be Omri's son.

**V. 27.** *{And say, Thus saith the king}* To stir them up to do what he bids, he sends the charge in his own name.

*{Put this fellow in the prison}* God's faithful prophets are oft used as malefactors.

*{and feed him with bread of affliction, and with water of affliction}* With no more than may sustain life, that he may be preserved, but in affliction and want.

*{until I come in peace}* Ahab's phrase implies that Micaiah should be kept fast until the victory were obtained, and that he would then bring him to trial for his life. But he made too sure account of victory.

**V. 28.** *{And Micaiah said, If thou return at all in peace}* Heb. *returning you return*. Of this Hebraism see ch. 8.13. It implies that he never should return.

*{the LORD hath not spoken by me}* He has reference to the trial of a prophet, Deut. 18.21,22. See Numb. 16.29.

*{And he said, Hearken, O people, every one of you}* He took them all to witness in this case.

**V. 29.** *{So the king of Israel, and Jehoshaphat king of Judah went up to Ramoth-Gilead}* Though Jehoshaphat in sundry other things gave good evidence that the true fear of God was in him, yet here he showed great failing, 2 Chron. 19.1. Micaiah's prophecy should have restrained him.

**V. 30.** *{And the king of Israel said to Jehoshaphat}* If the former circumstances and the event following be well weighed, it will appear, that it was no friendly part, to put that upon Jehoshaphat, which Ahab here put on him.

*{I will disguise myself, and enter into the battle}* Or, *when he was to disguise himself, and enter into the battle*. He meant to disguise himself by changing his habit, and going into the battle, not as a king, but as an inferior commander, that so he might not be known to be who he was. See ch. 14.2. & 20.38. Questionless he was possessed with no small fear upon Micaiah's prophecy: It may be also that he had had some inkling of the king of Syria's charge, v. 31. Yet perfidiously he advises Jehoshaphat to go into the battle as a king, it may be upon pretense of honor, in making him the generalissimo.

*{but put thou on thy robes}* Such as become a king. Or, such kind of armor as is fit for the general of the field. See v. 10.

*{And the king of Israel disguised himself}* He altered his royal habit, as before.

*{and went into the battle}* He had a mind to fight, though not to appear in the battle as a king.

**V. 31.** *{But the king of Syria commanded his thirty and two captains}* Probably, those who were put into the place of the two and thirty kings, ch. 20.24.

*{that had rule over his chariots}* These had the command of all the chivalry. Of chariots see 1 Chron. 18.4.

*{saying, Fight neither with small nor great, save only with the king of Israel}* This is a proverbial speech, whereby Benhadad does not forbid his servants to fight against any other than the king, but he commanded them to observe the king especially, and to bend their main force against him, and not to suffer themselves by fighting with others to be kept from setting upon him. This he might do in general for the more speedy obtaining of the victory: For *smite the shepherd, and the sheep will be scattered*, v. 17. or in special, to bring Ahab under his mercy, as he himself had formerly been brought under Ahab's, ch. 20.33.

**V. 32.** *{And it came to pass, when the captains of the chariots saw Jehoshaphat}* In such a royal habit and posture as declared him to be a king.

*{that they said, Surely it is the king of Israel}* They knew not the king of Israel and king of Judah so well as to distinguish the one from the other.

*{And they turned aside to fight against him}* They turned from all others in Ahab's army, as they were commanded, v. 31. and compassed about Jehoshaphat, 2 Chr. 18.31.

*{and Jehoshaphat cried out}* He earnestly prayed to the Lord to help him in that strait. See ch. 17.20,21. 2 King. 2.12. Exod. 14.15. Some take this his crying out to be to the Syrians, by manifesting that he was not the king of Israel. But the inference of *the Lord's helping him*, 2 Chr. 18.31. shows that crying to the Lord is here intended.

**V. 33.** *{And it came to pass, when the captains of the chariots perceived that it was not the king of Israel}* This they perceived by some special work of the Lord; and this may be an instance of God's helping Jehoshaphat.

*{that they turned back from pursuing him}* For he was not the man they aimed at.

**V. 34.** *{And a certain man drew a bow at a venture}* Heb. *in his simplicity*. Not purposely aiming at Ahab, nor yet knowing God's purpose to have Ahab slain. See 2 Sam. 15.11. This is an apparent evidence that God's providence orders even such things as are most casual.

*{and smote the king of Israel between the joints of the harness}* Heb. *joints and breastplate*. There are several pieces of harness, which use to be fastened together upon a man. Now between two pieces of Ahab's armor the arrow entered and pierced some of his vital parts.

*{Wherefore he said unto the driver of his chariot, Turn thine hand}* He was now marching on against the face of the enemy; but being wounded, he commands to turn the chariot.

*{and carry me out of the host}* Not clean out of the field; for he still continued against the enemy, v. 35.

*{for I am wounded}* Heb. *made sick*. Or, *being wounded I am ready to faint*. See 2 King. 1.2. He said I am sick, rather than mortally wounded, that his danger might not be divulged throughout the host, and the whole army be discouraged.

**V. 35.** *{And the battle increased}* Heb. *ascended*. Grew more and more fierce.

*{that day}* Whereon the king was wounded.

*{and the king was stayed up}* Or *stayed himself up*, 2 Chr. 18.34. He was not struck stark dead, but retaining some vigor, he extended it as far as he could, to keep his army together from flying away. For though he were carried aside out of the host, v. 34. to bind up his wound, yet did he not clean leave the army.

*{in his chariot against the Syrians}* Some read in the plural number, his chariots, and apply it to the chariots of his captains, that by the king's abiding in the field, they might be kept from flight.

*{and died at even}* Namely, of the same day whereon he was wounded.

*{and the blood ran out of the wound into the midst of the chariot}* Heb. *bosom*. i. e. that place whereon they rested their feet in the chariot: or: in which place abundance of blood might be contained without running through, that so it might be preserved for the dogs to lick up, v. 38.

**V. 36.** *{And there went a proclamation throughout the host}* When the commanders had notice of the king's death, they had no heart to maintain the fight any longer, and thereupon caused the proclamation to be made. See ch. 15.22.

*{about the going down of the sun}* They could not have had much time to continue the fight, the sun being set, or about to set.

*{saying, Every man to his city, and every man to his own country}* This was a phrase of retreat and giving over the battle. See a like phrase, but in another sense, ch. 12.16. Herein the word of the prophet was accomplished, v. 17.

**V. 37.** *{So the king died}* He died in the field, 2 Chron. 18.34.

*{and was brought}* Heb. *came*. He was now dead; so as his coming must needs be by the help of others, or rather it must be their act to bring him.

*{to Samaria}* There was his chief abiding-place while he lived.

*{and they buried the king in Samaria}* See ch. 16.24,28.

**V. 38.** *{And one washed the chariot in the pool of Samaria}* This pool reached from Samaria to Jezreel, where was the king's armory, and where his chariot stood. Thus though it be styled the pool of Samaria, yet the chariot and armor might be washed in Jezreel, where Naboth was stoned, ch. 21.19.

*{and the dogs licked up his blood}* Dogs use to lick up blood where they find it on the ground. But this was ordered by a special providence of God, as a just judgment on Ahab for consenting to Naboth's death, ch. 21.19.

*{and they washed his armour}* Which being about him when he was wounded might in the hollow places thereof contain some blood.

*{according unto the word of the LORD which he spake}* This may either in general have reference to Ahab's death, v. 17. ch. 20.42. or in particular to the dogs licking up his blood, ch. 21.19.

**V. 39.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Ahab}* See ch. 16.27.

*{and the ivory-house which he made}* Of things made of ivory see ch. 10.18. Amos 3.15. & on Cant. 7.4.

*{and all the cities that he built}* Ahab reigned two and twenty years, ch. 16.29. and having much peace in his time, and withal enemies round about him, he might build both houses for pomp and cities for defense.

*{are they not written in the book of the chronicles of the kings of Israel?}* See ch. 11.41. & 16.27.

**V. 40.** *{So Ahab slept with his fathers}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and Ahaziah his son reigned in his stead}* See v. 51.

**V. 41.** *{And Jehoshaphat the son of Asa began to reign over Judah}* 2 Chron. 20.31. That which was before set down concerning Jehoshaphat, was in regard of his joining with Ahab; so as it properly belongs to Ahab's story: which being finished, the beginning of Jehoshaphat's reign is now set down.

*{in the fourth year of Ahab king of Israel}* This was the fourth current. For Ahab began his reign in the thirty eighth of Asa, ch. 16.29. who reigned one and forty years, ch. 15.10.

**V. 42.** *{Jehoshaphat was thirty and five years old when he began to reign}* Heb. *a son of thirty five years*.

*{and he reigned twenty and five years in Jerusalem}* These are to be reckoned from the beginning of his reign to the end of his life; all which time he was true king, though his son were sometimes governor over the land. See 2 King. 1.17.

*{and his mother's name was Azubah the daughter of Shilhi}* See ch. 14.21. & 15.10. His parentage by the mother and grandmother is set down for honor's sake.

**V. 43.** *{And he walked in all the ways of Asa his father}* i. e. the former ways of Asa, 2 Chron. 17.3. for in his latter years he fell into many sins, 2 Chron. 16.2, &c.

*{he turned not aside from it}* This shows both his integrity in all duties, and his constancy therein.

*{doing that which was right in the sight of the LORD}* This sets out his sincerity. See ch. 5.5.

*{nevertheless the high places were not taken away}* See ch. 15.14.

*{for the people offered}* See ch. 15.14.

*{and burnt incense}* See ch. 9.25.

*{yet}* Still after a great reformation, 2 Chron. 17.9. & 19.4, &c.

*{in the high places}* See ch. 3.4.

**V. 44.** *{And Jehoshaphat made peace}* See v. 2. This peace with idolatrous Israel brought the wrath of God upon Jehoshaphat, 2 Chron. 19.2.

*{with the king of Israel}* He made peace both with Ahab, v. 4. and also with Jehoram Ahab's son, 2 King. 3.7.

**V. 45.** *{Now the rest of the acts of Jehoshaphat}* See ch. 14.19.

*{and his might that he shewed}* See ch. 15.23.

*{and how he warred}* See 2 Chr. 20.1, &c.

*{are they not written in the book of the chronicles of the kings of Judah?}* See ch. 15.23.

**V. 46.** *{And the remnant of the sodomites which remained in the days of his father Asa, he took out of the land}* See ch. 15.12. Sodomites were countenanced in Rehoboam's time, ch. 14.24. and rooted out in Asa's, ch. 15.12. Yet it appears that they grew again in Jehoshaphat's. So as either Asa at first destroyed them not all, but some escaped his hands; or in the latter end of his reign when he fell from God, 2 Chr. 16.10. they rose again; or when Asa was dead, they dreamed of a toleration, and thereupon exercised their filthiness.

**V. 47.** *{There was then no king in Edom}* From the time that David subdued Edom, 2 Sam. 8.14. till Joram's reign, 2 King. 8.20. Edom was in subjection to Judah. Then was Isaac's prophecy of Jacob's being lord over his brother, Gen. 27.29. accomplished. See on Gen. 25.23.

*{a deputy was king}* This deputy used to be put in by the king of Judah, and under him governed Edom.

**V. 48.** *{Jehoshaphat made ships}* Or, *had ten ships*.

*{of Tarshish}* Or, *for Tarshish*. See ch. 10.22.

*{to go to Ophir for gold}* See ch. 9.28. Tarshish might be the general name of the whole region or country whither the ships went, and Ophir the name of that particular place where choice gold was. Jehoshaphat herein imitated Solomon, ch. 10.22.

*{but they went not}* God can cross men's projects.

*{for the ships were broken}* This was ordered by the special providence of God, to punish Jehoshaphat for joining with wicked Ahaziah, 2 Chr. 20.37.

*{at Ezion-Geber}* See ch. 9.26. This shows that the ships had not put far on to sea.

**V. 49.** *{Then said Ahaziah the son of Ahab unto Jehoshaphat}* Though their former attempt of setting forth a navy were frustrated, yet Ahaziah would fain have made another, as if the loss they had suffered had been a mere casualty. Wicked ones seldom behold God in his judgments.

*{Let my servants go with thy servants in the ships}* He supposed that by association with Jehoshaphat's servants he might have the better success.

*{But Jehoshaphat would not}* He had been reproved by a prophet of the Lord for joining with Ahaziah in one expedition, 2 Chr. 20.37. and thereupon is made more wise than to join with him again.

**V. 50.** *{And Jehoshaphat slept, &c.}* See ch. 2.10.

*{and Jehoram his son reigned in his stead}* An impious son succeeded a pious father. Grace does not always descend from father to child. Of Jehoram's wickedness, see 2 Chr. 21.6, &c.

**V. 51.** *{Ahaziah the son of Ahab began to reign over Israel in Samaria}* See ch. 16.29.

*{the seventeenth year of Jehoshaphat king of Judah}* See 2 King. 1.17.

*{and reigned two years over Israel}* These are to be reckoned current, as ch. 15.2,25. His first year was in common with Ahab his father, his second with Jehoram his brother.

**V. 52.** *{And he did evil in the sight of the LORD}* See ch. 14.20.

*{and walked in the way of his father}* i. e. Ahab, whose way was as bad as bad could be. See ch. 16.33.

*{and in the way of his mother}* Jezebel, who was worse than his father. See ch. 16.31.

*{and in the way of Jeroboam the son of Nebat}* Jeroboam was he that caused the division betwixt Judah and Israel; and brought all Israel to worship the calves, ch. 12.28, &c.

*{who made Israel to sin}* See ch. 14.16.

**V. 53.** *{For he served Baal, and worshipped him}* i. e. Ahaziah yielded divine honor to Baal, as to a true God: So did Ahab before him. See ch. 16.31.

*{and provoked to anger}* See ch. 14.9.

*{the LORD God of Israel}* See ch. 8.15.

*{according to all that his father had done}* Men are prone to hold close to evil patterns.
